My Dearest Doctor,

Thirteen long moons ago, just as it looked like our little drama was about to hit "The End" with a tragic flourish, a very nice chap dragged you over to my bedside. Honestly, without your round-the-clock vigilance and your medical wizardry, I'm pretty sure I'd still be lying there, staring at the ceiling tiles until the heatstroke took me.

I simply must pause to thank you for your immense goodness—and by extension, the surprisingly high level of sympathy shown by everyone else during that dark hour. It's funny how people suddenly become angels when they're actually useful; usually, we just see them as obstacles or bad company. But back then, kindness wasn't just polite; it was life-saving.

Since you seem to operate on a strictly non-monetary basis (refusing any fee other than gratitude), allow me to put these thanks down permanently in black and white. Consider this my official receipt, signed on behalf of me and my entire family.

Yours most sincerely and gratefully,

If this collection of scribbles, representing two years of intermittent effort, fails to achieve artistic perfection—a failure it likely shares with almost every other work of its kind—it does possess one redeeming quality: a certain raw honesty that a more polished masterpiece might sacrifice. By maintaining a constant dialogue with you, the reader, I am compelled toward a frankness of expression, forced to voice my thoughts and feelings exactly as they surge within me. Consequently, I have spotted numerous slips of the pen and the press, along with words hastily uttered, that I wish I could retract upon reviewing this volume.

Think of this less as a formal publication and more as a confidential chat between writer and reader. Such conversations are prone to dragging and losing momentum. As I ramble, inevitably exposing my own frailties, vanities, and eccentricities, you must judge me not by a single outburst or a fleeting mood, but by the general tenor of my conduct. When a man speaks freely, you naturally ask: *Is he truthful? Does he strive to uncover and articulate the reality?* Is he a charlatan feigning sentimentality just for applause? Or is he merely chasing popularity through cheap tricks?

I cannot deny the role of luck in my success any more than anyone else can claim credit for random chance. I have attracted far more readers than I ever dared hope for. While I have no right to demand that you never criticize my craft or nod off while reading, I earnestly ask you to believe that the person holding the pen here is striving to tell the truth. Without that foundation, there is absolutely nothing.

Perhaps those who crave "excitement" should know that this book originally began with a rigid, thrilling blueprint that was promptly discarded. Ladies and gentlemen, you were promised—and both the author's and the publisher's pockets stood to gain—a tale of high-octane horror. Imagine a ruffian (possessing many admirable virtues) lurking in St. Giles's, constantly pursued by a young lady from Belgravia! Picture the electric contrast between high society and the gutter, the clash of slang and polite discourse, the daring escapes, the brutal battles, and the grisly murders! Indeed, until nine o'clock this very morning, my poor friend Colonel Altamont was sentenced to hang, and I only spared him when he appeared at the window.

That "thrilling" plan was abandoned—with great honor shown by the publishers—because, upon trying to execute it, I realized I lacked the necessary expertise. Having never been close to a convict in my life, and finding the manners of criminals and prisoners utterly alien to me, I decided that competing with M. Eugène Sue was a fool's errand. To describe a genuine scoundrel, one must render him so horrifically vile that he becomes unfit for display; conversely, if a painter depicts a villain fairly, I maintain they have no business showing him at all.

Even the gentlemen of our time—this book attempts to portray one such man, neither better nor worse than the average educated male—we cannot expose as they truly are, complete with their notorious pettiness and self-centeredness bred by their upbringing. Since the burial of the author of *Tom Jones*, no fiction writer among us has been allowed to depict a MAN to his fullest potential. We must drape him in convention, forcing him into a polite, artificial smile. Society simply will not tolerate the "Natural" in our art.

Many ladies have protested, and some subscribers have withdrawn, because, during the course of the narrative, I described a young man struggling against and ultimately succumbing to temptation. My intention was to demonstrate that he possessed the capacity to feel passion, yet retained enough manliness and generosity to overcome it. However, you will not hear about it—and it is perhaps best that you don't know—the gritty realities that move the world, the undercurrents of social life, the whispers in clubs, colleges, and mess rooms. This is where the true lives and conversations of your sons unfold.

A degree of frankness greater than custom dictates has been attempted here. With no malicious intent on my part and hoping for no harmful consequences to any reader, I offer this glimpse. If truth is not always pleasant, it remains superior to falsehood, whether argued from the lofty chairs of grave philosophers or delivered from the humble seat of the storyteller as he finishes his labor and bids his kind reader farewell.

KENSINGTON, November 26th, 1850.

One crisp morning during the height of London's social season, Major Arthur Pendennis arrived at his favorite club in Pall Mall, just as he did every day. To the members of this exclusive establishment, he was less a guest and more the undisputed manager. As one of England's premier connoisseurs of wine and a man whose inquiring spirit dominated every conversation, he sat atop the committee, treated by stewards and waiters with the deference usually reserved for Dukes or Field Marshals.

By ten fifteen, the Major made his entrance. He was a study in impeccable military fashion *en retraite*. His boots were the blackest in London, his cravat checked and untouched by wrinkles until dinner, and his buff waistcoat boasted buttons stamped with the royal crown. His linen was so pristine that Beau Brummel himself reportedly paused to ask the name of his laundrywoman (though Brummel, forced into exile by bad luck, never got the chance to hire her). From his perfect white gloves and "Wellington-pattern" nose to the gold buttons gifted by the Duke of York and the rings bearing the Pendennis crest, every detail screamed aristocratic authority. Even his cane was flawless. While a casual glance might suggest he was barely thirty, a closer look revealed the artificial sheen of his brown hair and the faint crow's feet around his handsome, mottled eyes.

He claimed his usual corner table by the fire, a spot so fiercely guarded that no one dared challenge him. Formerly, a few reckless louts had tried to evict him, but the sheer quiet dignity with which the Major settled in, surveying the room through his gold-rimmed spectacles, ensured his reign was unbroken. This table became his throne. Letters awaited him there, piled high with seals and franks that bewildered the younger set. Whether asking about proper etiquette, marital status, or the exact age of a specific Duke, everyone turned to Pendennis for the answer. Ladies-in-waiting drove up specifically to deliver notes, and young men flocked to walk beside him in the Park, simply because touching his hat to him felt like shaking hands with royalty.

As the waiters brought his toast and the morning papers, Pendennis began his daily ritual: sorting his correspondence. He carried his glasses with such flair that they seemed mere accessories rather than tools of vision. He methodically arranged the notes—solemn dinner cards promising heavy conversation, intimate missives from women, and official invites. There was a summons from the Marquis of Steyne to a party at the Star and Garter, requiring fluent French (which the Major spoke perfectly), and an invitation from the Bishop of Ealing and Mrs. Trail. He read them all with graceful satisfaction, particularly enjoying the spectacle of Glowry, the Scottish surgeon sitting opposite, glaring with envy at the mountain of invitations that never included the poor doctor.

With a swift calculation in his pocket-book, the Major decided his schedule. He tossed aside a dinner with Cutler, the East India Director, to prioritize Lord Steyne's French-speaking soirée. He accepted the Bishop's invite, reasoning that despite the slow service, dining with clergy was essential. Having cleared his calendar based on whim and interest, he turned to the newspapers, checking the birth and death columns and ensuring his name appeared on the guest lists of upcoming fêtes. Between glances at the gazette, he chatted cheerfully with the room's occupants.

But amidst the whirlwind of high society, one letter stood apart. It lay solitary, marked with a rustic country postmark and a simple seal, ignored while the grandees of London vied for attention. Written in a delicate female hand and stamped "Immediate" with a dash of anxious urgency, it had been waiting patiently in the metaphorical ante-chamber. Finally, it was its turn.

Breaking the seal, which bore the engraving "Fairoaks," the Major opened the envelope first. Inside, the letter from his sister, Helen, read:

*I beg and implore you to come to me immediately. I am in the very greatest grief and perplexity. My dearest boy, who has been everything a fond mother could wish, is grieving me dreadfully. He has formed—an infatuation—for an actress currently performing here. She is at least twelve years older than Arthur, who will not be eighteen until next February. The wretched boy insists upon marrying her.*

*Please, my dear friend, come instantly upon receiving this. As his guardian, I entreat and command you to dissuade this most deplorable resolution."*

"Fairoaks, Tuesday," the Major muttered, his face draining of color. "A damned pretty business at Fairoaks on Tuesday. Now let us see what the boy has to say."

He tore open the second letter, written in a frantic, floundering hand and sealed with a massive Pendennis signet, splattered with extra wax to indicate the writer's trembling agitation.

*In informing you of my engagement with Miss Costigan, daughter of J. Chesterfield Costigan, Esq., of Costiganstown—but better known professionally as Miss Fotheringay of the Theatres Royal Drury Lane and Crow Street, and the Norwich and Welsh Circuit—I know I offer news unlikely to please current societal prejudices. My dearest mother, whom I wish to spare no unnecessary pain, is deeply moved and grieved by this intelligence. I beseech you, my dear Sir, to come down, reason with her, and console her.*

*Though driven by poverty to maintain an honorable livelihood through her splendid talents, Miss Costigan's family is as ancient and noble as ours. Our ancestor, Ralph Pendennis, landed with Richard II in Ireland; my Emily's forefathers were kings of that land. Mr. Costigan, a military man like yourself, confirms this lineage.*

*Logic fails against my mother's prejudices. She refuses to welcome into her arms one who wishes to be her most affectionate daughter forever. Though Miss Costigan is several years older, this does not hinder my affection nor its duration. A love like mine is contracted once and for all. Never having known love until I saw her, I feel I shall die without experiencing another passion. It is the fate of my life.*

*It was Miss C.'s own delicacy suggesting the age gap might be a barrier, but having loved once, I would despise myself if I hesitated to follow my passion. I intend to give all where I feel all. I press for a speedy marriage—why delay when doubt is unworthy?*

*I entreat you, my dear Uncle, to reconcile my mother to our union. Addressing you as a man of the world (*qui mores hominum multorum vidit et urbes*), I trust you will not share the weak scruples that agitate a lady who has scarcely left her village. Come down immediately. Apart from fortune, I am confident you will admire and approve of my Emily.*

When the Major finished reading, his countenance twisted into an expression of pure rage and horror. Glowry, the surgeon, instinctively reached for his lancet in his card case, fearing his friend was having a stroke. The news was staggering: the head of the Pendennis house planning to marry an actress ten years his senior; a headstrong youth plunging blindly into matrimony.

"The mother has spoiled the young rascal!" the Major groaned internally. "Cursed sentimentality and romantic rubbish. My nephew marrying a tragedy queen! Good heavens, people will laugh until I dare not show my head!"

He realized with a sinking pang that he must cancel Lord Steyne's dinner at Richmond. Instead of enjoying select English society, he would spend the night in a foul-smelling mail coach. Worse still, he would have to cancel all other engagements indefinitely. Who knew how long this domestic crisis would drag on?

Quitting his breakfast table, the Major retreated to the adjacent writing room. With a rueful sigh, he penned formal refusals to the Marquis, the Earl, the Bishop, and every other host who had invited him. Then, he instructed his servant to book seats on the evening mail coach, naturally charging the exorbitant cost to the account of the widow and the young scapegrace he was sworn to protect.

In the early days of the reign of King George III—a monarch later dubbed "the Magnificent"—there resided in a modest West Country town named Clavering a gentleman of the name Pendennis. To those fortunate enough to recall the era, his name was famously painted on a signboard above a tiny shop in Bath. This humble establishment was distinguished by a gilded pestle and mortar mounted above the door, signaling the trade within: apothecary and surgeon. Here, Mr. Pendennis performed duties ranging from attending to the sickbeds of gentlemen and assisting ladies during their most delicate moments, to the humble task of selling brown-paper plasters to a farmer's wife or vending toothbrushes and London perfumes. While the memory of such transactions might fade in a bustling metropolis, the residents of Clavering remembered them vividly.

Yet, beneath the guise of a shopkeeper dealing in pennyworths of salts and fragrant cakes of Windsor soap, stood a man of excellent education and ancient lineage. The Pendennis family boasted a Cornish pedigree tracing back to the Druids, with connections stretching even further into the mists of antiquity. They had intermarried with the Normans at a late stage of their history and held kinship ties to the great houses of Wales and Brittany. Though John Pendennis possessed a university education and the potential for a brilliant academic career, fate intervened. In his second year at Cambridge, his father died insolvent, forcing young "Pen" to abandon his studies for the pestle and apron. He harbored a deep hatred for the trade, entering it solely due to necessity and the intervention of his uncle—a London apothecary of low standing whom his father had married into.

Upon completing his apprenticeship, John severed ties with his coarse-minded relative and established his own practice in Bath. Poverty dogged his steps initially; he struggled merely to maintain his shop's gilt ornaments and provide comfort for his bedridden mother. His fortunes changed dramatically one afternoon when Lady Ribstone, accompanied by an intoxicated Irish chairman, crashed into his doorway. The driver's pole shattered a prized pink bottle in his window, leaving Lady Ribstone screaming. Mr. Pendennis promptly seated her, revived her with cinnamon and sal-volatile, and displayed manners so uncommonly gentle that she appointed him personal physician to herself and her extensive household.

When her son, Master Ribstone, returned from Eton suffering from a fever induced by overeating, Pendennis treated him with such skill and tenderness that he won the enduring favor of the Codlingbury family. From that day forward, prosperity followed. The fashionable circles of Bath embraced him, particularly the ladies. His humble shop transformed into a smart establishment; soon, he deemed the sale of toothbrushes unworthy of his ancient bloodline. Eventually, he closed the shop entirely, retaining only a private surgery staffed by a genteel assistant. Before his mother passed, she watched with joy from her bedroom window as her beloved John stepped into his own close carriage—a single-horse vehicle, true, but adorned with the handsome arms of the Pendennis family. "What would Arthur say now?" she lamented, referring to her younger son, a captain in India who had never visited during his father's years of struggle.

John gently corrected her, asking that he be addressed as "Mr. Parkins" rather than "Johnny" in the presence of the young man. Soon, however, the old lady fell silent forever. Her house grew lonely without her querulous voice. John moved her belongings to a new room, installed a night-bell, and took up residence in her former bedchamber. By this time, he was past forty, living before the wars concluded and before George the Magnificent fully ascended to his legendary status. Yet, what defines a gentleman but his pedigree? Now, John proudly displayed a beautifully framed copy of his ancestry in his drawing-room, flanked by portraits of Codlingbury House and St. Boniface's College—the brief, happy days of his youth. Like Sterne's officer drawing his sword, he produced his pedigree from a trunk, asserting his gentility.

Coinciding with his mother's death, another notable patient, the virtuous Lady Pontypool, also passed away in Bath. She was the daughter of the twelfth Earl of Bareacres and the widow of the second Lord Pontypool. For her final five years, she had been cared for by Miss Helen Thistlewood, a distant relative of the Earls and daughter of a naval officer slain at Copenhagen. While Miss Thistlewood enjoyed comfortable lodging, she endured the infernal tyranny common among women. The visiting doctor, noting the angelic patience with which Helen bore her elder relative's insults, resolved to ask a serious question as they traveled in the funeral coach to Bath Abbey.

Pendennis, twenty years her senior and naturally reserved, approached the matter with the gravity of a man who had perhaps strangled his own youthful passions. At thirty-four, he was a collected gentleman in black stockings with a bald head. As he checked her pulse, holding her cold hand, he asked where she intended to reside now that the Pontypool estates were being dismantled, boxed, and shipped away. Tears filled Helen's eyes; she admitted she had a thousand pounds left by her aunt but no clear plan. Looking into her pale face, Pendennis proposed that she live with him. He was older, yes, but of good birth, sound principles, and improving prospects. Alone in the world, he needed a constant companion whom he would strive to make happy. He delivered this rehearsed speech with earnest sincerity.

Though Helen might have preferred a man less artificial, she recognized his worthiness, his honor, and his devotion to his mother. Blushing deeply, she curtsied low and requested time to consider his generous proposal. They were married during the dull Bath season, which coincided with the height of the season in London. Pendennis secured lodgings in Holles Street, Cavendish Square, and introduced his wife to the theaters, parks, and royal chapel. He presented calling cards to Lord Pontypool, the Earl of Bareacres, and his old patrons, the Ribstones. While Bareacres ignored the gesture, Pontypool called to admire Mrs. Pendennis, sending a proxy via her footman with an invitation to a future concert. Meanwhile, the Ribstones invited the couple to a grand entertainment, a fact of which Mr. Pendennis bragged until his dying day.

Mr. Pendennis's lifelong ambition was to become a true gentleman. Through frugality, luck, and shrewd investment in a copper mine, he amassed enough capital to buy a house and small estate near Clavering. He purchased shares wisely while the mine was prosperous, eventually selling his Bath business to Mr. Parkins for a lump sum and an annuity, retiring completely from the mortar and pestle.

His son, Arthur, was only eight at the time. Having left Bath so young, Arthur barely remembered the surgery or the smell of foul plasters. The father never spoke of the shop, adopting the persona of a landed squire instantly. He traded his black breeches for a bottle-green coat with brass buttons and drab gaiters, attended market sessions, and founded local charities like the Clavering Book Club and the Samaritan Soup Society. He redirected the mail route through Clavering and became an active vestryman. At market, he inspected livestock with a knowing eye, trading with farmers at the Clavering Arms. Once, being called "Doctor" was a source of pride; now, it was a shame, and he insisted on being addressed as "Squire."

His dining room walls were lined with portraits he claimed were Lelys and Vandykes depicting his ancestors—Roger of Agincourt, Arthur of Crécy, Generals of Blenheim and Oudenarde. While his skeptical wife tried to steer conversations away from these genealogical fictions, young Arthur accepted them as absolute truth, regarding these fictional heroes as real figures alongside Robinson Crusoe and the Seven Champions of Christendom.

With an income of roughly eight hundred pounds a year, the Pendennises belonged to the "second-best" society of the county. They hosted lavish moonlight dinners where guests traveled twelve miles to dine with them. Their social circle included Captain Glanders, a perpetual troublemaker involved in disputes with the vicar and schoolmaster, and Mrs. Pybus, who constantly meddled in Helen's charitable projects. Fortunately, their home, Fairoaks, sat a mile outside Clavering, shielding them from the prying eyes of the villagers.

Fairoaks itself was a magnificent freestone palace, built by a reckless ancestor and maintained in ruin by his successors. Its great halls were moldy and gloomy, terrifying young Arthur as a boy. Yet, at sunset, the lawn offered a breathtaking vista: the golden light reflecting off the neighboring Clavering Park, the river Brawl rushing westward, and the purple silhouette of the old abbey tower rising behind the trees. Young Arthur would recite poetic verses about God's creation, casting long blue shadows with his mother, ending in tender embraces. To love and pray defined his mother's life, leading her son to believe that heaven awaited them together.

At home, the atmosphere shifted when John Pendennis entered. His orders were obeyed with the strictness of Persian decrees. Meals were punctual, prayers were recited, and inspections of stables, kennels, and pigstyes happened at precise intervals. After dinner, he napped with *The Globe* on his lap, wearing a yellow bandanna handkerchief sent by his brother, Major Pendennis, from India.

The family eagerly awaited the biennial visit of Major Pendennis, the retired soldier returning from

Arthur Pendennis was about sixteen when his reign truly began. To judge by the artist commissioned to illustrate this biography—a man whose likeness of my friend is, frankly, a disaster—one might assume he possessed nothing but flaws. Yet, in reality, he had what his peers called a "dumpy" figure, while his mother insisted on calling it "neat." His hair was a healthy brown that shimmered like gold in the sun; his face was round, rosy, and speckled with freckles, radiating a perpetual good humor. When nature finally granted him the whiskers he so ardently desired, they proved to be a distinctly reddish hue. Though not conventionally beautiful, he possessed a frank, open countenance and a merry laugh that lit up his honest blue eyes. No wonder Mrs. Pendennis considered him the pride of the entire county.

Between the ages of sixteen and eighteen, he grew from five feet six to five feet eight inches, at which majestic height he paused. His mother, however, remained baffled. How could any man possibly grow three inches taller than his father, Mr. Pendennis?

One thing was certain: Arthur never returned to school. The rigid discipline of the establishment simply did not suit him; he preferred the comfort of home. The issue was debated fiercely, with his uncle advocating for a return to Greyfriars. The Doctor argued passionately that a thorough knowledge of a Greek play was essential for Arthur's future success. However, Pen adroitly hinted to his mother about the dangers of Greyfriars—the wild fellows, the rowdy atmosphere. Taking the alarm immediately, the timid soul of the mother relented, allowing him to remain at home.

Soon after, Pen's uncle offered to use his influence with His Royal Highness, the Commander-in-Chief, who was known for his kindness toward the family. The proposal was to secure a commission in the Foot Guards for Pen. His heart leaped with joy. He recalled a visit to St. James's Park, where he had seen Tom Ricketts, a fourth-former notorious for wearing jackets and trousers so comically tight that older boys treated him as a living target or 'cockshy.' Pen remembered seeing this same Ricketts, now transformed, arrayed in crimson and gold, staggering beneath a massive bear-skin cap and the heavy colors of the regiment. Tom had recognized Pen, offering a patronizing nod. This was the same wretch Pen had once struck over the back with a hockey-stick. Now, here he was, center stage amidst bayonets, crossbelts, and scarlet uniforms, rallying around the national flag while the band blared trumpets and cymbals. Surrounded by immense warriors sporting chin-tufts and Waterloo medals, Tom spoke familiarly to giants. What would not Pen have given to wear such epaulettes and join such a glorious service?

But Helen Pendennis, upon hearing this proposal, donned a face of pure terror. "I do not quarrel with those who think differently," she declared, "but in my opinion, a Christian has no right to make the army a profession. Mr. Pendennis never, never would have permitted his son to be a soldier. Finally, I should be exceedingly unhappy if you entertained such thoughts." Now, Pen would sooner cut off his nose and ears than deliberately, and with premeditated malice, make his mother unhappy. Being of such a generous disposition that he would give anything to anyone, he instantly surrendered his vision of red coats and epaulettes, trading his ardor for military glory to his mother.

She deemed him the noblest creature in the world. Meanwhile, Major Pendennis, upon learning that the commission offer had been acknowledged and refused, replied with a curt and somewhat angry letter to the widow, concluding that his nephew was merely a "spooney."

Pen was content enough, however, when he displayed his talents during the Christmas hunt, when the Major descended to Fairoaks as usual. Pen rode a magnificent mare with uncommon pluck and grace. He cleared his fences with great coolness and judgment, devoid of unnecessary bravado. He wrote boastful letters to his schoolmates about his top-boots and his exploits across the countryside. He began to dream seriously of a scarlet coat; his mother admitted it would look remarkably well on him, though she spent hours in anguish during his absences, daily expecting to find him brought home on a stretcher.

Amidst these amusements, it must be noted that Pen did not neglect his studies entirely. He possessed a natural appetite for reading every conceivable book outside his school curriculum. Only when forced to drink from the waters of formal knowledge did he refuse to swallow. At home, he devoured everything from *Inchbald's Theatre* to *White's Farriery*, ransacking neighboring bookcases. At Clavering, he discovered an old stockpile of French novels, which he consumed with vigor. He would perch for hours atop the highest step of Doctor Portman's library, a folio on his knees, whether it was *Hakluyt's Travels*, *Hobbes's Leviathan*, *Augustine's Opera*, or *Chaucer's Poems*.

Pen and the Vicar became fast friends. From his Reverence, Pen learned that honest appreciation for port wine which characterized his adult life. As for the dear Mrs. Portman, who was not in the least jealous despite her husband's avowed love for Mrs. Pendennis—which he proclaimed made her the finest lady in the county—her sole grief was that her daughter, Minny, was already too old for Pen. Miss Myra Portman was only two years younger than Pen's mother and weighed as much as both Pen and Mrs. Pendennis combined.

Are these details insipid? Look back, good friend, to your own youth and recall the truth. I like to imagine a well-nurtured boy: brave, gentle, warm-hearted, loving, facing the world with kind, honest eyes. What vibrant colors the world wore then, and how purely one enjoyed it! A man possesses few years of such time, often unaware of their value while they last. It is only when they are long past that he remembers how dear and happy they were.

To prevent Mr. Pen from succumbing to the idleness predicted by his friend, the Doctor of the Cistercians, Mr. Smirke, the vicar's curate, was hired at a liberal salary to ride or walk over from Clavering, spending several hours daily with the young gentleman. Smirke was a man perfectly faultless at a tea table, wore a curl on his fair forehead, and tied his neckcloth with a melancholy grace. He was a decent scholar and mathematician, teaching Pen as much as the lad was willing to learn, which was not much. Pen quickly measured his tutor, finding him ridiculous. When Smirke rode into the courtyard on his pony, he turned his toes out absurdly and left such a gaping space between his knees and the saddle that no lad with a sense of humor could respect his equestrian skills. Pen nearly terrified him to death by mounting him onto his mare, Rebecca (named after Pen's favorite heroine, the daughter of Isaac of York), and taking him for a ride over a common where the county fox-hounds, led by the staunch old sportsman Mr. Hardhead of Dumplingbeare, happened to appear. Smirke, astride Pen's mare, astounded the hounds and disgusted the huntsman, laming one of the dogs by persisting in riding among the pack and earning a speech from the huntsman more energetic than any oration he had heard since leaving the bargemen on the banks of the Thames.

Smirke confided his Latin and English poems to his pupil and presented Mrs. Pendennis with a volume of the latter, printed at Clapham, his hometown. Together, the two read the ancient poets at a pleasant, rapid pace, vastly different from the slow, methodical grubbing employed by the Cistercian monks, who scented out every word and dug up every root. Pen never liked to halt; instead, he made his tutor construe whenever he stumbled, galloping through the *Iliad*, the *Odyssey*, the tragedians, and the charmingly wicked Aristophanes (whom Pen vowed was the greatest poet of all). So swift was his pace that although he covered considerable ground in the ancient classics, he eventually forgot it entirely, retaining only a vague memory akin to a Member of Parliament keeping two or three quotations handy, or a reviewer hinting at a bit of Greek for decency's sake. We humans are the most prosaic creatures, yet the most faithful; with curious reverence, we transmit from generation to generation the superstition of what constitutes a gentleman's education.

Beyond the ancients, Pen read English literature with great gusto. Smirke sighed and shook his head sadly regarding Byron and Moore. But Pen was a sworn fire-worshipper and a Corsair; he had them by heart. He would take little Laura into the window and recite, "Zuleika, I am not thy brother," in tones so tragic that the solemn little maid would open her wide eyes even further. She would sit, sewing at Mrs. Pendennis's knee until bedtime, listening to Pen read aloud to her night after night without comprehending a single word.

He read Shakespeare to his mother (who claimed she liked it, though she didn't), Byron, Pope, and his favorite *Lalla Rookh*, which pleased her indifferently. But as for Bishop Heber and, above all, Mrs. Hemans, this lady would melt completely, dissolving into her

Once Mrs. Haller had retired to the George Hotel, Mr. Foker and his guest settled into a sumptuous meal within the coffee-room. Mr. Rincer, serving as waiter, presented the first course with a gravity that suggested he was attending to the Lord Lieutenant of the county itself. Mr. Foker devoured the turtle and venison with the same relish he had displayed a year prior when feasting on ginger beer and smuggled salami. Pen was forced to admire his host's discernment, especially when Foker declared the champagne tasted of "condemned gooseberries" and winked knowingly at the port. The latter, Foker insisted, was genuine; he claimed there was no fooling him. Indeed, he knew every attendant by their Christian name and displayed a profound interest in their family histories.

As the London coaches rattled up to the George—a common sight in those early days—Foker threw open the coffee-room window. He greeted the guards and coachmen by name, inquired warmly about their kinfolk, and mimicked the tooting of horns with remarkable vigor and precision. He perfectly captured the scene as Jem the ostler whipped the horses' cloths and the carriages departed gaily.

"A bottle of sherry, a bottle of sham, a bottle of port, and a shass caffy," Foker remarked, surveying the remnants of nuts and fruit. "It ain't so bad, hey, Pen?"

Having consumed these delicacies, Foker announced it was time to "toddle." Pen sprang up, eyes bright and face flushed, and they headed toward the theatre. They paid their admission to a wheezy old lady slumbering behind the money-box.

"This is Mrs. Dropsicum," Foker explained to his companion. "Bingley's mother-in-law, and she's great in *Lady Macbeth*." Foker knew her personally. They secured nearly the entire front row of boxes, a rarity for a venue that, despite Bingley's playbills promising a "universal burst of attraction and galvanic thrills of delight," remained sparsely populated. A score of spectators dotted the pit benches, a few kicked and whistled in the galleries, and a dozen free-admission guests occupied the remaining boxes alongside the young gentlemen.

Lieutenants Rodgers and Podgers, along with young Cornet Tidmus of the Dragoons, held a private box. They conversed with the actors whenever the dialogue paused and applauded them by name with excessive fervor. Bingley, the manager, who played every major role except when stepping aside for visiting London stars, starred as 'The Stranger.' Dressed in tight pantaloons, Hessian boots, a large cloak, a beaver hat adorned with a drooping hearse feather, and a raddled face partially concealed by a great buckled brown wig, he completed the look with stage jewelry. He selected the largest, shinier rings for himself, allowing his little finger to quiver conspicuously from beneath his cloak, displaying a sham diamond ring on the first joint. He delighted in letting the youth of his company handle this prop, often answering their questions about its history with theatrical flair. The stage possessed its own heirloom jewels, much like royalty; this ring had supposedly passed from George Frederick Cooke to Mr. Quin, perhaps purchased for a mere shilling. Bingley believed the world was utterly captivated by its glitter.

Reading from a stage-book that was less bound and more rouged and tawdry than the heroes holding it, Bingley pointed passages with dramatic flourish, wagged his head ominously at the audience, and lifted his eyes to the ceiling, claiming a divine connection between the script and heaven. Anyone familiar with great light comedians like "X," known for wearing impossible chintz dressing-gowns while pretending to be idle noblemen, would recognize the pose.

When 'The Stranger' spotted the young men, he immediately turned his attention to them. Lying on the stage bench, he eyed them solemnly over his gilt volume, calculating the impact of his hand, his ring, and his Hessian boots. Determined to fascinate them, knowing they had paid their money, he also noted the families arriving from the countryside to fill the cane chairs in his boxes.

His servant, Francis, interjected with commentary on his master's gloomy demeanor. "Again reading!" Francis observed. "From morn to night. To him, nature has no beauty, life no charm. Three years pass without seeing him smile. Nothing diverts him. Oh, if he would but attach himself to anything living—even an animal! Man must love something."

[Enter Tobias (Goll) from the hut.]

Tobias cried out, "Oh, how refreshing, after seven long weeks, to feel these warm sunbeams once again! Thanks, bounteous heaven, for the joy I taste!" Pressing his cap between his hands and looking upward, he prayed.

'The Stranger' watched him intently.

Francis addressed the actor: "This old man's share of earthly happiness is small, yet mark how grateful he is for his portion."

Bingley replied, "Because though old, he is but a child in the leading-string of hope." He fixed his gaze steadily on Foker, who continued to suck the top of his stick with utter unconcern.

Francis added, "Hope is the nurse of life."

"And her cradle—is the grave," Bingley finished, delivering the line with the mournful resonance of a bassoon in agony. He locked eyes on Pendennis so intensely that the poor lad felt exposed, believing the entire house was staring at him. He cast his eyes down, only to find Bingley's gaze returning instantly. Throughout the scene, the manager focused entirely on Pen. When 'The Stranger' prepared to perform a good deed, sending Francis away so the servant wouldn't witness the act of benevolence, Bingley carefully marked his page, ensuring he could resume reading off-stage if desired. Yet, everything was performed directly in the face of Pendennis, whom the manager intended to subjugate.

Relieved when the scene concluded, Foker tapped his cane and shouted, "Bravo, Bingley!"

"Give him a hand, Pendennis; you know every chap likes a hand," Foker urged. The good-natured young gentleman, Pen laughing, and the Dragoon officers in the opposite box began clapping with all their might.

The action shifted to a chamber in Wintersen Castle, closing over Tobias's hut. Servants bustled about with chairs and tables. "That's Hicks and Miss Thackthwaite," Foker whispered. "Pretty girl, ain't she, Pendennis? But stop—hurray—bravo! Here comes the Fotheringay."

The pit thrilled, umbrellas thumped, and a volley of applause erupted from the gallery. The Dragoon officers and Foker clapped furiously; the noise was so deafening it seemed the house was packed. Peeking from the side-scene was the red-faced, whiskered Mr. Costigan. Pen's eyes widened, bright as Mrs. Haller entered with a downcast look. Rallying at the roar of applause, she swept the house with a grateful glance, folded her hands across her breast, and sank into a magnificent curtsey. More applause, more umbrellas. Pen, flaming with wine and enthusiasm, clapped harder and sang "bravo" louder than anyone.

Mrs. Haller saw him, as did everyone else. Old Mr. Bows, the diminutive first fiddler of the orchestra (augmented tonight by a detachment of the Dragoons' band, courtesy of Colonel Swallowtail), looked up from his perch beside his crutch and smiled at the lad's fervor.

Those who have only seen Miss Fotheringay in later years, after her marriage and entry into London society, cannot imagine the creature she was when Pen first laid eyes on her. I warn my reader beforehand: the illustrator of this work, capable of drawing an ugly face tolerably well but utterly failing at beauty, cannot convey her true splendor. She was tall, twenty-six years old (though she vowed she was only nineteen), and in the prime of her beauty. Her forehead was vast, crowned with black hair waving naturally over it—a style modern beauties vainly try to replicate with crimping irons—confined in shining, voluminous braids at the back of a neck reminiscent of the Louvre Venus. When she lifted her eyes to gaze upon you, before dropping their purple, deep-fringed lids, they shone with tenderness and mystery unfathomable. Love and Genius seemed to peer out from them, then retreat coyly, as if ashamed to have been caught at the lattice. Who could possess such a commanding brow but a woman of high intellect? She rarely laughed (her teeth were not perfect), but a smile of endless sweetness played around her lips and dimpled her cheeks and chin. Her nose defied description; her ears resembled two little pearl shells, which the finest earrings in the theatre only served to insult.

Dressed in long, flowing black robes that she manipulated with wonderful grace, revealing her sandals only occasionally, she looked ravishing. Pen thought them as enchanting as Cinderella's slippers. Yet, it was her hands and arms that this magnificent being excelled in; somehow, one could never see her without noticing them. Whether folded over her bosom in resignation, dropped in mute agony, raised in superb command, or fluttering in sportive gaiety like snowy doves before the chariot of Venus, they beckoned, repelled, entreated, and embraced her admirers. No single person,

Rebecca, the spirited mare, didn't slow down a bit. She thundered straight toward Baymouth, where Pen finally pulled her up at the stable. Without wasting a second, he bolted for Mr. Foker's lodging. He'd gotten the address from Foker the day before, a detail that saved him a lot of wandering.

When Pen arrived, he found himself outside a chemist's shop. The place smelled faintly of tobacco and soda water, which vanished quickly thanks to the enthusiastic young customers inside. Inside the rooms above, Pen discovered Mr. Spavin, Foker's pal and co-owner of the tandem carriage that had recently toured Chatteris. Spavin was lounging around, smoking a pipe and showing off some fancy tricks to a tiny dog with a crumb of biscuit.

There was a striking contrast between the two men. Pen's face was flushed a healthy, vibrant red from his ride, looking full of life. Next to him sat Spavin, whose features were pale, waxy, and clearly the result of heavy drinking. Spavin noticed the difference immediately. "Who's that guy?" he muttered to himself. "He looks fresher than a green bean. His hand doesn't shake in the morning; I'd bet five to one on it."

But Foker wasn't there. Not a soul. That was a massive letdown. Spavin shrugged, saying he had no idea when his friend would show up. Sometimes Foker disappeared for a day, sometimes for a whole week. Spavin tried to fill the silence by asking Pen which college he attended and if he had anything interesting to share. There was a decent draft of ale available, so the two young men settled in for a chat. Spavin learned Pen's name from the calling card the latter produced—a card Pen seemed rather proud of displaying. After a brief exchange, they parted ways.

Defeated, Pen headed down to the beach. He paced along the sand, nervously biting his fingernails while staring out at the vast, noisy sea. The ocean stretched out before him, bright and endless. Waves crashed into the bay, foaming and roaring loudly. Pen stared at them with a blank expression, barely registering the sound. Yet, a tidal wave was crashing into his own mind, and he felt utterly powerless to stop it. He threw stones into the water, but the tide kept surging forward. He was furious about not finding Foker. He *had* to see Foker.

"Maybe I'll just ride over to the Chatteris road and catch him," Pen thought.

An hour later, Rebecca was saddled and trotting happily along the grass beside the Chatteris road. About four miles out, the road branched off toward Clavering. Naturally, the horse wanted to take that turn, but Pen cut her sharply across her shoulder, forcing her onto the main turnpike. Still, there was no sign of the black carriage with its distinctive red wheels.

By the time he reached the tollgate, Pen decided there was no point in stopping. He pushed on to the George Inn. The hostler confirmed that Mr. Foker was definitely there. "He's been causing quite a scene last night!" the hostler chuckled. "Drinking, singing, and trying to fight the postboy named Tom. I'm guessing Tom probably beat him up." With a mischievous grin, the hostler turned to Foker's servant, who was carrying a bundle of freshly laundered clothes. "Did you bring up your master's hot water for shaving?" he teased. "Take Mr. Pendennis upstairs to see him."

Pen followed the servant into the apartment. Dominating the room was an enormous bed piled high with feathers and pillows. Lying amidst the fluff was Mr. Harry Foker, his small, yellowish face visible beneath a red silk nightcap.

"Hullo!" Pen called out.

"Who's there? Brother, spill it quick!" a voice boomed from the mattress. "Oh, it's Pendennis again! Does your Mama know you're missing? Did you eat with us last night? No, wait—who ate with us last night, Stoopid?"

"The three officers, sir, and Mr. Bingley, sir, and Mr. Costigan, sir," the servant replied, answering every question with perfect seriousness.

"Aha! The cup circulated, and the jokes flew around. We sang, and I recall wanting to fight a postboy. Did I thrash him, Stoopid?"

"No, sir. The fight never happened," the servant said gravely. He was busy organizing Foker's dressing case—a magnificent trunk gifted by a loving mother, essential for any journey. It was filled with silver dishes, mugs, bottles of essences, and a selection of razor blades waiting for Foker's inevitable beard growth.

"Do it another time," Foker yawned, stretching his thin arms overhead. "No fight today, but plenty of singing. Bingley sang, I sang, the General sang—that's Costigan, right? Have you ever heard him belt out 'The Little Pig Under the Bed,' Pen?"

"The man we met yesterday," Pen stammered, trembling slightly. "The father of—"

"Of the Fotheringays! The very man. Isn't she a Venus, Pen?"

"Sir, Mr. Costigan is in the sitting room. He says you invited him to breakfast. He's been knocking for five minutes, but he couldn't wake you up. He's been standing there since eleven..."

"What time is it now?"

"One o'clock, sir."

"What would the best mother in the world say if she saw me in bed at this hour?" the lazy young man cried dramatically. "She sent me down here with a grinder. She wants me to fix my neglected self-image! Hey, Pen, this isn't exactly seven o'clock school routine, is it, old chap?" He burst into a hearty, boyish laugh. "Go talk to the General while I get dressed. Oh, and tell him to sing 'The Little Pig Under the Bed' for you. It's fantastic."

Panicked, Pen rushed off to meet Mr. Costigan, while Foker began his elaborate grooming ritual.

Let's take a moment to understand Mr. Foker. One grandfather was a wealthy brewer; the other was an Earl, leaving him a doting mother. The Foker family had attended Cistercian School for generations. At school, our hero—whose name appeared on a pub sign advertising "Foker's Entire"—was relentlessly bullied because of his background, his awkward looks, his lack of academic talent, his dirtiness, his gluttony, and various other flaws. Anyone who knows how bullying turns a sensitive boy into a sneaky, silent creature can understand how, shortly after escaping that prison, Foker transformed. He emerged as the witty, sarcastic, brilliant character we now know. Though he remained a dunce academically (you can't learn everything by skipping school), he evolved from a sloppy student into a fastidious dandy. By the time he joined his guests, he was scented, dressed in crisp white linen, and looked absolutely splendid.

Meanwhile, in the sitting room, Captain Costigan sat by the window, holding a newspaper at arm's length. His eyes were cloudy, and he struggled to read the print, mouthing the words as best he could. His hat was tilted precariously on one side, and one foot rested casually on the window seat. Looking closely at his boots—one could tell his finances weren't exactly booming. Poverty often attacks a man's extremities first: hats, shoes, and gloves suffer before the rest of the body. Costigan's attire reflected this; his headgear, footwear, and gloves were all shabby and worn.

Upon seeing Pen, the Captain hopped down from the window. He greeted the newcomer with a stiff military salute, touching his hat with a gloved finger, then removing the hat entirely. He was balding but covered his scalp with thick, greasy gray hair, with wisps hanging down his cheeks. Years of whiskey had ruined whatever complexion he once had, giving his face a copper hue. He wore a high collar, stained and torn in several places, and a tight-fitting coat where the buttons had long since surrendered.

"I am the young gentleman you introduced yourself to yesterday in Cathedral Yard," the Captain declared with a flourish and a wave of his hat. "I hope you are doing well, sir. I spotted you at the theater last night during my daughter's performance. I missed you on my way home. I merely escorted her back, sir, for Jack Costigan, despite our poverty, is a gentleman. When I returned to pay my respects to my jovial young friend, Mr. Foker—you were already gone. We had a delightful night, sir—Mr. Foker, the three gallant young dragoons, and your humble servant. Gad, sir, it reminded me of our old days when I served His Majesty in the Fighting Hundred and Third Regiment." He produced an antique snuffbox and presented it with dignity.

Arthur Pendennis was so flustered he couldn't speak. This ragged-looking fellow was—was her father. The Captain smelled strongly of last night's cigars and twirled the tuft on his chin with the flair of a young dandy.

"I hope, Miss F—, Miss Costigan is well, sir," Pen stammered, turning red. "She—she gave me far greater pleasure than—than I've ever experienced at a play. I believe, sir—I truly believe she is the finest actress in the world," he gasped.

"Your hand, young man! For

For a while, Cicero and Euripides ceased to occupy Mr. Pen's mind entirely. His tutor, the honest Mr. Smirke, found himself with a remarkably easy schedule. However, the true victim of this mental shift was Rebecca. While Pen occasionally announced his intention to travel to Chatteris for fencing lessons—a trip undertaken openly with his mother whenever he secured three free hours—he frequently vanished into the city instead. He would dash straight to Prior's Lane, leaving his guardian in the lurch.

When Rebecca eventually grew lame, Pen was as frantic with distress as Richard III was when his horse was shot beneath him at Bosworth Field. He plunged deep into the medical texts of the stable-keeper at Chatteris, studying remedies for his own animals and hiring others for rent.

Once a week, usually under the guise of reading a Greek drama with Smirke, the young rogue timed his departure perfectly to catch the *Competitor* coach. He would linger in Chatteris for two hours before returning on the *Rival*, departing London at ten o'clock that night. On one occasion, his secret nearly slipped due to Mr. Smirke's simplicity. Mrs. Pendennis had asked if they had spent the previous night reading extensively. Smirke, about to confess that he hadn't seen Mr. Pen at all, was saved by a swift, grinding blow from Pen's boot heel directly onto the curate's toe beneath the table. The warning was immediate and effective; the curate remained silent.

Naturally, conversations regarding this "tender subject" occurred. It is excellent sport, for those not personally involved, to overhear two men in love discussing their affections. Somewhere, someone must serve as the confidant. Under solemn vows of secrecy, Pen revealed his state of mind. The curate, trembling slightly, expressed hope that Pen's object was "worthy"—not an "unlawful attachment." If it were illicit, the poor curate feared his duty would compel him to inform Mrs. Pendennis, leading to a quarrel that would sever his access to the one thing he loved most in the world.

"Unlawful? Unworthy!" Pen exclaimed, bouncing in his seat. "She is as pure as she is beautiful; I would give my heart to no other woman. I keep this secret within the family because—because—there are weighty reasons I cannot disclose. But any man who breathes a word against her purity insults both her honor and mine, and—darn me, I won't tolerate it!"

Smirke offered only a faint laugh. "Very well, Arthur, don't call me out. You know I cannot fight." Yet, this compromise placed the wretched curate firmly under his pupil's thumb, causing Greek studies and mathematics to suffer accordingly.

Had the reverend gentleman possessed greater discernment and glanced into the Poet's Corner of the *County Chronicle* arriving in the Wednesday mail, he might have noticed entries titled 'Mrs. Haller,' 'Passion and Genius,' and 'Lines to Miss Fotheringay, of the Theatre Royal.' These verses appeared weekly, written in a gloomy, thrilling, and passionate style. However, since the author no longer signed them "NEP" but rather "EROS," neither the tutor nor Helen—the good-natured soul who clipped her son's verses from the paper—realized that Nep was merely a flamboyant alias for Eros, the fervent admirer of the new actress.

Eventually, Mrs. Pendennis asked, "Who is the lady your rival is constantly singing about in the *Chronicle*? He writes somewhat like you, dear Pen, but yours is far superior. Have you seen Miss Fotheringay?"

Pen confirmed he had. That very night, he attended a performance of *The Stranger*, where she played Mrs. Haller. He mentioned her upcoming benefit and her role as Ophelia. "Suppose we went?" he suggested. "Shakespeare, you know, Mother. We can get horses from the Clavering Arms." Little Laura sprang up with delight; she longed for a play.

Pen invoked Shakespeare's name because the late Mr. Pendennis, true to his character, held an uncommon reverence for the Bard of Avon, claiming his works contained more poetry than all of Johnson's collected poets combined. Though Mr. Pendennis rarely read them himself, he urged Pen to study them, promising great pleasure in taking his son and wife to see the immortal poet's plays when the boy reached a suitable age.

Tears welled in the kind mother's eyes recalling these speeches. She kissed her son fondly and agreed to go. Laura leaped for joy. Was Pen happy? Was he ashamed? Holding his mother, he longed to confess everything but chose silence. He intended to observe her reaction; the play would be the test, and he would try his mother as Hamlet tested Ophelia.

Helen, in her cheerful mood, invited Mr. Smirke to join them. The clergyman, raised by a doting parent in Clapham who abhorred theatricals, had never seen a play. Yet, Shakespeare! To ride in Mrs. Pendennis's carriage and spend a whole night beside her! He could not resist the prospect of such pleasure. Making a feeble speech about temptation and gratitude, he accepted her generous offer. As he spoke, however, he cast a look at her that made her exceedingly uncomfortable. She had seen that gaze many times lately, one that pursued her relentlessly. In the widow's eyes, he was becoming positively odious daily.

We shall not dwell extensively on Pen's courtship of Miss Fotheringay, as the reader has already sampled much of her conversation, which requires little reporting. Pen sat with her hour after hour, pouring forth his entire honest, boyish soul. Every thought, hope, feeling, book, or fancy he possessed flowed toward her. He never tired of talking and longing. As ideas surged in his hot, eager brain, he clothed them in words and delivered them. Her role was not to converse, but to feign understanding—a difficult task given the volume of nonsense he blurted out—while maintaining an appearance of extreme handsomeness and sympathy.

While Pen delivered twenty-minute tirades, delighted in his own eloquence, the lovely Emily, unable to comprehend a tenth of his ramblings, utilized the time to attend to her own affairs. She mentally arranged how to dress the cold mutton, how to turn the black satin, or how to fashion a bonnet from her scarf à la Miss Thackthwaite. Pen spouted Byron and Moore; passion and poetry. Her job was simply to raise her eyes, fix them briefly on his face, and cry, "Oh, 'tis beautiful! Ah, how exquisite! Repeat those lines again." Off the boy would go, and she would return to her practical thoughts of turned gowns or hashed mutton.

Soon, Pen's passion was no longer a secret to Emily or her father. On his second visit, his admiration was obvious to both. As he departed, the old gentleman winked at his daughter over his glass of grog. "Faith, Milly darling, I think ye've hooked that chap."

"Pooh, 'tis only a boy, papa dear," Milly replied. "Sure he's but a child." Pen would have been thrilled by such a dismissal. He was galloping home wild with pleasure, shouting her name.

"Ye've hooked 'um any how," said the Captain. "Let me tell ye he's not a bad fish. I asked Tom at the George and Flint the grocer about his background—fine fortune, drives a chariot, owns Fairoaks Park, sole heir, property at twenty-one. Ye might go further and not fare so well, Miss Fotheringay."

"Them boys are mostly talk," Milly said seriously. "Recall young Poldoody in Dublin. I have a desk full of verses he wrote me during Trinity College, yet he went abroad, and his mother married him to an Englishwoman."

"Lord Poldoody was a young nobleman; things differ there. Besides, ye weren't in the position ye hold now, Milly dear. But ye mustn't encourage this young chap too much, for bedad, Jack Costigan won't have any trifling with his daughter."

"No more will his daughter, papa, you may be sure of that," Milly assured him. "A little sip more of the punch—sure, 'tis beautiful. Ye needn't fear the young chap. I think I'm old enough to take care of myself, Captain Costigan."

Thus, Pen visited day after day, rushing in and galloping away, growing increasingly wild with every encounter. Sometimes the Captain attended, but trusting his daughter implicitly, he often left the young couple alone, cocking his hat over his eye and strutting off on some errand when Pen entered. How delightful those interviews were! The Captain's drawing-room was a low-wainscoted space with a large window overlooking the Dean's garden. Here Pen sat and talked endlessly. Emily, working nearby, looked beautiful and calm. Sunlight streamed through the great windows, illuminating her superb features. Suddenly, the great bell would boom, causing Pen to pause, smile, and fall silent until the music faded, or the rooks cawed in the cathedral elms at sunset, or the organ and choristers drifted over the quiet air, gently hushing Pen's torrential speech.

Incidentally, Miss Fotheringay, clad in a plain shawl, close bonnet, and veil, attended church every Sunday, accompanied by her indef

Major Arthur Pendennis finally rolled into Fairoaks, emerging from a night in the mail-coach that could only be described as a trial by torture. He had been squeezed into a corner by a burly passenger inflated by layers of greatcoats, who kept him awake with snoring loud enough to crack glass. Opposite sat a widow who had sealed the carriage against the fresh air, filling the space instead with the potent fumes of Jamaica rum and water, which she sipped perpetually from a flask in her reticule. Every attempt at rest was shattered by the shrill blast of a turnpike horn, the squashing of his neighbor, or the Widow's feet brushing against his toes. It was a chaotic symphony of discomfort that defined travel in the "jolly old times"—a life now vanished, surviving only in nostalgic memory. Yet, despite the eight-hour grind at eight miles per hour, the hard seats, the grumbling coachmen, and the gouty tendencies of the era, everyone managed to remain cheerful.

Among the chaos were two schoolboys heading home for summer break, sitting beside the Major's servant on the roof. At the inn in Bagshot, they devoured a feast of ham, eggs, pies, pickles, tea, coffee, and boiled beef, leaving the Major, sipping weak tea with a yellow complexion and a bristly beard, looking on with tender envy. He imagined Lord Steyne's lavish dinner happening simultaneously, contrasting sharply with his own sparse fare. However, the boys' innocent enthusiasm charmed the Major, especially when he learned one was a lord's son. This young nobleman slept soundly through the noise and crowding, waking up fresh as paint, while the Major descended at Fairoaks Lodge with a rheumatic groan, a wig out of curl, and a face full of suffering.

Helen watched him from her window but held her ground. She knew her brother-in-law hated surprises; he needed preparation, specifically regarding his dentures. Having once stolen the Major's false teeth from his dressing table as a boy, Pen knew exactly how devastating their absence could be. Similarly, Morgan, the servant, treated the Major's wigs with mysterious reverence, curling them in secret corners. Without his hairpiece, the Major refused to show his face to anyone. Once armed with his teeth and wig, the Major descended into the drawing-room, attempting a grave, majestic demeanor suitable for a man of business and fashion.

Inside, however, he found only Helen and little Laura, who was sewing. The Major offered Laura a stiff salute with a single forefinger. She trembled, accepted the gesture, and fled the room. The Major didn't care for her presence anyway, harboring private reasons for his disapproval that would be revealed later. Laura, meanwhile, wandered the grounds searching for Pen, eventually finding him in the orchard deep in conversation with Mr. Smirke.

When Laura sang out, "Uncle Arthur's come!" Pen reacted with sudden ferocity, gripping her small hand tightly. He glanced at Smirke with a look that screamed, *I am ready for anyone*, causing the tutor to sigh gently and step aside. "Lead on, Laura," Pen commanded, masking his internal panic with a half-fierce, half-comic laugh. He was steeling himself for the inevitable confrontation.

Over the past two days, Pen had poured his heart out to Smirke about Miss Emily Fotheringay. With his fiery eloquence, poetic metaphors, and unwavering optimism, he had convinced the tutor that marrying an actress was not only feasible but prudent. Pen had even argued with his own mother, using a logic so absurdly confident it bordered on madness. To him, waiting for wealth or status was cowardice; sharing fate with a beloved woman was the only honorable path. He painted a picture of a perfect union where Emily would be a paragon of virtue, sensitivity, and grace, while dismissing concerns about her background as trivial compared to the joy of love.

That night, unable to sleep, Pen tossed and turned, composing verses inspired by Hamlet, Byron, and the loves of angels. His mother, Helen, crept in softly, finding him surrounded by scattered papers. Seeing his wild-eyed passion, she simply took his hot hand and whispered, "Arthur, why don't you go to sleep?" Overwhelmed, Pen threw his arms around her, declaring, "O mother! I love her, I love her!"

The next morning, when the fully groomed Major presented himself to Helen, he discovered she was far more distressed than he anticipated. Not only did she oppose the marriage, but she feared the blow it would deal to her son's happiness. She pleaded with him to be gentle, citing Pen's high spirit and recent indignation toward Dr. Portman.

"Be very cautious," Helen urged, placing her hand on his sleeve.

The Major, playing the role of the experienced patriarch, kissed her hand gallantly. "Confess," he teased, "that you are trying to clear your conscience by letting the boy have his way." Helen blushed, admitting she worried about his unhappiness.

"To contradict him or let him have his wish?" the Major asked, secretly relieved. "I'm damned if he shall."

"But the match is foolish and cruel," Helen countered, fearing the agony of rejection.

"The issue shan't be marriage, my dear sister," the Major declared firmly. "We aren't going to have a Pendennis, the head of the house, marry a strolling mountebank from a booth. No, no, we won't marry into Greenwich Fair, ma'am."

"If it's broken off suddenly..." Helen warned, trembling at the thought of Pen's volatile temper.

"My dear madam," the Major sympathized, "Arthur will suffer terribly before recovering. But is he the only one who has suffered?"

"No," Helen admitted, recalling her own youthful heartbreak involving a wealthy suitor who left for India.

"I, myself," the Major recounted, "had a similar disappointment. A young woman with fifteen thousand pounds... I was a penniless lieutenant. We exchanged letters and locks of hair, suffered greatly, but recovered. She is now a baronet's wife with thirteen children." He paused, adjusting his wig. "Look at my own brother. He nearly married Miss Balls, daughter of a prominent apothecary, but difficulties arose. I am sure he regrets nothing; it gave him this hand," he said, pressing Helen's fingers again.

"These marriages between different ranks and ages are tragic," Helen murmured, thinking of Laura's father.

"Injudicious," the Major cut in. "Nothing pains a man more than marrying someone vastly older or lower in station. Imagine your house filled with vulgar relatives, or a wife who drops her 'h's! You'll be cut out of society entirely. I've seen men destroyed by the vulgarity of their wives' connections. Take Lady Snapperton, who refused to receive Lady Brouncker because she was a druggist's daughter. Good God, what a trifling pain a temporary separation is compared to the lifelong torment of living among low people!"

"And this woman is ten years older than Arthur," Helen noted, suppressing a laugh despite her distress.

"What happens in such cases?" the Major demanded. "In the highest society, misery is the result. Look at Lord Clodworthy with his mother-like wife, or Lord Willowbank, who cuts down his wife repeatedly for a dancer. You are romantic and sentimental, but I am of the world. Marry this woman! Marry an actress of thirty at eighteen? Bah! I'd sooner he married the cook."

As Helen sighed, lamenting the evils of premature engagement for the third time, the Major realized he needed to address the elephant in the room. "Since you seem so oppressed by the idea of long engagements and unequal matches, and since the circumstances we are discussing require explanation, it would be best to clarify who little Laura is, who has appeared before us several times. We shall reserve that discussion for another chapter."

Once upon a time, there was a young gentleman from Cambridge named the Reverend Francis Bell. During the long vacation, he stayed at a village where his cousin, Helen Thistlewood, was living with her mother—the widow of a lieutenant killed at Copenhagen. Since Mr. Bell was Mrs. Thistlewood's nephew and Helen's own cousin, it seemed only right for him to stay at his aunt's modest home. There he spent his holidays tutoring a few students.

Mr. Bell was a fellow of a college and renowned for his learning. By the time his relatives realized he was secretly engaged, he was merely waiting for a church living to make his vows official. His fiancée was the daughter of another parson, Mr. Coacher, who had actually been Bell's private tutor when Bell was just a boy of seventeen. It was under Mr. Coacher's roof, while helping Miss Martha Coacher pick peas in the garden, that the impulsive young Bell threw himself at her feet. Kneeling amidst the vegetables and the girl, he swore an eternal devotion.

Miss Coacher was significantly older than Bell and had suffered numerous heartbreaks. Three of her father's pupils had played with her affections; a local apothecary had cruelly dumped her; and a dashing dragoon officer, whom she'd danced with in Bath, had once galloped away on his horse and never returned. Wounded by such ingratitude, Martha Coacher desperately sought stability. She listened kindly to the awkward but honest Bell. After he finished speaking, she smiled and said, "Law, Bell, you're far too young for such thoughts!" but hinted she would ponder it in her "virgin bosom." Her father, a widower absorbed in his books, couldn't possibly guide a lady's heart, leaving Martha to manage it alone.

A single lock of her auburn hair, tied with blue ribbon, served as the token of her decision. She had cut off three locks previously and given them to faithless suitors, but this hair had grown back. Men were deceivers, she decided, handing the lock to the simple boy.

However, Number Six was different. Francis Bell proved to be the most faithful lover imaginable. When he eventually went to university, Mr. Coacher cried out in surprise, "God bless my soul, I had no idea what was happening!"—a reaction entirely understandable given he'd been tricked three times before. Francis went to Oxford determined to conquer honors so he could present them at Martha's feet.

He labored prodigiously. Term after term brought news of his triumphs. He sent his prize essay books to old Coacher and his silver declamation cup to Miss Martha. Soon, he was a top Wrangler and a college fellow. Throughout this rise, he maintained a tender correspondence with Miss Coacher, crediting her influence for his success.

Yet, by the time the Reverend Francis Bell, M.A., Fellow and Tutor, reached twenty-six, Miss Coacher was thirty-four. Her charms, manners, and temper had not improved since that sunny spring day in the pea garden. Having achieved his goals, Bell relaxed his studies; his judgment cooled. The sunshine of the garden faded for Miss Martha, and poor Bell found himself pledged via a thousand letters to a coarse, ill-tempered, unfavored, and middle-aged woman.

Following a heated argument—one in which Martha's eloquence usually shone—Francis refused to take his pupils to Bearleader's Green, where Mr. Coacher lived. Instead, he decided to spend the vacation at his aunt's village, a place he hadn't visited since little Helen sat on his knee.

Down he came. Helen was now a beautiful young woman. For four months, from June to October, the cousins spent time walking summer evenings, meeting at dawn, and reading aloud while the old lady dozed. Frank taught Helen everything she needed to know; she sang to him and gave him her artless heart. She knew his entire story. Had he hidden anything? Had he shown her the picture of the woman he was engaged to, blushing as he revealed her letters—hard, eager, and cruel? Days passed, growing happier and closer, filled with kindness, confidence, and pity. Then, one morning in October, Francis returned to college, and the poor girl felt her tender heart depart with him.

Frank too woke from his delightful midsummer dream to the horror of his own pain. He gnashed his teeth against the chains binding him, frantic to break free. Should he confess? Give his savings to the woman and beg for release? There was still time; he temporized. No living would fall in for years. The cousins corresponded sadly and fondly, while the betrothed woman complained bitterly of his changed tone, hard, jealous, and dissatisfied.

Finally, a crisis arrived. The new attachment was discovered. Francis admitted it openly, refusing to hide it, and rebuked Martha for her violent temper, angry imperiousness, and, worst of all, her inferiority and age.

Martha replied that unless he kept his promise, she would carry his letters to every court in the kingdom—letters pledging his love ten thousand times. After exposing him as a perjurer and traitor, she threatened suicide.

Frank had one final interview with Helen, whose mother had recently died and who was now living with old Lady Pontypool. They resolved that he must do his duty: redeem his vow, pay a debt cheated from him by a sharper, and make two honest people miserable. Thus, they judged their duty and parted.

The living fell in almost immediately, but Frank Bell was a gray, worn-out man by the time he was inducted. Helen wrote him a letter on his wedding day, beginning "My dear Cousin" and ending "always truly yours." She returned his other letters and the lock of his hair—all but a tiny fragment—which she kept in her desk when speaking to the Major.

Bell lived in his living for a few years until the Chaplaincy of Coventry Island became vacant. He secured it privately and told his wife. She objected to everything, including this move, so she stayed behind. Later, during Governor Crawley's administration, Bell became close to the governor. Years after his marriage and five years after hearing of Helen's son, Bell's own daughter was born.

She was not the daughter of his first wife, who died of island fever shortly after Helen Pendennis and her husband wrote to announce their child's birth. "I was old, was I?" the first Mrs. Bell had said. "I was old and her inferior, was I? But I married you, Mr. Bell, and kept you from marrying her?" And then she died. Bell remarried a colonial lady he loved deeply, but fate was cruel; she died in childbirth, and Bell gave up, too. He sent his little girl home to Helen Pendennis and her husband, praying they would befriend her.

The child arrived at Fairoaks from Bristol, dressed in black, accompanied by a soldier's wife acting as her nurse. Though she wept at parting, she quickly dried her tears under Helen's motherly care. Around her neck hung a locket containing hair Helen had given to poor Francis years ago. This child was all that remained of him, cherished by the tender creature as a precious legacy. Her name, per his dying letter, was Helen Laura. Yet John Pendennis, though accepting the trust, grew jealous of the orphan and gloomily insisted she be known by her mother's name, not the first one her father had chosen. She feared Mr. Pendennis until the day he died. Only after her husband passed did Helen dare openly show her tenderness toward the little girl.

Thus, Laura Bell became Mrs. Pendennis's daughter. Neither her husband nor the Major viewed her favorably. She reminded the first of painful circumstances in his wife's past that he preferred to forget, and the second saw no relation to his noble family or fortune, possessing only a couple of thousand pounds.

Now, let Mr. Pen enter, who has been waiting all this while.

Having steel-edged his nerves and prepared himself outside the door for a confrontation, he stepped inside, determined to face the formidable uncle. He convinced himself the encounter would be fierce, resolving to endure it with the courage and dignity of his famous family. He flung open the door and entered with a severe, warlike expression, armed cap-a-pie, lance couched, plumes displayed, glaring at his adversary as if to challenge him: "Come on, I'm ready."

The experienced old man, surveying the boy's pomposity, could hardly suppress a grin at his admirable simplicity. Major Pendennis had also assessed the situation. Seeing that the widow was already leaning toward the enemy and knowing that threats would fail against a stubborn, overly serious boy, the Major dropped his authoritative tone instantly. With a warm, natural smile, he held out his hands, shook Pen's passive fingers gaily, and said, "Well, Pen, my boy, tell us all about it."

Helen was delighted by the Major's generosity. Conversely, it completely baffled and disappointed poor Pen, whose nerves were strung for a tragedy. He felt his grand entrance had been thwarted and looked ludicrous. Blushing and wincing with mortified vanity, he stammered, "I—I—I didn't know you were coming until just now... Is—is town very full, I suppose?"

If Pen struggled to swallow his tears, the Major barely managed to keep from laughing. He glanced comically at Mrs. Pendennis, who also found the scene simultaneously ridiculous and sentimental. With nothing else to say, she

Let those who possess a genuine, heartfelt love for London society and the exclusive privileges of its highest circles admit that Major Pendennis was a man of extraordinary generosity and deep affection in the sacrifice he was now making. In May, he abandoned the capital—its newspapers, its morning routines, its endless circuit of clubs, his private calls upon "my Ladies," his gallops along Rotten Row, his dinners, his box at the Opera, his frantic weekend dashes to Fulham and Richmond, his bows to the Duke and the Marquis at grand entertainments, and even his name in the *Morning Post* the following day. He surrendered his quiet, select, and delightful festivals to lock himself inside a lonely country house. Here, he would reside with a simple widow, a greenhorn of a son, a sentimental curate, and a little girl of ten.

He made this magnificent sacrifice, and it was all the more impressive because few realized its magnitude. His letters arrived franked from town, and he presented the invitations to Helen with a visible sigh. It was a scene of beautiful tragedy to watch him decline party after party—at least for those who understood, as Helen did not, the melancholy grandeur of his self-denial. Helen merely smiled at the awful pathos with which the Major spoke of the *Court Guide*; young Pen, however, regarded the great names on the envelopes with profound respect. He listened with constant interest and sympathy to his uncle's stories of the fashionable world.

The elder Pendennis's memory was a treasure trove of thousands of such delightful tales, which he poured into Pen's eager ears with unfailing eloquence. He knew the name and pedigree of everyone in the Peerage and their intricate relationships.

"My dear boy," he would say, with mournful earnestness and absolute truth, "you cannot begin your genealogical studies too early. I wish to Heaven you would read *Debrett* every single day. Do not focus on the historical parts (for, between ourselves, many pedigrees are purely fabulous, and few families can boast such a clear descent as our own); instead, study the accounts of family alliances and connections. I have known a man's entire career blasted by ignorance on this vital subject. Just last month, at dinner at Lord Hobanob's, a newly admitted young man, Mr. Suckling (author of a certain work), began to joke lightly about Admiral Bowser's treachery to Ministers in the most audacious manner imaginable. But who do you think sat next to him? Why, it was Lady Grampound Bowser's daughter, and opposite him sat Lord Grampound Bowser's son-in-law. The infatuated youth went on cutting jokes at the Admiral's expense, fancying the whole world laughed with him. Imagine Lady Hobanob's feelings—Hobanob's!—and the sentiments of every well-bred man as this wretch exposed himself. He will never dine in South Street again. I promise you that."

With such discourses, the Major entertained his nephew while pacing the terrace during his two-hour constitutional or sitting together after dinner over wine. He grieved that Sir Francis Clavering had not moved to the park to create a local society, and he mourned that Lord Eyrie was absent, preventing an introduction.

"He has daughters," the Major noted. "Who knows? You might have married Lady Emily or Lady Barbara Trehawk. But alas, those dreams are over; my poor fellow, you must lie on the bed you have made for yourself."

These words inclined young Pendennis seriously, though they lacked the charm of oral delivery.
