Yet, the Major's anecdotes of Great George, the Royal Dukes, and the statesmen and beauties of the day filled Pen's soul with longing and wonder. While these conversations sadly bored and perplexed poor Mrs. Pendennis, they were anything but tedious for Pen.

One cannot claim that Mr. Pen's new guide, philosopher, and friend discoursed on the most elevated subjects, nor did he treat them with high-minded gravity. However, his morality was consistent. It may not have aided a man's progress in the next world, but it was excellently calculated to advance his interests in this one. Furthermore, the Major never doubted for an instant that his views were the only practical ones and that his conduct was perfectly virtuous and respectable. He was, in a word, a man of honor with his eyes wide open. Taking pity on this young greenhorn nephew, he sought to open his eyes as well.

No man attended church more regularly in the country than the old bachelor.

"It doesn't matter so much in town, Pen," he explained. "There, the women go, and the men aren't missed. But when a gentleman is *sur ses terres*, he must set an example for the country folk. If I could turn a tune, I'd even consider singing. The Duke of Saint David's, whom I have the honor of knowing, always sings in the country, and let me tell you, it has a damned fine effect from the family pew. And you are someone down here. As long as the Claverings are away, you are the first man in the parish, and as good as any. You might even represent the town if you played your cards right. Your poor dear father would have done so had he lived; so might you. Though, not if you marry an amiable lady whom the country people wouldn't recognize. Well, well... it's a painful subject. Let us change it, my boy."

Yet, if the Major changed the subject once, he returned to it a dozen times that day. The moral of his discourse was invariably that Pen was throwing himself away.

Now, it requires little coaxing to make a simple boy believe he is a fine fellow. Pen eagerly accepted his uncle's counsel. The conversation of Captain Costigan had become unpleasant to him, and the image of that tipsy, unshaven, punch-reeked father-in-law haunted him with terror. He could not bring that man to associate with his mother. Even regarding Emily, he faltered when the pitiless guardian questioned him.

"Was she accomplished?"
He was forced to admit, no.
"Was she clever?"
"Well, she possessed a very good average intellect," he stammered, unable to affirm she was truly clever.
"Come, let us see some of her letters."

Pen confessed he possessed only three—the trivial invitations and replies we have already mentioned.
"She is cautious enough," the Major said dryly. "She is older than you, my poor boy." Then, with utmost frankness and humility, he apologized, flinging himself upon Pen's good feelings and begging the lad to forgive a fond old uncle who acted solely for his family's honor. For Arthur was ready to flare with indignation whenever Miss Costigan's honesty was doubted, swearing he would never have her name mentioned lightly and would never, under any circumstances, part from her.

Arthur repeated this defense to his uncle and friends at home, and, it must be confessed, to Miss Fotheringay and the amiable family at Chatteris, where he still spent some time. When Miss Emily heard news of the guardian's arrival, she correctly assumed he came with hostile intentions toward herself.

"I suppose you intend to leave me now that your grand relation has come down from town. He'll carry you off, and you'll forget your poor Emily, Mr. Arthur!"

Forget her? In the presence of Miss Rouncy, the Columbine and Milly's confidential friend, and even the Captain himself, Pen swore he could never think of any woman but his beloved Miss Fotheringay. The Captain, looking up at the foils hanging as trophies on the wall of the room where they used to fence, grimly stated he would advise no man to rashly meddle with the affections of his darling child. He vowed he would never believe his gallant young Arthur—who he treated as a son and called his son—would ever commit conduct so revolting to every idea of honor and humanity. He rose to embrace Pen, crying and wiping his eye with one large, dirty hand while clasping the boy with the other. Arthur shuddered in that grip, thinking of his uncle at home. His father-in-law looked unusually dirty and shabby; the odor of whisky-and-water was even more pronounced than usual. How was he to reconcile that man with his mother? He trembled recalling that he had written to Costigan, enclosing a sovereign (a loan the worthy gentleman needed), stating that one day he hoped to sign himself "your affectionate son, Arthur Pendennis."

Gladdened to escape Chatteris that day—from Miss Rouncy the confidante, the old toping father-in-law, and the divine Emily herself—Arthur cried inwardly as he rattled homeward on Rebecca: "Oh, Emily, Emily, you little know what sacrifices I am making for you!—for you who are always so cold, so cautious, so mistrustful!" He thought of a character in Pope to whom he had often involuntarily compared her.

Pen never rode over to Chatteris for a specific errand without the Major discovering it. Faithful to his plan, the Major offered no direct hindrance; yet, the constant feeling that the senior's eye was upon him, accompanied by an uneasy shame anticipating the inevitable confession of the evening's conversation, caused Pen to visit his charmer less frequently than before. There was no use deceiving him; no pretext of dining with Smirke or reading Greek plays with Foker sufficed. Upon returning from a flying visit, Pen felt that everyone knew whence he came, appearing quite guilty before his mother and guardian while they studied books or played picquet.

Once,

Major Pendennis strolled leisurely back toward the George Inn, the weight of the evening's events settling comfortably upon his shoulders. Just as he approached the entrance, his faithful valet, Mr. Morgan, emerged from the shadows of the doorway. With that particular blend of deference and omniscience characteristic of long-serving servants, Morgan intercepted his master just as he reached for his bedroom candle.

"I think, sir, if you'd grace the coffee-room with your presence, there's a young gentleman inside you'd be keen to meet," Morgan announced.

"Is Mr. Arthur here?" the Major demanded, his voice rising an octave in genuine irritation.

"No, sir. But his bosom buddy, Mr. Foker, is. It's Lady Agnes Foker's son, sir. He's been comatose in the coffee-room since lunchtime and just rang for his coffee. I reckon, p'raps, you'd enjoy a chat with him," the valet suggested, swinging open the door.

The Major stepped inside. Indeed, there sat Mr. Foker, the sole inhabitant of the establishment. He was vigorously rubbing the sleep from his eyes, seated before a table cluttered with empty decanters and the skeletal remains of a heavy dessert. He had intended to attend the theater, but a copious meal had summoned a sudden, irresistible desire for slumber; consequently, he had thrown his legs onto the bench and surrendered to a nap rather than the drama.

The Major paused, mentally rehearsing an approach, but Foker anticipated the awkwardness.

"Care for the evening paper, sir?" Foker asked, his tone instantly warm and communicative. He picked up *The Globe* from the table and held it out.

"Much obliged," the Major replied, accepting the paper with a gracious bow. "If I'm reading the family tree correctly, I'm addressing Mr. Henry Foker, son of Lady Agnes. I've the distinct honor of knowing her ladyship, and you certainly carry the Rosherville countenance."

"Hullo! Pardon me," Foker interrupted, correcting himself mid-sentence. "I nearly called you a 'commercial gent,' but I caught myself in time. To whom have I the privilege of speaking?"

"To a relative of a friend and schoolmate of mine—Arthur Pendennis, my nephew, who speaks of you in the highest terms. I am Major Pendennis, of whom you may have heard. May I join you at your table? I've sat at your grandfather's before."

"Sir, you flatter me!" Foker exclaimed, displaying considerable courtesy. "So, you're Arthur's uncle?"

"And guardian," the Major added proudly.

"He's a swell fellow, sir," Foker observed. "Glad to hear you say so."

"And sharp, too. Though I confess, I'm a bit of a dullard myself. But I know a clever chap when I see one, and I like 'em."

"You display both taste and humility," the Major noted approvingly. "Arthur speaks highly of your talents."

"Not so much with the books," Foker admitted, shaking his head. "Never could crack that code. Pendennis could—he used to pen half the fellows' verses. Yet—" Here the young man broke into a candid confession. "Since you're his guardian, I'll risk it: I think he's what we call 'flat.'"

The Major immediately found himself ensconced in a fascinating, confidential dialogue. "In what way is Arthur 'flat'?" he asked, smiling.

Foker leaned in, winking conspiratorially. He possessed a rare quality of boldness born of intoxication and confidence; he would have winked at the Duke of Wellington with equal ease. "Flat, sir? About women, I mean."

"He's not unique in that department, my dear Harry," the Major replied smoothly. "Tell me more."

"Well, sir, it's partly my fault. He went to the play one night—you see, I'm currently down here reading for my small role during the Long Vacation, though I pop over from Baymouth frequently in my carriage. Anyway, we hit the theater, and Pen was absolutely smitten with Miss Fotheringay. Her real name is Costigan; she's a stunning creature. Next morning, I introduced him to the General, whom we call her father—a proper old rogue and a devotee of whisky-and-water. Naturally, he fell for her hook, line, and sinker. In fact, he's already proposed!" Foker slammed his hand on the table, causing the remaining dessert dishes to rattle ominously.

"What! You knew this?" the Major gasped.

"Knew it? Don't I? And plenty more besides. We were discussing it at mess yesterday, roasting Derby Oaks until he was frothing at the mouth. Do you know Sir Derby Oaks? We dined together recently. Remember standing at the door smoking when you slipped past for dinner?"

"I recall Sir Thomas Oaks, his father, before he became a Baronet. He lived in Cavendish Square and served as physician to Queen Charlotte."

"The young rake is spinning money fast, I assure you," Foker declared.

"So, Sir Derby Oaks is another suitor?" the Major asked, delighted and anxious simultaneously.

"Another what?" Foker queried innocently.

"Another admirer of Miss Fotheringay?"

"Lord bless you! We call him Mondays, Wednesdays, and Fridays, and Pen holds Tuesdays, Thursdays, and Saturdays. Mind you, no foul play involved! Miss Fotheringay is far too alert for that. She plays them off against each other; she's got two strings to her bow."

"I find you remarkably awake yourself, Mr. Foker," the Major laughed.

"Pretty well, thanks, sir. How are you?" Foker replied, unflappable. "I may not be brilliant, perhaps, but I'm 'downy.' Friendly folks say I keep fairly good track of the hour. Can I tell you the time?"

"On my word," the Major beamed, "you could be of immense service. You're a man of the world, someone one enjoys dealing with. Now, as such, I needn't hint that our family is less than thrilled by this absurd entanglement Arthur has gotten into."

"I shouldn't doubt it," Foker agreed. "Connection ineligible. Excessive beer consumption on the premises. No Irish need apply. That seems to be your drift."

"That is precisely it," the Major confirmed, though he wasn't entirely sure what Foker meant by the specific phrasing. He pressed on, interrogating his new acquaintance about the respectable Costigan household. To his surprise, Foker spoke highly of Mr. and Miss Costigan's moral fiber.

"You see," Foker explained, "I believe the General loves a hearty tankard. If I wanted to bet big, I wouldn't put my money in his pocket—but he keeps a hawk-like eye on his daughter. Neither he nor she tolerate anything but propriety. Pen's attentions are the talk of the entire company. I hear details from a young lady who used to be close to her; we occasionally share tea with her family. Miss Rouncy tells me Sir Derby Oaks has been hovering around Miss Fotheringay ever since his regiment arrived, but Pen has recently swooped in and ousted him. This has driven the Baronet nearly mad; he's almost ready to propose himself. Wish he would! Then you'd see which of the two Miss Fotheringay would leap at."

"I suspected as much," the Major said, relieved. "You bring me great joy, Mr. Foker. I wish I'd met you sooner."

"Didn't want to stick my oar in," Foker shrugged. "I wait to be asked, and if there are no objections, I speak freely. Heard your man was sniffing around my servant. Didn't realize what was brewing until Miss Fotheringay and Miss Rouncy started screaming about ostrich feathers, when Miss R. spilled the beans."

"Miss Rouncy, I gather, was the confidante?"

"Confidante? Yes. She's twice as smart as Fotheringay, literary and all that, while Miss Foth can barely read."

"She can write," the Major interjected, recalling the contents of Pen's breast pocket.

Foker erupted into a sardonic cackle. "He, he! Rouncy writes her letters. Every single one. Since they quarreled, she doesn't know how to function. Miss Rouncy has a lovely hand, whereas the old girl makes a terrible mess of spelling and grammar unless Bows is nearby. Rouncy has been setting her copies lately; she writes beautifully."

"I assume you know this well," the Major teased archly. On this, Foker winked again.

"I'd pay dearly for a sample of her handwriting," the Major continued. "Surely you could provide one?"

"No, no, that would be wicked," Foker protested. "Perhaps I shouldn't have said so much. Miss F.'s writing isn't so bad, I suppose; she just had Miss R. draft the first letter and has stuck with it. But mark my words: until they reconcile, the letters stop."

"I hope they never do," the Major stated sincerely. "I cannot express my delight at meeting you. As a worldly man, you must appreciate how fatal this step is for my nephew's future, and how eager we are to liberate him from this ridiculous engagement."

"He's come out strong," Foker noted. "I've seen his verses; Rouncy copied

The Major and Captain Costigan were veterans of the field, seasoned men who faced enemies without losing their composure. However, the rest of the gathering in Cos's sitting-room found themselves somewhat rattled by Pendennis's sudden appearance. Miss Fotheringay's heart, usually steady, began to flutter; a deep, healthy flush colored her cheeks as Lieutenant Sir Derby Oaks fixed her with a scowl.

Perched in the window-seat, a little crooked old man who had been trying to transcribe theatre music while watching the two gentlemen fence—a spectacle of stamping and jumping that had forced him to abandon his pen—looked up eagerly at the newcomer. As the Major, distinguished by his impeccably blacked boots, swept into the room offering the most graceful bows to everyone, the old man leaned forward. "Me daughter—me friend, Mr. Bows—me gallant young pupil and friend, I may call 'em, Sir Derby Oaks," announced Costigan, waving his hand grandly to introduce the trio. "In one moment, Meejor, I'm your humble servant!"

With surprising speed, the energetic Captain dashed into his adjoining bedroom. In less than a minute, he transformed himself: twisting his lank hair with an ancient brush, tearing off his worn stock to reveal a fresh one crafted by Emily, donning a crisp new collar, and slipping into a handsome coat ordered specifically for Miss Fotheringay's benefit. Following him, Sir Derby emerged from the same chamber, encasing his large frame in a tiny shell-jacket that fit so snugly it looked ridiculous, yet which Miss Fotheringay, Costigan, and even poor Pen admired immensely.

While the actress and the new arrival exchanged pleasantries regarding the weather, Costigan returned, now sporting his new "shoot" jacket. "I needn't apologize to ye, Meejor," he said in his richest, most courteous tone, "for receiving ye in me shirt-sleeves."

"An old soldier can't be better employed than in teaching a young one the use of his sword," the Major replied gallantly. "I recall hearing in old times that you wielded yours with skill, Captain Costigan."

"What? Ye've heard of Jack Costigan, Major?" the other exclaimed, feigning great surprise. The Major had indeed pried details from his nephew about this Irish officer. Whether he truly remembered the man or merely relied on second-hand gossip was unclear, but Major Pendennis was a man of honor and veracity. He stated firmly that he perfectly recalled meeting Mr. Costigan and hearing him sing at Sir Richard Strachan's table during the Walcheren campaign.

At this revelation, delivered with such bland cordiality, Bows looked up, utterly bewildered. "But we will discuss these matters another time," the Major continued, perhaps wishing to avoid committing himself further. "It is to Miss Fotheringay I have come today to pay my respects." He executed another bow so courtly and gracious that, if she had been a duchess, he could hardly have improved upon it.

"I had heard of your performances from my nephew, madam," the Major added. "Who raves about you, as I believe you know well. But Arthur is but a boy, a wild enthusiast whose opinions one must not take literally. I confess I was anxious to judge for myself. Permit me to say your performance delighted and astonished me. I have seen our finest actresses, and on my word, I think you surpass them all. You are as majestic as Mrs. Siddons."

"Faith, I always said so," Costigan winked at his daughter. "Major, take a chair."

Milly rose, retrieved an unripped satin garment from the only empty seat, and wheeled the chair toward the Major with one of her finest curtsies. "You are as pathetic as Miss O'Neill," the Major continued, settling in. "Your songs remind me of Mrs. Jordan in her prime, Captain Costigan; your manner evokes Mars. Have you ever seen the Mars, Miss Fotheringay?"

"There were two Mahers in Crow Street," remarked Miss Emily. "Fanny was decent enough, but Biddy wasn't much."

"Sure, the Major means the god of war, Milly, my dear," interjected the parent.

"It is not that Mars I meant, though Venus may be pardoned for thinking about him," the Major replied, directing a smile squarely at Sir Derby Oaks, who had re-entered in his shell-jacket. Yet, the lady failed to grasp the words, and the compliment did nothing to soothe Sir Derby. Far from pleased, the young baronet received it with sullen stiffness, scowling uneasily at Miss Fotheringay with an expression that clearly asked, *What the deuce is this man doing here?*

Major Pendennis was not in the least annoyed by the gentleman's ill humor; on the contrary, it delighted him. "So," he thought, "a rival is in the field." He silently vowed that Sir Derby might become not only a rival but a victor in this love match involving himself and Pen.

"I fear I interrupted your fencing lesson," the Major said aloud. "But my stay in Chatteris is brief, and I was eager to introduce myself to my old fellow-campaigner, Captain Costigan, and to see a lady who had charmed me so much from the stage. I was not the only man smitten last night, Miss Fotheringay—if I may call you so, though your family name is ancient and noble. There was a reverend friend of mine who went home in raptures over Ophelia; and I saw Sir Derby Oaks throw a bouquet that no actress ever deserved better. I should have brought one myself had I known what awaited me. Are not those the very flowers in a glass of water on the mantelpiece yonder?"

"I am very fond of flowers," Miss Fotheringay replied, casting a languishing glance at Sir Derby Oaks—but the Baronet remained sullen.

"Sweets to the sweet—isn't that the expression from the play?" Mr. Pendennis asked, determined to remain good-natured.

"'Pon my life, I don't know. Very likely it is. I ain't much of a literary man," answered Sir Derby.

"Is it possible?" the Major feigned surprise. "Then you don't inherit your father's love of letters, Sir Derby? He was a remarkably fine scholar, and I had the honor of knowing him well."

"Indeed," the other muttered, giving a sulky wag of his head.

"He saved my life," continued Pendennis.

"Did he now?" cried Miss Fotheringay, rolling her eyes first in surprise at the Major, then with gratitude toward Sir Derby—but the latter was immune to such glances. Far from being pleased that the Apothecary, his father, had saved Major Pendennis's life, the young man actually looked as if he wished the event had turned out differently.

"My father, I believe, was a very good doctor," the young gentleman said by way of reply. "I'm not in that line myself. I wish you good morning, sir. I've got an appointment—Cos, bye-bye—Miss Fotheringay, good morning."

Despite the young lady's imploring looks and appealing smiles, the Dragoon bowed stiffly out of the room. The clatter of his sabre echoed as he strode down the creaking stairs, followed by angry tones cursing little Tom Creed, who was playing in the passage, and whose peg-top Sir Derby had kicked away with an oath into the street.

The Major did not smile, though he had every reason to be amused. "Monstrously handsome young man that—as fine a-looking soldier as ever I saw," he told Costigan.

"A credit to the army and to human nature in general," answered Costigan. "A young man of refined manners, polite affability, and princely fortune. His table is sumptuous; he's adored in the regiment; and he rides sixteen stone."

"A perfect champion," said the Major, laughing. "No doubt all the ladies admire him."

"He's very well, in spite of his weight, now he's young," said Milly. "But he's no conversation."

"He's best on horseback," Mr. Bows noted.

On this, Milly replied that the Baronet had ridden third in the steeplechase on his horse Tareaways. The Major began to comprehend that the young lady herself lacked particular genius and wondered how she could act so well despite her stupidity.

Costigan, exercising Irish hospitality, pressed refreshments upon his guest. The Major, who was no hungrier than one would be after a Lord Mayor's dinner, declared he desired a biscuit and a glass of wine above all else, claiming he felt faint from fasting. He knew, however, that accepting small kindnesses flattered donors and ensured goodwill.

"Some of the old Madeira, Milly, love," Costigan winked at his child.

That lady, exchanging a glance of intelligence with her father, exited the room and descended the stairs, where she softly summoned her little emissary, Master Tommy Creed. Giving him a coin, she ordered him to rush to the Grapes for a pint of Madeira and the baker's for sixpence-worth of assorted biscuits, returning quickly so he might save two for himself.

While Tommy Creed was away on this errand, Miss Costigan sat below with Mrs. Creed, telling the landlady that Mr. Arthur Pendennis's uncle, the Major, was

Early in this chronicle, we introduced Mr. Garbetts, the Principal Tragedian—a promising, athletic youth of jovial habits and erratic tendencies. He shared a deep camaraderie with Mr. Costigan, serving as the chief decoration alongside him at the convivial gatherings of the Magpie Hotel. These two gentlemen frequently assisted one another in various financial maneuvers, lending each other their valuable signatures with reckless abandon. They were, in essence, fast friends; though Mr. Garbetts rarely ventured to Costigan's residence, largely because he was disliked by Miss Fotheringay, a fact that bred considerable jealousy in Mrs. Garbetts. The truth of the matter was that Garbetts had once courted Miss Fotheringay, only to be rejected before he ever considered proposing to Mrs. Garbetts. However, their romantic entanglements are irrelevant to our current narrative. Suffice it to say, Captain Costigan summoned Mr. Garbetts immediately after his daughter and Mr. Bows departed, seeking a confidant fit for the delicate situation at hand.

Mr. Garbetts was a towering figure with a booming voice and a ferocious demeanor. He possessed the finest legs in the entire company and could snap a poker in half merely for sport, crushing it against his muscular arm. "Run, Tommy," commanded Captain Costigan, dispatching the small messenger. "Fetch Mr. Garbetts from his lodgings above the tripe shop, and instruct them to bring two hot glasses of whisky-and-water from the Grapes."

Tommy obeyed promptly. Soon, Mr. Garbetts arrived, accompanied by the requested spirits. Captain Costigan withheld the full details of recent events from the actor, relying instead on the alcohol to soften his resolve. Under the influence, Garbetts drafted a menacing letter addressed to Major Pendennis. The note demanded that the Major cease all interference with the planned union between Arthur Pendennis and Miss Fotheringay, setting a date for the wedding forthwith. Alternatively, it threatened to grant the Captain the customary satisfaction due between honorable gentlemen. Should the Major refuse, the Captain ominously hinted that he would compel compliance using a horsewhip. The exact wording remains unknown to us, though it undoubtedly bore the Captain's finest rhetorical flourish and was sealed with the grand silver seal of the Costigan family—the sole piece of heirloom plate the Captain owned.

Garbetts was dispatched with this missive. After squeezing his ambassador's hand and wishing divine blessings upon him, the General retrieved his venerable, lethal flintlock pistols—weapons that had claimed the lives of many handsome youths in Dublin. Satisfied with their condition, he proceeded to the drawer where he kept Arthur Pendennis's letters and poems, items he invariably reviewed before allowing his daughter to read them.

Within twenty minutes, Garbetts returned, his face etched with anxiety and defeat. "Have you seen him?" asked the Captain.
"Yes," Garbetts replied.
"And when is the meeting?" Costigan inquired, testing the lock of an antique pistol and raising it toward his bloodshot eye.
"When is *what* for?" Garbetts asked, bewildered.
"The duel, my dear fellow?"
"You don't mean mortal combat, Captain!" Garbetts exclaimed, horrified.
"What the devil else do I mean, Garbetts? I intend to shoot the man who has betrayed my honor, or become a martyr myself!"
"Damn it, I won't carry challenges," Garbetts declared firmly. "I'm a family man, Captain, and I have no part in pistol duels. Take back your letter." To the astonishment and outrage of Captain Costigan, the actor threw the letter onto the floor, its sprawling script and blotched seal landing amidst the debris.

"You don't mean you saw him and didn't deliver the letter?" the Captain roared in fury.
"I saw him, but I could not speak with him, Captain," Garbetts explained calmly.
"Why the devil not?"
"There was someone present I wished to avoid, and surely you would too," the tragedian answered in a sepulchral tone. "Minion Tatham was there, Captain."
"The cowardly scoundrel!" Costigan screamed. "He's frightened and already plotting to swear the peace against me."
"I have nothing to do with fighting, mark my words," Garbetts insisted stubbornly. "Nor do I wish to have seen Tatham, nor that piece of..."
"Hold your tongue, Bob Acres. I believe you are no better than a coward," Costigan retorted, quoting Sir Lucius O'Trigger—a role he had mastered both on and off the stage. After further heated debate, the two parted ways in ill humor. This exchange has been summarized here, as the reader understands the core issue. Now, however, we must explain why it is impossible to provide a full account of the letter sent to Major Pendennis: the gentleman never even opened it.

When Miss Costigan returned from rehearsal, accompanied by her loyal servant Mr. Bows, she found her father pacing their apartment in a state of high agitation, surrounded by the potent scent of whisky-and-water, which evidently failed to soothe his troubled mind. Papers belonging to the Pendennis family lay scattered on the table around empty goblets and a discarded teaspoon used for mixing the Captain's drink. As Emily entered, he grabbed her in a tight embrace, crying out, "Prepare yourself, my child, my blessed child," his voice trembling with agony and his eyes filled with tears.

"You're tipsy again, Papa," Miss Fotheringay chided, pushing him away gently. "You promised you wouldn't drink before dinner."
"It is to forget my sorrows, my poor girl, that I've taken just a drop," the bereaved father wailed. "To drown my cares, I drain the bowl."
"Your cares require a great deal of drowning, Captain dear," Mr. Bows mocked, imitating his friend's heavy accent. "What has happened? Did that soft-spoken gentleman in the wig annoy you?"
"The oily miscreant! I'll have his blood!" roared Costigan.

Miss Milly had already retreated to her room to remove her bonnet and shawl. "I thought he meant mischief. He was so unusually civil," she noted later. "What did he want?"
"Oh, Bows! He has overwhelmed me," the Captain lamented. "There is a diabolical conspiracy against my poor girl. In my opinion, both the Pendennises—the nephew and the uncle—are infernal traitors and scoundrels who ought to be consumed from the face of the earth."
"What is it? What occurred?" Mr. Bows asked, growing increasingly agitated.

Costigan recounted the Major's assertion that young Arthur earned neither two thousand nor two hundred pounds annually. He expressed his rage that he had allowed such an impostor to woo and deceive his innocent daughter, nurturing a viper within his own household. "I have shaken the reptile free," Costigan declared. "As for his uncle, I shall exact a revenge so severe he will regret the day he insulted a Costigan."
"What do you mean, General?" Bows asked.
"I mean to take his life, Bows—his villainous, skulking life," the Captain growled, tapping the battered old pistol case ominously.

Bows had often heard him threaten to use that box of death against enemies, but since the Captain hadn't mentioned writing a challenge, Bows largely ignored the pistols at that moment. Just then, Miss Fotheringay returned to the sitting room, appearing perfectly healthy, happy, and unconcerned—a stark contrast to her father's delirious tremor of grief and anger. She carried a pair of nearly white satin shoes, which she intended to polish with breadcrumbs before wearing them the following Tuesday evening in the role of Ophelia.

She glanced at the papers on the table, paused as if to ask a question, but decided against it. Instead, she fetched a suitable piece of bread from the cupboard to clean her slippers. Returning to the table, she sat comfortably with the shoes and asked her father in her honest Irish brogue, "What are you doing with Master Arthur's letters, poems, and rubbish? Surely you don't intend to read that nonsense again?"

"Oh, Emilee!" cried the Captain. "That boy whom I loved as the apple of my eye is merely a scoundrel and a deceiver, my poor girl!" He cast a tragic look at Mr. Bows, who returned an anxious gaze.
"He! Pooh! That poor lad is as simple as a schoolboy," she dismissed lightly. "Children write verses and nonsense."
"He has acted as a viper at this hearth and a traitor in this family!" the Captain shouted. "Tell you what, he is no better than an impostor."
"What exactly has the poor fellow done, Papa?" Emily asked.
"Done? He has deceived us in the most atrocious manner," her father declared. "He trifled with your affections and outraged my noble feelings. He presented himself as a man of substance, only to reveal he is no better than a beggar. Didn't I often tell you he had two thousand a year? He is a pauper, I tell you, Miss Costigan; dependent entirely on his mother's bounty. A decent woman, true, who may remarry and likely live forever, earning only five hundred a year. How dare he ask you to marry into a family unable to support you? You have been grossly deceived, Milly, and I suspect his old ruffian uncle in the wig is complicit

Meanwhile, at Fairoaks, everyone was beginning to worry that the Major hadn't returned. Just then, Dr. Portman and his wife, heading home to Clavering, pulled up at Helen's lodge gate. They delivered a short note from Major Pendennis. In it, he explained he'd stay another day at Chatteris to discuss legal matters with Messrs. Tatham, whom he planned to meet that afternoon. Notably, he didn't mention the morning's dramatic encounter. The note was penned during a lull in the chaos, precisely when the Major had decisively lost the argument.

Young Pen, however, wasn't eager to venture into town while his uncle was around. He disliked the idea of his guardian potentially watching him from the Dean's lawn while he tried to woo Miss Costigan inside. The simple joy of a solitary walk—a rare luxury for him—would have been ruined by bumping into the "polished boot" of authority. Pen's affection was a secret thing, visible only in his eyes, which he gazed at Miss Costigan with violent intensity whenever possible. But in front of others, it remained silent. And honestly, that was better. Of all the chatter in the world, the ramblings of love-struck fools sound the silliest to anyone who hasn't lived through it. It's a vocabulary without a key, a lamp without a flame. Try reading some old love letters you've long forgotten; they seem utterly blank and meaningless now. What magical infatuation made that nonsense beautiful back then? One wonders how such wailing and trash could ever bring happiness. Yet, there were times when you kissed those silly pages with rapture, living on six absurd lines for a week, until the inevitable crash came, leaving you restless and miserable until you got a fresh supply of folly.

That is exactly why we refuse to publish the letters and verses Mr. Pen wrote during this phase of his life. Out of pure respect for the young man's character, we'll keep them bundled up with Miss Fotheringay's string and sealed with Captain Costigan's great silver seal. They are simply too *spooney* and wild for young ladies to read in cold blood. Bide your time, young women; perhaps you'll compose similar masterpieces yourselves soon. For now, let us respect Mr. Pen's first outpourings.

The Major emerged from his meeting with Captain Costigan in a state of concentrated fury that made him terrifying to approach. *"The impudent bog-trotting scamp!"* he fumed internally. *"Dares to threaten me! Dares to suggest his damned Costigans marry the Pendennises! Send me a challenge! If he can find a gentleman to carry it, I'm game. Psha! What would people say if I fought a tipsy mountebank over a row with an actress in a barn?"*

When he encountered Dr. Portman, who anxiously asked about the outcome of his battle with the "dragon," the Major decided not to reveal the General's insolence. Instead, he claimed the affair was ugly, disagreeable, and far from over. He strictly enjoined the doctor and his wife to say nothing about the business at Fairoaks, sending them off with the note we previously mentioned. Then, he retreated to his hotel, where he vented his wrath on Mr. Morgan, his valet, cursing upstairs and downstairs, according to Mr. Morgan's report to Mr. Foker's man.

The servant relayed the news to his master. Mr. Foker, finishing his breakfast at two in the afternoon, recalled his eagerness to learn the results of the interview between his two friends. Donning his brocade dressing-gown, he marched to the Major's sitting room and knocked.

Inside, the Major was indeed occupied with business regarding a lease for the widow's estate, consulting old Mr. Tatham, the lawyer who had managed his late brother's affairs. Mr. Tatham and his client were deep in discussion when Mr. Foker appeared, displaying his magnificent dressing gown and embroidered skull cap. Seeing the Major engrossed in papers and red tape alongside an elderly gentleman with a white head, the modest youth initially hesitated to interrupt. *"Oh, you're busy—call again another time,"* he murmured.

But Mr. Pendennis waved him in with a smile. Mr. Foker promptly removed his embroidered tarboosh (worked by the fondest of mothers) and advanced, bowing gracefully. Mr. Tatham had never seen such a splendid apparition before. The brocaded youth seated himself in an armchair, spreading out his crimson skirts, radiating kindness and frankness.

*"You seem to like my dressing-gown, sir,"* Foker remarked to Mr. Tatham. *"A pretty thing, isn't it? Neat, but not in the least gaudy. And how do you do, Major Pendennis, sir, and how does the world treat you?"*

There was something in Foker's manner that would have softened even an Inquisitor, smoothing the wrinkles beneath the Major's hair. *"I've had an interview with that Irishman—you may speak freely before my friend, Mr. Tatham, who knows all our family affairs—and it hasn't been very satisfactory. He refuses to believe my nephew is poor; claims we're both liars. He even hinted I was a coward as I took my leave. And I thought when you knocked, you might be the gentleman bringing a challenge from Mr. Costigan—that's how the world treats me, Mr. Foker."*

*"You don't mean that Irishman, the actress's father?"* cried Mr. Tatham, a Dissenter who held no patience for the drama.

*"That Irishman, the actress's father—the very man. Haven't you heard what a fool my nephew has made of himself about the girl?"* Mr. Tatham, who never stepped foot in a theater, knew nothing of the details. The Major recounted the story of his nephew's loves, with Mr. Foker adding appropriate commentary in his familiar style. Mr. Tathom was astounded. Why hadn't Mrs. Pendennis married a serious man? Why keep this unfortunate boy from perdition? As for Miss Costigan, he said nothing; her profession characterized her sufficiently.

Mr. Foker interjected that he had known some uncommonly good people in the "booths," as he called the Temple of the Muses. *"Well, it might be so,"* Mr. Tatham conceded hopefully. *"But the father—I know him personally—a man of the worst character, a wine-bibber, an idler in taverns and billiard rooms, and a notorious insolvent."*

*"I can understand the reason, Major,"* Tatham said. *"Why the fellow wouldn't come to my office to verify your statements. We have a writ out against him and another disreputable fellow, a play-actor, for a bill given to Mr. Skinner, a most respectable grocer and Quaker merchant. This Costigan came crying into Mr. Skinner's shop, and we haven't proceeded against either of them because neither was worth the powder and shot."*

Just as Mr. Tatham was weaving this tale, a third knock echoed at the door. An athletic gentleman in a shabby braided frock entered, holding a letter with a large, blotched red seal.

*"May I have the honor of speaking with Major Pendennis in private?"* he began. *"I bear a message from my friend, Captain Costigan."*

But the man with the bass voice paused, faltered, and turned pale. He caught sight of the red-faced, well-known visage of Mr. Tatham.

*"Hullo, Garbetts, speak up!"* cried Mr. Foker, delighted.

*"Why, bless my soul, it is the other party to the bill!"* exclaimed Mr. Tatham. *"I say, sir; stop!"*

Garbetts, his face as blank as Macbeth's upon seeing Banquo's ghost, gasped inarticulate words and fled the room. The Major's gravity shattered instantly, replaced by laughter. So did Mr. Foker. *"By Jove, it was a good 'un,"* he chuckled. Even the serious attorney laughed.

*"I don't think there'll be any fight, Major,"* young Foker predicted, beginning to mimic the tragedian. *"If there is, the old gentleman—your name Tatham?—will be happy to make your acquaintance, Mr. Tatham, and may send the bailiffs to separate the men."* Mr. Tatham readily agreed. The Major was not sorry for the ludicrous conclusion to the quarrel.

*"It seems to me, sir,"* he told Foker, *"that you always arrive to put me into good humor."*

Nor was this the only service Mr. Foker performed that day. Knowing Captain Costigan's habits, he decided to visit the General to hear firsthand what had transpired in the morning's conversation. Captain Costigan was absent, having received encouragement from his daughter to frequent the convivial club at the Magpie Hotel, where he was undoubtedly bragging about his desire to murder a certain ruffian. He was brave, knew it, and loved to air his courage among peers.

Miss Fotheringay, however, was at home, washing tea cups while Mr. Bows sat opposite.

*"Just done breakfast, I see—how do?"* poked Mr. Foker, inserting his funny little head.

*"Get out, you funny little man,"* cried Miss Fotheringay.

*"You mean come in,"* answered the other cheerfully. *"

Within a fortnight of the events previously recounted, Mr. Manager Bingley was delivering his signature role of 'Rolla' in *Pizarro* to an audience so sparse it threatened to collapse under the weight of its emptiness. It became evident that while the part of Rolla was a favorite with the accomplished actor himself, the citizens of Chatteris held little affection for it. Scarcely anyone filled the theater. Poor Pen occupied the boxes almost alone, sitting there with bloodshot eyes, leaning precariously over the ledge and staring haggardly toward the stage.

When Cora was not center-stage, he saw nothing. Spaniards, Peruvians, processions, battles, priests, and virgins of the sun marched in and out, engaging in their theatrical dialogues, yet Arthur paid them no heed. His entire world narrowed down to Cora, the object of his soul's longing. Later, he confessed that he marveled he hadn't grabbed a pistol to shoot her; he was consumed by a volatile mix of love, rage, and despair. Had it not been for his mother at home—who remained silent regarding his misfortune but offered a comforting, watchful sympathy—he might have succumbed to desperation, ending his days prematurely in front of Chatteris gaol.

There he sat, miserable, fixed on her image. Yet, she took no more notice of him than he did of the rest of the house. Miss Fotheringay was strikingly handsome, dressed in white raiment and leopard skin, with a golden sun pinned to her breast and fine, glittering bracelets adorning her beautiful arms. She delivered her few lines to perfection and embodied the role even better. The eyes that had once shattered Pen's soul rolled and gleamed with their usual luster, but tonight they were not directed at him. He did not know to whom they belonged, nor did he notice the two gentlemen seated in the box adjacent to his.

Nor had Pen observed the dramatic shift that occurred shortly after these men entered. With the house nearly empty, the first act dragged on interminably, leading to whispers of refunding tickets, reminiscent of another disastrous night when Pen had been driven out. The actors were indifferent, yawning through their lines and chatting loudly during pauses. Even Bingley was listless, and Mrs. Bingley, playing Elvira, muttered her lines under her breath.

Then, suddenly, Mrs. Bingley raised her voice and bellowed like a bull of Bashan. Bingley, shedding his apathy, darted across the stage yelling like Dean. Garretts, Rowkins, and Miss Rouncy abandoned all subtlety, strutting, scowling, and shouting their lines with maximum volume, all aimed squarely at the two gentlemen in Box Number Three.

One was a quiet, diminutive man in black with a grey head and a shrewd, jovial expression. The other was a magnificent figure: tall, portly, sporting a hooked nose and a profusion of curling brown hair and whiskers. His coat was encrusted with rich frog-braiding and velvet, layered with waistcoats, splendid rings, jeweled pins, and neck chains. When he unfolded his yellow pocket-handkerchief with a hand gloved in white kid, a delightful cloud of musk and bergamot drifted through the theater. He was clearly a personage of high rank, and the entire Chatteris troupe was performing solely for him.

In a word, he was Mr. Dolphin, the great impresario from London, accompanied by his inseparable secretary, Mr. William Minns, who never traveled without him. Within ten minutes of his arrival, his august presence transformed the theater. Bingley and the rest instantly switched gears, trying desperately to capture his attention. Even Miss Fotheringay's dull heart, usually undisturbed, fluttered slightly at the sight of the famous London manager. Her role required little more than looking handsome and standing in picturesque poses around her child, which she executed to admiration.

Despite the actors' frantic efforts to win the "Great Stage Sultan's" favor, *Pizarro* received no handshakes. Bingley yelled, Mrs. Bingley bellowed, and Mr. Dolphin merely took a pinch of snuff from his massive gold box. It was only in the final scene, when Rolla (played by Bingley, who was losing strength; his fourth son, Master Talma Bingley, being monstrously large for his age) staggered onto the stage holding the infant, rushing toward Cora who screamed, "Oh God, there's blood upon him!" that the London manager finally clapped his hands and erupted in an enthusiastic bravo.

Concluding his applause, Mr. Dolphin slapped his secretary on the shoulder and declared, "By Jove, Billy, she'll do!"
Old Billy, a sardonic gentleman, replied, "Who taught her that dodge?"
"I remember her at the Olympic," Billy mused. "Hang me if she could say 'Bo' to a goose."
It was Little Mr. Bows in the orchestra who had instructed her. As the curtain fell, the entire company swarmed around her, congratulating and hating Miss Fotheringay in equal measure.

Mr. Dolphin's sudden appearance in the remote Chatteris theater could be explained thus: despite his relentless advertising of triumphs, coruscations of talent, and victories of good old English comedy, his theater (the Museum Theatre) was failing. The great Hubbard had played legitimate drama for twenty nights without paying a single soul but himself. The celebrated Mr. and Mrs. Cawdor had toured in Mr. Rawhead's tragedies to no avail. Herr Garbe's lions and tigers had drawn crowds briefly until one animal bit a chunk out of the Herr's shoulder, prompting the Lord Chamberlain to ban the act. Even the grand Lyrical Drama, produced with unprecedented splendor featuring Monsieur Poumons as lead tenor, had nearly crushed poor Dolphin under its weight. Dragging on with half-salaries, small operas, feeble comedies, and a struggling ballet company, everyone awaited his obituary in the Gazette.

Among his illustrious patrons was the Most Noble the Marquis of Steyne, a refined lover of the arts who rarely attended plays due to state affairs. However, he occasionally arrived just in time for the ballet, receiving respectful nods from the manager. Secluded behind a curtain, invisible to the audience, Lord Steyne watched intently. His presence was known by the way the corps-de-ballet and principal dancers constantly glanced toward his box. I have witnessed dozens of pairs of eyes—like the hundred-and-twenty lovely female savages in the Palm Dance of *Cook at Otaheite*—ogling that specific box. I marvelled at the composure of Mademoiselle Sauterelle or Mademoiselle de Bondi ("la petite Caoutchoue"), who, while suspended in mid-air quivering like shuttlecocks, managed to wink lovingly at the box where the great Steyne sat. Occasionally, a harsh voice cried "Brava!" from behind the curtain, or white gloves waved in approval. Bondi or Sauterelle would land, curtsey, and smile specifically at those hands before resuming their panting ascent.

One evening, surrounded by friends, Lord Steyne's box was raucous enough to scandalize the pit, with indignant shouts demanding silence. Mr. Wenham amused the party with excerpts from a private letter from Major Pendennis, whose absence during the London season was noted with regret.

"The secret is out," Mr. Wenham announced. "There's a woman in the case."
"Why, d—— it, Wenham, he's your age," the gentleman behind the curtain retorted.
"Pour les âmes bien nées, l'amour ne compte pas le nombre des années," Mr. Wenham replied gallantly. "For my part, I hope to be a victim till I die, breaking my heart every year of my life."
The meaning was clear: *My lord, you need not worry; I'm three years younger and twice as resilient.*
"Wenham, you affect me," the great man laughed. "By ——— you do. I like seeing a fellow preserve the illusions of youth... Who's that gal in the second row with blue ribbons? Fine girl. Yes, you and I are sentimentalists. Wagg, I suspect you care less—it's the stomach rather than the heart with you, isn't it?"
"I like everything that's good," Mr. Wagg agreed generously. "Beauty and Burgundy, Venus and Venison. Though I admit Venus's turtles aren't cooked at the London Tavern..." He trailed off, sensing his patron's disinterest. Lord Steyne adjusted his glasses, examining the stage.
"Yes, I've heard that joke about Venus's turtle before—you're beginning to fail, my poor Wagg. If you don't mind, I shall require a new Jester." He turned back to Wenham. "Go on, about old Pendennis."

Mr. Wenham read the letter aloud: *"As you have had my character in your hands for the last three weeks... I think you can afford to be good-humoured... It is a delicate matter, entre nous, une affaire de cœur. There is a young friend of mine who is gone wild about a certain Miss Fotheringay, an actress at the theatre here... She does Ophelia, Lady Teazle, Mrs. Haller—that sort of thing. Upon my word, she is as splendid as Georges in her best days... I want a London engagement for her. Can

Major Pendennis had made up his mind to keep his garrison stationed at Fairoaks until the enemy had completely withdrawn. While he didn't explicitly micromanage Pen's behavior or impose strict rules, the lad remained under the constant surveillance of his uncle and his network of spies. Young Arthur's every move was meticulously tracked by his vigilant guardian.

Let us pause for a moment to consider the universal plight of romance. Scarcely anyone who reads novels—or lives in reality—hasn't been thwarted in love by fate, circumstance, deceitful women, or their own foolish choices. Recall your own heartbreaks and apply them to Mr. Pen's agony. Ah, the weary nights! The sickening fevers! Those mad desires crashing against rocks of indifference, only to be bounced back by unyielding granite! Imagine a list compiled tonight in London of the groans, thoughts, and curses of tossing lovers. What a catalog it would be! I wonder what percentage of the male population of the metropolis lies awake at 2:00 or 3:00 AM tomorrow, counting the hours, rolling restlessly from side to side, yearning and heart-sick. What a pang it is! I've never known a man literally die of love, but I have seen a twelve-stone man shrink to nine-and-a-half stone under the weight of a disappointed passion. Nearly a quarter of him perishes! Though he eventually regains his size, perhaps growing larger than before, thanks to a new affection wrapping around his heart and ribs like a comforting corset. Young Pen will surely console himself like the rest of us. We say this merely to prevent the ladies from prematurely lamenting him or worrying unduly about his "complaint." His mother worried, of course, but what won't maternal fondness fear or invent? "Depend on it, my dear creature," Major Pendennis would say gallantly to her, "the boy will recover. Once we get her out of the country, we'll take him somewhere lively. For now, don't fret. Half a fellow's pain over losing a woman stems from vanity rather than affection. Being left by a woman is terrible, to be sure; but look how easily *we* leave them."

Mrs. Pendennis, however, remained ignorant of the depth of her son's torment. Such complex emotions were beyond her simple understanding. Indeed, she disliked discussing the subject; her own heart had suffered a private misadventure which she had bravely endured and cured, leaving her with little patience for others' passions—except, naturally, Arthur's. She felt his sufferings as her own, experiencing many of his pains far more intensely than Pen did herself. She watched him through this grief with a jealous, silent sympathy, even though he refused to confide in her.

One must grant the Major considerable merit and forbearance, displaying a truly admirable degree of family affection. Life at Fairoaks was excruciatingly dull for a man accustomed to entering half the houses in London and making bows in three or four drawing rooms nightly. Occasional dinners with Doctor Portman or a neighboring squire, followed by a dreary game of backgammon with the Widow—who tried desperately to entertain him—were his chief pleasures. He longed for the mailbag bearing letters and devoured every word of the evening paper. He diligently prescribed himself a regimen of quiet living, believing it suited him better than London banquets. He dressed laboriously twice daily, taking regular exercise along the terrace walk. Armed with his cane, his meticulous toiletries, his medicine chest, his backgammon box, and his newspaper, this worldly philosopher constructed a fortress against boredom. If he couldn't improve each shining hour like bees buzzing around the widow's garden wall, Major Pendennis simply dragged one hour after another past as best he could, rendering his captivity merely tolerable. Later, it was noted that he grew fond of steering conversations toward the American War, the Massacre of Wyoming, and the Battle of Saint Lucia, largely because he possessed two volumes of the 'Annual Register' in his bedroom which he studiously reviewed. Thus, a well-regulated man accommodates himself to circumstances, showing himself calmly superior to fortune.

Pen occasionally joined the late-night backgammon games or listened to his mother's simple summer music, yet he remained restless and wretched despite everything. He was known to rise before dawn, descending to a carp-pond in Clavering Park—a dreary pool filled with whispering rushes and green alders, where a milkmaid had drowned in the Baronet's grandfather's time, and her ghost was rumored to still wander. Pen did not attempt suicide, as his mother feared. Instead, he loved to fish there, thinking and pondering as the float quivered in the eddies and fish flapped nearby. If he caught a bite, he was thrilled, occasionally returning with carps, tenches, and eels which the Major cooked à la Française.

Beneath his favorite tree by this pond, Pen composed numerous poems suited to his melancholy circumstances, verses he later blushed to remember, wondering how he could ever have invented such rubbish. As for the tree itself, it holds a hollow where he once stored his tin-box of ground-bait and fishing tackle—a spot significant for reasons we shall advance to later. Suffice it to say, he wrote poetry and found relief therein. When a man's grief reaches this stage, it may be loud, but it loses its severity. When a gentleman struggles to find rhymes for sorrow beyond "borrow" and "tomorrow," his woes are nearing their end sooner than he realizes. So it was with Pen. He experienced hot and cold fits, days of sullenness and peevishness, periods of blank resignation, and occasional wild paroxysms of rage and longing. During these episodes, Rebecca would be saddled and ridden fiercely through the countryside or into Chatteris, her rider gesticulating wildly atop her, startling carters and toll-gate keepers as he shouted the name of the false one.

Mr. Foker became a frequent and delightful visitor to Fairoaks during this time. His good spirits and eccentricities amused the Major and Pen, while astonishing the Widow and little Laura. His tandem carriage caused quite a sensation in Clavering marketplace, where he overturned a stall, sliced through Mrs. Pybus's poodle over its shaved quarters, and consumed a glass of raspberry bitters at the Clavering Arms. Everyone in the little town heard of him and eagerly checked his name in their outdated peerage books. Being so young, and their books so archaic, his name appeared in few volumes; his mother, now an elderly lady, was listed among the progeny of the Earl of Rosherville as Lady Agnes Milton. Yet, his name, wealth, and honorable lineage quickly became common knowledge in Clavering, where, naturally, poor Pen's little affair with the Chatteris actress was also widely discussed.

Viewing the quaint little town of Clavering St. Mary from the London road, passing the lodge at Fairoaks, one sees the swift, shimmering River Brawl winding down from the town and skirting the woods of Clavering Park. The ancient church tower and peaked roofs of the houses rise amidst trees and old walls, backed by a fair panorama of sun-drenched hills stretching westward toward the sea. The place appears so cheerful and comfortable that many a traveler's heart yearns toward it from the coach top, imagining it as a calm, friendly nook to shelter in at life's end. Tom Smith, who drove the Alacrity coach, often pointed to a tree near the river commanding a fine view of the church and town, telling his passenger on the box, "Artises come and take hoff the Church from that there tree—It was a Habby once, sir:" Indeed, it is a lovely view, recommended for Mr. Stanfield or Mr. Roberts' next tour.

Yet, like Constantinople seen from the Bosphorus, or Mrs. Rougemont viewed from across the room, or many objects we pursue and admire before attaining them, Clavering is prettier at a distance than upon close inspection. The town, so cheerful a few furlongs off, looks remarkably blank and dreary up close. Except on market days, the streets are empty. The clacking of pattens echoes through half the place, and you can hear the creaking of the rusty old ensign at the Clavering Arms without hearing any other sound. No balls have been held in the Assembly Rooms since the Clavering Volunteers hosted one for their Colonel, the old Sir Francis Clavering. The stables, once housing a brilliant but defunct regiment, are now cheerless and empty, save for Thursdays when farmers park their tilted carts and gigs, creating a feeble display of liveliness, or during Petty Sessions when magistrates gather in what used to be the old card-room.

On the south side of the market rises the church, with its great grey towers. Sunlight illuminates the delicate carving, deepening the shadows of the massive buttresses and gilding the glittering windows and flaming vanes. Centuries ago, the image of the Church's Patroness was torn from the porch; such statues of saints as were within reach of stones and hammers during that era of pious demolition are now maimed and headless. Of those spared from destruction, only Doctor Portman knows their names and histories, for his curate, Smirke, lacks antiquarian interest, and Mr. Simcoe (husband of the Honorable Mrs. Simcoe), incumbent and architect of the Chapel of Ease in the lower town, considers them the abomination of desolation.

The Rectory is a stout, broad-shouldered brick

News of Pen's latest escapade spread faster than wildfire, sparking fury in Doctor Portman while merely tickling the funny bone of Major Pendennis. For Mrs. Pendennis, however, the revelation was catastrophic. Upon hearing of the scuffle and her son's "unchristian" behavior, she teetered on the brink of distraction. In her mind, this single transaction spawned a universe of misery, discomfort, and potential crime. She desperately wished to see Pen shipped out of Chatteris immediately, anywhere but near the woman who, in her view, had dragged him into this maelstrom.

When the fond mother remonstrated and the stern Doctor rebuked him for his violence, Pen treated the situation with the deadly seriousness typical of youth. With a gravity that belied his age, he insisted he was deeply sorry, claiming the insult came entirely unprovoked. He vowed that no man could insult him regarding his honor without facing a dignified appeal to his uncle. He asked rhetorically whether any gentleman could have reacted differently to the outrage he suffered, or offered less satisfying restitution to the chastised party.

"You are moving too fast, my good sir," Uncle Pendennis replied, looking slightly baffled. Having recently indoctrinated his nephew with his own archaic views on honor—views steeped in the smell of campfire smoke and pistol powder rather than modern sobriety—he added, "Between gentlemen of the world, perhaps. But between two schoolboys, this sort of nonsense is perfectly ridiculous, my dear boy."

"It is wicked, and utterly unlike my son," Mrs. Pendennis sniffled, tears welling in her eyes as she struggled with her son's stubbornness.

Pen kissed her cheek and delivered a line of dialogue dripping with pomposity: "Women, dear mother, simply do not understand these matters. I placed myself entirely in Foker's hands; I had no other honorable course to pursue."

Major Pendennis grinned and shrugged. To him, the boys were making excellent progress. Meanwhile, Mrs. Pendennis declared Foker a wicked, horrid little wretch, convinced he would drag her beloved boy into further mischief if they attended the same college. "I have a great mind not to let him go at all," she lamented. Only the memory that her husband had always destined him for that specific university prevented her from issuing a total veto.

His attendance was already settled by all concerned. Foker had promised to introduce him to the elite circles, and the Major placed immense faith in this arrangement. "Mr. Foker knows the finest young men currently at the University," the Major boasted. "Pen will form connections that will benefit him for the rest of his life. There is the Young Marquis of Plinlimmon, eldest son of the Duke of Saint David's; Lord Magnus Charters, son of Lord Runnymede and a first cousin of Mr. Foker (you recall Lady Runnymede was formerly Lady Agatha Milton); and Lady Agnes will surely invite him to Logwood. Far from fearing his association with her son—who is singular, humorous, yet prudent and amiable, and for whom we owe a debt of gratitude regarding the Fotheringay marriage—I consider it one of the luckiest breaks Pen could have had."

Helen sighed, assuming the Major knew best. She acknowledged Foker's kindness in the recent Miss Costigan affair and felt grateful, yet a dim, unsettling presentiment of evil gnawed at her. The constant quarrels, riots, and displays of worldliness terrified her for her boy's future.

Doctor Portman, conversely, was adamant that Pen attend college. He hoped the lad would apply himself to studies while enjoying a moderate dose of high society. Confident that Pen would distinguish himself based on Smirke's glowing reports and his own observation of the boy's impressive Latin constructions, the Doctor agreed that leaving Chatteris was essential. Even Pen, usually distracted by his private griefs and local rowdiness, gloomily agreed to obey.

August and September brought assizes, races, and a flood of visitors to Chatteris. Miss Fotheringay continued her performances, bidding farewell to audiences until the final curtain fell. Strangely, aside from the few we've named, no one seemed particularly moved by her presence or impending departure. Even the polite county folk, who likely adored the Fotheringay star in London thanks to fashion trends, found nothing remarkable about her acting on the tiny Chatteris stage. History is littered with geniuses and quacks who met similar fates. Yet, Miss Fotheringay endured the public neglect and life's petty cruelties with her characteristic equanimity. She ate, drank, acted, and slept with a rhythmic comfort born of her temperament. How much grief and harmful excitement a healthy dullness avoids! I am not saying virtue isn't virtuous simply because it faces no temptation; rather, dullness is a magnificent gift often underrated, and some are blessed with Nature's finest anodyne.

During this season, Pen wandered dully between the theater and home, doing exactly as he pleased. His erratic behavior tormented his mother, who would have interfered frequently had not the Major constantly checked her impulses while simultaneously encouraging her. The wily man of the world sensed a favorable shift in Pen's condition. The violent outpouring of verse was a key symptom giving his guardian and physician cause for optimism. Pen could be heard declaiming poetry in the shrubberies or muttering rhymes between his teeth while sitting with the household in the evenings.

One day, while prowling the house in Pen's absence, the Major discovered a massive book filled with verses in Pen's study. Containing both English and Latin, complete with scholarly footnotes quoting classics, the book confirmed the Major's suspicions. *He can't be very bad,* reasoned the Pall-Mall Philosopher. Consequently, he pointed out to Mrs. Pendennis—with a hint of secret disappointment, for she loved romance like other soft-hearted women—that the young gentleman had returned home hungry for dinner nightly and displayed a hearty appetite at breakfast. "Gad, I wish I could," the Major muttered, thinking ruefully of his own medicinal pills. "The boy begins to sleep well; depend upon it." It was cruel, but undeniably true.

With no one else to confide in—unable to discuss his loves and disappointments with his mother—Uncle Pendennis treated the topic with a scornful, worldly tone. Though polite, this attitude grated heavily on Pen's sensitive feelings. Furthermore, Foker was far too coarse to appreciate such refined sentiments. Thus, Pen's friendship for the Curate deepened; he never grew tired of using Smirke as a listener. What is a lover without a confidant? Pen employed Mr. Smirke, much like Corydon uses an elm tree, to carve out his mistress's name. He made the Curate echo the name of the beautiful Amaryllis. Once men stop playing the tune, they rarely care for the pipe anymore, but Pen believed he possessed a profound friendship for Smirke precisely because he could pour his loves and sorrows into the tutor's ears. Smirke, in turn, had his own compelling reasons to always be available.

Pen's affection erupted in a flood of sonnets addressed to his "friend of the heart," which Smirke received with great sympathy. The Curate was bombarded with Latin Sapphics and Alcaics. The love songs multiplied under Pen's fluent pen, and Smirke declared them beautiful, believing wholeheartedly in their quality. Grateful that Heaven had sent him such a friend at such a pivotal moment, Pen showered his tutor with his best-bound books, a gold guard-chain, and even attempted to gift him a double-barreled gun. He ventured into Chatteris on credit to purchase a gold pencil-case—which he couldn't afford due to existing debts to Smirke from previous gifts—and presented it with an inscription declaring his unalterable, eternal devotion. Naturally, the Curate was delighted by every token of attachment.

However, the poor Curate was naturally dismayed by the prospect of his pupil's departure. When Arthur left, Smirke's occupation and joy would vanish. What pretext could he find for his daily visits to Fairoaks and the kind words or glances from the widow there, which were as vital to him as the frugal dinners served by Madame Fribsby? Without Arthur, he would be reduced to visiting like any ordinary acquaintance. Little Laura could not teach him the Catechism more than once a week. He had curled himself around Fairoaks like ivy; the thought of losing his grip on the place caused him to pine. Should he speak his mind and kneel before the widow? He weighed every indication in her behavior that might flatter his hopes. Three weeks prior, she had praised his sermons; she had thanked him profusely for a melon gift and for hosting a small dinner party; she had expressed enduring gratitude for his kindness to Arthur. When he declared his boundless love for the boy, she had responded romantically, indicating her own strong gratitude and regard for all her son's friends. Should he speak out? Or wait? If he spoke and was rejected, it was terrifying to imagine the gates of Fairoaks closing forever—behind that door lay the entire world for Mr. Smirke.

And so, dear readers, we observe how every man possesses private griefs that weigh heavier upon him than anyone else's sorrow. While Mrs. Pendennis anxieties about losing her son and breaking her protective hold, while the Major's great soul chafes inwardly at missing the glittering parties of London and the glances of D

The afternoon sun was shining with uncommon brilliance, prompting Mrs. Portman to extend her usual stroll from the Rectory all the way to Fairoaks. After settling her affairs with Mrs. Pybus, she decided the weather was simply too fine to waste indoors, eager to visit her dear friend, Mrs. Pendennis. Upon arrival, a revelation nearly caused a scandal among the ladies. Mrs. Portman confessed a "very great secret" concerning the Reverend Mr. Smirke: he harbored an ancient, deeply hidden attachment.

"And on whom has Mr. Smirke bestowed his heart?" Mrs. Pendennis asked, feigning a superior air while internally panicking.

"Why, my dear," replied Mrs. Portman, "when he first arrived to dine at the Rectory, everyone whispered we wanted him for Myra, forcing us to withdraw our inquiries. Later, rumors suggested he was smitten elsewhere, but I always denied it, insisting it was you—"

"That I!" cried Mrs. Pendennis, indignant. "People are dreadfully impertinent. Mr. Smirke came merely as Arthur's tutor; surely no one dares speak thus..."

"Pon my soul, it is a trifle too much," interjected the Major, lowering his newspaper and double eyeglasses.

"I have no patience with Mrs. Pybus," Helen added, joining the chorus of indignation.

Mrs. Portman quickly clarified the situation. "I told her there was no truth to it. Now, however, it is confirmed: my demure gentleman has been secretly engaged to Miss Thompson of Clapham Common for years. I am delighted, especially for Myra's sake, as an unmarried curate can be quite awkward in a household. Naturally, this remains strictly private, but I felt duty-bound to inform you to prevent future misunderstandings. Please, keep this between ourselves."

Mrs. Pendennis expressed sincere relief and wished Mr. Smirke a happy marriage with a deserving wife. Once the visitor departed, Helen and her brother discussed the news with great satisfaction. The kindly lady immediately chastised herself for her recent haughty behavior toward the Curate, realizing she should have been grateful for his constant devotion to Arthur.

"Gratitude to such people is commendable," the Major remarked dryly, "but familiarity is out of the question. This gentleman teaches lessons and collects fees like any other master. Distinctions in rank must be maintained. I warned you previously that you were too kind to Mr. Smirke."

Helen, however, disagreed. With Arthur preparing to depart, memories flooded her mind of Mr. Smirke's politeness: running errands, copying music, teaching Laura, and offering thoughtful gifts. Her conscience pricked her sharply regarding her past ingratitude. Consequently, when the Curate emerged from the study, looking eager to linger in the hall before leaving, Helen stepped forward. Blushing slightly, she invited him into her drawing-room—a space where he had rarely been admitted—and extended a warm invitation to dinner. Eager to accept such a delightful summons, Mr. Smirke agreed instantly.

Feeling relieved of her earlier suspicions, Helen treated Mr. Smirke with exceptional grace during the meal, perhaps doubling her attentiveness because the Major remained aloof and reserved toward his nephew's tutor. When Pendennis attempted to toast the Curate, he addressed him with the condescension of a sovereign speaking to a lowly retainer, a display so absurd that even Pen chuckled, despite his own youthful vanity.

Mr. Smirke, however, cared little for the Major's impertinence so long as his hostess remained kind. He spent a delightful evening beside her, deploying all his conversational skills to charm her. He spoke fluently of the Fancy Bazaar, the Great Missionary Meeting, the latest novels, and the Bishop's sermon on fashionable London society—all gleaned from the newspapers. In short, he employed every tactic available to a college divine possessing sprightly wit, serious talent, impeccable manners, and a susceptible heart, all aimed at winning the affection of his chosen object.

Shortly after Helen and little Laura retired, the Major yawned out of the dining room. "What an insufferable bore that man is, and how he talked!" he grumbled.

"He has been very good to Arthur, who adores him," Mrs. Pendennis noted. "I wonder who Miss Thompson is?"

"I always suspected the fellow was looking elsewhere," the Major muttered.

"And where?" asked Mrs. Pendennis, innocent as a child. "Toward Myra Portman?"

"Toward Helen Pendennis, if you must know," her brother-in-law replied bluntly.

"Himself? Impossible!" Helen exclaimed, knowing full well the truth. "His marriage will undoubtedly be happy. I only hope Arthur does not drink too much."

Indeed, Arthur, buoyed by the privilege of managing the cellar and anticipating shared meals with his favorite companion, had procured a generous supply of claret. As the elders and Laura departed, Pen and the Curate began to pour the wine with reckless abandon. One bottle vanished completely; another lost half its contents within thirty minutes. Pen, laughing with a hollow voice, toasted the falsehood of women, declaring that wine was a mistress who never deceived and always welcomed her lover.

Smirke countered gently that he knew women of truth and tenderness. Casting his eyes upward and sighing as if invoking a beloved, unmentionable presence, he drained his glass, his face flushing pink. Pen then recited verses he had composed that morning, lamenting the woman who had slighted his passion. He declared himself waking from love's fever, ready to leave a heartless deceiver, yet hoping to prove his worthiness to his race. Though death seemed preferable to life except for the sake of one kind being, he vowed that the false one would eventually learn the value of the treasure she had discarded.

Sentimental and emotional, Pen rolled out these lines in a rich, trembling voice, his honest gray eyes betraying a genuine sensitivity that would have melted anyone, even Miss Costigan. Smirke, equally moved, seized Pen's hand over the dessert dishes. "These verses are beautiful," he cried. "You are a poet, a great poet, destined for greatness by Heaven's permission. Go on and prosper, dear Arthur. Your current wounds are temporary; this grief will only cleanse and strengthen your heart. I have always prophesied your success once you correct certain character flaws. But you will overcome them. When you become famous, as I know you will, will you remember your old tutor and these happy early days?"

"I shall never forget," Pen swore, shaking hands across the glasses and apricots. "I don't know what I'd do without you. You are my best friend."

"Am I, really, Arthur?" Smirke asked, peering through his spectacles, his heart pounding audibly against his ribs.

"My best friend, my friend forever," Pen affirmed, draining the final glass of the renowned vintage imported by Lord Levant. "God bless you, old boy."

"We'll have another bottle!" Pen shouted, singing the drinking song from *Der Freischütz*. The dining room windows were open, and his mother paced softly on the lawn below, hearing the sweet, fresh notes of her son's voice. It cheered her heart immensely.

"You're taking too much wine, Arthur," Smirke warned softly.

"No," Pen insisted. "Women give headaches, but this doesn't. Fill your glass, old fellow. Let's drink—to her. Your 'her,' not mine, for whom I swear I'll care no more—not a penny, not a fig, not a glass of wine. Tell us about the lady, Smirke. I've seen you sighing for her."

"Oh!" Smirke gasped, his white shirtfront and glistening studs heaving with emotion.

"Oh, what a sigh!" Pen cried, becoming hilariously boisterous. "Fill, my boy, and drink the toast. No gentleman refuses a toast. Here's her health, good luck to you, and may she soon be Mrs. Smirke."

"Do you say so?" Smirke trembled. "Do you really say so, Arthur?"

"Of course! Down with it. Here's Mrs. Smirke's good health: Hip, hip, hurrah!"

Smirke convulsively gulped down his wine while Pen waved his glass overhead, cheering loudly enough to make his mother and Laura wonder on the lawn, and his uncle, dozing in the drawing room, start awake muttering, "That boy is drinking too much." Smirke set his glass down.

"I accept the omen," gasped the blushing Curate. "Oh, my dear Arthur, you—you know her..."

"What? Myra Portman? I wish you joy; she has a devilish large waist. But I wish you joy, old fellow."

"Oh, Arthur!" groaned the Curate, nodding speechlessly.

"Beg your pardon—sorry I offended you—but she has a large waist, you know—devilish large," Pen continued, the third bottle clearly affecting him.

"It's not Miss Portman," the other said in a voice of agony.

"Is it someone from Chatteris or Clapham? Someone here? Not old Pybus? Not Miss Rolt at the factory? She's only fourteen."

"It's somebody rather older than I am, Pen," the Curate cried, looking up guiltily before casting his eyes down into his plate.

Pen burst out laughing. "Madame Fribs

Every man, no matter how short or forgettable his time at university may have been, retains a special, tender spot in his heart for his old academic chums and those golden days. Life is just kicking off for the young man; the baby ropes are finally cut, and he's tasting the dizzying, brand-new flavors of total freedom. He hasn't met "tomorrow" yet, nor has he encountered worry, sickness, mischief, pennilessness, or crushing disappointments. The show hasn't been played enough times to get boring. Sure, the hangover you get later on tastes stale and bitter when you're stuck doing it over and over, but oh, how clear and sparkling that first sip of fun really was! How the lad lunges for the cup and gulps it down with wild, desperate eagerness!

Even old foodies who've been kicked out of the kitchen and are surviving on boiled eggs and tap water love watching people with big appetites. Besides, if seeing yourself in a comedy isn't possible, seeing your kids enjoy theirs is the next best thing. So, I'm hoping there isn't a single age or amount of wisdom a human can reach without still feeling a twinge of joy when looking at happy youth.

A few weeks ago, I returned from a quick trip to Oxford and Cambridge, where my friend Arthur Pendennis spent some time. I traveled by train alongside a young guy currently studying at Saint Boniface. He'd managed to sneak out early ("get an exeat") and was determined to spend a day chasing thrills in London. From the moment we boarded until we dropped off (which was way too soon for me—I never tire of listening to an honest kid's jokes and bright laughter), he wouldn't stop rattling on. At the station, nothing satisfied him but a hansom cab so he could rush into town and dive straight into whatever fun awaited him. Off he went, spinning away with joy lighting up his honest face. As for me, carrying only a small carpet-bag, I climbed onto the outside of the omnibus and sat quite happily sandwiched between a Jewish peddler puffing terrible cigars and a gentleman's servant guarding a poodle. Once we gathered our full load of passengers and boxes, the driver took it slow.
