We weren't in a rush to hit the smoky Babylon of the city, nor did we plan on dining at the Club or dancing at the Casino that night. Yet, in a few years, my young railway buddy won't be any less eager.

There were no trains when Arthur Pendennis attended the famous University of Oxbridge. Instead, he rode in a well-furnished coach, packed tight with dons, gown-wearing students, fresh freshmen ready to arrive, and their guardians escorting them. Inside sat a portly old gentleman in grey stockings from the City, traveling with his pale-faced son. They were terrified when they learned the coach had been driven for several miles by young Mr. Foker of Saint Boniface. This fellow was friends with everyone, including drivers, and could handle a whip as well as the legendary Tom Hicks. Meanwhile, Pen sat on the roof, inspecting the coach, the crowd, and the countryside with wide-eyed delight. His heart skipped a beat as the great university appeared, revealing a magnificent view of ancient towers, spires, towering elms, and the gleaming river.

Before leaving, Pen had stayed a few days with his uncle at the Major's lodgings in Bury Street. The Major felt the lad needed new clothes, and Arthur couldn't resist any plan involving fresh coats and waistcoats. His self-sacrificing uncle spared no expense for the youth. London felt terribly empty. The Pall Mall pavement was deserted; even the red-coated guards had left town. Scarcely a face could be seen in the club windows. The Major escorted his nephew into a couple of these ghostly mansions and signed his name on the candidate list of one. Arthur's delight at this gesture from his guardian was overwhelming. He read his name and title in the parchment ledger—"Arthur Pendennis, Esquire, of Fairoaks Lodge"—with a thrill of intense satisfaction.

"You'll be eligible for election in about three years, by which time you'll have your degree," the guardian noted. Pen wished those three years would vanish instantly, staring at the stucco halls, vast libraries, and drawing rooms as if they were already his private property. The Major chuckled softly at the pompous air the simple young man adopted as he strutted out of the building.

Later, the Major and Foker drove down to the Grey Friars in the latter's cab to renew old friendships. Boys crowded around the cab as it waited by the gates, admiring the chestnut horse and the impressive uniform of Stoopid, the tiger. Just as they were strutting about the playground chatting with old cronies, the bell for afternoon school rang. The terrifying Doctor marched into the school holding his grammar book. Foker slunk away nervously at his approach, but Pen stepped forward, blushing, to shake the dignitary's hand. He laughed thinking of how that same Latin Grammar had boxed his ears countless times in the past. Generous, good-natured, and utterly conceited, Pen was completely satisfied with himself.

Next, they headed to the parental brewery. Foker's Entire was built on an enormous site near the Grey Friars, with the firm's name gilded on countless pub signs throughout the neighborhood. The venerable junior partner treated the young lord of the vats and his friend to honor, serving them silver flagons of brown stout so potent that it seemed the young men, and even the horse Mr. Harry Foker was driving, were affected. Stoopid galloped home at a breakneck pace, endangering pie stalls, colliding with crossing women, and slamming cab steps against street posts, causing the horse to swing wildly on his boards.

The Major was delighted to see Pen with his young acquaintance. He listened intently to Mr. Foker's straightforward stories, treated the two boys to a fine dinner at a Covent Garden coffee house, and took them to the theater. But he was happiest when Mr. and Lady Agnes Foker, visiting London, invited the Major and Arthur Pendennis to dinner at Grosvenor Street.

"Once you gain entry into Lady Agnes Foker's house," he told Pen with affectionate seriousness, fitting the gravity of the moment, "you must guard it. Never neglect calling on Grosvenor Street when you return to London. I advise you to study Debrett thoroughly regarding the alliances and genealogy of the Earls of Rosherville. If you can, make some small, neat, complimentary historical references to the family—something a poet like you can handle easily. Mr. Foker himself is a decent man, though not of high birth or great education. He insists on serving some of the family porter after dinner; you must never refuse it, and neither will I, though all beer disagrees with me terribly." True to his word, the heroic martyr sacrificed himself on the day of the dinner. When the worthy old gentleman made his traditional joke about Foker's Entire, the Major grinned broadly.

Lady Agnes, wrapped entirely around her son Harry, was the kindest of mothers and one of the most good-natured (if not wise) women. She welcomed her son's friend warmly and surprised Pen by detailing the rigorous studies her darling boy was undertaking, fearing they might harm his delicate health. Foker the Elder burst into loud laughter at some of these remarks, while the heir winked knowingly at his friend. Lady Agnes then recounted her son's history from infancy, listing his miraculous escapes from measles, hooping-cough, and drowning, and the brutal treatment he endured at the dreadful school Mr. Foker had chosen for him simply because he'd attended it himself. She vowed never to forgive that disagreeable doctor. After an hour of non-stop chatter about her son, she declared the two Messieurs Pendennis delightful company. When pheasants arrived with the second course—which the Major correctly identified as coming from Logwood—she hoped they would visit her there at Christmas or during Harry's vacation.

"God bless you, my dear boy," Pendennis whispered to Arthur as they lit candles in Bury Street to retire. "That little nod to Agincourt, where a Rosherville hero shone, was very neat, even if Lady Agnes didn't fully grasp it. Excellent for a beginner—but don't blush so. Remember this forever: with a good introduction ('entree'), securing good society is just as easy as avoiding bad, and keeping your footing in the best London houses costs no more effort than dining with a lawyer in Bedford Square. Keep this in mind while you study at Oxbridge, and be extremely careful about the friends you make. The first step in life is the most crucial. Did you write to your mother today? No? Well, do it before you go, and ask Mr. Foker for a frank stamp—they like them. Goodnight. God bless you."

Pen wrote a humorous account of his London adventures, the play, the visit to the Friars, the brewery, and the party at Mr. Foker's to his dearest mother, who was praying alone in the lonely house at Fairoaks, her heart overflowing with unspoken love for her boy. She and Laura read those letters, and the ones that followed, many times, brooding over them as women do. It was the first step in life for Pen. Ah! What a perilous journey it is, and how even the bravest can stumble and the strongest fail. Fellow traveler, may you find a kind arm to support yours and a friendly hand to help those who fall. May truth guide, mercy forgive at the end, and love

Our hero, Pen, breathed a sigh of relief when his mentor finally departed on the second day of their arrival at Oxford. One can safely assume the Major felt exactly the same way; having successfully discharged his duty, he was eager to see it behind him. Think of it: more than three months of prime time sacrificed by this "martyr" of a Major for his nephew! Was there ever a more extreme case of selflessness? How many men, or even majors, could claim such devotion? A man will gladly risk his neck or his life for honor, but try asking him to surrender his comfort or his deepest desires, and you'll find most shrink from the task. Few of us possess the stamina for such trials. So, worthy reader, if you happen to sport a beard, would you have done the same? I won't argue the case for women—they are accustomed to sacrifice; we train them well enough. But let's be honest: the tally of self-denial you've accumulated over a lifetime likely doesn't fill the ledger much. Enough said on such grim topics; you're too polite to dwell on the vulgar. Still, I must declare my admiration for the Major's conduct over the last quarter. He has earned every bit of his well-deserved holiday.

Foker and Pen saw him off in the carriage, with the younger gentleman issuing specific instructions to the driver to ensure the safety of the senior Pendennis inside. The elder Pendennis was delighted to have his nephew accompanied by a young man who would introduce him to the university's finest circles. While the Major bolted back to London and then to Cheltenham, descending upon local estates where the families hadn't fled abroad, seeking good shooting and society, the stage was set for Pen's academic odyssey.

We have already dedicated a quarter of the serial's usual space to this single episode in Pen's life. It is obvious that recounting every adventure in such detail is impossible unless a future chronicler picks up the pen after his passing to carry the tale forward. We aren't going to dissect his entire university career with this level of granularity. Alas, the lives of such boys don't lend themselves to total narration. I wish they did! Ask yourself: does your life bear telling? If your honor remains intact, your conscience is likely at peace. Women are often portrayed as pure and unselfish, while men... well, not so much. I wouldn't dare claim Arthur Pendennis was worse than his peers, only that his peers, unfortunately, are mostly flawed. Let's have the courage to admit it. Can you name ten truly spotless men among your acquaintances? My circle is vast, yet I struggle to find ten saints.

During Pen's first term, he attended classical and mathematical lectures with reasonable diligence. However, realizing quickly that he lacked the aptitude or passion for exact sciences, and annoyed by a few boorish youths who couldn't even manage to strap their trousers properly—let alone hide their abominably thick, coarse shoes and stockings—he abandoned math entirely. He informed his doting parent that he intended to focus exclusively on Greek and Roman literature.

Mrs. Pendennis was thrilled by this decision, seeing it aligned with her son's inclinations. Her only concern was his health; she had heard harrowing tales of students succumbing to brain fevers due to overwork. She insisted that his delicate constitution mattered more than any vain honors. Pen, unaware of any lurking illness, promised not to stay up late reading. Surprisingly, he kept this promise with a resolve he rarely displayed in other areas, occasionally slipping up when his guard was down.

Soon, however, Pen discovered that the classical lectures offered little value. His fellow students were dull, and in mathematics, they were far too advanced for him. Mr. Buck, the tutor, proved no better scholar than a typical fifth-former at Grey Friars. He might possess some dry knowledge of Aeschylus or Aristophanes' meter, but he understood poetry no better than Mrs. Binge, his bed-maker. Pen grew weary of watching dull minds stumble through passages he could recite in a fraction of the time they spent. He concluded that private study was the only truly profitable pursuit and announced to his mother that he would read far more independently and attend fewer public lectures. His mother, knowing nothing of Homer herself, accepted his plan with confidence, trusting her boy would secure the position he deserved.

Pen didn't return home until after Christmas, disappointing his mother and Laura, who had been eagerly planning a magnificent snow fortress—a tradition he had enjoyed three winters prior. Instead, he was whisked away to Logwood, Lady Agnes Foker's estate, for private theatricals and a lavish Christmas party filled with high society figures the Major would never want his nephew to ignore. Nevertheless, he stayed home for the final three weeks of the break. Laura noted the impressive array of new clothes he brought, while his mother admired his refined appearance and matured demeanor.

He skipped Easter entirely. Upon returning for the long vacation, however, he arrived decked in even smarter attire: morning outfits featuring extraordinary shooting jackets with remarkable buttons, and evenings dominated by gorgeous velvet waistcoats, richly embroidered cravats, and exquisite linens. Peering into his room, his mother gasped at a stunning dressing case with silver mounts, overflowing with lovely rings and jewelry. Most notably, he had acquired a new French watch and gold chain, replacing the bulky old chronometer inherited from John Pendennis—the very instrument by which the late doctor had monitored countless patients' pulses. Just months ago, Pen had coveted this watch, declaring it the most splendid timepiece in existence. Before leaving for college, Helen had retrieved it from her trinket box (unwound since her husband's death) and gifted it to him, delivering a solemn speech on her father's virtues and the sanctity of time. Now, Pen deemed the heirloom outdated, comparing it disrespectfully to a warming pan. Laura thought this rude, but Pen simply shoved the watch into a drawer alongside soiled primrose gloves, discarded cravats, and another school watch previously mentioned in the family saga.

His old mare, Rebecca, also received the axe. Pen declared her insufficient for his needs, swapping her for a more powerful steed at a hefty price tag funded by Mr. Pendennis. Laura wept as Rebecca was led away.

Furthermore, Pen introduced a large crate of cigars bearing exotic names like *Colorados*, *Afrancesados*, *Telescopios*, and *Fudson Oxford Street*. He smoked them everywhere—from the stables and greenhouses (beneficial for Helen's plants) to his own study. Initially disapproving, his mother soon relented. Pen claimed he was composing a prize poem and required the cigar to concentrate, quoting the late Lord Byron's endorsement of smoking. One day, finding him engrossed in a novel (which he studied to cultivate an appreciation for both domestic and foreign light literature), Helen entered quietly. Rather than disturb him, she fetched a lighter and his case from the adjacent bedroom, lit the match, and placed the cigar in his mouth. Pen laughed, kissing her hand as it rested fondly on the sofa back. "Dear old mother," he quipped, "if I told you to burn the house down, I think you'd do it." Given her devotion, he was likely right; she might have burned the house down for him.

Beyond English light literature, Pen imported volumes of French fiction. When Helen dipped into them, she encountered content that left her wide-eyed with surprise. Pen explained that while he wasn't the author, maintaining fluency in French required engaging with contemporary masters, including the eminent Paul de Kock, just as seriously as Swift or Molière. Mrs. Pendennis sighed in perplexity but acquiesced. Miss Laura, however, was strictly barred from these books by both her anxious mother and the rigid moralist, Arthur Pendennis himself. Despite his broad curriculum requirements, he drew a hard line against prescribing such reading material to a young lady whose societal role differed vastly from his own.

Throughout this long vacation, Pen consumed the entire bin of claret his father had stored—a reserve the son had once praised for lacking headaches. Once depleted, he ordered a fresh supply from "wine merchants" Messrs. Binney and Latham of Mark Lane, following the recommendation of Old Doctor Portman. The honest rector had advised him to host wine parties for his young friends at Boniface, suggesting reputable London suppliers over unreliable Oxbridge traders whose liquor was often poisonous and overpriced. Obeying this counsel, Pen patronized Binney and Latham.

When he requested a shipment to be sent to the cellars at Fairoaks, he cleverly instructed the merchants to include his university wine accounts with the delivery bill. The poor widow was terrified by the total. Pen laughed off her frugality, insisting the bill was moderate and noting that everyone nowadays drank claret and champagne. Reluctantly, the widow paid, sensing her household expenses were swelling beyond her narrow income, though she knew these costs were merely occasional. During his short visits, they pinched when he was gone, but reasoned that while apart, they should maximize happiness together.

Arthur's allowances were remarkably generous, surpassing those of sons from wealthier families. Years prior, the thrifty and loving John Pendennis, determined to provide his son with the university education denied to his own father by previous extravagance, had established an "Education Fund." Year after year, executors found entries labeled "A.E.F." in his books. Even after his death, the widow contributed additional sums, ensuring that by the time Arthur arrived at Oxford, the fund amounted to a significant sum. The Major's philosophy was clear: let the boy be generously allowanced. Make his debut into

Just prior to Mr. Foker's departure from Oxford, a certain gentleman arrived at Boniface College. By turns of fate, this visitor had previously attended Cambridge, leaving abruptly due to a falling-out with the tutors and authorities there. His name was Horace Bloundell, a scion of the ancient Suffolk lineage known as the Bloundell-Bloundells of Bloundell-Bloundell Hall (a title the local undergraduates affectionately shortened). It was likely his pedigree, combined with the fact that Dr. Donne, the Master of Boniface, hailed from Suffolk and was possibly related to the family, that secured Bloundell's admission. After St. George's and a couple of other institutions rejected him, Boniface opened its doors.

A church living awaited the family, a position crucial for Mr. Bloundell to secure. While serving in a dragoon regiment, his third brother—who was meant to inherit the benefice—fell ill and died. Seizing the moment, Mr. Bloundell resolved to trade his crimson breeches and black shako for the sober black coat and white cravat of the English clergyman. Though setbacks at Cambridge briefly disrupted his plans, the determined former soldier remained undaunted, pivoting to win his victory elsewhere.

During Pen's second year, Major Pendennis visited his nephew, introducing him to a motley crew of university friends: the gentle and polished Lord Plinlimmon; the gallant, open-hearted Magnus Charters; the sly and witty Harland; the intrepid Ringwood, known as "Rupert" in the Union Debating Club for the courage displayed in his blunders; Broadbent, dubbed "Barebones" for his republican leanings and dissenter roots; and finally, Mr. Bloundell-Bloundell, who quickly claimed his spot among the elite.

Though the Major struggled to parse Harland's Greek citations or fully grasp Broadbent's heavy footwear and grimy hands, he was charmed by the gathering. He delighted in recounting stories to the lads. While London audiences grew weary of anecdotes, the Oxford students listened with honest sympathy, eager delight, raucous laughter, or deep respect—a reaction far superior to the metropolitan indifference. Occasionally, however, Mr. Bloundell's face twisted with scorn, revealing he already knew the tales. On one occasion, he boldly challenged the accuracy of a detail, offering his own version based on hearsay from the Club. The students watched in awe as their peer dared to interrupt the Major. Few understood the melancholy grace with which Major Pendennis simply accepted the correction, thanking Bloundell for fixing his error. Later, Bloundell openly disparaged "Old Pen," labeling him a glutton at Gaunt House, a notorious bore, and a complete fogey.

The Major reciprocated this disdain instantly. Sympathy between people is often mutual; if someone abuses me, I instinctively dislike them. Our preferences for others are as arbitrary as our likes and dislikes for flowers, wines, or books. We cannot explain why, but generally, if you hate Dr. Fell, he hates you.

"My boy," the Major declared over breakfast the morning after the party, "your dinner was *à merveille*. You carved splendidly—an art still vital in good households, aiding one in middle age. Young Lord Plinlimmon is amiable, a mirror of his mother, Lady Aquila Brownbill. Lord Magnus's republicanism suits a young patrician, though it grows tiresome later. Broadbent shows promise with his eloquence. Your friend Foker is delightful. But your acquaintance, Mr. Bloundell... strikes me as wholly ineligible."

"Bless my soul, sir!" Pen laughed. "Why, he's the most popular man in the university! We elected him President of the Barmecides the very first week. He's from an excellent family—the Suffolk Bloundells, descended from Richard the Lionheart's Blondel, bearing a harp in chief and the motto 'O Mong Roy'."

"A man may have a magnificent coat of arms and still be a tiger," the Major retorted, cracking his egg. "That man is a tiger. I'll wager he left his regiment—which was respectable, mind you—in bad odor. Look at him; there's the unmistakable scent of slang and bad habits. He frequents low gambling dens and billiard halls, haunts third-rate clubs. Notice the quantity of rings and jewelry? 'Scamp' is written across his face. Avoid him. Let us change the subject. Your dinner was slightly too lavish, but I don't mind you treating friends occasionally, provided you stay within your income. The cutlets were excellent, the soufflé light, and while the third bottle of champagne was unnecessary, you have a good allowance."

Poor Pen! The Major had no idea how often these extravagant dinners actually occurred. The reckless young host reveled in displaying his hospitality and culinary flair. Boys are obsessed with appearing knowledgeable about food and wine, viewing such expertise as the hallmark of a sophisticated rogue. They wink knowingly at glasses of claret and debate sauces, unaware that true wisdom comes only with age, realizing that a simple leg of mutton is often preferable to the feasts of popes. Little Pen, aspiring to be the "Admirable Crichton," felt compelled to master dining arts. Soon, however, his staircase would no longer be clogged with servants and ice-chilled champagne; instead, it would be besieged by creditors with sulky faces demanding payment.

Yet, the Major's advice fell on deaf ears. What young men crave is a perceived knowledge of life. A fellow who claims worldly experience, a "roué" with tales of adventure, commands admiration. It is human nature to respect such prowess, even if it is fabricated. How many schoolboys admit to loving *Don Juan*? It is a dangerous propagation of falsehood. One such corrupt individual can poison an entire college. The tutors at Boniface soon noticed a decline in moral tone and gentility following Bloundell's arrival.

While the magnates of neighboring St. George's College, including Pen, resisted Bloundell's flashy charms, others were wary. Broadbent labeled him "Captain Macheath," predicting a hanging. Foker, exercising extreme caution, avoided direct criticism but subtly warned Pen: "Better play whist with him than against him; better back him at Ecarte than bet against him. He plays remarkably well, and doesn't seem flush of cash either." Beyond these veiled hints, the prudent Foker said nothing.

But Pen, driven by an insatiable hunger for pleasure, ignored warnings. He equated enjoyment with "seeing life," quoting Terence, Horace, and Shakespeare to justify his actions. At this pace, he risked becoming a ruined rake in mere years.

One evening, after a supper party featuring a quiet game of *vingt-et-un*, the men prepared to depart. Bloundell, seizing a green wine glass meant for iced water, produced a pair of dice from his waistcoat pocket. With practiced ease, he tossed them onto the table, swept them up, and repeated the motion. Pen, usually content with humble backgammon, watched in fascination. Bloundell then sang the chorus from *Robert the Devil*, drawing Pen and others into the song. Before long, the group abandoned their caps and homes for the table, passing the green glass around until Pen, after throwing six consecutive wins, shattered it.

That night marked Pen's plunge into the deadly game of chance. Dice became a morning ritual as well as an afternoon pastime. Bloundell would arrive at Pen's rooms post-breakfast, and time would vanish as the dice rattled. They held secret sessions behind closed doors; Bloundell even crafted a felt-lined box to silence the telltale clatter. Once, they nearly caught Mr. Buck, a tutor, who heard "Two to one on the caster" through Pen's window. When Buck entered, he found them studying Homer, with Pen gravely discussing the navigability of the River Scamander.

Pen gained little wealth from these dealings, save for a smattering of odds and ends he could have read in a book. However, Captain Macheath possessed other talents. His stories of garrison conquests and exploits in the countryside held a magnetic, albeit destructive, allure. He spun tall tales of Parisian nights at the Palais Royal and the Café de Paris, boasting of waking to find twelve thousand francs under his pillow and a boot stuffed with Napoleons. Pen's eyes gleamed with envy, respecting the man who could command such riches. Macheath dismissed Pen's infatuation with Miss Fotheringay, mocking the idea of marrying her legitimately. Instead, he suggested Pen might as well wed a notorious actress, listing names of men who had squandered fortunes on her.

"It wasn't a shred of a character," Macheath sneered. Pen, naturally credulous and honest, swallowed these lies whole. He accepted Macheath's fabrications as historical fact, leading to profound humiliation. If he had been ashamed of his passion before, how could he feel now that the object of his adoration was revealed as a fraud? He never questioned the reliability of a man who spoke ill of all women.

During an Easter vacation, Pen decided to remain at Oxford, yet Bloundell dragged him to London anyway. They checked into a Covent Garden hotel where Bloundell held a credit line ("tick") and indulged in the city's pleasures. Bloundell, still a member of a military club, took Pen to dine there, driving them in a cab with trembling hearts lest they encounter Major Pendennis on patrol. Here, Pen mingled with dashing youths sporting spurs and

To anyone with even a passing familiarity with heraldry and the peerage, the crest of Helen Pendennis's noble lineage is well-known: a nest teeming with tiny pelicans busily pecking at the bleeding chest of a large, maternal bird, which generously replenishes the young with blood drawn from itself. While it is plausible that female pelicans enjoy such a sacrifice, it is undeniable that human women do. There exists a peculiar pleasure, unknown to men, that accompanies the agony of being scarified; indeed, some women would endure such suffering willingly. They derive joy in self-sacrifice for the object their instincts dictate they love. Whether it be a reckless husband, a dissipated son, or a darling rogue of a brother, their hearts are eager to pour out their finest treasures for the sake of the beloved. How readily we, on the other side, offer these soft creatures the luxury of forgiveness! We rarely mind how we live ourselves, yet when the prodigal returns, we rejoice, slaughter the fatted calf, and send Mercy and Forgiveness smiling out to greet the sinner at the first sign of return. May this sentiment hold true forever; otherwise, justice alone would leave us wanting.

During the latter stages of Pen's tenure at Oxford, his uncle's favoritism toward the lad grew considerably. The Major was proud of Arthur, admiring his high spirits, frank demeanor, good character, and aristocratic bearing. It delighted the old London bachelor to watch Pen stroll alongside the university's young patricians. Though stingy to a fault—a reputation that had become something of a club joke among his friends, who envied his constant engagements despite his poverty—he was charmed to treat his nephew and the young lords to cozy dinners at his lodgings, regaling them with excellent claret, sharp wit, and tales so refined they would lose their charm if retold. He paid court to their parents through the youths and honored himself by their company. He visited Oxford multiple times, amusing the students with his presence while they hosted him with parties, breakfasts, and fetes, partly to jest and partly to honor him. He regaled them with stories, adopting a youthful and jovial persona. At a grand debate at the Union, he cheered wildly, tapping his stick in rhythm with the crowd, astounded by the boy's eloquence and fire. He believed he had secured a young Pitt for a nephew.

His affection bordering on paternal, he wrote playful letters filled with town gossip and advice. He bragged at his clubs, introducing Arthur with delight, claiming that "Egad, the young fellows are putting the old ones to the wall!" He spoke of friends like Young Lord Plinlimmon and Young Lord Magnus Charters, predicting they would eclipse their fathers. He begged permission to bring Arthur to a grand fête at Gaunt House, watching with infinite satisfaction as he danced with the ladies of the aforementioned nobility. He scrambled to secure invitations for the lad to prestigious homes with the fervor of a mother seeking a match for her daughter, rather than an old half-pay officer in a wig. Everywhere he boasted of the boy's genius and oratorical prowess, writing repeatedly to Helen about the brilliant degree Arthur was set to achieve. Lord Runnymede would take him on an embassy, or the Duke would seat him in Parliament. Helen, naturally, was too eager to believe anything favorable regarding her son. Yet, all this pride and affection had been trampled by Pen's wicked extravagance and idleness.

One does not envy Pen's position as he reflected on his actions. He had slept while the tortoise won the race. He had sabotaged a potential brilliance at its inception. He had dipped ungenerously into a generous mother's purse, basely and recklessly spilling her small store. Oh, what a cowardly hand strikes and robs such a tender creature! If Pen felt the wrong he had done others, surely a vain young gentleman felt the shame he had brought upon himself even more acutely. There is no cruelty comparable to this remorse, nor groans more pitiful than those of wounded self-love. Like Joel Miller's friend, the Senior Wrangler, who bowed to the audience merely because he and the King entered the theater simultaneously—an act of fatuity far less agreeable to himself—poor Arthur Pendennis was convinced that all England would notice the absence of his name from the examination lists and discuss his misfortune. His wounded tutor, the creditors ("duns"), the skipper and bed-maker who attended to him, and the undergraduates he had once patronized or scorned—all seemed impossible to face.

Rushing to his rooms, he locked himself inside and penned a letter to his tutor, overflowing with thanks, regard, remorse, and despair. He requested his name be removed from the college books and hinted that death would soon end the woes of the disgraced Arthur Pendennis. Slipping out, he wandered mechanically through the unfrequented lanes behind the colleges, clearing the university grounds until he reached the banks of the Cam, now deserted compared to its usual throngs of cheering gownsmen during boat races. Wandering miles away from Oxford, he found himself spotted by acquaintances leaving the city.

Ascending a hill under a drizzling January rain, his ragged academic gown fluttering behind him, a post-chaise rattled up the road. A servant sat on the box, while inside, leaning half-out of the window, sat a young gentleman smoking a cigar and loudly urging the postboy. It was Mr. Spavin, our acquaintance from Baymouth, driving home in triumph in his yellow carriage after earning his degree. Spotting a figure gesticulating madly up the hill with a pale, ghastly face, Spavin roared to the driver. The horses halted abruptly, stopping fifty yards ahead of Pen.

Pen heard his name shouted and saw Spavin thrust his upper body from the window, beckoning violently. Pen stopped, hesitated, nodded fiercely, and pointed onward, signaling the postilion to proceed. Silent, his face desperate, he watched as Spavin stared in blank alarm, jumped out, ran to Pen, and grasped his hand.

"I say—hullo, old boy, where are you going, and what's the row?" Spavin asked.

"I'm going where I deserve to go," Pen replied, with an imprecation.

"This ain't the way," Spavin smiled. "This is the Fenbury road. Don't take on because you're plucked. It's nothing when you're used to it. I've been plucked three times, old boy—and after the first time, I didn't care. Glad it's over, though. You'll have better luck next time."

Pen looked at his former acquaintance—who had been plucked, rusticated, and forced to learn reading and writing correctly only after repeated failures, yet somehow managed to earn a degree. *This man has passed,* he thought, *and I have failed!* It was almost too much to bear.

"Good-bye, Spavin," Pen said. "Very glad you're through. Don't let me keep you; I'm in a hurry—I'm going to town tonight."

"Gammon," Spavin scoffed. "This ain't the way to town; this is the Fenbury road."

"I was just going to turn back," Pen admitted.

"All the coaches are full with the men going down," Spavin noted. Pen winced. "You'd not get a place for a ten-pound note. Get into my yellow; I'll drop you at Mudford, where you can catch the Fenbury mail. I'll lend you a hat and a coat; I've got plenty. Come along; jump in, old boy—go it, leathers!"

Thus, Pen found himself in Mr. Spavin's post-chaise, riding with him to the Ram Inn at Mudford, fifteen miles from Oxford. Here, the Fenbury mail changed horses, and Pen secured a seat to London.

The following day, immense excitement gripped Boniface College. Rumors spread, terrifying Pen's tutor and tradesmen, that Pendennis, maddened by the loss of his degree, had taken his own life. A battered cap, barely legible with his name, and a seal bearing his crest of an eagle gazing at an extinct sun, were found three miles down the Fenbury road near a mill-stream. For twenty-four hours, it was believed poor Pen had thrown himself into the water, until letters bearing the London postmark finally arrived.

The mail reached London at the dreary hour of five. Hastening to his usual inn in Covent Garden, the ever-vigilant porter admitted him and showed him to a bed. Pen scrutinized the man, wondering if "Boots" knew he had been plucked. Unable to sleep, he tossed until the dismal gray of London dawn broke, then sprang up desperately and walked to his uncle's lodgings in Bury Street. The maid, scouring the steps, looked up suspiciously at the unshaven figure in yesterday's linen. She clearly suspected his mishap.

"Good 'eves! Mr. Harthur, what as 'appened, sir?" asked Mr. Morgan, the valet, who had just polished his master's boots and carried in his wig.

"I want to see my uncle," Pen cried in a ghastly voice, collapsing onto a chair.

Morgan recoiled, eyes wide with terror and wonder, and vanished into the Master's apartment. The Major emerged from his bedroom, wig freshly adjusted.

"What? Examination over? Senior Wrangler, double First Class, hay?" the old gentleman exclaimed cheerfully. "I'll come directly;" and his head disappeared again.

"They don't know what has happened," Pen

The letter from the Major arrived with the speed of bad news, prompting Dr. Portman to dash off to Fairoaks with the alacrity of a dentist rushing to extract a molar. He treated the situation with grim efficiency: *the tooth must be pulled, and you sit still.* While he might hesitate if it were his own tooth, he applied his forceps with vigorous determination, convinced it was simply the widow's duty to endure the pain. After reading the epistle aloud to Myra and Mrs. Portman, accompanied by heavy sighs and damning commentary on the young scapegrace sliding further toward perdition, the Doctor departed. The ladies, true to their reputation for accuracy and dispatch, spread the tidings through Clavering society before striding over to Fairoaks to deliver the blow personally.

They found the widow already prepared. Having read Pen's letter herself, a gloomy presentiment that had haunted her for months had lifted. She knew the worst, yet she saw her darling boy returning, repentant and tender-hearted. Was there more she desired? The Rector's sensible, antique remarks failed to stir any indignation in Helen, save a pang that her son should suffer. She questioned the degree—the fuss over it—and why they had sent him to a place teeming with temptation and devoid of virtue, rather than keeping him at home. Regarding his debts, she argued pragmatically: wasn't his father's money his own? Didn't he have a right to spend it? Thus, the widow met the virtuous Doctor, and his arrows of indignation struck no spark in her gentle bosom.

Meanwhile, a significant change had occurred within the household regarding Pen and his younger sister, Laura. The childish ritual of brotherly affection, once frequent in their youth, had ceased by mutual consent. Upon returning from college after months away, Pen discovered he was no longer greeted by the simple girl he had left behind. Instead, he faced a tall, slim, handsome young woman who offered a gracious curtsy and a trembling hand, blushing furiously at the very spot Pen had once kissed.

Describing Miss Laura Bell at sixteen is a delicate task. She stood five-foot-four, deemed "tall" and "gawky" by smaller peers, though adorned with "beautiful roses" around her head that attracted many suitors. Ordinarily pale, her cheeks flushed readily at the slightest provocation, retaining the color long after the emotion faded. Her eyes, large since childhood, shone with an innate brilliance that no amount of criticism could dim; Nature had endowed them with such radiance that they required thick lashes merely to soften their glow. Her complexion rivaled Lady Mantrap's without the aid of powder, her smile revealed pearly teeth, and her voice possessed a low, sweet melody akin to music itself. Though her long dresses hid her feet, her overall demeanor was undeniably sweet.

However, Laura's admiration for her brother had cooled. Over the past two years, her mind and body had matured alongside her disappointment. Pen's letters had grown sparse and brief. When Helen urged him to prioritize his studies, Laura countered that losing a prize was preferable to forgetting his mother. She questioned why he spent his holidays at his rich friends' houses instead of staying home, where he would be loved more deeply.

Helen sighed, wondering if Laura truly loved Pen less than she claimed, or if she simply refused to accept Helen's arguments that Pen needed to forge his own path. Helen tried to believe in her excuses—that Pen needed to cultivate influential acquaintances and navigate male social obligations incomprehensible to women—but deep down, she shared Laura's skepticism. Yet, the maternal instinct remained unbreakable; once a woman loves, her tenderness and forgiveness seem eternal.

Laura also resented Pen's newfound freedom and audacity. He was polite by nature, yet he spoke loosely of women, neglected small courtesies, smoked his horrid pipes indoors, and preferred yawning over novels in his dressing gown while his mother attended church. The hero of her infancy, the brave boy of endless bedtime stories, had transformed into a bold, sarcastic, defiant young man who scorned the simple devotions of the women around him. The scandal of the Fotheringay affair shattered her completely. Hearing of a Pendennis throwing himself at an actress, drinking with her father, and potentially bringing such a woman into their home, Laura was outraged. "I would have run away," she declared, "even walking barefoot through the snow!" Helen reassured her that she would leave too, and the two embraced warmly, their shared outrage momentarily forgotten.

Perhaps Laura's fury stemmed from ignorance of how men and women mirror each other's follies, or perhaps she was misled by the bitter gossip of the Claverings and the Doctor. Or, as some suggest, she was jealous—a vice ladies supposedly avoid. Regardless, her anger was directed solely at Pen, not his mother. Devoted to Helen with the passionate intensity of a young woman seeking a surrogate confidante, she showered her with caresses and endearments. But the moment news reached them that Pen was truly miserable, Laura's wrath evaporated, replaced by unreasonable compassion. The Pen of old days returned to her memory: frank, generous, and tender.

She immediately sided with Helen against the Doctor's accusations. Debts? A trifling matter! He'd been forced into expensive society by his uncle. Failed to get his degree? He was ill during the exams, distracted by debt, perhaps unfairly targeted by jealous tutors. Others were cruel and unfair. With flushed cheeks and bright eyes, Laura defied the Doctor in his presence, seizing Helen's hand and asking how he dared speak against her darling mother's son. The discomfited clergyman retreated, leaving the two women to embrace with renewed fervor. Helen, sensing the logic in Laura's defense, began to suspect foul play among the examiners. Together, they barely registered the calamity. To women, calamity often feels like a summons for absent affection; if you reduce your mistress to a crust, she won't complain, provided you share the meal.

Directly the Doctor left, Laura ordered fires lit and beds aired. By the time Helen finished a tearful letter to Pen, the rooms were warm and inviting. Laura added a postscript calling him her "dearest Pen," urging him to come home instantly to happiness with his mother and herself. That night, unable to sleep despite their Bible readings, the two women lay awake. Helen stared at the night-lamp, thinking of Pen for hours, while Laura pretended to be motionless, her own thoughts racing. Finally, Laura proposed a scheme: since Helen had raised her for ten years without spending a penny of her savings, Laura owed her a fortune. They would demand the five hundred pounds from Mr. Rowdy at Chatteris, save two more, and send it all to Pen to clear his debts. Then, they would live happily ever after. Helen responded with incoherent exclamations and embraces, and both slept soundly until dawn.

When the sun rose over the purple hills, Helen woke to find Laura sleeping peacefully beside her, dreaming of happier times. Meanwhile, in London, Pen, driven by remorse and a vow of economy, traveled to Fairoaks in the cold January air. He sat outside the coach with the guard, refusing the warmth of an inside seat. At the lodge gate, his old servant John, now dressed in a fustian jacket rather than livery, grinned with a blush of pride. "I'm garner and stable man now," John noted, before vanishing as Pen entered the grounds.

Moments later, Helen appeared, her face radiant with love and forgiveness. She had already written to Pen detailing Laura's "magnificent" financial offer, filled with blessings for both children. Seeing Laura in the hall, Pen blushed intensely; this was the first time in years she had broken their tacit agreement of distance. The prodigal had returned, the fatted calf was slaughtered, and the two women made him as happy as possible.

For weeks, no mention was made of the Oxford disaster. Pen fretted internally, eventually riding to Chatteris to retrieve the funds Laura had secured. He handed the cheque to his mother, an act of "virtue" in her eyes and "strict justice" in Laura's. Pen rarely spoke of the loan, though he thanked her hesitantly, his vanity warring with his gratitude. He quit wine, switched to whisky-and-water, abandoned cigars for pipes, and fell into a melancholy routine of napping, reading newspapers, playing cribbage with Captain Glanders, and avoiding the stern glares of Dr. Portman. He attended church regularly and prayed for his mother, but the house had changed. The silver covers stayed dark, the fires burned low, and the staff dwindled to two maids and a gardener. Life was austere, and Pen's spirits sank lower.

He refused to return to Oxford, claiming no power could induce him to face his failure. But Laura, blushing with earnestness, insisted that as penance for his idleness, he must try to earn his degree. Reluctantly, Pen returned to Cambridge. A "plucked" man is a dismal creature, belonging to no set and owned by none. Stripped of his fine feathers, he shut himself in his rooms, attending chapel but avoiding the noise of undergraduates. His creditors vanished as he paid them off, and the cards stopped arriving. His yearmates graduated and moved on.

In his second attempt, Pen passed his bachelor's exam with ease. Returning to Fairoaks, he visited his uncle in London, only to be met with icy silence and a refusal of a handshake. Dejected, he returned to his books,

While the residents of Fairoaks drifted through their daily routine in a state of sleepy lethargy, the great house perched atop the hill across the River Brawl was waking up. After sleeping soundly through the lives of two generations of masters, it was suddenly shaking off its slumber, radiating signs of vigorous renewal.

This transformation coincided perfectly with a minor mishap involving Pen, who was so consumed by his own sorrow that he failed to notice the seismic shift occurring around him. Meanwhile, a rumor was sweeping the countryside like wildfire. Provincial newspapers had announced that Clavering Park was to be inhabited again. The news traveled fast: from Clavering Market to Cackleby Fair, from Chatteris Sessions to the roadside encounters between the squire's carriage and the vicar's humble conveyance. Whether at Tinkleton Church gate amidst the tolling bells and the procession of white smocks and scarlet cloaks, or in countless village gatherings, the word was unanimous: *Clavering Park is coming alive.*

To understand the magnitude of this event, one must look back five years. At that time, the county papers reported the marriage in Florence, at the British Legation, of Francis Clavering, Esq., the only son of Sir Francis Clavering, Bart., of Clavering Park, to Jemima Augusta, daughter of Samuel Snell of Calcutta and widow of the late J. Amory, Esq. The prevailing gossip suggested that the bankrupt Clavering had finally secured a wealthy widow from India. A select few travelers, including the Kickleburys in Italy, glimpsed the newlyweds. They were spotted residing opulently in the Poggi Palace, hosting lavish parties, yet strangely unable—or unwilling—to return to England.

Later, young Peregrine of Cackleby encountered them at Schloss Schinkenstein on Lake Mummel during his vacation. Rumors of the couple drifted through Rome, Lucca, Nice, and the gambling dens of the Rhine and Belgium, reaching Clavering Park in sporadic gusts. Their final known location was Paris, where, following the death of Samuel Snell in Calcutta, they reportedly lived in immense style and splendor.

As for Sir Francis Clavering's background, it offered little material for flattering biographies. Born to an outlaw hiding in a grim chateau near Bruges, he attempted a career in the dragoons but collapsed almost immediately. Gambling debts quickly ruined him; after two years in the army, he was forced to sell his commission, spent time in the Fleet Prison, and eventually sailed to Ostend to join his gout-ridden father in exile. For years, this decayed prodigal could be found lurking in billiard rooms and watering holes across Belgium, France, and Germany, punting at casinos, dancing at boarding-house balls, and riding steeple-chases on borrowed horses.

It was at a boarding house in Lausanne that Francis Clavering executed what he termed his "lucky coup" by marrying the recently returned Widow Amory. Upon her father's death, she inherited a fortune rumored to be staggering, and her step-father, Mr. Snell, doubled her allowance before passing away shortly thereafter, leaving her and her children a magnificent legacy.

However, prior to this windfall, Lady Clavering's reputation was anything but pristine. The respectable English expatriates avoided her; her manners were considered crude, and her origins dubious. Retired East Indians scoffed at her father, a disreputable lawyer and indigo smuggler, and her first husband, a ship's mate who had never set foot in Calcutta's society. Even Old Sir Jasper Rogers, the former Chief Justice, claimed to possess a scandalous secret about her first marriage but refused to share it, much to the disappointment of his daughters.

Yet, once the family moved to Paris and established themselves at the Hôtel Bouilli in the Rue Grenelle, Lady Clavering transformed into a beacon of generosity. The Faubourg Saint-Germain embraced her. Viscount Bagwig, the Ambassador, courted her attentively; princes flocked to her salons. Even the most austere figures of London society—Lady Elderbury, Lady Rockminster, and the Countess of Southdown—publicly endorsed her. Ten thousand pounds a year seemed to have miraculously purified her character. Her charitable contributions were boundless: funding schools and convents for French pious ladies, supporting the Armenian Patriarch, Father Barbarossa, Baptist missions, and even the Orthodox settlement in the cannibalistic islands of Feefawfoo. Legend had it that on the same day she donated five Napoleons to aid persecuted Jesuits, she also funded the Reverend J. Ramshorn's visionary project to convert the Pope. Beyond charity, she hosted the finest dinners and balls in Parisian society.

During this era, she seemingly resolved her husband's English debts. Suddenly, Sir Francis reappeared in his homeland, free from arrest, announced in the *Morning Post* and local papers as a resident of Mivart's Hotel. One day, the anxious housekeeper at Clavering House witnessed a carriage and four horses ascending the long avenue, stopping before the moss-covered steps of the vast, melancholy portico.

Inside the open carriage sat three gentlemen. On the rear bench was Mr. Tatham of Chatteris, a familiar face. Beside him, occupying seats of honor, sat a handsome, portly gentleman draped in mustaches, whiskers, fur collars, and elaborate braiding. Next to him reclined a pale, languid man who stepped out weakly, while the lawyer and the man in fur leaped out with nimble agility.

They ascended the mossy steps toward the hall door. A foreign attendant, wearing earrings and a gold-laced cap, vigorously pulled the handle of the cracked, sculptured gate. The bell rang loudly, echoing through the gloomy mansion. Footsteps echoed on the marble pavement inside, and the doors swung open. Finally, Mrs. Blenkinsop, the housekeeper, Polly, her aide-de-camp, and Smart, the keeper, emerged, bowing humbly.

Smart, pulling at his wispy, hay-colored hair, kicked his left heel as if warding off imaginary dogs, and bowed deeply. Old Mrs. Blenkinsop curtsied. Little Polly performed a series of rapid bows. Then, with great emotion, Mrs. Blenkinsop quavered, "Welcome to Clavering, Sir Francis. It does my poor eyes good to see one of the family once more."

These words were directed at the grand gentleman in fur and braiding, who wore his hat jauntily to one side and twirled his mustaches regally. However, he burst into laughter. "You've saddled the wrong horse, old lady! I am not Sir Francis Clavering returning to revisit the halls of my ancestors!" He gestured dramatically. "Friends and vassals! Behold your rightful lord!"

He pointed to the pale, languid gentleman, who retorted, "Don't be an ass, Ned."

"Yes, Mrs. Blenkinsop, I am Sir Francis Clavering," the Baronet continued, ignoring the interruption. "I remember you quite well. Did I forget? How do?" He took the old lady's trembling hand, nodding kindly in her astonished face.

Mrs. Blenkinsop insisted on her conscience that she would recognize Sir Francis anywhere; he was the very image of his father and grandfather.

"Oh yes—thanky—of course—very much obliged—and that sort of thing," Sir Francis said, gazing vacantly around the hall. "Dismal old place, isn't it, Ned? I never saw it but once, when my governor quarreled with grandfather in 'twenty-three."

"Dismal? Beautiful! The Castle of Otranto! The Mysteries of Udolpho, by Jove!" exclaimed the man addressed as Ned. "Look at that fireplace! You could roast an elephant in it! Splendid carved gallery! Inigo Jones, I swear! I'd lay five to two it's Inigo Jones."

"The upper section by Inigo Jones; the lower was altered by the eminent Dutch architect, Vanderputty, during the reign of George the First," corrected the housekeeper.

"Oh indeed," said the Baronet. "Gad, Ned, you know everything."

"I know a few things, Frank," Ned replied. "That isn't a Snyders over the mantelpiece—bet you three to one it's a copy. We'll restore it, my boy. A lick of varnish, and it will shine wonderfully. That old fellow in the red gown is presumably Sir Richard."

"Sheriff of the county and Member of Parliament in Queen Anne's reign," explained the housekeeper, marveling at the stranger's knowledge. "That on the right is Theodosia, wife of Harbottle, second baronet, painted by Lely as Venus... her son Gregory, the third baronet, beside her as Cupid... and that on the next panel is Sir Rupert, knighted by Charles the First, whose property was confiscated by Cromwell."

"Thank you—no need to continue, Mrs. Blenkinsop," interrupted the Baronet. "We'll explore the place ourselves. Frosch, give me a cigar. Would you care for one, Mr. Tatham?"

Mr. Tatham accepted a cigar offered by Sir Francis's courier, spluttering slightly as the lawyer inhaled deeply. "No need to accompany us, Mrs. Blenkinsop. What is his name, Smart? Feed the horses and wash their mouths. We

There is a saying that every household harbors a skeleton in its closet, and for the fortunate few, it is a comforting thought to assume that their wealthier neighbors carry heavier burdens of sorrow. In Clavering House, however, the "skeleton" was not a rotting bone hidden in the attic, but a living, breathing, singing entity named Blanche Amory. To the outside world, she was a radiant fairy; inside the house, she was the Nemesis, the bore, and the source of endless disquiet. Just as a single pebble in a shoe can ruin an entire journey, Blanche's presence turned the smooth path of domestic life into a minefield of annoyance. Who could have imagined that such a smiling sprite could be the architect of family discord?

One afternoon, after dinner, the Baronet sat at the billiard table with Captain Strong, puffing on a cigar—the universal confessor of male secrets.

"I say, Strong," the Baronet declared, his tone dripping with malice. "I wish to the doose your wife was dead."

"So do I," Strong replied instantly, laughing. "That's a cannon, by Jove. But she won't; she'll live forever—you see if she doesn't. Why do you wish her off the hooks, Frank?"

"Because then you might marry Missy," the Baronet drawled. "She isn't bad-looking. She'll bring ten thousand pounds, and that's a fortune for a poor old devil like you."

"And gad, Strong, I hate her worse and worse every day. I can't stand her!"

"I wouldn't take her at twice the price," Strong chuckled. "I've never seen such a little devil in my life."

"I should like to poison her," the Baronet said gravely. "By Jove, I should."

"Why, what has she done now?" Strong asked.

"Nothing particular," Sir Francis admitted with a grimace. "Just her old tricks. That girl has a knack of making everyone miserable. Last night she sent the governess sobbing away from the dinner table. Later, I heard the poor little boy howling in the dark because she'd terrified him with stories of ghosts haunting the house. At lunch, she gave my wife a turn. Though my wife is a simpleton, she's a good soul—I'm hanged if she isn't."

"What exactly did Missy do to her?" Strong inquired.

"She started talking about the late Amory, my predecessor," the Baronet explained, grinning wickedly. "She pulled a picture from *The Keepsake* and insisted it looked like her dear father. Then she demanded to know where her father's grave was. Hang her father! Whenever Miss Amory mentions him, Lady Clavering bursts into tears. The little devil does it on purpose to spite her mother. Today, when she started, I flew into a rage. I claimed to be her father, and then... well, she took a shot at me."

"And what did she say about you, Frank?" Strong laughed.

"Gad," the Baronet retorted, "she said I wasn't her father. She claimed I wasn't fit to comprehend her. She insisted her father must have been a man of genius and fine feelings, whereas I had married her mother solely for money."

"Well, didn't you?" Strong teased.

"It doesn't make it any less painful to hear because it's true, you know," Sir Francis snapped. "I'm not a literary man, but I'm not such a fool as she paints me. She always manages to put me in the hole. She turns the whole house around her with her quiet ways and those confounded sentimental airs. I wish she were dead, Ned."

"It was my wife you wanted dead just now," Strong pointed out, maintaining his perfect good humor.

Upon hearing this, the Baronet confessed with his usual candor: "Well, when people bore my life out, I do wish they were dead. And I wish Missy were down a well, with all my heart."

From this candid exchange, it becomes clear that our accomplished little friend possessed peculiarities that made her unpopular. She was a young lady of genuine genius, exquisite sensitivity, and considerable literary talent, living with relatives who couldn't comprehend her. Neither her mother nor her stepfather shared her intellectual depth; the Baronet read *Bell's Life* and the *Racing Calendar*, while Lady Clavering still wrote like a thirteen-year-old schoolgirl with a total disregard for grammar. Feeling keenly that she was surrounded by inferiors, Blanche seized every opportunity to highlight their inadequacies. She played the martyr, ensuring everyone knew she was suffering. If she cried, as she believed she must, was anyone surprised? Without sympathy, life is meaningless. Would it not be dishonest to feign cheerfulness toward those she revered? If a poetess cannot mourn her lot, what use is her lyre? Blanche struck hers only to the saddest tunes, singing elegies over dead hopes and dirges for nipped buds of affection.

Her actual troubles hadn't been severe yet, but her griefs resided entirely in her own soul. Being habitually dissatisfied, why shouldn't she weep? Tears flowed from her eyes on command; she had mastered the art of shedding them, and like the dropsy mentioned by Horace, her ability to cry increased with practice.

Blanche had begun to gush at a very young age. Lamartine was her favorite bard since infancy, followed by a voracious diet of French novels by Balzac and George Sand. By sixteen, she had devoured every romance available. Though she sympathized poorly with her human relatives, she claimed friends in the "spirit-world"—characters like the tender Indiana, the passionate Lelia, the amiable Trenmor, the convict-hero Stenio, and the fiery Djalma. She had fallen in love with fictional princes while still wearing pinafores, settling complex legal questions regarding women's rights alongside Indiana. She played at love with these imaginary lovers just as she once played at maternity with her doll. It is fascinating to watch such poetic spirits shift their affections daily; today the blue-eyed hero is favored, tomorrow the black-eyed villain is neglected until he is deemed odious, only to be dandled again the next day.

History reveals that at eleven, "Mademoiselle Betsi" (as she was then known) fell for a young Savoyard organ grinder she believed to be a displaced prince. At twelve, an old, hideous drawing master captured her heart. By thirteen, attending Madame de Caramel's boarding school near Monsieur Rogron's pension, she corresponded romantically with two students from the College of Charlemagne.

Note that we have used her Christian name, Betsy, here. While known as "Missy" at home, Blanche had chosen the name Blanche for herself. The Baronet's favorite method of discipline was threatening to call her "Betsy" in public, a tactic that occasionally kept the rebellious child in line.

Like her changing dolls, Blanche treated her friendships with similar fickleness. She maintained a treasure chest full of hair locks collected during her sentimental travels. Some friends married, others moved schools, and one beloved sister vanished from the pension only to be rediscovered working the books in her father's grocery store in the Rue du Bac. Blanche viewed these estrangements and disillusionments as the inevitable penalties of genius.

Meanwhile, she successfully tormented her mother and drove her stepfather to wish for her death. Except for Captain Strong, whose good humor was impenetrable, Blanche ruled the house with her tongue. When Lady Clavering mistook asparagus for sparrows or referred to objects as "hobjects," Blanche would calmly correct her, driving the poor woman into nervous fits.

Outside the house, the arrival of the Claverings sparked immense curiosity in the small town. Madame Fribsby, the local milliner, was naturally intrigued. She scrutinized every detail of the ladies' attire, from bonnets to brodequins, ignoring the sermon of Dr. Portman entirely. Soon, she secured an audience with Lady Clavering's housekeeper, presenting business cards claiming she supplied the latest Parisian fashions and crafted dresses for the nobility. These claims were warmly received.

Mrs. Bonner, the lady's maid, quickly became a regular at Madame Fribsby's drawing room, enjoying green tea, scandal, hot Sally-Lunn cakes, and novels. In contrast, Blanche's own maid, Pincott, was rarely allowed a holiday. Worked harder than a factory girl, Pincott spent her days and nights altering Blanche's clothes, rising early and sleeping late to satisfy her mistress's capricious desires. Pincott came from respectable English parents, including a crippled father who relied on her support. Leaving would mean adding to their misery, so she stayed.

With characteristic candor, Blanche often reminded Pincott of her difficult situation. "I should send you away, Pincott, you are too weak and your eyes are failing. But I endure you for your parents' sake," she would say. Other times, she complained that Pincott's wretched appearance gave her migraines and ordered her to wear rouge. Yet, after terrifying the girl with threats to dismiss her, she would settle down at one o'clock, ask Pincott to kiss her goodnight, and demand the pink dress be ready for the morning. Blanche considered herself a superior being, expecting to be waited upon as such. One wonders if there aren't other ladies who treat their servants with a cruelty disguised by a soft voice and a well-bred

Our friends have already heard Sir Francis Clavering's honest take on the woman who bought him back from the brink of ruin and restored him to his ancestral home. Let's just say the Baronet wasn't entirely off-base regarding his wife. Lady Clavering, bless her heart, wasn't exactly the sharpest tool in the shed, nor the most polished specimen of female intellect. Her entire education consisted of two years spent in a London suburb she stubbornly insisted on calling "Ackney" until the moment of her death. At fifteen, she was whisked away to Calcutta to join her father aboard the *Ramchunder*, an East Indiaman captained by the formidable Mr. Bragg.

It was on this voyage that she met her first husband, Mr. Amory, the ship's third mate. Now, we aren't here to rewrite the whole biography of Lady Clavering, but suffice it to say that Captain Bragg, who was also her father's business partner, grew so fed up with Mr. Amory's rebellious antics that he threw him in irons. Once they hit the Cape, Bragg dumped the officer there and sailed on, delivering his wealthy ward safely to her father in Calcutta after a voyage that threatened to sink the ship, the cargo, and several passengers' sanity.

A few months later, Mr. Amory showed up in Calcutta. Having worked his way up from the deckhands, he managed to marry the rich attorney's daughter despite her father's protests. He tried his hand at indigo planting (failed), became an agent (failed again), edited a local newspaper (*Sunderbund Pilot*—another failure), and throughout it all, he argued constantly with his father-in-law and wife. His final act was a spectacular financial crash that sent him packing to New South Wales. Somewhere amidst this chaos, he likely crossed paths with Sir Jasper Rogers, the respected judge. And here is the uncomfortable truth: Mr. Amory's downfall was sealed when he shamelessly forged his father-in-law's signature. Since the old gentleman could write perfectly well and didn't need a ghostwriter, the forgery was easily exposed, and fortune abandoned Mr. Amory forever.

With her husband dead and her life in India proving miserable, Lady Clavering decided to return to Europe. She traveled with her four-year-old daughter, Blanche (known as Betsy or Blanche depending on the mood), and their loyal nurse, Mrs. Bonner. They settled near Captain Bragg in Pocklington Street, but the summer was unbearably rainy, and Bragg was a pompous, disagreeable host who clearly couldn't wait to get rid of the "Indian lady." Blanche assumed everyone in London was gossiping about her husband's disgrace and that the Royal Family knew her secrets. With a generous allowance from her father and no desire to live in England, she packed her bags for the continent.

She loved to travel. She still called Hackney "Ackney," spoke English with a charming foreign lilt, dressed to kill, and possessed an appetite that bordered on the legendary. She cooked curries and pilau in every boarding house she visited. While her quirks might seem odd, they made her incredibly popular. She was jovial, generous, and impossible to resist. If you wanted a picnic, she brought triple the amount of champagne, chicken, and ham. She handed out theater tickets and masks like candy. She prepaid her rent months in advance and kept a purse full of cash to bail out struggling bachelors and dowagers whose money hadn't arrived. From Brussels to Rome, she was a whirlwind of laughter and generosity.

Tragically, news of Mr. Amory's death caught up with her in Rome, where Captain Clavering was currently stuck unable to pay his hotel bill (along with his friend, the Chevalier Strong). The good-hearted widow, showing no particular grief for the scoundrel she'd lost, married the descendant of the ancient Clavering line. Thus, she became the mistress of Clavering Park.

Her daughter, Missy (Blanche), followed suit, learning a great deal about life. She had a governess for a while and later attended Madame de Caramel's prestigious school in the Champs-Élysées. When the Claverings returned to England, Blanche came with them. Only recently, following her grandfather's death and the birth of her little brother, did Blanche realize her status had shifted. Without her father's fortune, she was merely a minor figure in a large house, overshadowed by Master Francis Clavering, the heir to the baronetcy. Though she was a romantic soul who didn't care much for money, she couldn't ignore the fact that her stepfather was dull, her mother dropped her H's and lacked refinement, and little Frank was a spoiled brat who constantly tripped her, spilled food on her dress, and stood between her and her inheritance. Feeling misunderstood and lonely, Blanche's heart yearned for connection outside the family.

This desperation made her difficult at home, frightening her mother and boring her stepfather so much that they desperately wanted her to marry. Sir Francis even hinted to his friend that if Mrs. Strong died, he'd take Blanche as a second wife—but since that was unlikely, anyone else was welcome to try. Enter Arthur Pendennis ("Pen"), a handsome, well-educated young man. Despite his obvious suitability, Pen hesitated. He was plagued by insecurity about his recent failures, his lack of a title, and the poverty he'd brought upon his mother. How could he compete with Blanche, who lived in a grand park with a staff of servants, while his mother struggled to make ends meet at Fairoaks? Paralyzed by vanity and fear, he dallied with his hopes rather than acting.

Meanwhile, Pen's mother, Helen, saw the writing on the wall. She viewed Blanche as frivolous, lacking reverence, and dangerously worldly. Laura and Pen initially defended Blanche, but as the romance deepened, Helen's fears grew. Watching her son and the girl meet daily, her maternal instincts kicked into overdrive. In a moment of vulnerability, she confessed to Pen that she felt her time was short and hoped to see her two children united before she passed. Pen, trying to lighten the mood, joked that Laura would surely reject him, imagining her as too "grand" for someone like him. He pictured Laura bringing him roses, a symbol of her kindness, and felt a surge of triumph thinking, *"I might have this prize for the asking!"*

However, reality soon set in. Before long, Laura began to see the flaws in Blanche. Through private conversations, Blanche poured out her grievances: how her family didn't understand her, how her mother's neglect ruined her upbringing, how her stepfather was intellectually bankrupt, and how her mother's love had shifted entirely to the new baby brother. Blanche painted a picture of isolation and longing. Yet, Laura couldn't shake her suspicions. For instance, just because little Frank was annoying didn't justify Blanche slapping him upside the head for spilling water or screaming insults at him in both English and French. Similarly, Blanche treated the household staff with imperial disdain, ordering them around rudely, whereas Laura was always polite and grateful.

The breaking point came after a particularly nasty incident involving little Frank. Blanche had boxed the boy's ears and berated him in front of everyone. Laura, horrified by such cruelty toward a defenseless child, exploded. She lectured Blanche on her wickedness, telling her she needed to kneel and beg forgiveness from God. Enraged and humiliated, Blanche stormed out, leaving Laura to rush home in a panic. That evening, Laura confessed to her mother that Blanche was selfish and cruel, declaring she was giving up the friendship forever. Helen secretly cheered, believing this meant Blanche was unfit for Pen.

But there was more to the story. Later that day, while sitting together, Blanche turned the conversation to Pen's past affair with the actress. She claimed Pen had been false to her. Laura, blushing, insisted Pen had actually been generous and that the actress had betrayed him. To test Laura, Blanche produced a hidden stash of love letters Pen had written to her—poems about nymphs and rivers. Laura read them, confused by their vagueness, and noted that Blanche had never replied. Blanche laughed, hid the letters, and sang a few Rossini songs, leaving Laura to wonder what she was thinking.

Despite the tension, the next morning, as they walked arm-in-arm to lunch, Laura paused at the top of the stairs. Looking Blanche squarely in the eye, she kissed her with genuine warmth. Whether this was a gesture of sisterly love or a final goodbye remains unclear, but it marked the end of an era. Whatever petty irritations—Frank's eating habits, her mother's blunders, or Sir Francis's cigar smell—had sparked earlier, the bond between the two girls had fractured. Blanche's bad day had culminated in a battle royal that nearly destroyed their friendship, leaving Laura disillusioned and Blanche alone in her tantrums.

The truce between the sisters didn't hold. Laura, ever the peacemaker, was quick to forgive, while Miss Blanche's grudges were notoriously short-lived anyway. After the recent row, Blanche wasn't even truly offended; accusations of wickedness rarely sting a vain woman's pride. In fact, she found her friend's righteous fury rather entertaining, especially since both knew exactly why Laura was so indignant, even if neither dared admit it aloud.

So, Laura sighed, conceding that the romantic phase of their friendship had expired. She realized her beloved Blanche was merely deserving of polite, ordinary regard. Meanwhile, Blanche, feeling deserted, promptly penned a series of tear-jerking verses lamenting her "cold" treatment and "neglected" fidelity. Just as the old clichés go, love met ice, and loyalty met indifference.

Coincidentally, some new neighbors arrived from London—daughters included. Seizing the moment, Miss Amory adopted a fresh confidante, pouring out her sorrows to this new sister. The flow of love letters to Laura dried up; the pony carriage stopped visiting Fairoaks regularly. When Laura did drop by, Blanche wore a mask of saintly suffering, while Laura simply laughed at her friend's melodramatic mood, treating it with cheerful disrespect.

However, Blanche's circle expanded beyond women. Any young man she encountered—a stroll in the garden, a chat through a drawing-room window, or a pause during a waltz—became the recipient of her dramatic monologues. She would bat her lashes, speak in a trembling, tender tone, and leave him breathless, only to repeat the performance with the next available suitor.

At first, when the Claverings moved to the Park, Blanche had few suitors to practice on, leaving Pen to monopolize her glances and confessions. But the local male population was sparse: mostly a couple of curates, a rustic squire with oversized shoes and shabby clothes, and the local dragoon regiment, which the Baronet avoided due to past squabbles over horses, gambling debts, and a "white feather" incident.

Things changed with the autumn session and the end of the London season. County families trickled down to Baymouth, Mr. Bingley's theater opened in Chatteris, and the Assizes and race-meets drew crowds. Previously, the established locals—the Fogies of Drummington, the Squares of Tozely Park, and the Welbores of The Barrow—had kept their distance, whispering scandalous rumors about the Claverings' origins, Sir Francis's wife, and Captain Strong. It took three months of silence before the elite finally extended invitations.

The turning point came when the Earl of Trehawk visited Eyrie Castle and the Dowager Countess of Rockminster settled at Baymouth. Their arrival in state forced the lesser nobility to follow suit, filling the avenues of Clavering Park.

This influx gave Mirobolant (the artist) a chance to forget his heartbreaks amidst his craft, and freed the footmen from delivering notes to Fairoaks. Blanche found new admirers, and Pen found himself alone by the river, whipping the stream in vain. The "fish" refused to bite, and the elusive "Peri" never surfaced.

Strictly speaking, let's address the elephant in the room: Pen had turned a hollow tree near the stream into a secret post office. For years, he'd slipped poems and letters under a moss-covered stone, addressed to an imaginary "Undine" or "Naiad." Occasionally, a pink note or a single French word would replace his missive. Miss Amory, who loved pink scented paper, likely walked past this spot often. But once the grand carriages started arriving nightly, the ritual ended. No one fetched Pen's mail anymore. The white paper remained untouched beneath the stone while the river roared by.

Perhaps the letters weren't deep anyway—just playful banter between two young people pretending at romance. But when the game stops working, tempers flare. Pen grew serious, desperate for an explanation.

He tried several times. Once, the house was packed with guests. Another time, Blanche was asleep, preparing for a ball. Pen cursed the balls and his poverty. On a third attempt, he found her in the garden, walking in high procession with the Bishop and Bishopess of Chatteris. The clergyman, recognizing Pen from a previous encounter in the Dean's garden, scowled.

"The Bishop says you're a sad young man," Lady Clavering whispered kindly. "Have you done something to vex your dear Mar? How is she? Why doesn't she come round? We haven't seen her in ages. We've been traveling so much lately. Send our love to her and Laura, and join us for dinner tomorrow."

Mrs. Pendennis was too ill to attend, but Laura and Pen came. Amidst the chatter, Pen managed a hurried exchange with Blanche.

"You never come to the river now," he said.

"I can't," Blanche replied. "The house is full of people."

"Undine has left the stream," Pen continued, trying to maintain his poetic facade.

"She shouldn't have gone there," Blanche corrected sharply. "She won't return. It was foolish, wrong. It was just play. Besides, you have other comforts at home," she added, locking eyes with him for a split second before lowering her gaze.

If he wanted her, why didn't he speak then? She could have said yes. Instead, hearing her mention "comforts at home," he thought of Laura—so pure, so devoted—and his mother, who had dreamed of marrying him to her adopted daughter.

"Blanche!" he started, annoyed. "Miss Amory!"

"Laura is looking at us, Mr. Pendennis," Blanche said smoothly. "I must return to the group." She fled, leaving Pen biting his nails in confusion, staring blankly at the moonlit garden.

Meanwhile, Laura watched Pen. She was conversing with Mr. Pynsent, the son of Lord Rockminster, a tall, grave figure seated in honor. Pynsent and Pen had attended Oxford together; back then, Pen, in his prime of wealth and style, had looked down on the young patrician. Now, they nodded politely across the table—a classic university gesture meaning, *"Confound you, what are you doing here?"*

"I knew that man at Oxford," Pynsent told Miss Bell. "A Mr. Pendennis, I believe."

"Yes," Miss Bell confirmed.

"He seems quite taken with Miss Amory," Pynsent observed. Laura glanced at them but said nothing.

"A wealthy county man, isn't he? Used to talk about running for Parliament. Where are his estates?"

Laura smiled. "On the other side of the river, near the lodge gate. He's my cousin, and I live there."

"Where?" Pynsent laughed.

"Why, on the other side of the river, at Fairoaks," Miss Bell answered.

"Many pheasants there? The cover looks promising," the naive gentleman remarked.

"We have nine hens, a rooster, a pig, and an old pointer," Laura quipped, amused by the idea that her Pen was a major sporting landlord.

"Pendennis doesn't shoot, then?" Pynsent pressed.

"You should come and see him," Laura laughed, clearly enjoying the joke that her cousin pretended to be a grand country squire.

"Indeed, I'd love to renew our acquaintance," Pynsent said gallantly, his eyes fixed on Laura.

Just then, Blanche approached with a dazzling smile, inviting Laura to sing a duet. Laura obligingly went to the piano. Pynsent listened intently until Blanche took the lead, then strode away.

"What a lovely, frank, well-bred girl that is," Pynsent told a friend from Baymouth, gesturing to Laura. "Tall, with ringlets and terribly red lips."

"And the mistress of the house?" his friend asked.

"I think she's a lean, scraggy fraud," Pynsent admitted candidly. "She strains against her dress, never rests her eyes, and simper-ogles like a French maid."

"Pynsent, be civil! Someone might hear," his friend warned.
