"Oh, it's Pendennis of Boniface," Pynsent called out cheerfully. "Lovely evening, Mr. Pendennis. We were discussing your charming cousin."

"Related to my old friend, Major Pendennis?" asked Mr. Wagg, joining them.

"Nephew. Met you at Gaunt House," Pen replied with practiced elegance. The two men bonded instantly.

The next afternoon, Pen returned from a fruitless fishing trip to find Pynsent and Wagg chatting comfortably in his mother's drawing room with the widow and her ward. Pynsent, tall and gaunt with red whiskers, loomed over Laura, who found his simple, slightly slang-heavy humor amusing. This was Laura's first exposure to a true London dandy; previously, she'd only known Mr. Foker, a boyish schoolboy.

Mr. Wagg, observing everything with the eye of a detective, noted the old gardener, the laundry drying on gooseberry bushes, the blue aprons, and the white duck trousers. "Rather a shy spot for a debut MP, eh, Pynsent?" he mused.

"A snug little crib," Pynsent agreed. "Cosy lawn."

"Master Pendennis at

Arthur Pendennis decided to adopt the most grumpy expression possible, hiding behind the shadowy alcove of a window draped in cheap calico. From this vantage point, he watched Miss Bell attempt her maiden quadrille with Mr. Pynsent as her partner. Mr. Pynsent was a study in rigid propriety; he moved through the dance with the same solemn gravity one might expect of a man walking directly to his church pew, his face devoid of smiles and utterly immune to the festive atmosphere. Yet, Miss Laura Bell was a different story entirely. Beaming with joy, she seemed intoxicated by the lights, the crowd, and the music. As she swept across the floor in her white gown, her brown curls cascading over her rosy cheeks, she radiated such infectious happiness that several gentlemen couldn't help but stare. Even Lady Fogey, who treated the countryside as merely a temporary stopover before returning to her London mansion, leaned over to Lady Rockminster. "Do you know that young lady?" she asked, comparing Laura favorably to a reigning London beauty and declaring, "She'll do."

Lady Rockminster, armed with her magnifying glass, surveyed Laura with majestic indifference. She approved of the girl's natural charm and innocent demeanor, noting only that her arms were slightly red—a youthful imperfection easily overlooked. She contrasted this with Miss Blanche Amory, dancing opposite, whose manner was far too "pert" for her liking. Indeed, while Laura danced with genuine enthusiasm, Miss Blanche executed her moves with calculated flair. Her shoulders were impossibly white, her posture perpetually stiff, and her eyes darting around the room with predatory intent. Every movement seemed to scream: *Look at me! Not at that healthy country bumpkin, Miss Bell, who barely knows how to move without my instruction. This is the true Parisian style. Notice my feet, Mr. Pynsent. Notice them, Mr. Pendennis, lurking behind the curtain. I know you're dying to dance with me.*

Laura, meanwhile, kept a sharp eye on Pen in the window embrasure. He didn't leave his post until the second quadrille, when the obliging Lady Clavering summoned him to the dais of honor reserved for the dowagers. Pen approached, blushing furiously and acting as awkward as any vain young man, offering a haughty bow to Lady Rockminster, who barely acknowledged him, before paying his respects to the Widow Amory, resplendent in diamonds, velvet, and excessive millinery.

Next came Young Mr. Fogey, currently in the fifth form at Eton and eagerly anticipating his beard and a commission in the dragoons. He was captivated by Miss Bell. "I prefer you immensely to the French girl," he admitted candidly, referring to his previous partner, Miss Amory. Laura laughed, looking even more cheerful, and caught sight of Pen watching her with an absurdly pompous scowl. They shared a silent, mutual amusement.

The music shifted to a waltz. Mr. Fogey sighed, admitting he didn't know how to waltz and vowing to hire a teacher for the holidays. Then, Mr. Pynsent claimed Miss Bell's hand again. Pen watched in fury as she twirled around the room, her waist encircled by the stiff arm of the serious gentleman. This was a stark contrast to their summers at Clavering Park, where Pen, the Chevalier Strong, and the two young ladies often improvised lively balls. Back then, Laura's animation could even lift Mr. Pynsent's spirits.

However, tonight belonged to Miss Blanche, who was paired with Captain Broadfoot of the Dragoons. Despite his energetic efforts, the Captain lacked timing and musicality, moving too slowly. Consequently, Miss Blanche grew increasingly jealous of Laura's success. After a few turns with the heavy dragoon, she feigned exhaustion and returned to her mother's side, complaining to Pen about being abandoned to the "odious man in spurs and a red coat."

"I assumed spurs and scarlet were the most captivating sights for young ladies," Pen replied dryly. "I never dared compete my black coat with that magnificent red jacket."

"You are unkind, cruel, and sulky!" Miss Amory retorted, shrugging her shoulders. "Go away. Your cousin is glaring at us over Mr. Pynsent's shoulder."

When Pen finally asked if she would waltz, she declined, citing her current partner. "I'm promised, but I'll tell Mr. Poole I've forgotten our engagement," she offered, leaving Pen to mutter that women forget readily but always return with regret.

As the dances concluded, Laura approached Pen, extending her hand while Mr. Pynsent offered a stiff bow. "So glad to see you, dear Pen," she said kindly. "Three dances are over; I'm yours for the next."

"I've just engaged myself to Miss Amory," Pen announced. Miss Amory nodded politely. "Then he shall waltz with me, dear Blanche," Laura countered. "Won't you, Pen?"

"I promised Miss Amory," Pen stated firmly.

"Provoking!" Laura exclaimed, curtsying before slipping under the protective wing of Lady Rockminster. Pen, delighted by the chaos, leaned casually against the wall, chatting mockingly with Blanche. He ridiculed the heavy dragoons, the eccentric country dandies, and the bizarre hairstyles of the ladies—one resembling a bird's nest, another adorned with six pounds of grapes. "A coiffure of almonds and raisins," he quipped, suggesting it would serve better as dessert. His wit was biting and vivacious, keeping Blanche in stitches while ensuring Laura could clearly see and hear their intimate banter.

Across the room, separated only by Cornet Perch's shell-jacket, Blanche whispered to Laura, "Arthur is charming tonight."

"Who?" Laura asked.

"Arthur," Blanche replied in French. "Such a lovely name!"

During the interval, Pen asked Laura if she found Cornet Perch amusing, effectively planting two daggers in her gentle heart before returning to his teasing with Blanche. Laura, confused by Pen's sudden sulkiness but naturally forgiving, chose to enjoy herself with the talented Chevalier Strong instead. Meanwhile, Captain Broadfoot struggled with a large partner, Miss Roundle, a stout young woman in a strawberry-colored dress.

Enter Mr. Arthur Pendennis. Feeling triumphant, he whirled with Blanche, hoping to catch Laura's approving gaze, but she was too busy with Strong to notice. Their brilliance was short-lived. As they spun lightly, they collided violently with the lumbering Captain Broadfoot and Miss Roundle. The center of gravity vanished for all four dancers. Captain Broadfoot and Miss Roundle were toppled, and so was Pen. Unlike his partner, who landed safely on a bench, Pen sprawled flat on the floor amidst the general wreckage.

Captain Broadfoot, good-natured despite his weight, burst into laughter at his own misfortune. Miss Amory, however, was furious about her fall. Miss Roundle sat up, presenting a sight that drew inevitable giggles from the crowd. Pen, unable to tolerate ridicule, especially directed at himself, boiled with rage. He stormed away, clumsily apologizing to Miss Amory, blaming the "woman in pink," and asking if she was injured. Miss Amory, petulant, refused further dancing and graciously accepted a glass of water from a stranger wearing a blue ribbon and a three-pointed star. She thanked him, turned her shoulder haughtily toward Pen, and allowed the stranger to lead her away.

To Pen's astonishment, the man was none other than Mirobolant, the kitchen chef. Pen shouted, "By Jove, it's the cook!" Immediately realizing the insult, he wished he hadn't spoken. Blanche, oblivious, had simply invited the distinguished-looking foreigner to escort her. Mirobolant, hearing Pen's exclamation, glared back with a furious intensity that amused Pen. The chef, a proud Gascon accustomed to serving fine ladies in his homeland, felt deeply humiliated by the implication that he was beneath a lady's notice.

Chevalier Strong, aware of the chef's precarious mental state, stepped in. Speaking rapidly in Spanish, he convinced Mirobolant to step aside, promising the chef he would lose his job if he continued to walk with Blanche. The chef bowed profoundly and retreated, grinding a Gascon oath between his teeth.

Just then, a commotion erupted in the ballroom. Strong rushed out to investigate, leaving Blanche alone with the lingering mystery of the chef's reaction. The scene shifted to the refreshment room, a select area reserved for the elite, where Colonel Altamont, an officer in the service of the Nawab of Lucknow, had already commandeered a bottle of champagne. Dressed in a black wig and sporting military orders, the Colonel had charmed the staff and guests alike with his confident demeanor and constant jingling of sovereigns.

When Sir Francis Clavering and Mr. Wagg entered, the Colonel's odd behavior raised suspicions of drunkenness among the locals. Mr. Pynsent, ever eager to converse with everyone, approached the Colonel with grave deference, tasting a glass of "Sham" and praising its excellence. This exaggerated politeness baffled the Colonel, who muttered that Pynsent was a "rum one."

Later, when Strong and Miss Amory returned to the refreshment room, the Colonel, now with bloodshot eyes and an unsteady gait, approached Blanche. "And so you're Betsy Amory," he

A civil war was currently raging inside the ballroom, centered around a single open window in the corner. People were shoving and squeezing together in an undignified heap, while sarcastic shouts from the street below—"Pitch into him!" "Where's the police?"—drifted upward. On one side of the window stood a ring of people including the formidable Madame Fribsby, besieging Monsieur Alcide Mirobolant; on the other, a group of gentlemen and ladies surrounded Arthur Pendennis. Just then, the Chevalier Strong shouldered his way through the door and cut directly into the fray, elbowing past Madame Fribsby, who shrieked, "Save him, save him!" in tones of frantic pathos.

The trouble stemmed from the tiny, fiery chef of Sir Francis Clavering's kitchen. Moments earlier, Mr. Pen had retreated to the window to cool his frayed nerves. He was furious about his disastrous waltz—which had made him look foolish in the eyes of the entire nation—and equally stung by Miss Amory's behavior. Trying desperately to regain his composure while watching the sparkling sea, he suddenly felt a hand on his shoulder. Turning, he encountered absolute horror: the hand belonged to Monsieur Mirobolant, whose pale face and wild eyes glared at him. Being tapped on the shoulder by a French cook was an indignity that made the blood of every Pendennis boil; Pen was stunned, bordering on enraged.

"You speak French?" Mirobolant asked in his native tongue.

"What is that to you?" Pen retorted in English.

"At any rate, you understand it?" the cook continued, bowing deeply.

"Yes, sir," Pen stamped his foot. "I understand it pretty well."

"Then you will understand, Monsieur Pendennis," Mirobolant rolled out his *r* with Gascon force, "that I tell you: you are a coward. Monsieur Pendennis—a coward, do you hear?"

"What?" Pen spun around.

"Do you comprehend the weight of that word among men of honor?" the artist demanded, hand on hip.

"The consequence," Pen bellowed, lunging forward, "is that I will throw you out the window, you impudent scoundrel!"

He certainly would have executed this threat, given the proximity of the window and the chef's lack of martial prowess, had not Captain Broadfoot and another heavy officer interposed themselves. Had not the ladies screamed, the music stopped, and the crowd rushed in? Had not Laura, looking terrified, asked what was happening? And had not the timely arrival of Strong, finding Alcides grinding his teeth and Pen looking wickedly calm, prevented disaster?

"What has happened?" Strong asked the chef in Spanish.

"I am Chevalier de Juillet," the man slapped his chest proudly. "He insulted me."

"He called me... 'Chef'?" Strong asked.

*"Il m'a appelé Cuisinier!"* the little Frenchman hissed.

Strong couldn't help laughing. "Come away with me, poor Chevalier. We cannot quarrel before ladies. Let me carry your message to Mr. Pendennis. The poor fellow isn't right in his head," he whispered to the surrounding guests.

Gathered around Pen, the ladies and gentlemen learned the truth. Pen admitted he had simply pointed out the man was a cook when he offered his arm to a young lady, prompting the chef to call him a coward and challenge him to a duel.

"D—him! Serve him right!" the gentlemen grumbled.

"I'm very sorry if I hurt his feelings," Pen added weakly. Laura, relieved to hear him apologize, smiled gently. "Foreigners may be more sensitive. If you hurt a poor man's feelings, wouldn't you ask his pardon, dear Pen?" Looking like an angel, she held out her hands. Pen, touched by her kindness, promised he would.

"How fond that girl is of me!" he thought, watching her gaze. "Should I speak to her now? No, not yet. I must resolve this absurd business with the Frenchman first."

Laura tried to keep him dancing. "Won't you stay and waltz with me, Pen? I'm not afraid."

This was an affectionate but fatal suggestion. In his mind's eye, Pen saw himself tumbling over Miss Roundle and a dragoon, throwing Blanche against the wall, and lying on the floor while everyone laughed—including Laura and Pynsent.

"I shall never dance again," he declared grimly. "Never. I'm surprised you'd ask."

"Is it because you can't find a partner for Blanche?" Laura asked with malicious sharpness.

"No," Pen snapped. "I don't want to make a fool of myself for others to laugh at. Especially for you, Laura. I saw you and Pynsent. By Jove! No man shall laugh at me."

"Pen, don't be so wicked!" Laura cried, hurt by his savage vanity. "Who thinks less of you for stumbling in a waltz? If I do, we don't. Why are you so sensitive?"

Just then, Mr. Pynsent arrived. "Lady Rockminster commands me to ask if I may escort you to supper?"

"I was going with my cousin," Laura lied.

"Oh, pray, no!" Pen interrupted. "You're in excellent hands. I can't do better than leave you. I'm going home."

"Good-night, Mr. Pendennis," Pynsent said dryly—a polite way of telling Pen to go to hell for being a jealous, insolent jackanapes. Pen merely bowed and fled the room despite Laura's pleading looks.

Pynsent later remarked to Laura how pleasant the night was and how tedious the social obligations had become. Laura feigned interest, claiming she had been worried about the quarrel. Pynsent sympathized with Pen's bad luck, listing the various people Pen had displeased, and eagerly accepted Laura's hand to lead her to supper.

Meanwhile, Laura sat in the supper room, distracted and miserable. She wondered how her mother could expect her to marry such a capricious, jealous, and violent man. Her relief came only when a waiter delivered a penciled note from Pen: *"I met Cooky below and asked his pardon. I'm glad I did. I wanted to speak to you tonight but will wait until you return. God bless you. Dance all night with Pynsent and be happy.—PEN."*

Pen, meanwhile, regretted his harshness toward Laura but remembered leaving her with Pynsent, so he decided to make peace with the chef instead. He found the Chevalier pacing the hall, offering to fight him with pistols on the beach or swords near the water.

"I can't fight a cook!" Pen shouted in fury.

"He is a Chevalier of July!" Strong laughed, intervening. "They present arms to him."

"And do you ask me to fight a servant?" Pen roared.

"Thank you for nothing," Strong chuckled. "I came to settle quarrels, not start them. I've calmed Mirobolant. He says you used the word 'cook' unintentionally, as a hired officer shouldn't offer his arm to the hostess's daughter." Strong then revealed the true secret: Madame Fribsby had confessed that the poor artist was suffering from a violent, delirious passion.

Upon hearing this, Pen burst into hearty laughter, his rage vanishing instantly. He realized he had been absurdly jealous himself all evening, longing to insult Pynsent just as he had once envied Oaks. Ready to forgive anyone driven mad by love, he entered the coffee room where Mirobolant waited.

"Monsieur Pendennis has disproven my accusations," Alcide said politely. "He has shown himself a *galant homme*." They shook hands and parted. Before joining Strong for a ride, Pen sent a final note to Laura: *"Dance away all night with Pynsent and be very happy."*

As they drove to the Brawl, Strong praised Pen's French, noting he spoke it like Chateaubriand. Pen modestly claimed he had been learning since childhood, earning five minutes of silence from Strong before the latter erupted into uncontrollable laughter.

By dawn, the ball at Baymouth had ended. Madame Fribsby and Mirobolant departed in the Clavering carriage; Laura slept soundly at Lady Rockminster's; and the Claverings rested at the inn. Later, Blanche emerged from the refreshment room pale as a lemon, whispering to her maid about meeting a mysterious man who knew her murdered father, composing a sonnet to his memory before retiring.

Pen returned to Fairoaks with Strong, forgetting entirely the message he intended for Laura. When his mother, Helen, asked anxiously about the events at Baymouth, Pen dismissed them lightly as a calamity unsuitable for romance, promising to declare his love properly when Laura returned.

While waiting, Pen busied himself packing books, arranging papers, and polishing his gun, convinced that at twenty-three it was time to launch his career. He planned to rent chambers, enter an Inn of Court, and write plays and novels to fund his life until he established himself at the bar. Only then would he return to marry Laura, the "good and sweet-tempered" girl who would steady him. He talked to his dog, Ponto, building castles in the air, unaware that his future depended on timing far more than talent.

Two days later, Laura returned home, driven by Pynsent. Her presence

Arthur Pendennis's journey to London marked a decisive turning point. He had to leave behind the quiet woods and rocky shores of the West, the endless chatter of Clavering, and the dull, comfortable existence of Fairoaks. Now, aboard the *Alacrity* coach, he hurtled toward the capital, ready to confront the world and seize his fortune.

As the carriage thundered through the night, escaping the welcoming gates of home, Arthur's mind raced with schemes for the future. He dreamed of prudence, success, and fame. He knew, with growing confidence, that he was superior to many who had previously outrun him. His first failure had stung him deeply, sparking remorse and deep reflection, yet it had not shattered his spirit or his self-esteem. A hundred eager fantasies and restless hopes kept him wide awake.

How much wiser he felt now compared to twelve months prior, when he had traveled this same road between Oxford and Cambridge! His thoughts drifted with tender nostalgia to his mother. He recalled her blessing him as they parted, trusting and loving him despite his past errors. "Blessings on her!" he prayed, gazing upward at the stars. "O Heaven, grant me strength to work, to endure, to remain honest, to resist temptation, and to prove myself worthy of such a devoted soul!" Perhaps she was awake too, offering purer prayers for her beloved boy. Her love was his talisman, the shield against danger.

There was also Laura. He wished he could carry her affection with him, but she had rejected him, judging him unworthy. With shame and regret, he admitted that her nature was far nobler than his own. Yet, strangely, he felt glad to be free. "I am not good enough for such a creature," he confessed to himself. He drew back from her spotless beauty and innocence as one retreats from something terrifying. He felt unfit for such a partner, much like a reformed prodigy who avoids the church he once attended—not out of hatred, but because he feels he has no rightful claim to its purity.

These thoughts occupied him until the crisp dawn of an October morning. When the coach halted at the old breakfast stop at B——, where he had enjoyed dozens of hearty meals during his school and college travels, Arthur woke feeling remarkably refreshed.

As they departed, the sun burst forth brilliantly. The pace quickened, the horn blared, and the milestones flashed by. Pen smoked, joked with the guard and fellow passengers, and mingled with travelers on the familiar road. The scene grew busier and more vibrant with every mile. Finally, the last team of grey horses emerged from H——, and the coach roared into London.

Who among the young hasn't felt that electric thrill entering the vast beast of a city? Hundreds of carriages, packed with thousands of men, surged toward the metropolis. "Here is my place," Arthur thought. "Here begins my battle, where I must fight and conquer, or fall. I have been nothing but a boy and a dawdler thus far. Oh, I long, I long to prove I can be a man." From his perch on the roof, the eager youth gazed down upon the sprawling city with the intense longing a soldier feels on the eve of a campaign.

Along the way, Pen had struck up a conversation with a cheerful passenger in a shabby cloak. This man, a reporter for a London newspaper who had recently covered a major wrestling match in the West, spoke passionately about literature. He seemed to know every leading author of the day intimately, discussing figures like Tom Campbell, Tom Hood, and Sydney Smith as if they were his oldest friends. Passing Brompton, he pointed out Mr. Hurtle, the renowned reviewer, walking with his umbrella. Pen craned his neck to get a good look at the giant. "He's a Boniface man," Pen noted. Mr. Doolan, the reporter, confirmed it with a nod, adding that he knew Hurtle very well. To have seen the great Mr. Hurtle was, Pen believed, a true honor.

Still, Arthur held a naive faith in authors, critics, and editors. Even Wagg, whose books didn't strike him as intellectual masterpieces, he secretly revered as a successful writer. Mentioning that he'd met Wagg in the country, Doolan revealed that the famous novelist earned three hundred pounds per volume. Pen immediately began calculating whether he, too, might earn five thousand a year.

Upon arriving at the Gloster Coffee-house, Arthur's first encounter was with his old friend Harry Foker, who arrived prancing down Arlington Street behind a massive cab-horse. Foker wore white kid gloves and white reins, and nature had already gifted him with a significant tuft on his chin. A tiny cab-boy, a replacement for the retired Stoopid, hung precariously behind the vehicle, knock-kneed and clad in tight leather breeches.

Foker eyed the dusty coach and the sweating horses of the *Alacrity*, recalling his own past journeys. "What, Foker?" cried Pendennis. "Hullo! Pen, my boy!" shouted the other, waving his whip in a gesture of friendship. Arthur was delighted to see his eccentric friend's kind face. Mr. Doolan respected Pen immensely for knowing such a distinguished driver. Pen was thrilled to be free and in London. He invited Doolan to dinner at the Covent Garden Coffee-house, where he was staying, hailed a cab, and sped off in high spirits. He rejoiced to see the bustling waiters and the polite, bowing landlord. He asked for the landlady, missed seeing the old Boots, and wished he could shake hands with everyone. He had a hundred pounds in his pocket. Dressing in his finest attire, he dined modestly in the coffee-room with a single pint of sherry (determined to be frugal) before heading to the adjacent theater.

The lights, music, crowds, and general merriment enchanted Pen, as they often do for young men fresh from college and the countryside. He laughed at the jokes and cheered the songs, delighting some weary regulars in the boxes who had long lost their zest for the evening's entertainment and were happy to witness someone so fresh and amused. After the first act, he strutted through the lobby as if he belonged to the highest circles of fashion. Who among the tired frequenters of London's pavement doesn't recall having similar youthful delusions, wishing to relive them?

Young Foker appeared again, an ardent devotee of pleasure. Walking alongside Grandy Tiptoff of the Household Brigade, Lord Tiptoff's brother, and Lord Colchicum—a venerable peer who had been a man of pleasure since the French Revolution—they spotted Pen. Foker eagerly ushered him into his private box. Seated there was a lady with the longest ringlets and the fairest shoulders: Miss Blenkinsop, the eminent actress of high comedy. Snoozing in a wig in the back row was her father, the veteran Blenkinsop. Described in theatrical prints as "the useful Blenkinsop" and "that old favorite of the public," he played the role of the heavy father both on stage and in private life.

Meanwhile, back at Fairoaks, Mrs. Pendennis was asleep, wondering if her dear Arthur had rested well after his journey. In London, however, the farce was proceeding successfully. Mrs. Leary, dressed in a Hussar jacket and braided pantaloons, captivated the audience with her archness, lovely figure, and delightful ballads.

Being new to the town, Pen wanted to enjoy Mrs. Leary's performance, but the others in the box were too busy chatting. Tiptoff knew the origin of her maillots; Colchicum remembered seeing her debut in '14. Miss Blenkinsop claimed she sang out of tune, causing pain and astonishment to Pen, who thought her an angelic beauty singing like a nightingale. When Hoppus appeared as Sir Harcourt Featherby, the young hero of the play, the gentlemen declared him stale, and Tiptoff suggested throwing Miss Blenkinsop's bouquet to him.

"Not for the world!" cried the actress. "Lord Colchicum gave it to me."

Pen remembered the nobleman's name. Bowing and blushing, he thanked Lord Colchicum for proposing him to the Megatherium Club at the request of his uncle, Major Pendennis.

"What, you're Wigsby's nephew, are you?" asked the peer. "Forgive me, we always call him Wigsby." Pen blushed to hear his venerable uncle referred to so casually. "We balloted you last week, didn't we? Last Wednesday night. Your uncle wasn't there."

Delightful news! Pen expressed profound gratitude and delivered a handsome speech of thanks, which the peer listened to intently through his double opera-glass. Pen was electrified by the prospect of joining this polite club.

"Don't always stare at that box, you naughty creature," scolded Miss Blenkinsop.

"She's a devilish fine woman, that Mirabel," said Tiptoff, "though Mirabel was a damned fool to marry her."

"A stupid old spooney," agreed the peer.

"Mirabel!" exclaimed Pendennis.

"Ha! Ha!" laughed Harry Foker. "We've heard of her before, haven't we, Pen?"

It was Pen's first love: Miss Fotheringay. The previous year, she had been led to the altar by Sir Charles Mirabel, G.C.B., formerly envoy to the Court of Pumpernickel, who had played a key role in the negotiations before the Congress of

Colleges, schools, and Inns of Court cling stubbornly to the ghosts of antiquity, preserving customs that sensible moderns have long discarded. One might argue that a well-run workhouse offers better hygiene and comfort than these venerable institutions. Inside, scholars and lawyers are content to sleep in dusty closets, paying exorbitant rents for rooms that serve double duty as bedrooms and storage cupboards—spaces that cost more than a nice suburban villa or a decent townhouse. Meanwhile, a poor mechanic in Spitalfields commands an endless stream of fresh water from his tap. In contrast, the "gentlemen" of the universities and Inns rely on laundresses and chambermaids to lug water in jugs, inhabiting buildings constructed before the concept of cleanliness ever existed.

Some still sneer at the common folk, but let's be honest: your ancestors were likely the "Great Unwashed." Especially in the Temple, practicing even basic decency required immense effort. Take Old Grump of the Norfolk Circuit, who lived for decades beneath the chambers of Warrington and Pendennis. He was constantly woken by the roar of their shower baths, with water occasionally dripping through the ceiling onto his floor. Grump, now past fifty, declared the whole thing an absurd, dandified folly, cursing the laundress who tracked mud across his stairs. He'd managed perfectly well without running water, just as his fathers had. Yet, among the knights and baronets whose coats of armor line the Upper Temple halls, not one philanthropist devised a system of communal baths for their legal brethren. The Temple historian remains silent on such schemes. While Pump Court and Fountain Court boast hydraulic marvels, no bencher is ever seen splashing in the fountains. Still, one wonders how many a counsel of old might have refreshed himself at the pumps.

Despite their filth, these ancient Inns possess a unique charm, offering a rough-and-ready freedom that lingers in memory. Passersby often indulge in poetic reverie: *"Yonder Eldon lived; here Coke mused on Littleton; here Chitty toiled..."* The man of letters loves a place haunted by such giants. To me, Sir Roger de Coverley strolling the Temple Gardens feels as vivid as Samuel Johnson trudging through the fog toward Dr. Goldsmith's chambers, or Harry Fielding rushing to print midnight articles for *The Covent Garden Journal*.

Imagine a diary of a single day in one of those dingy four-story houses where Pen and Warrington lived. On the ground floor, a successful parliamentary counsel drives off to Belgravia at lunchtime, leaving his clerk—who recently couldn't even get credit for polishing his master's boots—to enjoy the fruits of his mentor's success. Just above, a venerable scholar lives alone, hoarding knowledge and wealth for fifty years, only to die a lonely recluse whose heirs burn half his library. Would you wish for such an end? Above him sits Mr. Grump, playing whist with three seniors after drinking three bottles of port, or sleeping soundly in the Temple Church on Sundays.

On the third landing, where Pen and Warrington reside late into the night, sits Mr. Paley. A college fellow, he reads and annotates cases until dawn, rising at seven to hit the chambers before breakfast. Unlike Pen, who returns from wild escapades, Paley works tirelessly. He hasn't wasted his time; he has forced a brilliant mind to grind against the dull reality of the law, shutting out poetry, philosophy, art, and love. He sees nothing but his books. When he turns off his lamp, he feels satisfied, thankless, and remorseless. Yet, he shudders when meeting Warrington on the stairs, avoiding the younger man as if he were damned. Perhaps it is Paley's cadaverous ambition and smug meanness that repels him, or perhaps it is simply Pen's innate love for joy.

Pen enjoys the Temple life immensely. Though his relatives think he is studying hard, retreating from the boredom of high-society balls, the truth is he is embracing the rough pleasures of a London bachelor. He walks in the Park instead of riding Rotten Row, pulls boats on the river, hikes to Hampstead, and eats modest tavern dinners. His evenings involve pipes, British spirits (always tasted by the laundress, Mrs. Flanagan), and quiet camaraderie. During term-time, he dutifully attends Hall dinners.

The Upper Temple Hall is a fascinating spectacle. With minor anachronisms, it feels like stepping back into the 17th century. Barristers and students eat separately; the Benches sit at a high table adorned with portraits of judges and royalty. Pen observes with amusement as his mess mates range from elderly attorneys to dandies in rowing shirts, colonial students, and Irishmen. The food is hearty—soup, meat, pies, and wine—but the clever lower-rank students employ various tricks to upgrade their meals.

"We're waiting," whispers Mr. Lowton, tugging Pen's gown. "If we wait, maybe the Benches will drop something."

Lowton, a gourmand with ambitions to be mess captain and control the final glass of port, knows exactly how to maneuver. He spots the Benches' table, where three elders stand before covered dishes while a clerk chants grace.

"This is boiled beef day, sir," Lowton tells Pen. "Boosey is there, bald and sipping soup. Is it turtle? Then comes Balls, KC, and Swettenham... and old Grump, who's dined here forty years!"

Lowton points out the elite guests: the Bishop of Ealing's son, Honorable Fred Ringwood, Lord Cinqbar's brother, and Bob Suckling.

"I like messing with them," Lowton explains, winking. "They don't actually eat. They go to fancy clubs. But if you mess with them, you get the bottle to yourself. No wine for them, so you drink it all."

"And why do you like them if they don't eat?" Pen asks.

"How green you are!" Lowton laughs. "It's about the wine, not the food."

As Lowton catalogs the Irish contingent—including the braggart Molloy Maloney and the fiery rebel Jack Finucane—he invites Pen to refill his glass. Pen declines the port, surprising Lowton, yet Lowton remains friendly.

Just as Pen settles in, a gentleman in a faded, oversized gown strides over the crowded tables to sit beside him. He wears a ragged shirt and old clothes, a stark contrast to the flashy pink attire of Molloy Maloney nearby.

"We ought to know each other," the stranger smiles. "We're both Boniface men. My name is Warrington."

"St—— Warrington?" Pen exclaims, delighted.

"Stunning Warrington," the man replies with a laugh. "I recall you from your freshman year. You thrashed that bargeman, Bill Simes, didn't you?"

"The college still talks about you," Pen admits, admiring the talent.

"Hush! Glad to meet you, Pendennis. Heard a lot about you."

Instantly, they bond over college memories. Seeing Warrington enjoy the port despite his earlier claims, Pen stops pretending to dislike it, much to Lowton's dismay. Afterward, Warrington suggests they skip dressing for the opera and instead smoke a quick pipe at his chambers in Lamb Court.

"You had better come home and smoke a pipe with me," Warrington urges. "We'll talk over Boniface and old times."

Lowton sighs as they leave, knowing Warrington is a baronet's son and looking up to the aristocracy with reverence. From that night, Pen and Warrington become sworn brothers. Warrington's cheerful nature, good sense, and constant pipe charm Pen far more than the stiff politeness of the exclusive Polytechnic Club. Soon, Pen abandons his expensive lodgings in St. James's, moving into Warrington's spare room in Lamb Court. It is cheaper, and conveniently, Pidgeon the page and Flanagan the laundress now divide their loyalty between the two friends.

Arthur Pendennis, buoyed by a sudden, intoxicating desire to experience "real" life, threw himself into the chaotic embrace of London's underworld. He fancied himself a grand explorer of human nature, wandering from one eccentric haunt to another. He delighted in observing the raw vitality of coal-heavers in their taverns, watching boxers lounge in inn parlors, and admiring honest citizens enjoying the river breeze. In his heart, he longed to share a pint with a celebrated pickpocket or raise a tankard with a band of burglars, provided fate granted him the chance.

He watched with amusement as Warrington listened intently to the ramblings of the "Tutbury Pet" or the "Brighton Stunner" at the Champion's Arms, and noted the genuine interest Warrington showed toward the rough crowd gathered at the Fox-under-the-Hill. Pen's knowledge of London's pubs and their patrons grew prodigious. He was the favorite guest of landlords and landladies alike, welcomed at the bar as if he belonged in a private club. He confessed a preference for this rough-and-tumble society over his own refined class, finding the manners of the upper crust annoying and their conversations tedious.

"In society," Pen would declare, "everyone dresses the same, eats the same, and says the same things. One young dandy at a club looks exactly like another; one debutante at a ball is indistinguishable from the rest. But *here*? There is character!" He preferred the company of England's strongest man, its finest beer-drinker, or that radical hatter who revered Thomas Hardy Thistlewood as history's greatest hero. "I'd rather drink gin-and-water than claret," he admitted, preferring the grit of a sanded floor in Carnaby Market to the polished marble of Mayfair. "I admit it: I prefer snobs." Indeed, Pen was a self-proclaimed social republican. Yet, while chatting with Jack and Tom, he never felt superior to them, though he likely savored the secret deference they paid him.

Pen followed Warrington to these dives with great enthusiasm. However, being considerably younger, Pen carried himself with a pompous stateliness that Warrington lacked. He was essentially a young prince in disguise, touring the provinces of his father's kingdom. To the locals, he appeared as a high-ranking chap, a fine fellow, a regular swell. He possessed an air of imperious good humor and royal frankness, despite being the heir-apparent to nothing more than twopence-halfpenny and descending from a lineage as humble as a gallypot. Humans are remarkably quick to accept roles assigned to them and eager to assert superiority over equals. Pen's condescension during this phase of his life was a spectacle worth witnessing. Such assumptions pass unnoticed in capable youths, but watching the conceit of a generous, clever lad is strangely touching—a display of early simplicity and folly.

After rigorous mornings spent devouring not just law, but also politics, history, and literature—deemed essential for a young man's education—they would apply themselves diligently to letters, reviews, legal texts, and newspapers until dinner approached. Then, with spirits high and appetites whetted, the duo would sallied forth to enjoy a merry night, following a pleasant afternoon. This was the glorious era of twenty-four, when every muscle of mind and body operated in healthy harmony, the world felt entirely new, and one moved forward spurred by good cheer and the delightful capacity to enjoy oneself. If we ever feel truly young later in life, it is thanks to the companions of those days; the tunes we hum in our twilight years are those we learned then. Occasionally, the festivities of that period resurface in memory, but how dingy the pleasure gardens seem now, how tattered the garlands, how sparse the company, and how many lights have gone out since then! Gray hair arrives like daylight, bringing headaches in its wake. Pleasure herself retires to bed with rouge still on her cheeks. So, friend, let us walk through the day sober and sad, yet friendly.

One wonders what Laura and Helen would have said if they had witnessed, as they might have occasionally, the early morning scenes in London. Imagine them seeing Mr. Pen and Mr. Warrington rattling over the echoing bridge flags toward the Temple after a wild night of carousing. These nights were wild, certainly, but not wicked, for Warrington despised women, and Pen, being too lofty for vulgarity, avoided coarse intrigues. Our Young Prince of Fairoaks could never address a member of the opposite sex without respectful courtesy, shrinking instinctively from anything crude. Though he had undoubtedly fallen in love with fools—as his superiors and inferiors had done—he always viewed the object of his affection as a goddess during the delusion, choosing to wait upon her. As the saying goes: "Men serve women kneeling; when they rise, they depart."

Such sentiments were voiced by an acquaintance of Pen's in his blunt, homely manner—an old friend named Mr. Bows of the Chatteris Theatre, now employed as a pianist accompanying the star attraction at the Fielding's Head in Covent Garden. Here was located the little club known as the Back Kitchen.

Numerous friends of Pen frequented this lively gathering spot. The Fielding's Head had been a house of entertainment almost since the time Henry Fielding presided as magistrate in nearby Bow Street; his seat was still occupied by the president of the evening's entertainment. Usually, this role fell to the worthy Cutts, the landlord, unless gout or illness prevented him. With his jolly appearance and fine voice, Cutts was a familiar figure to many male readers. He sang profusely during the harmonic meetings, performing songs of the "British Brandy-and-Water School"—tunes like "The Good Old English Gentleman" and "Dear Tom, this Brown Jug," blending pathos and hospitality with praise for good liquor and social bonds. Ballads of this school often celebrated women's charms and the heroic deeds of naval commanders. Cutts could work an audience to patriotic fervor, recounting how brave Abercrombie received his death wound, shedding liberal tears himself as he faltered through lines about autumn leaves proclaiming an old man's mortality. Suddenly, Cutts the singer transformed back into Cutts the landlord, and amidst the applause, he would shout, "Now, gentlemen, give your orders! John, a champagne cup for Mr. Green. Did you say sausages and mashed potatoes? John, attend to the gentleman."

"And I'll thank ye for a glass of punch too, John, and take care the water boils," a familiar voice would cry, causing Pen to blush and startle. This was the voice of the venerable Captain Costigan, now established in London and a pillar of the harmonic meetings at the Fielding's Head.

Captain Costigan's manners and conversation attracted many young men. He was a character whose fame spread rapidly after arriving in the metropolis, particularly following his daughter's marriage. He loved to talk endlessly about "my daughter," recounting her wedding, the carriages she kept, Mirabel's adoration for both her and him, and the hundreds of pounds he was entitled to withdraw from his son-in-law whenever needed. He would frequently propose borrowing a half-crown from his friend until the arrival of that mythical Saturday—the fourteenth—which he promised to repay with honor.

Sir Charles Mirabel did not possess the enthusiastic attachment his father-in-law claimed, though Costigan would sometimes tearfully inveigh against the ingratitude of his beloved child and the stinginess of the wealthy old man who married her. Nevertheless, the couple treated Costigan kindly, settling a small pension on him that was paid regularly, often pre-paid by the poor captain himself. The payment dates were well known to his friend at the Fielding's Head, where the honest Captain would appear with banknotes, loudly demanding change amidst the singing. "I think ye'll find that note won't be refused at the Bank of England, Cutts, my boy," Captain Costigan would declare. "Bows, have a glass? Ye needn't stint yourself tonight; a glass of punch will make ye play *con spirito*." For when money flowed his way, he was lavishly generous, rarely buttoning his breeches pocket unless the coins were gone or a creditor arrived.

It was during one such moment of exultation that Pen spotted his old friend swaggering at the singers' table, ordering brandy-and-water for anyone who appeared. Warrington, on confidential terms with the bass singer, made his way to this section of the room, and Pen followed close behind.

Pen started and blushed upon seeing Costigan. He had just returned from Lady Whiston's party, where he had encountered the Captain's daughter again for the first time in many years. He approached with an outstretched hand, greeting the old man warmly, still holding a strong memory of the time when Costigan's daughter meant everything to him. Although Pen was somewhat capricious in his attachments, transferring affections from one woman to another, he always respected the place where Love had once dwelt. Like the Sultan of Turkey, he desired honors be paid to the lady toward whom he had once thrown his royal pocket-handkerchief.

The tipsy Captain returned the handshake with all the strength of a palm weakened by years of lifting heavy pots of brandy-and-water. He stared hard at Pen, muttering, "Grecious Heavens, is it possible? Me dear boy, me dear fellow, me dear friend;" before breaking down with muddled curiosity, "I know your face, me dear dear friend, but, bedad, I've forgot your name." Five years of constant punch

In the middle of his modest celebrations, Pen suddenly felt a shadow loom over his fun. While his parties were humble and his spending moderate, his funds were running dangerously dry. His club subscriptions had already eaten up a third of his cash, and he'd just bought the essential furniture for his tiny bedroom. Now, staring at the final five-pound note in his wallet, he realized he couldn't afford to replace it. After all, he was raised like a royal heir—or perhaps like a crying infant fed by a devoted mother.

His friend Warrington, however, remained blissfully unaware of Pen's financial crisis. To Warrington, Pen looked like a man with a bottomless vault: an only child, a wealthy mother at her country estate, and a dandy uncle who dined daily with high society. Pen wore gold chains and carried a dressing case worthy of a duke. His lifestyle screamed aristocracy—not because he splurged wildly on specific luxuries, but because he refused to be penny-pinching. He wouldn't tip a waiter a penny, wouldn't walk in the rain unless absolutely necessary, and whenever he hailed a cab, he invariably tipped the driver double the fare. He despised clean gloves and frugal habits. If he'd been born to a ten-thousand-a-year inheritance, he likely would've been even more generous. Yet, despite his apparent wealth, he possessed a heart of gold; he couldn't resist helping beggars or pitiful children. It was a sumptuous nature, perhaps, unable to grasp the value of money, driven by natural kindness and a vanity that loved praise—even from waiters and cab drivers.

Warrington didn't know this, nor had Pen deemed it wise to share his true financial history. Everyone knew Pen had been wild at college, but no one suspected the depth of his debts or the severity of his mother's limited means.

The truth finally surfaced one evening as Pen grimly counted the change from his last five-pound note, lying on the pub tray beside Warrington's pot of ale.

"It is the last rose of summer," Pen sighed dramatically. "Its bloommates have long departed; behold, the final flower of the garland has shed its petals." He then confessed everything: his mother's dwindling resources, his own follies, and Laura's generous support. Warrington smoked his pipe, listening intently.

"Poverty will do you good," Warrington declared, tapping ash from his pipe. "For an honest man, nothing is more refreshing than a tight squeeze. For others, it's useless medicine. It acts as a tonic, keeping your moral spirit in a constant state of alertness. Think of a rider facing a fence or an opponent wielding a stick; necessity forces you to brace yourself. A little hardship reveals your grit and nerves you to fight fortune. You'll soon realize what you can live without: no need for new gloves, polished boots, cologne, or cabs. You've been spoiled by women, Pen. A healthy, brainy bachelor who can't find work doesn't belong in this world. Pay your last halfpenny, jump over Waterloo Bridge, steal a leg of mutton, get transported, and leave the country. *Dixi*—I have spoken. Pass the ale."

"But how does one survive?" Pen asked. "Beef and bread are plentiful in England, but they require coin or labor. Who will hire me, and what work can I do?"

Warrington burst into laughter. "Advertise in the *Times*!" he suggested. "Seek an usher's position at a classical academy: 'Gentleman, B.A. of St. Boniface College, plucked for his degree.' Offer lessons in classics, math, and basic French. Mention you can cut hair, supervise younger students, and play second chair piano for the principal's daughters. Address: A.P., Lamb Court, Temple."

"Stop it," Pen groaned.

"Men adapt to any profession," Warrington continued. "Your friend Bloundell is a professional rogue traveling Europe, fleeceing young noblemen. Bob O'Toole, my schoolmate, now drives the Ballynafad mail, carrying honest Jack Finucane's letters. I know a doctor's son who argued with his boss over finances, ran out of money, grew his mustache, moved to a provincial town, claimed to be 'Professor Spineto, Chiropodist to the Russian Emperor,' cured the local newspaper editor, and practiced happily for three years. He's back home now, inheriting his father's medical supplies."

"Hang medical supplies!" Pen cried. "I can't drive coaches, trim feet, or cheat at cards. What else do you suggest?"

"There's always writing," Warrington said. "Before you told me your troubles, I assumed you were rich based on your airs. Since you can't drain your mother further, you must settle with Laura—Laura, here's to you!—and carry bricks instead of asking for shillings."

"How do you earn a living?" Pen asked.

"On my brother's allowance," Warrington admitted, his face falling slightly. "I squandered that five years ago. If I hadn't killed myself sooner, maybe it would have been better. I've been playing my own game ever since. I don't need much. When my purse empties, I work to refill it, then lounge like a serpent until I'm satisfied. Look," he held up a thin leather pouch containing a few coins. "I feel empty again."

"I write," Warrington whispered, blushing. "Not publicly, of course. I don't want people saying George Warrington writes for bread. But I contribute to law reviews and newspapers. Here," he handed Pen a file of the *Dawn*. "Read these."

Pen scanned the pages, delighted. He recognized the sharp wit, concise sentences, satire, and scholarship immediately.

"I'm no match for that," Pen admitted admiringly. "I know little of politics or history, and my Latin is shaky. I can't soar on wings like yours."

"But you have your own flight, lighter and perhaps higher," Warrington encouraged gently. "Those little verses I've seen show a rare talent. Don't blush, you conceited young jackanape. You've had the poetic spark for ten years. Our oil lamps are dim compared to your inner fire. You're a poet, Pen."

Touched, tears welled in Pen's eyes. "How kind you are, Warrington!"

"I like you, old chap," Warrington replied warmly. "Chambers were lonely; your honest face cheered me. Remember how you laughed at Lowton? Anyway, I needed someone to keep me company."

So absorbed in his own joy, Pen missed the melancholy glint in Warrington's eyes. "Thank you for your friendship and your words," Pen said. "I've always suspected I was a poet. Will I be one? Do you mean the *Ariadne in Naxos* I wrote at eighteen, or the Prize Poem?"

Warrington roared with laughter. "Young goose! Of all my failures, *Ariadne* is the most sickening nonsense. The Prize Poem is so pompous and weak I'm amazed it didn't win. You don't imagine you're a serious poet trying to rival Milton, do you? Setting yourself up as a Pindar? No, my boy, you can write magazine pieces and charming verses. That's all."

"By Jove!" Pen stamped his foot. "I'll prove you wrong!"

Warrington just chuckled and blew twenty-four rapid puffs from his pipe.

Soon, an opportunity arose. Mr. Bacon, the eminent publisher of Paternoster Row, released annually a lavish volume called the *Spring Annual*, edited by Lady Violet Lebas. It featured poems from the elite—young lords like Lord Dodo and Percy Popjoy, alongside illustrations of beauties. Because the engravings were created months in advance, poets had to write specifically to match the images.

One day, while visiting Mr. Hack, Bacon's manager, Warrington discovered a disaster. Percy Popjoy had written dreadful verses for a picture titled *The Church Porch*: a Spanish maiden rushing to church while a cloaked youth watched her from a niche. The image was lovely, but the poem was atrocious.

"It won't do," Mr. Hack lamented. "Bungay is releasing a superior collection. We can't lose the plate; the Governor paid sixty pounds for it. Lady Violet admits it's terrible. What's the solution?"

"I know someone," Warrington said. "Let me take the plate home. Send for the verses tomorrow. You'll pay generously, of course."

Agreed, Warrington returned home with the plate. "Here's your chance, boy. Write some verses for this."

"What is it?" Pen asked, confused. "A church porch? A lady entering, a lecher looking from a wine shop? What am I supposed to do?"

"Try," Warrington urged. "Earn your living, you who dream of doing so."

"All right," Pen agreed.

Warrington left for dinner. When he returned late, Pen was ready. "Done," he said proudly. "Squeezed them out. I think they'll work."

After reading, Warrington approved. The poem, *The Church Porch*, depicted the scene with elegant restraint:

> Although I enter not, Yet round about the spot
> Sometimes I hover, And at the sacred gate,
> With longing eyes

Captain Shandon, our incarcerated hero, delivered a prospectus with the crisp authority of a general addressing his troops. He proclaimed that the hour had finally arrived for the gentlemen of England to unite in defense of their "glorious order," which faced threats from every quarter: foreign revolutions, domestic radicalism, the cunning slander of mill-owners, and the foolish antagonism of the masses they had duped.

"The ancient monarchy was insulted by a rabble of ferocious republicans!" the Captain declaimed. "The Church was abandoned by jealous dissenters and eroded by stealthy infidels. Our noble institutions, the bedrock of our glory, and the title 'English Gentleman'—the proudest in the world—were left undefended, exposed to assault from men who worshipped nothing holy, knew no venerable history, and obeyed no law stronger than their leader's command to plunder."

Mr. Shandon paused for effect, then turned to Mr. Bungay, the publisher. "Consider the French kings," he noted wisely. "Saint Louis fell because his monarch distrusted his gentlemen. England survived its greatest foe precisely because the people trusted theirs. It was our gentlemen who drove the eagles back from the Don to the Garonne; it was a gentleman who shattered the lines at Trafalgar and swept the plains of Waterloo."

At the mention of Waterloo, Bungay nodded sagely, winking knowingly. Warrington, however, burst into laughter.

"Observe how our venerable friend Bungay is affected," Shandon whispered to Warrington, glancing up from his papers with a sly smile. "That is the true test. I've invoked the Duke of Wellington a hundred times, and the Duke never fails."

The Captain then confessed, with admirable candor, that until now, English gentlemen, confident in their rights and indifferent to critics, had delegated their political interests just as they managed their estates or legal affairs—to specialists. They had allowed their representation in the press to fall into the hands of professional proctors. "But that era is over," Shandon declared. "Gentlemen must become their own champions. Their enemies are brave, strong, numerous, and uncompromising. They must meet them in the field, not be defamed by hired scribblers in Grub Street publishing gazettes from Whitehall."

"That's a dig at Bacon's people, Mr. Bungay," Shandon added, turning to the publisher.

Bungay slammed his stick onto the floor. "Hang him! Pitch into him, Captain!" he shouted triumphantly. Turning to Warrington, he wagged his dull head vigorously. "For a slashing article, sir, there is no one like the Captain—nobody like him."

Shandon continued, revealing that certain gentlemen, whose names were withheld for "obvious reasons," intended to launch a new journal. "These men are proud of their order and eager to uphold it!" cried the Captain, waving his paper with a grin. "They are loyal to their Sovereign by faith and ancestry; they love their Church, where they wish their children to worship; they love their country and intend to preserve it as the gentlemen of England have made it: the greatest and freest in the world. As the names of some of them appear on the deed securing our liberties at Runnymede—"

"What's that?" asked Mr. Bungay, leaning forward.

"An ancestor of mine sealed it with his sword-hilt," Pen interjected gravely.

"It's the Habeas Corpus, Mr. Bungay," Warrington corrected. The publisher merely yawned, muttered "All right, I dare say," and urged, "Go on, Captain."

"At Runnymede," Shandon resumed, "they were ready to defend that freedom today with sword and pen, rallying around the old laws and liberties of England."

"Bravo!" cheered Warrington. Little Mary stood watching in wonder; her mother worked silently nearby, casting looks of fond admiration. "Come here, little Mary," Warrington said, patting her fair curls with his large hand. She shrank back from his rough affection, preferring to seek refuge at Pen's knee to play with his fine watch-chain. Pen was delighted; he was a soft-hearted soul, though he hid his gentleness beneath a shy, pompous exterior. Thus, she climbed onto his lap while her father continued reading the program.

"You were laughing," the Captain observed to Warrington, "about the 'obvious reasons.' Well, I'll show you what they are, ye unbelieving heathen. 'We have stated,' he continued, "'that we cannot reveal the names of the parties involved... We possess influential friends in both Houses of Parliament and allies in every diplomatic circle in Europe. Our sources of intelligence are so exclusive that no other London or European journal could possibly acquire them. Yet, we can assure you that the earliest news of English and Continental politics will be found exclusively in the columns of *The Pall Mall Gazette*."

He listed the intended subscribers: *The Statesman*, *The Capitalist*, *The Country Gentleman*, and *The Divine*. "Why should the Gentlemen of England be unrepresented in the Press?" he asked rhetorically. "The field-preacher has his journal, the radical free-thinker has his; why not the Gentlemen?"

With modesty bordering on arrogance, Shandon detailed the literary and fashionable departments of the new paper, promising they would be run by men of acknowledged reputation—men famous at the universities (which caused Pen to blush and chuckle involuntarily), known in the clubs, and belonging to the very society they described. He politely informed potential advertisers that *The Pall Mall Gazette* was not suitable for selling goods, then eloquenty called upon the nobility, the baronetage, the clergy, the bar, the matrons, and the daughters of England to rally to the good old cause. By the end, Bungay had woken from a second nap, nodded approvingly, and declared everything was perfect.

Once the reading concluded, the group discussed the paper's management. Bungay sat listening, nodding as if understanding complex matters, though his approval was based on simplicity. He believed the Captain could write the finest "smashing article" in England. To him, anything that destroyed the opposition house of Bacon qualified as smashing. Whether the Captain wrote a letter in the style of Junius or copied the Church Catechism, Bungay would be satisfied. Pocketing the manuscripts with immense satisfaction, he paid for them, called little Mary over, and gave her a penny before departing.

Afterward, Shandon, sporting a jaunty, fashionable air, entertained his guests with anecdotes of the "beau monde." Though he knew little of the great world, he had seen enough to mimic it convincingly. He spoke of current characters and great figures with easy familiarity, recounting private lives and social gatherings as if he lived among them daily. Pen found it amusing to hear the shabby prisoner in a tattered dressing gown discuss the elite so fluently. Mrs. Shandon listened with delight, believing every word. She didn't aspire to mix with high society herself, nor was she clever enough, but she cherished the idea that her Charles shone there. In fact, she still kept an invitation card from the Earl of X in her workbox.

Soon, Mr. Bungay grew tired of the chatter and rose to leave. Warrington and Pen followed, though Bungay lingered, hoping to revisit this interesting family. Shandon grinned ruefully, assuring him he was always at home and delighted to see Mr. Pennington.

"I'll see you to my park gate, gentlemen," Captain Shandon announced, grabbing his hat despite Mrs. Shandon's faint cry of "Charles." Leading the way through the dismal prison passages, he shuffled out in his shabby slippers, his hand already fiddling with his waistcoat pocket where Bungay's five-pound note rested. At the wicket, he bid farewell to the three gentlemen, including Pen, who breathed a sigh of relief to finally step onto the flags of Farringdon Street.

Left alone, Mrs. Shandon sadly resumed her work by the window overlooking the courtyard. She watched Shandon sprint toward the prison tavern with two men trailing behind. She had hoped to share a dinner of meat and salad with her husband and daughter that evening, but now there was no hope. He would spend the night drinking or playing cards elsewhere, returning late with glazed eyes and a staggered walk requiring her nursing. *Oh, what varieties of pain we inflict upon our women!* she thought.

She prepared tea instead. How many tears have flowed over that humble pot? How many sickbeds have warmed beside it? From lovers whispering secrets to widows finding solace, the tea-pot has witnessed it all. Finally, Mrs. Shandon and little Mary sat down to drink their tea together while the Captain went forth to seek his pleasures.

Just then, Mr. Jack Finucane, a townsman and frequent benefactor of the Shandon household, arrived. He carried a sweetmeat for little Mary and viewed Shandon as the greatest genius alive, a view supported by the occasional guinea or helpful word from the generous prodigal. Jack was ready to run errands, handle finances, and manage the myriad debts of an embarrassed Irish gentleman—a role filled by a whole hierarchy of indebted dependents marching behind their chieftain.

"He won't hold onto that five-pound note long," Bungay predicted as he walked away with Warrington and Pen. The publisher proved correct: when Mrs. Shandon emptied her husband's pockets later, she found only a couple of

Captain Shandon, usually a man of action rather than administration, found himself unexpectedly thrust into the role of editor-in-chief by his wife's persistent nagging. Reluctantly agreeing to the arrangement, he secured for John Finucane, Esquire—a lawyer from the Upper Temple with a penchant for dramatic loyalty—the position of sub-editor at the newly launched *Pall Mall Gazette*. It was a fitting reward; Finucane had long been Shandon's sanctuary, a place where the Captain hid from bailiffs and debt collectors. Though the Sheriff's men eventually learned the secret location of this refuge, turning Finucane's staircase into a regular checkpoint, the bond remained unbroken.

Finucane's chambers became a haven for Mrs. Shandon. Often veiled and trembling, she would descend the stairs to recount her woes, seeking strategies to liberate her "adored" husband. Finucane treated her visits as a high honor, leaning over the balustrade to ensure no rogue "Temple Lovelaces" intercepted her on her way home. In fact, he secretly hoped a villain might attack her, allowing him to rush forward and break the intruder's bones—a fantasy born of genuine affection. He even attended to their child, little Mary, tucking her into her crib in his cramped quarters. While Mrs. Shandon prayed for her husband's safety, Finucane, despite being a Roman Catholic, would fervently add "Amen" to her petitions, his heart swelling with sympathy.

With the editorial structure settled, Finucane departed at six o'clock to meet Mr. Bungay, the publisher, for a strategic dinner. Dressed in his finest (if somewhat garish) attire and carrying four pounds and two shillings—his weekly salary minus the cost of new gloves—he headed to Dick's Coffee House. There, alongside Mr. Trotter, Bungay's business manager, Finucane laid out a master plan for the *Gazette*. With dazzling speed, he outlined the staffing needs: who should cover the markets, the turf, church news, and society gossip. He claimed intimate knowledge of every journalist in London and possessed a genius for saving capital through "ingenious economic contrivances." His calculations left Bungay bewildered yet impressed, leading the publisher to admit privately that the Irishman was remarkably clever.

Seizing the moment, Finucane pivoted to his true objective: freeing Captain Shandon. He detailed the Captain's current debts and argued passionately that confinement hindered productivity. He painted a heartbreaking picture of Mrs. Shandon wasting away and the child falling ill in the Fleet Prison. Moved by this appeal, Bungay agreed to consider the matter the following morning. The meeting concluded with rounds of brandy-and-water, though Bungay insisted on paying the bill himself, leaving Finucane with his full salary intact.

That evening, Finucane arranged a special outing for Mrs. Shandon and Mary. When the Captain expressed concern about his headache, urging them to stay, Mrs. Shandon gently insisted they go. "Go along with you, and Polly," the Captain relented with good humor, handing over his medical texts to Finucane before retreating to his "abominable devices"—his writing.

As the trio walked from the prison gates toward Paternoster Row, they encountered Mrs. Bungay. Dressed in a flamboyant shot-silk gown of red and purple, complete with a yellow shawl and a parasol, she looked every bit the wealthy matron compared to Mrs. Shandon's worn black silk. Despite the contrast, the two women exchanged polite curtseys. Mrs. Bungay, feigning interest in the child, offered to watch Mary while Mrs. Shandon met with her husband. However, the sight of the frightened little girl clinging to her mother softened the stern housewife. Seeing the opportunity, Mrs. Shandon kindly offered to accompany them upstairs, where a second batch of biscuits quickly won Mary's trust.

In the drawing room, the dynamic shifted entirely. While Finucane faced a cold reception downstairs—Mrs. Bungay having lectured her husband earlier on the futility of aiding a "good-for-nothing" Irishman—the charm of the mother and child proved irresistible. Mrs. Shandon spoke with an artless sweetness, detailing her husband's virtues and the child's frailty. Mrs. Bungay, though initially grim, began to melt, recalling her own daughter, Bessy, who bore a striking resemblance to little Mary.

When the couple returned to the drawing room, Mrs. Bungay revealed her change of heart. "The Captain is an Irishman," she explained, "and I've always disliked them. But his wife is a lady... and look at that little girl!" Reminded of her past sorrows, Mrs. Bungay's resolve crumbled. She instructed her husband to assist Mrs. Shandon.

Mr. Bungay, though clumsy in his delivery, announced that he would advance sixty-five pounds to clear the Captain's remaining debt, deducting the sum from future salaries. On the condition that Mrs. Shandon personally negotiated with the creditors, the deal was struck.

The celebration was immediate and raucous. "Bedad, Bungay, you're a trump!" shouted Finucane, tossing Mary into the air and jumping with joy. The ladies, accompanied by the delighted Finucane on the back seat of a one-horse carriage, drove triumphantly away from Paternoster Row. Back at his desk, Bungay reflected wistfully on the transaction, noting that while helping the Captain wasn't strictly wise financially, it soothed his wife's conscience, especially given the recent loss of his own daughter.

True to form, Mrs. Shandon proved herself a skilled negotiator. Armed with the advance payment, she bribed the creditors with ten shillings in the pound, securing their release. By the following Sunday, Captain Shandon was finally free, marking the end of his imprisonment for the foreseeable future.

On the appointed day, our two friends, Pendennis and Warrington, presented themselves at the front door of Mr. Bungay's establishment in Paternoster Row. They did not enter through the public portal, where bookshop lads usually lumbered in with sacks overflowing with Bungay's latest volumes, surrounded by nervous aspiring authors clutching their virgin manuscripts, hoping for a sale to the Sultan himself. Instead, they sought the private entrance, the exclusive exit used by the magnificent Mrs. Bungay. From this door, she would emerge to mount her chaise, settle luxuriously onto the cushions, and cast defiant glares toward the windows of Mrs. Bacon's house directly opposite.

Mrs. Bacon, currently a woman without a carriage, was often seething with envy at her sister-in-law's splendor. On such occasions, she would throw open the sash of her drawing-room window and stare out at Mrs. Bungay's chaise with her four children clinging to her skirts. Her message was clear: *"Behold these four darlings! Flora Bungay! This is precisely why I cannot afford a carriage; you would give a coach-and-four just to have the excuse!"* With these verbal arrows, Emma Bacon relentlessly pelted Flora Bungay, who sat in her chariot, envying the motherhood she lacked.

As Pen and Warrington approached Bungay's door, a carriage and a cab pulled up to Mrs. Bacon's residence. Old Dr. Slocum descended heavily from the carriage; his vehicle was as ponderous as his writing style, yet both possessed a fine, booming resonance among the publishers of the Row. Two dazzling white waistcoats emerged from the cab.

Warrington chuckled. "See? Bacon is throwing a dinner party too. That's Dr. Slocum, author of *'Memoirs of the Poisoners.'* You'd hardly recognize our friend Hoolan in that gallant white waistcoat. Doolan is one of Bungay's men, and look, there he comes." Indeed, Messrs. Hoolan and Doolan had arrived together from the Strand in the same cab, tossing a coin to decide who would pay the shilling fare. Mr. Doolan stepped out the other side, dressed in black, sporting a large pair of white gloves that he spread wide on his hands, regarding them with evident delight.

At Mr. Bungay's house, porters in evening coats and gentlemen wearing gloves as oversized as Doolan's—but crafted from the famous Berlin web—were bustling about to collect hats and coats, shouting names up the stairs. Some guests had already arrived, but inside the drawing-room, only Mrs. Bungay stood alone, radiant in red satin and a turban, representing the charms of her own sex. She curtsied to each newcomer as they entered, though her mind was clearly elsewhere. The reality was that Mrs. Bacon's dinner party was tormenting her. As soon as she had ushered in each member of her own circle, Mrs. Bungay would retreat to the window embrasure to scan the carriages rolling up the Row. The sight of Dr. Slocum's massive carriage, drawn by gaunt job-horses, nearly crushed her spirit; nothing but hack cabs had dared approach her door that day.

The gathering consisted entirely of literary gentlemen, though Pen knew them only by reputation. There was Mr. Bole, the actual editor of the magazine (with Mr. Wagg serving as the nominal chief); Mr. Trotter, who had once burst onto the world as a tragic, suicidal poet before retreating to one of Bungay's back shops as a reader; and Captain Sumph, an ex-beau and distant relative of Literature and the Peerage. He claimed to have authored a book, to have been a friend of Lord Byron, and to be connected to Lord Sumphington. In fact, anecdotes of Byron formed his entire vocabulary. He rarely spoke without invoking the ghost of that poet or his contemporaries: *"I recall poor Shelley, sent up for good behavior at school for a copy of verses, every line of which I wrote, by Jove!"* or, *"I remember, when I was at Missolonghi with Byron, offering to bet gamba..."* Pen observed that Captain Sumph was listened to with rapt attention by Mrs. Bungay. His tales of the aristocracy, of which he considered himself a middle-aged member, delighted the publisher's lady. If he had merely arrived in his own carriage, Mrs. Bungay would have ordered Bungay to print anything he wrote.

Mr. Bungay circulated among his guests, performing the duties of hospitality with excessive cordiality. "How are you, sir? Fine day, sir. Delighted to see you, sir. Flora, my love, allow me to introduce Mr. Warrington. Mr. Warrington, Mrs. Bungay; Mr. Pendennis, Mrs. Bungay. I hope you've brought good appetites, gentlemen. You, Doolan, I know you have, for you've always had a deuce of a twist."

"Lor, Bungay!" exclaimed Mrs. Bungay.

"Faith, a man must be hard to please, Bungay, who can't enjoy a good dinner in this house," Doolan declared, winking and stroking his lean cheeks with his oversized gloves. He launched affectionate appeals toward Mrs. Bungay, which the honest woman rejected with scorn. "She couldn't stand that Doolan," she confided to her friends. Indeed, all his flattery failed to charm her.

While chatting, Mrs. Bungay surveyed the street from her window when a magnificent vision appeared: an enormous grey cab-horse galloping rapidly. Behind it, a pair of white reins held by small white gloves fluttered; a pale face, richly adorned with a chin-tuft, and the bobbing head of a diminutive groom were revealed. To the delighted Mrs. Bungay, this was perfection. "The Honourable Percy Popjoy is punctual, I declare!" she cried, rushing to the door to await the nobleman's arrival.

"It's Percy Popjoy," Pen confirmed, watching an individual in extremely polished boots descend from the swinging cab. It was indeed Lord Falconet's eldest son, the publisher's patron, come to dine with his benefactor.

"He was my fag at Eton," Warrington noted. "I suppose I should have licked him a little more." He and Pen had previously sparred at the Oxford Union debates, where Pen had largely dominated Percy, who now appeared with his hat tucked under his arm. His face displayed an indescribable blend of good humor and fatuity; Nature had erupted with a chin-tuft but, exhausted by the effort, had left the rest of his countenance smooth-shaven.

The chamberlain bellowed, "The Honourable Percy Popjoy," causing the young lord some discomfiture at hearing his title announced so loudly.

"What did the man want to take my hat?" Percy complained to Bungay. "I can't do without it—to make my bow to Mrs. Bungay. How well you look today, Mrs. Bungay! Haven't seen your carriage in the Park; why weren't you there? I missed you, truly."

"I'm afraid you're a sad quiz," Mrs. Bungay replied dryly.

"Quiz! Never made a joke in my—" Suddenly, he spotted someone else. "Hullo! Who's here? How d'ye do, Pendennis? How d'ye do, Warrington? These are old friends of mine, Mrs. Bungay. Say, how on earth did you manage to get here?" he asked the two young men, turning his lacquered heels away from Mrs. Bungay, who suddenly realized that respecting her husband's guests meant acknowledging their connection to a lord's son.

"Do they know him?" she asked Mr. Bole urgently.

"High fellers, I tell you—the younger one is related to all the nobility," Bungay assured her. Both young men smiled and bowed, greeting figures almost as significant as the young lord himself: no less than the great Mr. Wenham and the great Mr. Wagg, who were now announced.

Mr. Wenham entered with his customary demure expression and stealthy smile, usually reserved for inspecting the tips of his neat, shining boots, rarely directed at anyone addressing him. Conversely, Wagg's white waistcoat spread with profuse brilliance; his burly, red face shone resplendent above it, illuminated by thoughts of good jokes and a hearty meal. He loved to make his entrance with laughter and depart leaving a joke exploding behind him. No personal misfortunes could dampen his spirits; whatever his griefs, the prospect of dinner rallied his great soul. Upon seeing a lord, he immediately greeted him with a pun.

Wenham approached Mrs. Bungay with a smug smile and a whisper, examining her from beneath his eyelids while showing off his shoe tips. Wagg complimented her on her charm and marched straight to the young nobleman, whom he dubbed "Pop," instantly recounting a funny story seasoned with what the French call *gros sel*. He was delighted to see Pen, shaking his hand and slapping him on the back cordially. Full of spirits, he spoke loudly of their past meetings at Baymouth, inquired about their friends at Clavering Park, and asked if Sir Francis was visiting London for the season, and whether Pen had visited Lady Rockminster, who had recently arrived—a fine old lady, Lady Rockminster! These remarks were made less for Pen's benefit than for the edification of the company,

The new journal, *The Pall Mall Gazette*, enjoyed a burst of initial success. Rumors swirled that an influential political party stood behind it, bolstered by a roster of "great names" contributing to its pages. Were these whispers true? We cannot officially confirm or deny them. However, we can reveal a few secrets: a scathing article on foreign policy, widely attributed to a prominent Noble Lord known for his ties to the Foreign Office, was actually penned by Captain Shandon while sitting in the parlour of the Bear and Staff public house near Whitehall Stairs—a spot he'd reached after being tracked down by a printer's boy, where his literary associate, Mr. Bludyer, was temporarily staying. Furthermore, a string of financial analyses universally believed to be the work of a towering statesman in the House of Commons were, in fact, the brainchild of Mr. George Warrington of the Upper Temple.

There may well have been secret dealings between the *Gazette* and this powerful faction. Evidence suggests Percy Popjoy—who, thanks to his father Lord Falconet, belonged to the party—was frequently spotted climbing the stairs to Warrington's chambers. Indeed, the paper printed information so peculiar it could only originate from strange sources. Several poems, weak in substance but loud and vigorous in tone, appeared signed "P.P."; and it must be admitted, his novel was reviewed in the new journal with outrageously enthusiastic praise.

Mr. Pen, however, played no role in the political department. Instead, he became a tireless literary contributor. The *Gazette*'s offices were located in Catherine Street, Strand, and here Pen often arrived, bursting with manuscripts tucked in his pockets. He moved with a bustling energy and genuine delight, typical of anyone embarking on a literary career. To see oneself in print remains a novel sensation, and one naturally delights in imagining that one's words are already causing a stir in the world.

Here, Mr. Jack Finucane, the sub-editor, busied himself with paste and scissors, compiling the journal under his supervision. With an eagle eye, he scanned every paragraph of every newspaper dealing with the fashionable world he oversaw.
