Not a death or a dinner party among the aristocracy escaped his notice; he ensured every event found a column in his publication. From obscure provincial prints and distant Scottish and Irish broadsheets, he dredged up astonishing gossip and intelligence regarding the upper classes. It was a truly moving sight for a philosopher to witness Jack Finucane, Esquire, eating a plate of meat from the cookshop and drinking porter from a local pub, yet recounting the feasts of the great as if he had personally attended them. Dressed in tattered trousers and dirty shirt sleeves, he cheerfully described and arranged the world's most brilliant social fetes. The sheer incongruity of Finucane's job, combined with his demeanor and appearance, greatly amused his new friend, Pen. Having left his native village—where his family rank was hardly lofty—Jack had rarely encountered society outside the tavern parlours he frequented. Yet, based on his writing, one would assume he dined with ambassadors and lounged in the bow-windows of White's Club.

Errors of description occasionally slipped from his pen, but the *Ballinafad Sentinel*, of which he was the sole correspondent, bore the brunt of these mistakes. The London chiefs of the *Gazette* preferred his skill with scissors and paste over his ability to hold a pen, allowing him little space to write directly for them.

Pen, meanwhile, devoted immense effort to his reviews. Possessing a decent amount of desultory reading acquired in his youth, along with an eager imagination and a sharp sense of humor, his articles delighted both his editor and the public. He took pride in earning his keep. We can be certain the *Pall Mall Gazette* was eagerly consumed at Fairoaks, read with joy by the two ladies there. It also made its way to Clavering Park, where a young lady of refined literary tastes resided. Even old Doctor Portman, to whom the widow sent the paper after memorizing her son's articles, approved of Pen's work, declaring the lad possessed spirit, taste, and fancy. He noted that while Pen didn't write like a scholar, he certainly wrote like a gentleman.

What was the sheer astonishment and delight of Major Pendennis when he walked into the Regent Club, finding Wenham, Lord Falconet, and other distinguished gentlemen gathered around a copy of the *Gazette*. They were discussing an article that savagely mocked a recent book published by the wife of a celebrated opposition figure. The book in question was *Travels in Spain and Italy* by the Countess of Muffborough. It was difficult to determine whether the French or English portions were worse; her ladyship wrote indifferently in both, and the critic pounced on her blunders with delightful malice. This critic was none other than Pen. He danced around his subject with boundless jocularity and high spirits, exposing the noble lady's flaws with admirable mock gravity and decorum. Every word remained polite and gentlemanly, yet the unfortunate subject was thoroughly scarified and laughed at throughout the process.

Wenham's bilious face puckered with malignant pleasure as he read the critique. Lady Muffborough hadn't invited him to her parties in the last year. Lord Falconet giggled and laughed heartily; he and Lord Muffborough had been rivals since childhood. These lords complimented Major Pendennis, who had previously ignored hints from his Fairoaks correspondent warning of "Arthur's constant and severe literary occupations," fearing they might undermine the poor boy's health. The Major had considered any mention of Mr. Pen and his newspaper connections beneath his dignity as a Major and a gentleman. But when the oracle Wenham praised the boy's production, when Lord Falconet (who had heard the news from Percy Popjoy) approved of young Pen's genius, and when the great Lord Steyne himself laughed, sniggered, declared the piece capital, and predicted the Muffborough would writhe under it like a whale under a harpoon, the Major felt compelled to change his tune.

As duty demanded, he began to admire his nephew immensely. "By gad," he exclaimed, "the young rascal has some stuff in him, and he'll do something! He always said he would!" Trembling with pleasure, the old gentleman sat down to write to the widow at Fairoaks detailing all the praise from the great figures, and he also wrote to the young rascal himself, asking when he would visit to eat a chop with his old uncle. He added that he was commissioned to take him to dinner at Gaunt House, noting that Lord Steyne appreciated anyone who could entertain him, whether through folly, wit, or even dullness.

Pen tossed the letter across the table to Warrington, perhaps expecting a reaction that didn't materialize. The courage of young critics is prodigious; they climb onto the judgment seat and deliver opinions on the most intricate works with barely a pause. If Macaulay's *History* or Herschel's *Astronomy* had been placed before Pen at this stage, he would have glanced through the volumes, pondered his verdict over a cigar, and offered an august approval as if he were their superior patron. With access to the *Biographie Universelle* or the British Museum, he could summarize historical periods rapidly, citing names, dates, and facts with such mastery that his mother at home would wonder where he had acquired such a prodigious library. When he revisited his articles two or three months later, having forgotten the subjects and books consulted, he would appear to possess a memory far sharper than reality warranted.

At twenty-four, Mr. Pen wouldn't hesitate, given twenty-four hours' notice, to judge the greatest scholars or render a verdict on the *Encyclopædia*. Fortunately, he had Warrington to curb his impertinence with constant, wholesome ridicule. Without this check, he might have become intolerably conceited. Captain Shandon, who admired the dash and flippancy of his young aide-de-camp, preferred Pen's light, brilliant flashes over the heavier arguments of his elder coadjutor. Though blameable for impertinence and premature judgment, Pen was a perfectly honest critic—too candid for Mr. Bungay's liking, who grumbled sadly at his impartiality.

One day, Pen and his captain had a disagreement over this very point.

"In the name of common sense, Mr. Pendennis," Shandon asked, "what have you been doing—praising one of Mr. Bacon's books? Bungay was in a fury this morning upon seeing a laudatory article on one of the works of that odious firm."

Pen's eyes widened in astonishment. "Do you mean to say," he asked, "that we are to praise no books Bacon publishes? Or that, if they are good, we must call them bad?"

"My good young friend—for what do you suppose a benevolent publisher undertakes a critical journal, to benefit his rival?" Shandin inquired.

"To benefit himself certainly, but to tell the truth too," Pen replied. "*Ruat coelum*, to tell the truth."

"And my prospectus," Shandon said, laughing and sneering. "Do you consider that a work of mathematical accuracy?"

"Pardon me, that is not the question," Pen countered. "I don't think you care to argue it. I had qualms of conscience about that prospectus and debated it with Warrington. We agreed, however," he added, laughing, "that because the prospectus was declamatory and poetic, and the giant painted on the showboard larger than

Let us kindly skip ahead a few months in the chronicle of Mr. Arthur Pendennis's life. During this interval, countless events surely transpired that thrilled him deeply yet would bore the average reader of these memoirs to tears. Last time we saw him, he had officially embarked on his career as a professional writer—or, as his pal Mr. Warrington prefers to call it, "literary hack." Now, anyone who knows the grind of a hack, whether legal, literary, clerical, military, or commercial, understands that existence is defined by a monotonous routine and a tedious lack of drama. One day's labor mirrors the next with uncanny precision. A writer often toils for his crust of bread against the clock, his own will, his failing health, his sheer laziness, or his revulsion toward the subject matter, much like any other daily drudge. When you need to monetize Pegasus (assuming you possess no other marketable assets), forget poetry and soaring flights. Modern Pegasus ascends only like Mr. Green's balloon: at pre-advertised intervals, after the spectators have parted with their cash. He trots in harness across cobblestones, pulling a cart or cab, often panting with trembling knees and frequently receiving a sharp cut from his whip-wielding driver.

However, let us not be overly lavish with our pity for Pegasus. There is no justification for exempting this creature from labor, sickness, or decay any more than any other creation of God's earth. If he receives a lash, Pegasus often deserves it. I, for one, stand ready to defend my friend George Warrington against the sentimental notion held by poetic sympathizers: that letters and genius are immune from the prosaic duties of daily life, tax-paying, and earning a living alongside one's neighbors.

Thus, with the *Pall Mall Gazette* firmly established and Arthur Pendennis recognized as a flippant, witty, and amusing critic, he threw himself into his work every week. He prepared reviews of whatever fell into his department, writing with assured flippancy, yet with honesty and to the best of his ability. Occasionally, a historian of seventy years, who had spent twenty-five years crafting a tome that our young gentleman devoured in two days at the British Museum, felt unfairly treated by such a facile reviewer. Or perhaps a poet, laboring over sublime sonnets deemed fit for immortality, was annoyed by two dozen snappy lines in Mr. Pen's critique, wherein the critic acted as a stern judge and the author a trembling defendant. The theater actors complained bitterly, too, and likely deserved it. Yet, little harm was ultimately done. Times have changed since then; but in Pen's era, there were so few truly great historians, poets, or actors that almost none appeared before his critical desk. Those who received a slight beating generally found it beneficial; not that the judge was inherently wiser than those he sentenced, nor did he ever imagine himself superior. Pen possessed a robust sense of humor and justice, lacking the arrogance to overvalue his own opinions. Furthermore, he had Warrington at his side—a terrifying critic if Pen grew conceited, and far more savage toward Pen than toward anyone else he tried at his literary assizes.

Through these critical labors and occasional contributions to leading articles—where, without offending his paper, this eminent publicist could speak his conscience—Mr. Arthur Pendennis earned four pounds and four shillings weekly, accompanied by significant effort. Additionally, he supplied magazines and reviews with original articles and is rumored to have served as the London correspondent for the *Chatteris Champion*, which at the time featured brilliant metropolitan correspondence. These endeavors enabled the fortunate youth to generate an income nearing four hundred pounds annually. On the second Christmas following his arrival in London, he actually delivered a hundred pounds to his mother as repayment on the debt he owed to Laura. Mrs. Pendennis read every word of her son's writings, deeming him the profoundest thinker and most elegant writer of the day. She viewed his return of the hundred pounds as an act of angelic virtue, feared he was sacrificing his health, and rejoiced whenever he mentioned the society he encountered or the great figures of letters and fashion he met. Any reader acquainted with maternal son-worship can imagine the scene: the simple, charming love with which country women track their darlings' careers in London. Whether John secured a brief, Tom attended a specific ball, or George dined with a celebrity, it brings immense joy to mothers and sisters in Somersetshire. Young Hopeful's letters become sacred texts, fueling village gossip and friendly congratulations. In the second winter, Pen visited briefly, cheering the widow's heart and brightening the lonely house at Fairoaks. Helen had her son entirely to herself; Laura was visiting old Lady Rockminster; the guests of Clavering Park were away; and the few remaining old friends, led by Dr. Portman, treated Mr. Pen with marked respect. Between mother and son, there was pure fondness, confidence, and affection. It was the happiest fortnight of the widow's life, perhaps of both theirs. The holiday vanished too swiftly, and Pen returned to the bustling world, leaving the gentle widow alone once more. She forwarded Arthur's money to Laura; I cannot determine why this young lady chose to leave home precisely when Pen arrived, nor whether he felt more piqued or relieved by her absence.

By this time, through his own merits and his uncle's introductions, Arthur was well-integrated into London, known in both literary and polite circles. His fashionable reputation proved invaluable; he was regarded as a gentleman of decent current means and promising future prospects, a trait considered the ultimate recommendation for a young literary aspirant. Bacon, Bungay & Co. proudly accepted his articles; Mr. Wenham invited him to dinner; Mr. Wagg regarded him favorably. They recounted how they met him at fashionable houses, where he was warmly welcomed regardless of his financial standing, thanks to his impeccable appearance, address, and reputation as a clever fellow. Eventually, he was invited to one house simply because he had been spotted at another. Thus, the young man experienced a vast array of London life, becoming familiar with everyone from Paternoster Row to Pimlico, feeling equally at home at Mayfair dining tables as at the tavern boards where his scribbling companions gathered.

Brimming with high spirits and curiosity, easily adapting to everyone he met, the young fellow delighted in this strange jumble of humanity, making himself welcome or at least comfortable wherever he went. For instance, he might breakfast at Mr. Plover's in the morning alongside a Peer, a Bishop, a parliamentary orator, two fashionable ladies, a popular preacher, the author of the latest novel, and the newest celebrity imported from Egypt or America. Then, he would depart this distinguished assembly for the back room of the newspaper office, where pens, ink, and wet proofs awaited. Here sat Finucane, the sub-editor, with fresh news from the Row; soon Shandon would arrive, nodding to Pen before beginning his leading article at the other end of the table, flanked by a pint of sherry that the serving boy would silently deliver upon spotting the Captain. Or Mr. Bludyer's roaring voice would echo from the front room, where that truculent critic would seize books from the counter despite the timid protests of Mr. Midge, the publisher. After inspecting the volumes, he would sell them at his stall, consume the proceeds in a tavern box, and then demand ink and paper to "smash" the author of his dinner and the novel. As evening approached, Mr. Pen would stroll toward his club to pick up Warrington for a constitutional walk. This exercise cleared the lungs and stimulated an appetite for dinner, after which Pen enjoyed the privilege of paying calls at various pleasant houses or exploring the town for amusement. There was the Opera, the Eagle Tavern, a ball in Mayfair, a quiet night with a cigar and a book and a long chat with Warrington, or a new sensation at the Back Kitchen. At this stage of his life, Mr. Pen witnessed all manner of places and men, likely unaware of the depth of his enjoyment until much later, when balls ceased to please him, farces failed to make him laugh, tavern jokes produced no excitement, and the loveliest dancer never stirred him from his chair after dinner. At his present mature age, all these pleasures are past; the times have moved on. It has been only a very short while since Bludyer bullied authors or cheated landlords, since Shandon slept his final sleep, and since honest Doolan was buried. Never again will he cringe or flatter, nor pull a long bow or drain a whisky-noggin.

The London season was now in full bloom, with fashionable newspapers teeming with details of grand banquets, routs, and balls enlivening the polite world. Our gracious Sovereign held levees and drawing rooms at St. James's; the bow-windows of the clubs were packed with the heads of respectable, red-faced, newspaper-reading gentlemen; thousands of carriages trailed along the Serpentine; squadrons of dandy horsemen trampled Rotten Row. Everyone was in town, and naturally, Major Arthur Pendennis, who was someone, was not absent.

On a certain morning, with his head wrapped in a smart bandana handkerchief and his meager frame enveloped in a brilliant Turkish dressing gown, the worthy gentleman sat by his fireside, letting his feet gently simmer in a bath while sipping his early tea and perusing the *Morning Post*. He could not face the day without his two-hour toilette, his early tea, and his *Morning Post*. I suspect no one in the world, not even his

Better folk than Morgan, the valet, were not so well instructed as that gentleman regarding the true extent of Lady Clavering's riches. Upon her arrival in London, the city buzzed with a legend of her fortune that bordered on the mythical. Whispers circulated of indigo factories, opium clippers, and banks swollen with rupees. Stories swirled of diamonds and jewels belonging to native princes, alongside vast sums of interest paid by those very princes for loans contracted by their ancestors to Lady Clavering's father. Her account at the London banker was said to contain so many zeros that they created a corresponding number of "O"s of admiration in the minds of the listening crowd.

It was a known fact that an envoy from an Indian Prince, Colonel Altamont—the Nawab of Lucknow's favorite, an extraordinary figure rumored to have embraced Islam and survived a thousand wild adventures—was currently in England attempting to negotiate the sale of the Nawab's famous nose-ring diamond, *'The Light of the Dewan.'* Under the title of the Begum, Lady Clavering's fame rippled through London before she even descended upon the capital. Just as legal scholars like Delolme and Blackstone boasted that the British Constitution admits merit of every kind into its aristocracy, allowing the lowliest-born to wear the robes of a peer if they deserve it, so too did our good society operate on a similar, albeit more mercenary, principle. Though proud and jealous of its privileges, high society removes all barriers instantly for the wealthy. If an individual possesses enough gold, they are welcomed with open arms, invited to dinners, dances, and marriages, treated with the same affability one would show a common citizen.

Having overseen the arrangements for the country mansion, our friend the Chevalier Strong applied his taste to the fashionable London upholsterers preparing the town house for the Claverings. In decorating this elegant abode, honest Strong's soul rejoiced as if he were the proprietor himself. He hung and re-hung pictures, studied sofa placements, and interviewed wine merchants and purveyors. Simultaneously, the Baronet's factotum utilized the opportunity to furnish his own chambers and stock his cozy cellar. Friends complimented the neatness of Strong's quarters, and select guests sharing his cutlets discovered bottles of excellent claret to accompany their meals. The Chevalier was, as he put it, "in clover," residing comfortably in Shepherd's Inn. He was attended by a former Spanish Legionary and comrade, whom he had rescued from a breach at a Spanish fort and elevated to the rank of body-servant for both himself and his roommate. This loyal retainer was none other than the favorite of the Nawab of Lucknow, the valiant Colonel Altamont.

Ned Strong was a man of few curiosities and immense discretion; he avoided inquiring into the mysterious connection established shortly after their meeting at Baymouth between Sir Francis Clavering and the Nawab's envoy. The Colonel clearly held some secret leverage over Clavering, forcing the Baronet to submit. Strong, aware of Clavering's irregular past and lackluster military career in India, suspected the Colonel possessed a hold on Sir Francis that compelled obedience. In truth, Strong had long viewed Sir Francis as a man utterly weak in purpose, principle, and intellect—a moral and physical trifler.

After their initial meeting at Baymouth, Strong witnessed several private interviews between His Excellency and Sir Francis, the nature of which the Baronet refused to disclose. However, correspondence flowed via Altamont, who served as Clavering's ambassador in all matters. On one occasion, the Nawab's envoy was in a foul mood, crushing Clavering's letter in his hand and declaring with characteristic emphasis: "A hundred, be hanged! I'll have no more letters nor no more shilly-shally. Tell Clavering I'll have a thousand, or by Jove I'll split and burst him all to atoms. Let him give me a thousand, and I'll go abroad. I give you my honor as a gentleman I won't ask him for more for a year. Deliver this message, Strong, my boy. Tell him if the money isn't here next Friday at twelve o'clock, as sure as my name is what it is, I'll publish a paragraph in the newspaper on Saturday, and next week I'll blow up the whole concern."

Strong returned these dire words to his principal, whose reaction was immediate. On the appointed day and hour, the Chevalier appeared once more at Altamont's hotel at Baymouth with the required sum. Altamont, behaving as a true gentleman, settled his bill and departed on a foreign tour, embarking at Dover. Strong concluded it must be forgery that placed Clavering in the Colonel's power, noting that the Colonel likely held the original bill.

However, within the year, the Colonel returned. A disastrous run on the red had ruined him at Baden-Baden; no gentleman could withstand a color rising fourteen times in succession. Obligated to draw upon Sir Francis Clavering for funds to return home, Clavering—despite pressing financial needs for elections, his country estate, and the furnishing of his London home—found means to accept the Colonel's bill, though reluctantly. Overheard by Strong, Sir Francis cursed the heavens, wishing the Colonel were locked in a German debtor's prison for life. These subsidies were raised secretly from Clavering's wife, who, despite her sumptuous spending habits, inherited a sharp business acumen from her father, Snell. She granted her husband only a modest allowance, demanding precise accounts for any extra expenditures. Regarding the Colonel's demands, Clavering frankly told Strong he could not discuss it with his wife.

Part of Strong's duties involved procuring these funds. Consequently, in the Chevalier's apartments at Shepherd's Inn, numerous negotiations occurred between moneyed gentlemen and Sir Francis Clavering, resulting in the exchange of valuable banknotes and stamped paper. For a man accustomed to trading promises for cash since youth, prosperity rarely brings permanent relief; the money-lender inevitably returns. Finding it more convenient to meet creditors at Strong's lodgings than his own, Clavering relied heavily on the Chevalier. Although Strong possessed no personal wealth, his name appeared as the drawer on nearly all the bills of exchange Clavering accepted. Strong would discount these bills in the City, then parley with holders when they matured, securing installments or fresh acceptances in exchange for time. Much like the troops at Comorn, who maintained cheerfulness amidst the threat of assault and execution, hundreds of gallant spirits in this town dined in gaiety and slept comfortably, burdened only by the proximity of a bailiff and the noose of debt. Ned Strong, the old soldier, bore these trifling inconveniences with remarkable ease.

But we must leave these inhabitants of Shepherd's Inn to focus on Lady Clavering and her friends, who had been waiting at the doorstep of Grosvenor Place. They entered a gorgeous dining room, furnished in a middle-aged style that puzzled Lady Clavering until she laughed, remarking, "Unless... because me and Clavering are middle-aged people!" They were offered the copious remnants of the luncheon she and Blanche had just enjoyed.

Our little Sylphide, who barely touched food at dinner compared to the six grains of rice eaten by Amina, the friend of the Ghouls in the Arabian Nights, proved surprisingly active with her knife and fork, consuming a substantial portion of mutton cutlets. In this display of hypocrisy, she was believed to resemble other young ladies of fashion. Pen and his uncle declined the refection but admired the room with fitting compliments, pronouncing it "very chaste"—the proper term for such decor. Indeed, there were high-backed Dutch chairs of the seventeenth century, a sculptured carved buffet of the sixteenth, a sideboard looted from a Low Country church, and a large brass cathedral lamp over a round oak table. Old family portraits from Wardour Street, French tapestries, bits of armor, double-handed swords and battle-axes made of carton-pierre, looking-glasses, saintly statuettes, and Dresden china filled the space. Nothing, in short, could be chaster.

Behind lay the library, fitted with busts and books of uniform size, wonderful easy-chairs, and solemn bronzes in the severe classic style. Here, guarded by double doors, Sir Francis smoked cigars, read *Bell's Life in London*, and fell asleep after dinner, unless occupied at the billiard table or gambling houses in St. James's. Yet, what equaled the chaste splendor of the drawing-rooms? The carpets were so magnificently fluffy that footsteps made no sound louder than shadows. Upon their white grounds bloomed roses and tulips as large as warming-pans. High and low chairs, including bandy-legged specimens so attenuated that only a sylph could sit upon them, surrounded marquetry tables covered in marvelous gimcracks. There were china ornaments of all eras, bronzes, gilt daggers, *Books of Beauty*, yataghans, Turkish papooshes, and boxes of Parisian bonbons. Everywhere one sat, Dresden shepherds and shepherdesses stood ready at the elbow. Porcelain poodles, ducks, cocks, and hens populated the shelves, alongside nymphs by Boucher and shepherdesses by Greuze—truly chaste subjects. Muslin and brocade curtains framed gilt cages housing parrots and lovebirds, two squealing cockatoos that out-chattered one another, a clock singing tunes on a console table, and another booming the hours like Great Tom on the mantelpiece.

On the appointed day, Major Pendennis—who had made no special plans—and Arthur, who desired none, arrived together to dine with Sir Francis Clavering. Upon entering the drawing-room, they found only Sir Francis, his wife, and Captain Strong. Arthur was genuinely delighted to see Strong, though the Major received him with a distinctly sour countenance. He was far from pleased to sit down to dinner with Clavering's "house-steward," as he irreverently dubbed Strong. However, the atmosphere shifted slightly with the arrival of Mr. Welbore Welbore, Clavering's country neighbor and fellow MP. Though Welbore was perfectly dull and contributed less to the conversation than the footman trailing behind his chair, he was a respectable gentleman of ancient lineage and seven thousand pounds a year. Such solid, unpretentious society always put the Major at ease.

The party swelled further with the addition of the Dowager Lady Rockminster, who had her own reasons for favoring the Claverings, and Lady Agnes Foker accompanied by her son, Mr. Harry. Mr. Pynsent could not attend due to parliamentary duties, which weighed heavily on the two other senators present. Last to arrive was Miss Blanche Amory. She was dressed in a dazzling white silk gown that showcased her pearly shoulders to perfection. Foker whispered to Pen that she was "a stunner." Indeed, Blanche treated Arthur with exceptional grace on this particular evening, shaking his hand warmly, asking after Fairoaks, Laura, and his mother, and expressing a longing return to the country. She was entirely simple, affectionate, and artless. Harry Foker, rarely finding much to say in the presence of ladies, found himself unusually loquacious beside her. He would have loved to escort her down the stairs, but fate decreed that Pen should accompany her, leaving Harry to walk Mrs. Welbore Welbore, a task assigned to him due to his status as an earl's grandson.

Despite being separated during the descent, Harry managed to secure a seat next to Miss Amory at the dinner table, convinced he had executed a brilliant maneuver. Whether it was his doing or someone else's, the result was the same: Blanche sat between the two young men, each striving to be gallant. From her vantage point, Foker's mother marveled at her son's sudden vivacity. Harry chatted incessantly about current events, ordering soup for himself and humming the famous air from *La Sylphide*.

"Seen Taglioni in the *Sylphide*, Miss Amory?" he asked. "Bring me that *soupe* de volaille again, if you please... Can't think where the chicken legs go, I wonder? She's clipping in the *Sylphide*, isn't she?" He began to hum the melody of that most graceful of all dances.

"Will the young folks ever see anything so charming, so classic, anything like Taglioni?" he continued.

"Miss Amory is a sylph herself," remarked Mr. Pen.

"What a delightful tenor voice you have, Mr. Foker," said the young lady. "I'm sure you've been well taught. I sing a little myself. I'd love to sing with you."

Pen recalled that similar sentiments had once been directed toward him, and that Blanche had enjoyed singing duets with him in the past. Sneering inwardly, he wondered how many other gentlemen she had shared her vocal talents with since his time, but deemed it inappropriate to ask aloud. Instead, adopting the tenderest possible tone, he said, "I should like to hear you sing again, Miss Blanche. I never heard a voice I liked so well as yours, I think."

"I thought you liked Laura's," Blanche replied.

"Laura's is a contralto; that voice is very often out, you know," Pen said, with a hint of bitterness. "I've heard plenty of music in London. I'm tired of those professionals—they sing too loud—or perhaps I've grown too old or too blasé. One ages quickly in London, Miss Amory. Like all old fellows, I only care for the songs of my youth."

"I prefer English music. Foreign songs aren't really my thing. Get me some saddle of mutton," interrupted Mr. Foker.

"I adore English ballads above all else," agreed Miss Amory. "Sing me one of the old songs after dinner, will you?" requested Pen, imploringly.

"Shall I sing you an English song after dinner?" asked the *Sylphide*, turning to Mr. Foker. "I'll do it if you promise to come up soon," she added, flashing a gaze that might have been described as a broadside.

"I'll come up after dinner, fast enough," he replied simply. "I don't mind much wine afterward—I take my share at dinner, you know. Once I've had enough, I toddle up to tea. I'm a domestic character, Miss Amory; my habits are simple. And when I'm pleased, I'm generally in a good humor, aren't I, Pen? That jelly, please—not that one, the other with the cherries inside. How on earth do they get those cherries inside the jelly?"

In this fashion, the artless youth prattled on, and Miss Amory listened with inexhaustible patience. When the ladies retired to the upper rooms, Blanche secured promises from both young men to leave the table shortly before departing with kind glances cast at each. She dropped her gloves on Foker's side of the table and her handkerchief on Pen's. Her politeness toward Foker seemed marginally more encouraging than her kindness to Arthur, yet the benevolent little creature did her utmost to ensure both gentlemen were content. As she rushed out the door, Foker caught her final glance—a bright look that swept over Captain Strong's broad white waistcoat and landed squarely on Harry Foker. The door closed on the charmer; he sighed deeply and swallowed a bumper of claret.

Since this was not one of the grand seasonal parties, dinner had been served early. With the company small and Miss Blanche eager to reach her piano, Lady Clavering signaled her departure promptly. It was still daylight when the ladies ascended to the upper apartments, where flower-embroidered balconies offered views of the two parks, poor couples strolling in one, and the equipages of dandies passing through the other. The sun had not yet set behind the elms of Kensington Gardens, still gilding the statue of the Duke of Wellington, when the ladies left the gentlemen to their wine.

Windows were thrown open to admit the fresh air, offering passersby a tantalizing glimpse of six gentlemen in white waistcoats surrounded by decanters and fruit. Little boys jumping the railings shouted, "Hi, Jim, wouldn't you like to be there and have a slice of that pineapple?" Meanwhile, noble carriages conveyed their occupants to Belgravia, while a policeman with heavy boots patrolled the front. Evening shadows lengthened, the gasman lit the lamps before Sir Francis's door, and the butler illuminated the antique Gothic chandelier over the carved oak table. Thus, from the street, the house appeared as a nocturnal feast of candles and wine; from within, it was a vision of a calm summer evening overlooking St. James's Park, where a few stars began to twinkle.

Jeames, leaning casually against the doorpost, gazed outward at the tranquil park scene. Nearby, a spectator clinging to the railings observed the interior. Policeman X, passing by, ignored both, fixing his attention instead on the figure standing by the railings and peering into the dining room. This individual was gorgeously attired in chains, jewelry, and a waistcoat that gleamed under the house lights. His boots were polished to a shine, his coat adorned with brass buttons, and large white wristbands covered his knuckles. He looked so imposing that Policeman X initially mistook him for a Member of Parliament or a person of significant standing.

However, whatever his rank, this "person of consideration" was clearly intoxicated. He lurched and reeled, his hat cocked precariously over wild, bloodshot eyes. His black hair appeared suspiciously artificial, and his whiskers dyed a deep Tyrian purple. As Captain Strong's laughter rang out from the window following one of his own crude jokes, the man on the pavement laughed and sniggered in a bizarre manner, slapping his thigh and winking at Jeames as if to say, "Plush, my boy, wasn't that a good story?"

Jeames, distracted from the moon by this sublunary spectacle, felt a growing unease. "A holtercation," he muttered later in the servants' hall. "A disturbance with a feller in the streets is never no good; and indeed, he wasn't hired for any such purpose." After observing the man, who continued to laugh, reel, and nod with tipsy confidence, Jeames stepped out of the portico and softly called, "Policeman," beckoning Officer X.

X marched up, resolute, with one Berlin glove tucked into his belt. Jeames pointed silently with his index finger at the laughing figure by the railings. Saying nothing else, he stood there in the calm summer evening, pointing calmly—a grand sight.

"Now, sir, will you have the kindness to move on?" asked X.

The individual, in perfect spirits, seemed deaf to the officer, merely nodding and wagging his grinning head at Strong until his

Since that fateful, yet delightfully chaotic night in Grosvenor Place, the heart of Mr. Harry Foker had undergone a transformation so profound that one might struggle to reconcile it with the reputation of a man previously regarded as a philosopher of the highest order. Recall, if you will, the sagacious counsel Harry had once dispensed to Arthur Pendennis; recall the early maturity that marked this gifted youth. By all rights, a constant regimen of self-indulgence, befitting a gentleman of his station and prospects, should have accelerated his cynicism, rendering him increasingly indifferent to the human race—with the singular exception, naturally, of Mr. Harry Foker himself. Yet, against all logic and precedent, this "wise" man fell prey to the universal misfortune that strikes everyone once or twice in a lifetime: he fell in love. Like Achilles, Ajax, Lord Nelson, or Adam himself, Harry could not escape the common lot. Now that his hour had arrived, the All-Conqueror named Love claimed its due.

That evening, upon returning to the Back Kitchen after bidding farewell to Arthur Pendennis at the staircase door in Lamb Court, the usual delights held no sway. The gin-twist and deviled turkey tasted like ash; the jests of his companions fell on deaf ears. Even when Mr. Hodgen, the chronicler of 'The Body Snatcher,' launched into a new ballad even more terrifying and hilarious than his famous composition, Foker offered the requisite polite applause—"Bravo, Hodgen!"—bound by social obligation and his status as a club leader, yet he failed to comprehend a single syllable of the lyrics. Exhausted and weary, he retreated to his private quarters, seeking refuge in a downy pillow. But sleep proved elusive. The moment he awoke from his feverish dreams, the visage of Miss Amory materialized, declaring, *"Here I am, your princess and beauty. You have found me, and henceforth, nothing else shall matter."*

Suddenly, his former pursuits and friendships seemed dreadfully stale. Until this moment, Harry had rarely mingled with women of his own social standing. When discussing them, he referred to them as "modest women"—a term implying a virtue that, heaven forbid, they possessed, yet which failed to compensate for the lack of livelier qualities found among his relatives. These relatives included his mother, who was good but not amusing; his cousins, the daughters of the Earl of Rosherville, who bored him to tears. One was a blue-stocking geologist; another rode horses and smoked cigars; a third held heterodox religious views, while her sister turned her room into a High Church chapel and fasted Fridays. Their ancestral home, Drummington, was a place Harry preferred the treadmill to. To the household staff, he was unpopular. Lord Erith deemed him vulgar; Harry, in turn, labeled Erith a dull prig and the Speaker's shame. The memory of Harry poking his uncle in the side with a billiard cue and calling him "old cock" remained a sore point, causing the Earl to avoid him like the plague, though he dared not sever ties entirely due to financial entanglements involving bank cheques and IOUs signed in ink.

Furthermore, there was the matter of Lady Ann Milton, the fifth daughter of the Earl of Gravesend. From infancy, she had been earmarked for Harry. The arrangement was as natural to her as it was to Harry, who, despite being the dirtiest little boy with black eyes from school, readily accepted the decree. His father, the old gentleman, had explained the bargain clearly: *"She has no money, but she is good blood and looks fine. If you refuse, you'll have your mother's jointure and two hundred a year during my life."* Harry, trusting his sire implicitly, had acquiesced instantly, noting merely, *"She's not a bad-looking girl."* Consequently, Lady Ann led a sheltered life, rarely venturing out, her sole narrative being her engagement to Harry. It was a story devoid of drama, much like the rest of her existence.

However, the instant Harry awoke the morning following Lady Clavering's dinner, the reality of his situation crashed down. Blanche's image loomed large, her grey eyes piercing, her smile winning. Her tune echoed in his mind: *"Yet round about the spot, ofttimes I hover..."* He hummed it pitifully from his bed, surrounded by crimson silk. On the walls, French prints depicted Turkish ladies, Greek lovers, and opera nymphs in tight pantaloons, alongside autographed sketches of Miss Calverley and Miss Rougemont. These were the treasures of honest Harry, a jovial fast man about town richer than most. Beside him lay a letter from Miss Calverley, written in a loose, misspelled scrawl, addressing him as "dear Hokey-pokey-fokey," along with invitations to various sporting events and a reminder of a promised dinner at the Elephant and Castle.

Now, all these pleasures seemed faint and repulsive. Who cared for Conkey Sam or the Worcestershire Nobber? The French prints stared vacuously; Calverley's spelling was atrocious. The prospect of dining with a thirty-seven-year-old spinster filled him with disgust.

His mother, Lady Agnes, noticed the change immediately. "Why do you frequent that wicked Spratt's?" she asked, concerned over his pallor. "Those billiards will kill you."
"It isn't the billiards," Harry muttered gloomily.
"Then it's the Back Kitchen," she suggested, offering to intervene with the landlady regarding the strength of the negus.
"Do, ma'am. Mrs. Cutts is kind," Harry replied, adding with a ghastly sigh, "But it isn't the Back Kitchen, neither."

Lady Agnes, ever obliging, accepted his confession wholeheartedly. She spun tales of his adventures to her friends, painting Spratt's as a place of high culture where gentlemen played billiards as ancestors played hazard, and the Back Kitchen as a hub of literary enlightenment where Dr. Johnson and Mr. Sheridan once drank wine. Meanwhile, Harry practiced his diplomatic skills, praising the Claverings' furniture and Sir Francis's wit, carefully selecting a date in his mother's pocket-book for an invitation to Grosvenor Street.

Upon drafting the card, Harry spotted a critical error. "You haven't asked Miss Whatdyecallem—Miss Emery, Lady Clavering's daughter."
"Oh, that little creature!" Lady Agnes dismissed.
"We *must* ask Miss Amory," Harry insisted. "Pendennis is very sweet on her. Does she sing well, ma'am?"
"I thought her rather forward... but I will ask her if you wish," Lady Agnes conceded. Thus, Miss Amory's name was added to the list.

Triumphantly embracing his mother, Harry retired to his apartment. Dressed in a magnificent dressing gown and puffing his pipe in silence, he lay on his ottoman, brooding over the upcoming introduction. His polyglot valet, Anatole, watched with silent curiosity. When Harry emerged from his room, he was notoriously difficult to please, cursing his trousers, boots, and shirts. Finally, he summoned Anatole.
"I say, Anatole, when I engaged you, didn't you say you could dress hair?"
"Yes, sir," came the reply.
*"Cherchy alors une paire de tongs,—et—curly moi un peu,"* Harry commanded casually.
Anatole, confused by his master's sudden coquettishness, fetched tongs from the elderly butler and borrowed hair curlers from Lady Agnes. Within moments, Harry's head was transformed into a nest of curls resembling a negro's.

Dressed to the splendor, Harry prepared to leave.
"At what time shall I order the drag, sir, to Miss Calverley's door?" Anatole whispered.
"Confound her! Put the dinner off—I can't go!" Harry snapped. Then, reconsidering, "No, hang it—I must go. Poyntz and Rougemont are coming. The drag at Pelham Corner at six."

The hired drag was not his own; he commandeered his personal carriage instead. For what purpose? To drive down to Lamb Court, Temple, passing Grosvenor Place en route. There, he peered upward at Miss Amory's pink curtains, satisfied, before speeding to Pen's chambers. Why such urgency? Why this sudden longing for a friend he had barely visited in two years?

To any young lady reading this, let it be known: when this specific mishap befalls you, the world flips upside down. Those you ignored yesterday become fascinating; those you adored become insipid. Letters sent to distant cousins vanish; suddenly, you crave the company of his mother, his father, his sisters. You will send frantic notes to his unmarried sister, crying if prevented from visiting. You will despise his younger brother for intruding. You will undergo the hot and cold fits of this pretty fever. Mothers have walked this path before you; fathers are usually the object of such passions. Brothers, more selfish and headstrong, rush toward their destiny when the charmer appears.

So, why was Henry Foker, Esquire, in such a hurry to see Arthur Pendennis? Because Pen had suddenly become invaluable. Perhaps Pen wasn't the rose, but he had stood near that fragrant flower of love. Did Pen not walk her streets? Know her secrets? What would Lady Ann Milton, Harry's intended cousin, have said if she knew the true state of affairs in the bosom of this funny

Mr. Strong's business in life was largely to procure funds, but for Mr. Foker, the business of the evening was to endure a social disaster. The dinner at Richmond proved to be the most dreary entertainment on which any mortal man had ever squandered a guinea.

"I wonder how the deuce I could ever have liked these people," Foker muttered to himself, staring blankly at the table. "Why, I can clearly see the crow's-feet crinkling under Rougemont's eyes, and the paint on her cheeks is applied as thick as a clown's in a pantomime! And that Calverley—his slang is absolutely disgusting. I hate chaff in a woman. And look at old Colchicum! That ancient peer arriving in his brougham, wearing his coronet, and squeezing himself bodily between Mademoiselle Coralie and her mother! It's too bad. An English peer and a horse-rider of Franconi's! It simply won't do. By Jove, it won't do. I'm not proud, but it *won't* do!"

"Twopence-halfpenny for your thoughts, Fokey!" cried Miss Rougemont, extracting a cigar from her truly vermilion lips as she spotted the young man lost in contemplation at the head of the table. Around him lay melting ices, sliced pineapples, half-empty bottles, and cigar ashes scattered over the wreckage of a dessert that held no pleasure for him.

"Does Foker ever think?" drawled Mr. Poyntz. "Here is a considerable sum offered by a fair capitalist at this end of the table for the present emanations of your valuable and acute intellect, old boy!"

"What the deuce is Poyntz talking about?" Miss Calverley asked her neighbor, wrinkling her nose. "I hate him. He's a drawling, sneering beast."

"What a droll little man is that little Fokare, my lord," Mademoiselle Coralie remarked in her native tongue, accentuating the words with the rich twang of sunny Gascony that matched her swarthy cheeks and bright black eyes. "What a droll man! He doesn't look to have twenty years."

"I wish I were of his age," sighed the venerable Lord Colchicum, inclining his purple face toward a large goblet of claret.

"C'est la jeunesse. Peuh! je m'en fiche," said Madame Brack, Coralie's mother, taking a generous pinch from Lord Colchicum's delicate gold snuffbox. "Je m'aime que les hommes faits, moi. Like Milord. Coralie! N'est-ce pas que tu n'aimes que les hommes faits, ma bichette?"

My lord grinned. "You flatter me, Madame Brack."

"Taisez-vous, Maman, vous n'êtes qu'une bête," Coralie retorted with a shrug of her robust shoulders. Upon hearing this, my lord conceded that she wasn't flattering him anyway, promptly pocketing his snuffbox before Madame Brack's dubious fingers could plunge too frequently into his Mackabaw tobacco.

There is no need to detail the animated conversation that followed during the remainder of the banquet; suffice it to say, it would not much edify the reader. Nor is it necessary to assert that all ladies of the *corps de danse* are like Miss Calverley, any more than all peers resemble the late, lamented Viscount Colchicum. Yet, history remembers instances where members of high society preferred the company of riotous youth to their own age group, offering them the precious benefits of experience and example. Even respectable men often cared less for the nature of the entertainment than for its lack of privacy.

Our friend Major Pendennis, for instance, would have made no objection to joining such a party, provided it were held *en petit comité*, excluding men like Lord Steyne and Lord Colchicum.

"Give the young men their pleasures," the worthy guardian had told Pen more than once. "I'm not one of your strait-laced moralists, but an old man of the world, begad. I know that as long as it lasts, young men will be young men." He even granted that seventy years was the proper period for sowing wild oats—for men of fashion.

That night, Mr. Foker drove his lovely guests home to Brompton in the drag. However, he remained thoroughly thoughtful and gloomy throughout the short journey from Richmond. He ignored the jokes of the friends behind him and failed to lighten the mood with his usual facetious sallies. When the ladies alighted at their doors and asked their accomplished coachman if he wouldn't step in for a drink, he declined with such a melancholy air that they suspected a falling-out with the Governor or some personal calamity. He kept his reasons hidden, leaving Mesdames Rougemont and Calverley to call out from their balconies like Jezebels, begging him to throw another party soon.

Foker sent the drag back under the care of a groom and continued on foot, hands in his pockets, plunged in deep thought. The stars and moon shone tranquilly overhead as he sentimentally regarded them, gazing upward at the house in Grosvenor Place and the windows he imagined belonged to his beloved object. He moaned and sighed with a pitiful intensity that drew the attention of Policeman X. The officer later informed Sir Francis Clavering's household, while enjoying a beer on the coach-box at a nearby pub, that there had been another chap loitering about the premises that evening—a little fellow dressed like a swell.

With the perspicacity and enterprise born of pure passion, Mr. Foker began to dodge Miss Amory through London, appearing wherever he could intercept her. If Lady Clavering attended the French play, Mr. Foker, whose linguistic skills were otherwise not conspicuous, managed to secure a stall. He discovered her engagements—perhaps thanks to Anatole, his servant, who knew a contact within Sir Francis's circle—and attended many of these parties to the surprise of everyone, including his mother. He instructed his mother to apply for cards to events he previously despised, claiming he attended to "see the world." He lied to the unsuspecting Lady Agnes that he danced to perfect his language, asserting he had learned in Paris. In reality, Anatole knew his young master slipped away privately to an academy in Brewer Street to practice for hours in the mornings.

Meanwhile, at Saint James's Church, Old Pendennis fulfilled his duty as a gentleman by patronizing public worship. One Sunday, he and Arthur walked from the park to attend a charity sermon, though the Major noted he usually skipped services due to thriftiness and other plans.

"We'll go to church across the Park," the Major suggested. "Then, begad, we'll go to the Claverings' and ask for lunch in a friendly way. Lady Clavering likes to be asked; she's uncommonly kind and monstrously hospitable."

"I met them at dinner last week at Lady Agnes Foker's, sir," Pen replied. "The Begum was very kind. She's in the country, so she's everywhere. But I share your opinion about Miss Amory—one of yours, that is, Uncle, since you were changing your tune last time we spoke."

"And what do you think of her now?" the elder asked.

"I think her the most confounded little flirt in London," Pen laughed. "She made a tremendous assault on Harry Foker, who sat next to her, giving him all the talk, though I tried to bring her down."

"Bah! Henry Foker is engaged to his cousin; the whole world knows it. Not a bad coup of Lady Rosherville's, that. Young Foker stands to inherit fourteen thousand a year from the brewery, plus properties in Logwood and Norfolk. I've no pride, Pen. I like birth certainly, but damn it, I like a brewery bringing in fourteen thousand a year. Hey, Pen? Ha, ha! That's the sort of man for me. Stick to fellows with a stake in the country, begad."

"Foker sticks to me, sir," Arthur interjected. "He's been at our chambers recently, asked me to dinner. We're almost as close as we were in our youth, and his talk is nothing but Blanche Amory from morning till night. I'm sure he's sweet on her."

"I'm sure he's engaged to his cousin, and they'll keep the bargain," the Major insisted. "Marriages in these families are matters of state. Lady Agnes was made to marry old Foker by the late Lord, despite her notorious partiality for her cousin who was killed at Albuera. Remember, sir, Lady Agnes was an exceedingly fine woman. But why didn't she marry her father's man? Well, Mr. Foker sat for Drummington until the Reform Bill and paid dearly for his seat. Depend on it, sir, that Foker Senior, a parvenu who loves a great man, has ambitious views for his son as well as himself. Your friend Harry must do as his father bids. Lord bless you! I've known hundreds of cases of love in young men and women. They kick, they resist, they make a deuce of a riot, but they end by listening to reason, begad."

"Blanche is a dangerous girl, sir," Pen admitted. "I was smitten with her myself once, very far gone, too... but that was years ago."

Mr. Pen's life had recently taken a turn toward literary ambition. Following his defeat at Oxford and his return home, he had spent his days composing various works, including a substantial draft of a novel. Written while he was drowning in youthful embarrassments—both romantic and financial—the book was a tempest of Byronic despair, Wertherian melancholy, and Mephistophelian bitterness. Having just begun studying German, he had naturally mimicked his favorite poets, pouring his anguished soul onto the page. Tears had once stained the manuscript, and his pencil had slashed furiously across the paper. Yet, years later, he lacked the energy or the tears to recreate that "early dew." Reading his old work now, he found the passages pompous and the emotions weak. He recognized the borrowed phrases he had once proudly claimed as original, and the specific memories attached to certain lines made him blush at the faint echoes of his past self. Just as one finds camel tracks and withered grass in a desert, signaling a vanished oasis, Pen realized his own inner landscape was no longer green; the *fons lacrymarum* was dry.

One morning, he recounted this metaphor to Warrington, who sat smoking his pipe. Excited, Pen slammed his manuscript onto the table, rattling the tea service and causing the milk to slosh violently. He explained that the book, titled *Leaves from the Life-book of Walter Lorraine*, was born of his infatuation with Miss Amory, to whom he had secretly dedicated verses hidden in trees.

"That was a clever pun," Warrington observed, puffing his pipe. "'Amory' becoming 'Amori.' Show some of the rubbish." Using his fire-tongs, he snatched the manuscript and began reading aloud: *"False as thou art beautiful! Heartless as thou art fair! Mockery of Passion!"*

"Stop!" Pen cried, lunging for the book. "Don't read that. That's about my second flame, Lady Mirabel. I saw her last night at Lady Whiston's. She invited me to a party, seemingly only because she saw me talking to the famous Monsieur Dubois. Imagine meeting both my flames at the same table!"

"Two flames?" Warrington scoffed. "More like two piles of cold ash."

"They're both in the book," Pen insisted. "Leonora, who marries the Duke, is based on Magnus Charters from Oxford; Miss Amory is Neaera. Despite its flaws, there's good stuff here. If Bungay won't publish it, maybe Bacon will."

"All poets are humbugs," Warrington declared philosophically. "We suffer, we cry, we write it down, and we sell it. Once a man starts selling his feelings for coin, he's a fraud. Even Homer and Shakespeare are just bigger versions of you, pigmies trying to cash in on their misery."

"But surely," Pen argued, defending his craft, "poets possess a unique sensibility. They see and feel more keenly. Why shouldn't a man sell his sentimental thoughts as easily as you sell your political opinions or Paley sells his legal knowledge? A poet paints his experiences on paper just as a painter paints a landscape. If I have the talent to write an epic, I'll try. If I can just crack a joke, I'll do that."

"Not a bad speech," Warrington conceded, though he remained skeptical. "But that doesn't stop you from being a humbug."

"What about Shakespeare?"

"Their names aren't spoken in the same breath as yours," Warrington retorted. "There are men, and then there are... amateurs."

Pen countered that Shakespeare wrote for money just like anyone else, leaving Warrington momentarily speechless. It became clear that *Walter Lorraine* was a direct product of Pen's private griefs and follies. Although he had stored the manuscript in a chest with his old academic regalia for several years—not to let it mature, but simply because he didn't know where else to put it—he now felt ready to judge it. He wasn't convinced it was a masterpiece, but compared to the current flood of fashionable novels, he thought it held genuine passion and vigor.

After reviewing the text, Warrington admitted, "There's some good stuff in it, Pen. It has a fresh greenness. The bloom fades from poetry once you start shaving and wearing curling irons. Springtime was the best."

"Do you think we can sell it?" Pen asked hopefully.

"I'd rather burn it," Warrington joked, picking up the manuscript with his tongs. "But since you value it so much, let's not destroy it. Let's take it to Egypt and sell it for silver, gold, and liquor. We'll give it a modern touch, trim the overly green parts, add some satire, and sell it to Bacon or Bungay for a hundred piasters."

Delighted by this high praise, Pen agreed. Warrington playfully slapped him on the cheek, turning his face bright red, and Pen retired to his room to revise the manuscript accordingly. Meanwhile, Warrington worked his charm on the literary agents for Messrs. Bacon and Bungay, exaggerating Pen's status as a fashionable society figure. When the ambitious publisher Mr. Bacon arrived to inspect the ramshackle rooms shared by Pen and Warrington, he expected poverty-stricken impostors. Instead, he found invitations to balls hosted by the Marchioness of Steyne and other high society figures.

"You see?" Warrington explained smoothly. "Pendennis dines with the elite, yet he's perfectly happy eating mutton chops with us. He's a true gentleman."

Impressed by the social connections hinted at by the cards, Bacon pressed Pen for details about the novel. Pen feigned indifference, claiming he was already negotiating with Bungay, which sparked a bidding war between the two rivals. Ultimately, thanks to Warrington's manipulation and Pen's own luck, the novel was sold for a handsome sum.

Rather than splurging on a carriage or moving to a luxury flat—as his Uncle Major Pendennis angrily suggested, dismissing the genre as mere "rubbish"—Pen chose a different path. Remembering his debts, he wrote a tender letter to Laura, enclosing an installment that nearly cleared his obligation. The gesture moved everyone around him: the Widow Pendennis wept, and Dr. Portman, overcome with emotion, kissed his wife and quoted poetry about the joy of prosperity. For Pen, the real victory wasn't just the money, but the opportunity to repay those who had supported him.

Shepherd's Inn, tucked away somewhere behind the soot-stained gables and grim chimneys of Wych Street and Holywell Street, sits right next to Lincoln's-Inn Fields and the Temple. It's a place the sun seems to have given up on, accessible only through winding, smoke-filled alleyways where shadows stretch endlessly. Here, the air smells of brimstone and cheap perfume. Slop-sellers hawk their wares alongside vendors of hard-bake and brandy balls, while peddlers of lurid theatrical prints and dusty furniture crowd the narrow lanes. The doors are battered and bell-ridden, surrounded by gangs of dirty children clamoring over shellfish trays on damp, mud-slicked pavement. Ballad singers drone out scathing tunes against the Whigs, the bishops, and even the German relatives of royalty, while Punch sets up a makeshift theater hoping to coax a halfpenny from the passersby. Amidst the screaming mothers chasing loitering kids and drunken husbands stumbling home from the gin shop, there is a chaotic, ceaseless noise—a stark contrast to the quiet, old-world courtyard of Shepherd's Inn itself.

In the center of this courtyard stands a patch of ragged grass, guarded by iron rails, holding the statue of Shepherd. Around it lie the Inn's halls and ancient chambers. Once a haven for lawyers, it has long since become a hub for the general public. Today, the ground floors house the offices of the Polwheedle and Tredyddlum Copper Mines and the "Registry of Patent Inventions and Union of Genius and Capital Company." The only real lawyer left is Mr. Campion, a man with prominent mustachios who arrives in his cab twice a week. While known as Mr. Somerset Campion in Mayfair, where his wife hosts high society, here in the Inn he operates as "Campion & Co." His uniform includes a tuft of hair on his chin shared by his entire firm. It is a spectacle worth seeing: his cab-horse, adorned with blinding heraldic crests, foams at the mouth as the groom, dressed in gleaming white, guides him to the door.

Our old friend, Captain Costigan, spends his afternoons inspecting Campion's flashy carriage, trailing through the court in his carpet slippers and dressing gown. After breakfast, he visits the porter's lodge, patting the heads of the children and chatting with Mrs. Bolton about theaters and her daughter, Leedy Mirabel. Mrs. Bolton, formerly a dancer at the Wells Theatre, knows a thing or two about show business. She lives upstairs with Mr. Bows, a former tenor singer, and Captain Costigan. They share the rooms once belonged to Charley Podmore. When the windows are open, you can hear Mr. Bows practicing his piano, teaching Fanny Bolton—the porter's daughter—who dreams of following in her mother's footsteps. Despite Mr. Bows' tendency toward drink, Fanny sees the best in everyone, especially the Captain, whom she believes to be the finest gentleman alive.

Captain Costigan has certainly hosted distinguished visitors in the past. One might imagine, listening to him in the mornings, that Sir Charles and Lady Mirabel drop by daily to visit "poor old Jack." The reality is far less romantic. Lady Mirabel stopped visiting years ago. Upon marrying Sir Charles, she settled a small pension on her father, who initially tried to maintain dignity in high society, using grandiloquent language and formal manners that baffled his guests. However, his caution quickly evaporated. By the third or fourth visit, Costigan would succumb to premature intoxication, disgracefully embarrassing his daughter-in-law. He would eventually be driven away, returning later to lament his fate to his friends at the Back Kitchen, comparing himself to King Lear and claiming his daughter was ungrateful.

When his funds ran dry, Costigan turned to begging. He once wrote a desperate letter asking for three pounds and five shillings to avoid jail, which Lady Mirabel promptly sent along with a stern warning to be more economical. Another time, he accidentally smashed a plate-glass window in the Strand. Again, she paid the bill, sending the money via her servant to Costigan's "aide-de-camp"—a fellow Irishman who actually took the cash to the Roscius Head pub, where Costigan was already in debt for rounds of rum-and-water.

On a particularly dire occasion, Costigan claimed illness and needed money for a doctor. Lady Mirabel, worried and perhaps feeling guilty for neglecting him, drove to Shepherd's Inn. She found the door marked "Podmore" opening to reveal Costigan in his shirt sleeves, preparing a gridiron for chops bought by Mrs. Bolton. Meanwhile, Sir Charles was suffering greatly. Letters addressed to him at Brookes's announced Costigan's presence in the hall; invitations to clubs required him to rush out of his carriage lest his father-in-law intercept him. Sir Charles, a frail and aging man, grew hysterical, threatening to move abroad or die if he ever saw Costigan again. Only after Lady Mirabel visited Costigan and issued a final ultimatum—that any further harassment or loan requests would result in the total withdrawal of her allowance—was peace restored. She also reprimanded Mr. Bows for failing to curb the Captain's drinking habits.

Next door, at number three, reside two other characters: Colonel Altamont, agent to the Nawab of Lucknow, and Captain Chevalier Edward Strong. Unlike their neighbors, they keep their names off the door. Strong prefers the anonymity of a Jermyn Street hotel address, while Altamont claims to be on a confidential mission unrelated to the East India Company. In reality, Altamont is trying to sell some of the Nawab's precious diamonds and rubies directly to private capitalists.

Strong's chambers are surprisingly comfortable, furnished nicely since Sir Francis Clavering arrived in London. The space includes an office, bedrooms, and a private staircase leading to upper apartments used by Altamont and the cook, Mr. Grady. The three men living there—Strong, Altamont, and Grady—are all accomplished cooks. Grady excels at Irish stew, the Colonel at pilau and curry, and Strong is a master of French and Spanish cuisine. When money is tight, Strong becomes incredibly hospitable, offering friends excellent dinners, fine wine, and loud, raucous singing. Poor Costigan often envies these gatherings from his room nearby, though he is rarely invited due to his drinking problems and maudlin storytelling.

The group surrounding Strong is a motley collection of schemers and dreamers. Jack Holt, a veteran of Don Carlos's army, plans to smuggle tobacco into London. Tom Diver, ex-Mexican navy, boasts knowledge of a sunken ship loaded with gold and silver, promising a return to Parliament within ten months. Keightley manages the underwater copper mines and dreams of sponge and quicksilver ventures. Filby has a mysterious inheritance from his deceased father, the location of which remains unknown. And then there is Sir Francis Clavering, a wealthy baronet who enjoys their company despite his own checkered past involving prisons and stamped papers.

Clavering brought Altamont to stay at Strong's chambers after meeting him in Paris. However, Strong finds the Colonel unbearable. He complains bitterly to Clavering about Altamont's lack of gentility, his garish dress, and his behavior at the theater, where he screamed and cried during *The Brigand*. Strong suspects Altamont is a criminal, possibly linked to church robberies, and urges Clavering to cut ties. Yet, Clavering merely replies, "Hang him! I wish he was dead!" before falling silent, leaving Strong to wonder about the secret bond connecting his patron and the troublesome Colonel.

Early one morning, shortly after the dinner at Grosvenor Place where Colonel Altamont had made such a scene, the Colonel emerged from his room in the upper story of Shepherd's Inn. He walked straight into Captain Strong's sitting-room, where the Chevalier sat comfortably in his chair, reading the newspaper and puffing on a cigar.

Captain Strong was a man who managed to make his quarters feel like home wherever he stopped. Long before Altamont arrived, he had already demolished a hearty breakfast of fried eggs and broiled bacon, prepared by Mr. Grady with military precision. Cheerful and loquacious, Strong preferred any company to solitude. Though he held no affection for his fellow lodger and wouldn't have minded if the accident Sir Francis Clavering prayed for had befallen him, he maintained polite relations with the Colonel. He had tucked Altamont into bed with genuine kindness the night before, even removing his candle to prevent fires. Finding his spirit bottle empty—which he had relied on for midnight comfort—he had happily washed it down with a glass of water over his pipe before drifting off to sleep. Such contentment was his constant companion; possessing an easy temper, a flawless digestion, and a perpetually rosy complexion, the worthy captain slept soundly whether facing battle or the Fleet, waking each day ready to tackle whatever struggles, difficulties, or pleasures awaited him.

The Colonel's first move was to roar for Grady to bring a pint of pale ale. Grady poured it into a pewter flagon, which the Colonel then lifted directly to his lips. He drained the tankard, drew a deep breath, and wiped his mouth with his dressing gown. (Captain Strong had long noticed the discrepancy between the color of the Colonel's beard and his dyed whiskers, and suspected his hair was fair beneath the black wig, but wisely chose not to comment). Refreshed by the draught, the Colonel declared, "Nothing beats that beer when the coppers are hot. I've drunk a dozen pints of Bass in Calcutta, and—"

"And at Lucknow, I suppose?" Strong laughed. "I fetched the beer specifically for you; I knew you'd need it after last night."

Seizing the opportunity, the Colonel launched into a tale of his previous evening's exploits.

"I simply can't help myself," the Colonel exclaimed, thumping his head with his large hand. "Once I have liquor aboard, I'm a madman unfit to handle a spirit bottle. Once I start, I can't stop until the vessel is empty, and then—who knows what I say or don't say? I dined quietly at home. Grady gave me exactly two tumblers, and I planned to spend the evening at the Black and Red as sober as a parson. Why did you leave that cursed sample bottle of Hollands in the cupboard, Strong? Grady had to step out, leaving the kettle boiling for tea. It was futile; I couldn't resist it. I washed it all down, by Jove, and I'm convinced I had more later at that infernal little thieves' den."

"Thieves' den?" Strong asked. "Were you there too, before you reached Grosvenor Place? That seems early for a clear-out."

"Early hours for drinking and disappearing before nine, yes. But that's how it was. Like a great fool, I went there anyway. Found Blackland and young Moss dining, along with two or three other rogues. If we'd tried Roulette, I surely would have won. But we didn't gamble on black and red. Hang 'em, they knew I'd crush them at the tables—I must have crushed them, I tell you! But they were too cunning. That rascal Blackland rigged the bones, and we played Hazard on the dining table. I dropped every penny you lent me that morning. Damn my luck! That drove me wild, and I assume my head was feverish, for I set off to borrow more money from Clavering. Then... then I don't remember much until this morning, when I heard old Bows at Number 4 banging on his piano."

Strong lit his cigar with a piece of coal and pondered for a moment. "I'd like to know how you manage to extract money from Clavering, Colonel," he said.

The Colonel erupted in laughter. "Ha, ha! Because he owes it to me!"

"That isn't a valid reason for Frank to pay," Strong countered. "He owes plenty to others besides you."

"Well, he pays me because he adores me," the Colonel grinned with a sneer. "He loves me like a brother, you know he does, Captain. —No? He doesn't? Well, perhaps he doesn't. And if you promise not to ask questions, perhaps I'll promise not to lie, Captain Strong—put that in your pipe and smoke it, my boy."

"But I shall quit that confounded brandy bottle," the Colonel continued after a pause. "I must, or it will ruin me."

"It makes you say strange things," the Captain observed, staring Altamont squarely in the eye. "Recall what you said last night at Clavering's table."

"Said? What did I say?" the other demanded hastily. "Did I spill anything? Darn me, Strong, did I spill anything?"

"Ask me no questions, and I'll tell you no lies," the Chevalier replied. Strong recalled the words Altamont had uttered and his sudden exit from the Baronet's dining room upon recognizing Major Pendennis, or Captain Beak as he called him. However, Strong decided to seek an explanation elsewhere and refused to dredge up the memory for the Colonel. "No," he said calmly, "you didn't 'spill,' as you call it. It was merely a trap of mine to see if I could make you speak. But you said nothing anyone could understand; you were far too intoxicated for that."

Relieved, Altamont sighed heavily. Noticing the emotion, Strong said nothing, and since the Colonel was in a storytelling mood, he pressed on.

"Yes, I admit my faults," the Colonel continued. "There are three things I cannot resist, no matter what I try: a bottle of brandy, a box of dice, and a beautiful woman. No man of courage and spirit, no man worth his salt, has ever escaped them. Hardly a country exists where these three haven't landed me in trouble."

"Indeed?" Strong asked.

"Yes, since I was fifteen, when I ran away from home to become a cabin boy on an Indiaman, until now, at nearly fifty, women have always been my undoing. Why, it was one of them—with jet-black eyes and jewels around her neck, draped in ermine like a duchess—that swept away most of the thousand pounds I carried when I fled. Wasn't I telling you about this before? Well, I don't mind repeating it. Initially, I was cautious, keeping my money tight and living like a gentleman—Colonel Altamont at Meurice's Hotel, and so forth. I rarely played, sticking to public tables where I usually won more than I lost.

"One day, however, I spotted a chap at the hotel and the Palace Royal, a proper swell with white kid gloves and a tuft on his chin. His name was Bloundell-Bloundell, and somehow we became acquainted. He invited me to dinner and took me to the soirées of Madame the Countess de Foljambe. Such a woman, Strong! Such an eye! Such a talent at the pianoforte! Bless you, she would sit down and sing to you, gazing intently until she warbled your soul right out of your body. She invited me to her Tuesday evenings without fail. Did I buy opera boxes and treat her to dinners at the restaurateur? Of course I did. But despite my luck at the tables, it wasn't the dinners or the boxes that drained poor Clavering's funds. No, by Jove, it vanished in another way.

"One night at the Countess's, we were supping—Mr. Bloundell-Bloundell, the Honourable Deuceace, the Marquis de la Tour de Force—all top-tier nobility, sir, the pinnacle of fashion. We had supper, followed by ample champagne, and then some of that cursed brandy. I demanded more—I insisted on continuing. The Countess mixed punch for me, and after supper, we played cards alongside grog. I played and drank until I don't know what I did. I was just like last night. Taken away and put to bed somehow, I didn't wake until the next day, suffering a throbbing headache, to find my servant saying the Honourable Deuceace wished to see me and was waiting in the sitting-room.

"'How are you, Colonel?' he asked, entering my bedroom. 'How long did you stay last night after I left? The game was getting too expensive for me, and I'd lost enough to you for one night.'

"'To me?' I asked. 'How's that, my dear fellow? For though he was an Earl's son, we were as familiar as you and I.' He explained he had borrowed thirty louis from me at Vingt-et-Un, giving me an I.O.U. the night prior, which I had placed in my pocket-book before he departed. I produced my card-case—the Countess had crafted it for me—and there was the I.O.U., sure enough. He paid me thirty louis in gold right on my bedside table. So I praised him as a gentleman and asked if he needed anything while my servant

After the lavish entertainments at Grosvenor Place and Greenwich, Major Pendennis's devotion toward the Clavering family seemed to deepen daily. This worthy old gentleman, a fixture of London society, possessed the uncanny ability to gain entry into households where a woman of Lady Clavering's stature was expected. "Would Her Ladyship not delight in attending the grand gala at Gaunt House?" he would inquire eagerly. "There is a delightful breakfast ball at Viscount Marrowfat's in Fulham tomorrow. Everyone will be there—even august figures of the highest rank—and there will be a Watteau quadrille in which Miss Amory is certain to look ravishing." To these and countless other diversions, the obsequious elder kindly offered to escort Lady Clavering, while simultaneously positioning himself as a tireless utility to the Baronet.

Despite his current status and fortune, the world maintained a chilly demeanor toward Clavering. Suspicious rumors trailed him like shadows. He was blackballed from two clubs in quick succession. Within the House of Commons, he found solace only among a select group of the most disreputable members, possessing a rare talent for selecting bad company and adapting to it with natural ease, much as others adapt to the company of their superiors. To list every senator Clavering consorted with would be invidious; suffice it to name a few. There was Captain Raff, the Member for Epsom, who retired after the last Goodwood races under the guise of a mission to the Levant; there was Hustingson, the patriotic Member for Islington, whose voice ceased its denunciations of corruption upon becoming Governor of Coventry Island; and there was Bob Freeny of the Booterstown line, a marksman of note. Of all these gentlemen, with whom Mr. Hotspur was professionally compelled to converse, none inspired in the hot-headed Whip a deeper contempt than Sir Francis Clavering, the representative of an ancient lineage who had sat for their own borough time immemorial. "If that man is needed for a division," Hotspur grumbled, "ten to one he is lurking in a hellscape. Educated in the Fleet and still smelling of Newgate, he'll muddle away the Begum's fortune at thimble-rig, get caught pickpocketing, and finish his days on the hulks." Yet, if the high-born Hotspur could maintain civility for professional reasons, why should Major Pendennis lack his own motives for attending to this unluckily situated gentleman?

"He possesses a magnificent cellar and a superb cook," the Major observed. "So long as he remains silent, he is harmless, and he speaks very rarely. If he chooses to frequent gambling tables and lose his coin to blacklegs, what concern is it of mine? Do not pry too deeply into anyone's affairs, Pen, my boy. Every fellow has a cupboard in his house, begad, which he would prefer you and I not peek into. Why bother trying when the rest of the house is wide open to us? Besides, it is a devilishly good house, as you and I know. And if the master of the family falls short of perfection, the women are excellent. The Begum is not overly refined, but she is the kindest woman alive and remarkably clever. As for little Blanche, you know my opinion, you rogue; you know I believe she is sweet on you and would have you for the asking. But you are becoming such a great man, I suppose you won't settle for a Duke's daughter—hey, sir? I recommend you ask one of them and try."

Perhaps Pen was slightly intoxicated by his rising success in the world; perhaps, too, his uncle's perpetual hints encouraged the notion that Miss Amory was favorably disposed to renew the flirtation begun by the banks of the rural Brawl. However, he was currently disinclined toward marriage. Adopting some of his uncle's worldly cynicism, he spoke contemptuously of matrimony in favor of bachelorhood.

"You are very fortunate, sir," he remarked, "and you thrive splendidly alone, as do I. With a wife at my side, I would lose my footing in society; and I do not particularly fancy retreating to the countryside with a Mrs. Pendennis, nor dragging my wife into lodgings to be served by a servant-of-all-work.
