My period of youthful illusions is over. You cured me of my first love, who was certainly a fool and would have made a fool of a husband, and a very sulky, discontented one at that. We young fellows live fast, sir; and I feel as aged at twenty-five as many of the 'old fogies' I see in the bow-windows at Bays's. Do not take offense; I merely mean I am blasé regarding love matters. I could no more fan myself into a flame for Miss Amory now than I could adore Lady Mirabel again. I wish I could; I actually like old Mirabel for his infatuation, and consider his passion the most respectable aspect of his life."

"Sir Charles Mirabel was always a theatrical man, sir," the Major snapped, annoyed that his nephew should speak lightly of someone of Sir Charles's rank. "He has been immersed in theatricals since his early days. He acted at Carlton House when he was Page to the Prince; he has been involved in that sort of thing. He could afford to marry whomever he chose, and Lady Mirabel is a most respectable woman, received everywhere—*everywhere*, mind. The Duchess of Connaught receives her, Lady Rockminster receives her—it does not become young fellows to speak lightly of persons in that station. There is no more respectable woman in England than Lady Mirabel. Those 'old fogies,' as you call them, at Bays's, are some of the first gentlemen in England, of whom you youngsters had best learn a little manners, a little breeding, and a little modesty." The Major began to suspect that Pen was growing exceedingly pert and conceited, and that the world was making far too much of him.

The Major's anger only amused Pen. He studied his uncle's peculiarities with constant relish, remaining perpetually in good humor with his worldly mentor. "I am a youngster of fifteen years' standing, sir," he replied adroitly. "If you deem us disrespectful, you should observe the present generation. A protégé of yours came to breakfast with me the other day. You instructed me to ask him, and I did so to please you. We spent the day sightseeing, dined at the club, and attended the play. He remarked that the wine at the Polyanthus was inferior to Ellis's at Richmond, smoked Warrington's Cavendish after breakfast, and when I presented him with a sovereign as a farewell token, he said he had plenty but would accept it to demonstrate he wasn't proud."

"Did he? Did you ask young Clavering?" cried the Major, instantly appeased—"Fine boy, rather wild, but a fine boy. Parents appreciate that sort of attention, and you cannot do better than pay it to our worthy friends of Grosvenor Place. So you took him to the play and tipped him? That was right, sir, that was right!" With this verdict, the Mentor dismissed Telemachus, convinced the young men were not so terrible after all and that he could make something of that fellow eventually.

As Master Clavering matured in years and stature, he became too robust for the authority of his fond parents and governess, governing them rather than allowing himself to be led. With his father, he was silent and sullen, rarely appearing near the gentleman; with his mother, he roared and fought whenever contests arose regarding the gratification of his appetite or other desires; and in disputes with his governess over books, he kicked her shins so fiercely that she was entirely overpowered. He would have treated his sister Blanche similarly, attempting to dominate her on one or two occasions, but she displayed immense resolution and spirit, boxing his ears so soundly that he refrained from molesting Miss Amory, as he did the governess, his mother, and his mother's maid.

Eventually, when the family arrived in London, Sir Francis declared, "The little beggar had best be sent to school." Thus, the young son and heir of the house of Clavering was dispatched to the Reverend Otto Rose's establishment at Twickenham, where young noblemen and gentlemen were prepared for admission to the great English public schools.

It is not our intention to chronicle Master Clavering's scholastic career; the paths to the Temple of Learning were paved easier for him than for many of our predecessors. He advanced toward that fane in a carriage-and-four, halting for refreshments whenever he pleased. From the earliest days of youth, he wore varnished boots, cambric handkerchiefs, and lemon-colored kid gloves of the smallest size ever manufactured by Privat. At Mr. Rose's, the young gentlemen dressed formally for dinner, donned shawl dressing-gowns, enjoyed fires in their bedrooms, exercised with horses and carriages occasionally, and had oil applied to their hair. Corporal punishment was entirely abolished by the Principal, who believed moral discipline sufficed to guide youth. Consequently, the boys were so rapidly advanced in various branches of learning that they acquired the art of drinking spirits and smoking cigars before reaching the age for public schools. Young Frank Clavering stole his father's Havannahs, conveying them to school or smoking them in the stables at a surprisingly early age, and at ten years old drank champagne almost as stoutly as any whiskered cornet of dragoons.

When this interesting youth

Noble Henry Foker, whom we haven't seen for a few pages, has been busy, as expected of a man of such stubborn devotion, pursuing and indulging his all-consuming passion for love.

I'd like to suggest that some of my young readers, who seem fond of this sort of thing, take the trouble to calculate exactly how much time is wasted on it. They'll discover it's one of the most expensive hobbies a human being can afford. What sacrifices don't you make, you young gentlemen and ladies of unruly minds? First, you burn through hours of precious sleep, tossing and turning thinking about your beloved object, only to drag yourself down to breakfast late, while everyone else is already off to their daily duties. Once you finally join them, you pay no attention whatsoever; your mind is elsewhere, engaged in nothing with any real energy. You slough off the day's work, ignore your friends and family—the people naturally meant to accompany you in life—just so you can catch a glimpse of your darling, peek at her window, or spy her carriage in the park. At night, the simple charms of home bore you; your mother's chatter grows tiresome; the delicious meals she prepares for her favorite are sent away untouched. Indeed, the entire feast of life loses its flavor, save for one specific dish. Everything useful and dear once becomes unbearable, and you feel no peace unless you are actively chasing your flame.

I believe this is frequently the state of mind among poorly regulated young men, and certainly it was Mr. H. Foker's condition. Having been raised to indulge every inclination he possessed, he threw himself into this one with his usual selfish enthusiasm. Don't let anyone be surprised that Mr. Foker became a slave to passion, even after giving his friend Arthur Pendennis excellent advice on a previous occasion. Who among us hasn't given our friends the very best advice? Who hasn't preached sermons while failing to practice them ourselves? Perhaps you, madam, are a perfect being, never harboring a wrong thought in your frigid, blameless existence. Or maybe you, sir, are so strong-minded that no foolish passion can disturb your calm in the chambers or your focus on the Stock Exchange; you're so robust you don't need sympathy. Well, we won't give you any; we save ours for the humble and weak, those who stumble, rise again, and march along with the rest of humanity. What need do you have of a hand that never falls? Your serene virtue is never clouded by passion, ruffled by temptation, or darkened by remorse; compassion would be an insult to such an angel. But then, companionship becomes impossible for someone so elevated; you are necessarily lonely due to your high attributes. We can't reach up to chat familiarly with such potentates, so goodbye to you; our path lies with humble folk, not serene highnesses like you. Just so, we declare there are no perfect characters in this history, except perhaps one tiny exception—and even she isn't truly perfect, for she doesn't realize it to this day. With a pathetic misunderstanding and twisted humility, she believes herself to be as great a sinner as anyone needs to be.

This young lady happens not to be in London right now, and it certainly isn't for someone like her that Mr. Henry Foker's mind is currently agitated. But what difference does a few flaws make? Do we need to be angels, male or female, to be worshipped as such? Let us celebrate the diversity of human tastes. The oldest, ugliest, stupidest, most pompous, silliest, vapid, greatest criminal, tyrant, booby, Bluebeard, Catherine Hayes, George Barnwell—we need never despair. I've read of a transported pickpocket's passion for a female convict (both aged, repulsive, ignorant, quarrelsome, and prone to drinking) that was as magnificent as the loves of Cleopatra and Antony, or Lancelot and Guinevere. The passion Count Borulawski, the Polish dwarf, inspired in the bosom of the most beautiful Baroness at the Dresden court is common knowledge. The flame that burned in young Cornet Tozer's heart recently, causing him to flee and marry Mrs. Battersby, who was old enough to be his mother—all these stories fill the pages of history or newspaper columns. Should we be ashamed or pleased to know our hearts are formed such that the biggest, highest-placed Ajax among us might someday find himself prostrate before the pattens of his kitchen maid? That there is no poverty, shame, or crime that won't be supported, embraced with delight, and cherished more closely than virtue itself, by the perverse fidelity and admirable, constant folly of a woman?

Thus, Henry Foker, Esquire, longed for his love and cursed the fate separating them. When Lord Gravesend's family retreated to the countryside (leaving his proxy with the venerable Lord Bagwig), Harry remained lingering in London, certainly not to the sorrow of Lady Ann, to whom he was betrothed, who missed him not in the slightest. Whichever way Miss Clavering went, this infatuated young fellow followed; knowing his engagement to his cousin was public knowledge, he was forced to keep his passion a mystery, confined strictly to his own chest. It was so pent up and pressed down inside him that it's a miracle he didn't eventually explode with the stormy secret and perish collapsed after the outburst.

A grand entertainment took place at Gaunt House on a beautiful June evening, and the following day's journals filled nearly two columns with the names of the nobility and gentry honored with invitations. Among the guests were Sir Francis and Lady Clavering, Miss Amory (whose invitation the indefatigable Major Pendennis had secured), and our two young friends, Arthur and Harry. Both exerted themselves, dancing extensively with Miss Blanche. As for the worthy Major, he assumed charge of Lady Clavering, ensuring she was escorted to that department of the mansion where her ladyship particularly excelled: the refreshment room. Surrounded by paintings of Titian and Giorgione, regal portraits of Van Dyck and Reynolds, enormous golden and silver salvers, pyramids of large flowers, and constellations of wax candles—in short, with a disregard for expense that bordered on the absurd—a supper was served throughout the night. How many creams, jellies, salads, peaches, white soups, grapes, pâtés, galantines, cups of tea, champagne, and so on, Lady Clavering consumed, it is not proper for us to detail. How much suffering the Major endured as he followed the honest woman around, summoning solemn male attendants and lovely servant-maids, attending to Lady Clavering's various needs with admirable patience, remains unknown; he never confessed. He never allowed his agony to show on his face in the least; instead, with constant kindness, he brought plate after plate to the Begum.

Mr. Wagg attempted to tally every dish Lady Clavering partook of as long as he could count (though, having partaken freely of champagne himself during the evening, his calculating powers were unreliable by the end). He recommended Mr. Honeyman, Lady Steyne's physician, to watch over the Begum carefully and check on her the next day.

Sir Francis Clavering appeared, skulking briefly through the magnificent rooms, but finding neither the company nor the splendor to his taste, he tossed off a couple of glasses of wine at the buffet and departed for the neighborhood of Jermyn Street, where his friends Loder, Punter, little Moss Abrams, and Captain Skewball were gathered at their familiar green table. In the rattling of the carriage and their pleasant conversation, Sir Francis's spirits rose to their customary level of feeble hilarity.

Mr. Pynsent, who had requested Miss Amory's hand in dance, approached to claim her, but having already exchanged scowls of recognition with Mr. Arthur Pendennis in the ballroom, Arthur suddenly rose and claimed Miss Amory as his partner for the current dance. Mr. Pynsent, biting his lips and scowling even more savagely, withdrew with a profound bow, stating he relinquished his claim. Some men are simply destined to cross your path in life. Pynsent and Pen held this mutual view; each regarded the other accordingly.

"What a confounded conceited provincial fool that is!" thought one. "Because he wrote a twopenny novel, his absurd head is turned; a good kicking would take his conceit out of him."

"What an impertinent idiot that man is!" remarked the other to his partner. "His soul resides in Downing Street; his necktie is foolscap; his hair is sand; his legs are rulers; his insides are tape and sealing wax; he was a prig in his cradle and hasn't laughed since birth, except three times at the same joke of his boss. I share the same dislike for that man, Miss Amory, as I do for that cold boiled veal." Upon this, Blanche naturally remarked that Mr. Pendennis was wicked, malicious, and utterly abominable, wondering what he would say when her back was turned.

"Say! Say that you possess the most beautiful figure and the slimmest waist in the world, Blanche—I mean Miss Amory. I beg your pardon. Another turn; this music would make an alderman dance."

"And you've stopped tumbling when you waltz now?" Blanche asked, archly looking up at her partner's face.

"One falls and one gets up again in life, Blanche; you know I used to call you that in old

The exertions of the previous night at Gaunt House had nearly broken Major Pendennis's constitution. As soon as he could move his weary frame without danger, he limped groaningly to Buxton, seeking solace in the region's famous healing waters. By the time Parliament adjourned, Sir Francis Clavering and his household had departed the city, leaving the affairs previously discussed largely untouched amidst the fleeting weeks that followed. Consequently, London itself seemed to empty out.

The season drew to a close. Pen's neighbors, the legal fraternity, had scattered onto the circuits; his fashionable circle had purchased passports for the Continent or fled to the Scottish Highlands for health and thrills. The bow-windows of the clubs were deserted, and the solitary pavement of Pall Mall echoed with silence. The scarlet coats of the Guards vanished before the Palace gates; the merchants of St. James's wandered abroad for leisure; the tailors had sported mustaches and migrated up the Rhine; the cobblers were found at Ems or Baden, flushing crimson when encountering their patrons among the resorts, or punting alongside their creditors at gambling tables. The clergy of St. James's preached to dwindling congregations devoid of notable sinners. The band in Kensington Gardens silenced their brass and silver trumpets. Only a handful of dilapidated carriages crawled along the Serpentine's banks. Even Clarence Bulbul, the Treasury clerk bound to the capital by duty, compared the emptiness of Rotten Row during his afternoon rides to the vastness of the Arabian Desert, casting himself as a lone Bedouin traversing dusty solitude. Meanwhile, Warrington stuffed his carpet bag with Cavendish tobacco and retreated to his brother's estate in Norfolk, as was his custom during vacations.

Left somewhat isolated in his chambers, Pen could not simply abandon the metropolis whenever he pleased; he was currently tethered by the demands of *The Pall Mall Gazette*. Acting as editor and *chargé d'affaires* in the temporary absence of the chief, Captain Shandon—who was enjoying the salutary waters of Boulogne-sur-Mer with his family—Pen found himself managing the paper's operations.

Though Pen had long professed himself a *blase* soul, thoroughly wearied of life, the reality was that he remained an exceptionally vigorous young man. He possessed a magnificent appetite, satisfying it with gusto daily, and a relentless craving for society that marked him as anything but a misanthrope. If a good dinner eluded him, he ate a mediocre one with perfect contentment; if witty, great, or beautiful company was unavailable, he accepted whatever presented itself. He felt entirely at home in a tavern parlour, aboard a Greenwich steamboat, on a jaunt to Hampstead with his colleague Mr. Finucane, at the summer theaters across the river, or at the Royal Gardens of Vauxhall. There, he maintained friendly terms with the great Simpson and shook hands with the lead comic singer of the equestrian troupe. While observing the grimaces and graces of the performers with a satirical humor undeniably tinged with sympathy, he also viewed the spectators with kindness: the boisterous youths chasing pleasure, the honest parents watching delighted children clap their hands, and the unfortunate outcasts whose laughter was less innocent but perhaps louder, bringing their shame and youth to dance until dawn, hoping to drown their cares. Arthur often boasted of this universal sympathy, claiming it as his favorite pursuit. Just as others ardently loved art, music, or science, Pen declared anthropology his passion, keeping his eyes wide open to its infinite varieties. Whether it was a coquettish dowager in a ballroom, a high-born beauty blushing in her prime, a hulking guard coaxing a maid in the Park, or an innocent child feeding ducks while a nurse watched, he contemplated them all with unfailing delight. A man with a relatively clean heart can indulge in such observation with ceaseless joy, perhaps even more keenly because it remains secret, carrying a touch of melancholy, for he feels both connected and apart, lonely yet not alone.

Indeed, Pen used to brashly tell Warrington, "In my youth, I was so fiercely in love that I've burned that flame out forever. If I ever marry, it will be a marriage of reason—a union with a well-bred, good-tempered, good-looking person with a little money to cushion our carriage through life. As for romance, it's all done; I've spent it, and I'm old before my time—I'm proud of it!"

"Stuff!" grunted Warrington. "You fancied you were going bald the other day and bragged about it just as you do everything else. But you started using bear's grease the moment the hairdresser mentioned it, and you're smelling like a barber shop ever since."

"You are Diogenes," Pen retorted, "expecting every man to live in a tub like yourself. Violets smell better than stale tobacco, you grizzly old cynic." Yet, as he spoke, Pen blushed, caring far more about his own appearance than a true philosopher ought. Considering his claimed indifference to the world, he adorned himself with considerable effort to remain agreeable, wearing tight boots and bright polish like a weary pilgrim determined to look his best.

It was on such a dull, shining Friday night in autumn that Mr. Pendennis, having penned a brilliant leading article at his newspaper office—one that Captain Shandon himself might have authored had he been in a working mood, which he rarely was unless compelled—reviewed his work with approval on the wet proof-sheets. Deciding to cross the water, he resolved to treat himself to the fireworks and amusements of Vauxhall. He tucked his special order—"Editor of *Pall Mall Gazette* and Friend"—into his pocket and paid the requisite toll to cross Waterloo Bridge. The walk toward the Gardens was pleasant, the stars twinkling overhead, gazing down upon the royal property before the rockets and Roman candles ascended to outshine them.

Before entering the enchanted grounds, where twenty thousand extra lamps burned nightly, visitors usually traverse a black, dreary passage guarded by wickets that conceal the splendor from the uninitiated. Illuminated holes in the walls served as checkpoints, where desk-bound officials accepted cash or admission orders. Pen approached the latter booth, only to find a gentleman and two ladies already engaged in conversation ahead of him.

The gentleman, sporting a hat tilted precariously and a short, shabby cloak worn with excessive flair, shouted in a voice Pen instantly recognized.

"Bedad, sir, if ye doubt me honor, will ye oblige me by stepping out of that box and—"

"Lor, Capting!" cried the elder lady.

"Don't bother me," the man in the booth snapped.

"And ask Mr. Hodgen himself, who's inside the gardens, to let these ladies pass. Don't be frightened, me dear madam; I'm not going to quarrel with this gentleman, especially before ladies. Will ye go, sir, and desire Mr. Hodgen (whom I keep in with, and he's me most intimate friend, and I know he's going to sing 'The Body Snatcher' tonight) to step out and let the ladies in—for myself, sir, I've seen Vauxhall, and I'd scarce allow any interference on my account: but for these ladies, one has never been there, and I'd hardly take advantage of my misfortune in losing the ticket to deprive her of her pleasure."

"It ain't no use, Captain. I can't go about your business," the ticket-checker replied peevishly. Whereupon the Captain swore an oath, and the elder lady exclaimed, "Lor, oh, provokin'!"

The younger girl looked up at the Captain and said, "Never mind, Captain Costigan, I'm sure I don't want to go at all. Come away, Mamma." Though she claimed no desire to enter, her emotions overwhelmed her, and she burst into tears.

"My poor child!" the Captain sighed. "Can ye see that, sir, and will ye not let this innocent creature in?"

"It ain't my business," the doorkeeper cried irritably. At that precise moment, Arthur stepped forward, recognizing Costigan immediately.

"Don't you know me, Captain? Pendennis!" he said, removing his hat and bowing to the ladies.

"Me dear boy! Me dear friend!" the Captain cried, extending a grasping hand. He quickly explained his "most unluckey contract." He held an order from Mr. Hodgen (who was currently inside singing favorites like *The Death of General Wolfe* and *The Banner of Blood*) admitting two persons. Believing it might accommodate three, he had brought his friends. However, on his way, Captain Costigan had lost the admission paper entirely. The ladies were forced to return, much to the dismay of the younger one, as Arthur observed.

Arthur possessed immense good nature and sympathy for all kinds of misfortune; how could he refuse in such a case? He had witnessed the innocent upward glance of the girl, the pleading expression, the pitiful quiver of her mouth, and the final eruption of tears. If it were his last guinea, he would have paid it to grant her pleasure. She turned her sad, imploring eyes away upon seeing a stranger and began wiping them with a handkerchief. Arthur looked handsome and kind, standing before the women with his hat off, blushing, bowing, generous, and truly gentlemanly. "Who are they?" he asked himself. He felt he had seen the elder lady before.

"If I can be of any service to you,

Costigan's arrival failed to rouse Pen from his slumber, nor did any hostile missive from Mr. Huxter disturb his peace. When Arthur Pendennis finally opened his eyes, he found himself buoyed by a vitality far exceeding the usual groggy awakening of a weary Londoner.

Consider the City man: as sleep vanishes, the specters of 'Change and the counting-house immediately seize him, demanding his attention before breakfast. Consider the lawyer: dawn brings not hope, but the crushing weight of the day's case and the looming deadline for the attorney who expects his papers by nightfall. Who among us does not feel this immediate, heavy anxiety the moment consciousness returns? Yet, this anxiety is also a "kind strengthener," a divine ordinance that grants rest precisely to fuel labor.

Mr. Pendennis, however, possessed a different constitution. His labors held little fascination for him. The thrill of literary creation fades quickly in the mind of a hired hand, just as the joy of seeing one's name in print lasts only until the third issue. Pegasus, once yoked to the daily grind of the stage, becomes as mundane as any hack horse, requiring a whip or a fresh ear of corn to move. Thus, Arthur performed his duties at the *Pall Mall Gazette*—now bolstered by a novelist's salary—with zero enthusiasm. He wrote adequately, occasionally brilliantly, but mostly mechanically. He was a literary sprinter, naturally fast and flashy, yet devoid of endurance.

Society offered him neither amusement nor comfort. Despite his boasts, he was too young for the refined company of women, which seems attainable only when a man stops worrying about his own image and ceases to view romance as a conquest. Simultaneously, he was too young to be an equal among established men, whose conversations he could only eavesdrop upon, and too old for the reckless revelry of his peers. Destined to be largely solitary, fate had dealt him a hand many envy in practice but few enjoy in theory. Pendennis accepted this solitude with surprising equanimity, though he grumbled about it incessantly.

"What a lovely, artless creature that was," Pen murmured to himself immediately upon waking, recalling the Vauxhall incident. "Such a natural manner! How infinitely preferable to the calculated coquetry of the ballroom belles!" His mind drifted to Miss Blanche and the stiff, artificial graces of polite society. "Who imagined a rose could bloom in a porter's lodge, or flourish in that dreary pot at Shepherd's Inn? Does she learn to sing from Old Bows? If her voice matches her charm, it must be exquisite. I adore those low, veiled tones. 'What would you like me to call you?' Indeed, poor little Fanny! It tempted me to strike a grand pose and demand she call me 'Sir.' But no nonsense of that sort—no Faust and Marguerite drama for me. That old Bows! He taught her to sing, didn't he? A dear soul in those antique garb, a true philosopher with a kind heart. He was wonderful during the Fotheringay affair. Men have their sorrows, too. I must cultivate Old Bows. One sees too much of genteel circles; besides, there's nobody in town anyway. Yes, I shall visit Bows and Costigan. What a rich character Costigan is! Begad, I'll study him for a novel."

With Saturday arriving—a day of leisure for the *Gazette*, allowing contributors a rare reprieve from ink and brainwork—Pendennis decided to act on his impulse. He would visit Shepherd's Inn, ostensibly to see Old Bows.

In truth, even if Arthur Pendennis had been the most cunning rogue or the most artful seducer in history, he could not have devised a finer strategy to captivate poor Fanny Bolton than the one he employed the previous night. His dandified demeanor, his air of protection, his conceit mixed with genuine generosity, and his inherent honesty—all factors that allowed him to gently rebuff her trembling advances without crushing her spirit—conspired to make her adore him. His flaws and virtues worked in tandem.

If one could peek into Fanny's bedchamber—a cramped cupboard shared with two younger sisters currently being fed gingerbread and apples by Mr. Costigan—one might find the poor girl tossing restlessly on her thin mattress. Her dreams were filled with the delights of that eventful night, replaying every glance, word, and gesture of Arthur, her splendid hero. Fanny, having consumed countless novels in secret through the circulating library managed by Miss Minifer alongside her millinery trade, viewed Arthur as the living embodiment of every gallant in those greasy, thrilling volumes.

Pen was undeniably a dandy regarding shirts and accessories. Fanny had gazed with awe at the fineness of his linen, the brilliance of his studs, the elegance of his cambric handkerchief, and the gleaming whiteness of his gloves. Even his boots possessed a jet-black radiance. The Prince had arrived to subjugate the humble handmaid. His image haunted her restless sleep: the tone of his voice, the blue fire in his eyes, the look of benevolent pity, the protective smile, and the winning laugh. She felt his arm around her, saw him filling that delicious glass of champagne.

She contrasted him with her peers: Emma Baker, proud of her engagement to a cheesemonger in a white apron near Clare Market; Betsy Rodgers, fussing over her young man, an attorney's clerk who carried a bag.

At approximately two o'clock, after the Bolton family had finished dinner (with Mr. B., the porter, absent in the countryside attending to the Countess of Estrich's hearse for Messrs. Tressler), a figure appeared beneath the inn archway. A gentleman in a white hat and white trousers halted at the wicket. Fanny was not merely surprised; she was overwhelmed with delight and blushes. She knew it was Him. There stood His Royal Highness, beaming from the gate.

"Mamma, mamma!" she cried, running to open the wicket, shoving aside her siblings. How red her cheeks turned as she handed him her hand! How gracefully he removed his hat as he entered, drawing stares from the children. He asked Mrs. Bolton if she had rested well after last night's exertions and expressed concern for any headaches. Then, feigning chance, he remarked that since he was passing, he couldn't leave without checking on his "little partner."

Mrs. Bolton, initially shy and wary of such advances, melted under the torrent of Pen's inexhaustible good humor. Unable to perceive any unwelcome intent, she welcomed him. Seeking a seat, Pen found none free—covered by dishes or workboxes—so he perched atop a child's chair, an uncomfortable eminence that sent the children into fits of laughter. Fanny, however, was the most amused, marveling at His Royal Highness's condescension. To sit in a child's chair! Years later, she would gaze at that very piece of furniture, her imagination populating it with the smiling face of the man who had sat there.

Pen settled in and launched into a voluble monologue. He inquired about the undertaking business, the number of mourners accompanying Lady Estrich, and the state of the inn. He showed keen interest in Mr. Campion's cab, mentioning he'd met the driver in society. He asked about shares in the Polwheedle and Tredyddlum, whether Mrs. Bolton rented chambers there, and if any were available. He declared the inn superior to the Temple and expressed a desire to reside there permanently.

Turning to the local notables, he asked about Captain Strong and Colonel Altamont. "Wasn't he a stout man?" Pen queried with immense wit, describing the Colonel's jewelry, wig, and "very black" whiskers—which he jokingly described as "blue black," or a rich greenish-purple. This description elicited hysterical giggles from the ladies. Pen claimed to have met the Colonel at Sir Fr——'s house.

"Oh, we know," the ladies replied. "Sir F—— is Sir Francis Clavering; he visits twice weekly with the Captain. My little boy fetched bill-stamps for him." Mrs. Bolton suddenly realized she had breached confidence. "O Lor! I beg pardon, I shouldn't have mentioned no secrets," she stammered, completely charmed by Pen's manner. "But we know you're a gentleman, Mr. Pendennis, for I'm sure you've behaved like one. Hasn't Mr. Pendennis, Fanny?" Fanny loved her mother for this endorsement, casting up her dark eyes to the low ceiling. "Oh, that he has, I'm sure, Ma," she replied with deep feeling.

Curious about the bill-stamps and Strong's chambers, Pen pressed further. When Altamont arrived to join the group, Pen questioned him about his identity and social connections with practiced adroitness, driven by private motives regarding Sir Francis Clavering. The answers were simple and honest, limited by the Boltons' modest understanding of high society.

When Pen found himself at a loss for topics, he invoked his status as a journalist. "Would you ladies like any orders for the play?" he asked. Theater tickets were the ultimate delight for anyone connected to the stage. With Mr. Bolton often away due to his drinking habits and unbecoming behavior, the family longed to escape to the theater, leaving little Barney to guard the lodge. Pen's generous offer was received with boundless gratitude. Fanny clapped her hands

Arthur Pendennis spotted Mr. Bows in the hallway of Shepherd's Inn and immediately launched into a booming, cheerfully loud greeting. "How d'ye do, Mr. Bows!" he declared, offering a salute that was as dashing and offhand as a cavalry charge. Yet, despite the bravado, a distinct blush burned across Arthur's cheeks—a secret signal instantly mirrored by Fanny, whose own complexion erupted in a fluttering crimson flare.

After the requisite handshake and an ironic nod regarding Arthur's intention to visit Mr. Costigan's chambers, a heavy, guilt-ridden silence descended upon the group. It was a silence so profound that Pendennis felt compelled to break it with a violent, rattling noise. While the sound successfully shattered the quiet, it failed to dispel the gloom; indeed, the atmosphere grew darker, much like lighting a single tallow candle in a vast, unlit vault.

Desperate to lift the mood, Pendennis attempted a jocular recounting of the previous night's events, complete with a clumsy impersonation of Captain Costigan pleading with the ticket-taker at Vauxhall. It was a disaster. No one laughed. Mrs. Bolton stared blankly, utterly confused as to whether she was witnessing a satire of the ticket-taker or the Captain himself. Fanny wore an expression of genuine alarm, attempting a timid giggle that sounded more like a squeak. Even old Mr. Bows looked as grim as he did when fiddling in the orchestra or tackling a difficult sonata on the Back Kitchen piano. Realizing his performance had flopped, Pendennis's voice trailed off, flickering out until the room was plunged into darkness once more.

"You were coming to see me, sir?" Mr. Bows interjected, breaking the spell. "Won't you have the kindness to walk up to my chambers with me? You do them a great honor, I'm sure. They are rather high up, but—"

"Oh! I live in a garret myself," Pendennis interrupted sharply. "And Shepherd's Inn is twice as cheerful as Lamb Court."

"I knew you had third-floor apartments," Bows replied, his voice dripping with practiced sadness. "And I was just about to say—please don't take my remark as discourteous—that the air up three flights of stairs is far wholesomer for a gentleman than the stagnant breeze of a porter's lodge."

"Sir!" Pen snapped, his candle metaphorically reigniting in a blaze of righteous indignation. "Will you permit me to choose my society without—"

"You were so polite as to say you were about to honor my humble domicile with a visit," Bows countered, adopting a tone so artificially melancholic it sounded rehearsed. "Shall I show you the way? Mr. Pendennis and I are old friends, Mrs. Bolton—very old acquaintances. At the earliest dawn of his life, we crossed paths."

The old man gestured toward the door with a trembling finger, clutching a hat in the other hand, striking a pose that was undeniably theatrical. Though his words were drawn from the vocabulary of orators he'd heard before the stage lamps, he wasn't merely acting. Pendennis recognized the sincerity beneath the melodrama, even as he internally scoffed at the display.

"Come along, sir," Bows urged, "since you are so very pressing. Mrs. Bolton, I wish you a good day. Good-bye, Miss Fanny; I shall always cherish our night at Vauxhall. And be sure I will remember the theatre tickets." With a press of Fanny's hand, he turned and vanished.

"What a nice young man, to be sure!" Mrs. Bolton exclaimed, watching him depart.

"Do you think so, ma?" Fanny asked softly.

"I was thinking who he reminded me of," Mrs. Bolton continued, gazing through the window curtains as Pen ascended the courtyard with Bows. "When I was at the Wells with Mrs. Serle, there was a young gentleman from the City who used to arrive in a tilbury, wearing a white coat—the very image of him, except his whiskers were black, whereas Mr. P.'s are red."

"Law, ma! They're a most beautiful hair color," Fanny observed.

"He used to come courting Emly Budd," Mrs. Bolton recounted, her voice weaving a tale of local lore. "She danced Columbine in *Arlequin Orphee*, or 'The Battle of Navarino,' when Miss De la Bosky fell ill. A pretty dancer, a fine stage figure. He was a great sugar-baker in the City, with a country house at Omerton. One day they drove in the tilbury down Goswell Street Road, got married at St. Bartholomew's Church, Smithfield, with bands playing quite privately. Now she keeps her carriage, and I saw her name in the paper as the patroness of the Mansion House Ball for the Washwomen's Asylum. And look at Lady Mirabel—Captain Costigan's daughter. She was professional, as everyone knows."

Thus spoke Mrs. Bolton, alternating between peering through the curtains and scrubbing mugs into the corner cupboard, finally folding the dinner cloth while shaking hands with Fanny.

Although Captain Costigan had previously detailed Pendennis's financial prospects with some accuracy, he may have forgotten the specifics from Chatteris years prior, or perhaps his natural enthusiasm led him to exaggerate Pen's income. That evening, he had painted Fairoaks Park in glowing hues for Mrs. Bolton, boasting of Pendennis's wealthy uncle, the Major, and displaying an intimate knowledge of Arthur's funded and landed assets. Likely, Mrs. Bolton had spent the night speculating on these riches, envisioning Fanny driving in a carriage like her old comrade, the dancer of Sadler's Wells.

As the final folds of the tablecloth were completed, the two women stood close. Fanny took the cloth, giving it the last fold, while her mother slid a finger under the young girl's chin to kiss her. Once again, the red signal flew across Fanny's cheeks, fluttering wildly. This time, however, it was not alarm. It was pure, sweet pleasure causing the poor little Fanny to blush.

*Poor little Fanny!* Is love a sin because it tastes so sweet at the start and so bitter at the end?

Mrs. Bolton then announced she was going out on business, leaving Fanny to guard the lodge. After a faint protest, Fanny agreed. Mrs. Bolton departed with her bonnet and basket, and the moment she was gone, Fanny moved to the window overlooking Bows's door, refusing to look anywhere else.

In a corner, Betsy-Jane and Amelia-Ann buzzed around a picture book held upside-down, pretending to read a grave and dreadful tract from Mr. Bolton's collection. Fanny ignored them entirely, her eyes fixed solely on the door. Finally, she gave a little shake, her eyes lighting up. *He had come out. He would pass the door.*

But her hope died instantly. Pendennis emerged, yes, but Bows followed closely behind. They passed under the archway together. Bows merely tipped his hat and bowed as he glanced in; he didn't stop to speak. Within a few minutes, Bows returned alone and entered the porter's room.

"Where's your Ma, dear?" he asked Fanny.

"I don't know," Fanny replied with an angry toss of her head. "I don't follow Ma's footsteps wherever she goes, I suppose, Mr. Bows."

"Am I my mother's keeper?" Bows muttered, his usual melancholy bitterness returning. "Come here, Betsy-Jane and Amelia-Ann. I've brought a cake for the one who can read her letters best, and another for the runner-up."

Once the girls were examined and awarded their gingerbread medals, they scampered off to eat in the courtyard. Fanny retreated to her work, pretending to sew while her mind raced with excitement and anger, her needle moving mechanically. Bows sat positioned to command the view of the entrance from the lodge to the street. But the person he seemingly awaited never appeared.

Soon, Mrs. Bolton returned from the market to find Mr. Bows sitting exactly where she had expected to see her husband. The reader might guess the identity of the missing man.

The interview between Bows and his guest in the cramped apartment shared with the descendant of the Milesian kings was hardly satisfactory for either party. Pendennis was sulky. If Bows had anything to say, he clearly preferred not to utter it in front of Captain Costigan, who had vacated his bedchamber mere moments before Pen's arrival. Having witnessed the undressing of Major Pendennis, surely no one wishes to serve as valet to our other hero, Costigan? Before stepping out, the Captain had applied a generous amount of otto-of-whisky, emitting a rich, pungent aroma as he extended a cordial handshake. The grip was shaky; it was a marvel how the poor gentleman managed to hold the razor he used daily on his chin.

Bows's room, conversely, was immaculate. His wardrobe hung neatly behind a curtain, his books and manuscript music were trimmed on shelves, and a lithographed portrait of Miss Fotheringay as Mrs. Haller hung above his bed, signed sprawlingly by the actress. Lady Mirabel, however, wrote with far greater elegance. Since her marriage, she had diligently cultivated penmanship, signing notes with genteel grace. Bows, though, cherished the older, fairer hand of the artist. He kept only one specimen of her new style: a note replying to

Fashion has long abandoned the verdant, picturesque Temple Garden. Where Shakespeare once placed York and Lancaster to pluck the innocent white and red roses that would later stain the fields of battle, today the air is thick with smoke, and the hardiest blooms refuse to unfurl their petals. Most of the current residents of the surrounding buildings neither notice nor care if a rose exists there, unless they are rushing past the old gate en route to a lawyer's office. Attorneys' clerks do not carry bouquets in their satchets or posies tucked under their arms as they dash to counsel chambers. Even the few lawyers who still take constitutional walks have lost interest in the rivalry of York and Lancaster, particularly since the railway boom has taken center stage.

Only antiquarians and literary enthusiasts remain to gaze upon the gardens with genuine affection, conjuring up visions of Sir Roger de Coverley and the sharp-faced Mr. Spectator pacing the gravel paths. Or perhaps they imagine Oliver Goldsmith resting in the summerhouse, pondering his next *Citizen of the World*, worrying about the new suit Mr. Filby is tailoring for him, or fretting over a dunning letter from Mr. Newbery. One might even spot the Great Doctor approaching Goldsmith, clad in a snuff-colored suit and a wig desperately needing a barber's touch, accompanied by his Scotch lackey trailing behind like a loyal dog slightly tipsy from port wine at the Mitre. Mr. Johnson invites the poet home for tea with Miss Williams. Oh, the enduring power of Fancy! These fictional figures feel as tangible as the historical characters we've just described.

Since Arthur Pendennis possessed a distinctly romantic and literary soul, far removed from the dry legalities dominating the neighborhood, it is safe to assume he was indulging in similar poetic reveries. This occurred on the evening following the events of the previous chapter. Having chosen the Temple Gardens for exercise and contemplation, he found the atmosphere uniquely suited to his mood. Sundays usually bring a profound calm to the Temple. The chambers lie mostly empty; the senior lawyers are hosting grand dinners in the Belgravia or Tyburn districts, while the promising young barristers are off paying homage to Mr. Kewsy's fine claret or Mr. Justice Ermine's accomplished daughters. Those left behind at the Club are enjoying an economical joint and a modest pint of wine, regaling one another with circuit jokes and points of law.

The only souls inhabiting their offices are the unfortunate Mr. Cockle, bedridden and subsisting on gruel prepared by his laundress; Mr. Toodle, a flute enthusiast whose solo practice echoes from his second-floor chamber; and young Tiger, the student, whose open window belches clouds of cigar smoke while piles of dirty dishes from "Dicks" or "The Cock" accumulate outside. But wait! It is vacation time. With the exception of Pendennis, the chambers are entirely deserted. Perhaps it was this solitude that drew him to the garden. Though he had previously passed the gate with casual indifference, noting the neat flower beds and the leisurely citizens strolling the lawn, tonight something compelled him. After dining alone at a nearby tavern, he decided to return home via the gardens, seeking the fresh evening air and the gleaming waters of the Thames.

After a brief stroll, admiring the peaceful groups scattered about, fatigue set in. He retreated to one of the summerhouses flanking the main path and sat down modestly. What filled his mind? The evening was brilliantly clear and tranquil. The sky held not a cloud; the chimneys across the river remained silent; the wharves and warehouses glowed rosy in the sunlight, appearing freshly scrubbed for the holiday. Steamers raced up and down the stream, packed with holidaymakers. From the multitude of city churches, the evening bells rang out for prayer—peaceful Sabbath sounds that reminded Pen of his childhood, when he had walked with his arm around his mother's waist on the terrace at home. The sun was casting a golden light on the little village of Brawl as well as the broad Thames, dipping majestically behind the Clavering elms and the tower of the familiar village church. Were these memories, or simply the beauty of the sunset, causing the flush on the young man's cheeks?

He tapped his foot to the rhythm of the distant bells, flicked dust from his polished boots with his handkerchief, then sprang up, stamped his foot, and declared, "No, by Jove, I'll go home." This resolve suggested an internal struggle regarding propriety—he debated whether to linger or depart—and he stepped out of the summerhouse. Almost immediately, he collided with two small children, who barely reached his knees. They were trotting along the gravel path, their long blue shadows stretching toward the east. One cried out, "Oh!" while the other burst into laughter, delivering a knowing, infantile chuckle: "Missa Pendennis!"

Arthur looked down and recognized his two little friends from the day before, Mesdemoiselles Ameliar-Ann and Betsy-Jane. A deeper blush covered his face. Seizing the child he had nearly bumped into, he lifted her into the air and kissed her. To this sudden assault, Ameliar-Ann responded with a scream of terror. Her cry summoned two ladies dressed impeccably in clean collars, new ribbons, and grand shawls: Mrs. Bolton, draped in a rich scarlet Caledonian Cashmere and black silk, and Miss F. Bolton, wearing a yellow scarf, sprigged muslin, and carrying a parasol—truly the epitome of ladyhood.

Fanny said not a word. Yet, her eyes flashed a warm welcome, shining brighter than the most dazzling windows in Paper Buildings. Mrs. Bolton, however, after scolding Betsy-Jane, exclaimed, "Lor, sir—how very odd that we should meet you year! I ope you ave your ealth well, sir. Ain't it odd, Fanny, that we should meet Mr. Pendennis?"

*What do you mean by sniggering, Mesdames?* Arthur thought internally. *When young Croesus stays at a country house, haven't you, by some singular coincidence, been walking with your Fanny in the shrubberies? Haven't you and your Frances happened to listen to the band of the Heavies at Brighton when young De Boots and Captain Padmore arrived? Haven't you chanced upon old Widow Wheezy's cottage when the young curate dropped in with a tract for rheumatism?* He reasoned that if coincidences occurred at the Hall, they surely happened at the Lodge. It was merely fate.

Earlier, during conversation, Mr. Pendennis had innocently remarked that while some courts were gloomy, parts of the Temple were cheerful, especially the riverside chambers and the gardens, which made for delightful Sunday walks crowded with people. Thus, by sheer chance, everyone gathered together, much like the genteel crowds of polite society. How artless, good-natured, and natural it all seemed!

Pen appeared grave, pompous, and dandified. His attire was strikingly brilliant: white duck trousers, a white hat, a multi-colored neckcloth, a light waistcoat, gold chains, and shirt studs gave him the air of a prince of the blood. How splendidly the outfit suited his figure! Someone whispered, "Was anybody ever like him?" Another added, "How his blushes become him!"

The children, having been captivated by him the previous day, had played at being him afterward. Ameliar-Ann, mimicking Pen's habit of sticking her fingers into the armholes of her pinafore, had declared, "Now, Bessy-Jane, I'll be Missa Pendennis." Fanny had laughed until she cried, burying her sister in kisses for the imitation. How joyful she was to see Arthur embrace the child! While Arthur's face burned red, Fanny appeared remarkably pale and weary.

Noticing her condition, Arthur asked kindly why she looked so fatigued.
"I was awake all night," Fanny replied, blushing slightly.
"You were reading!" Pen observed, amused. "And what was it that interested you so?"
"Oh, it's so beautiful!" Fanny sighed. "'Walter Lorraine.' How I do hate that Neaera—I don't know the pronunciation. And I love Leonora, and Walter, oh, how dear he is!"

How had Fanny discovered the novel *Walter Lorraine* and realized Pen was its author? This little person remembered every word Mr. Pendennis had spoken the night before, recalling how he wrote in books and newspapers. Eager to learn more, she almost considered being civil to old Bows, who had been under her displeasure since yesterday, but decided instead to approach Captain Costigan.

She began by coaxing the Captain, smiling in her most winning manner as she arranged his dinner and tidied his humble room. She noted his linen needed mending (indeed, his closet held curious specimens of flawed fabric) and offered to mend all his shirts. "What horrid holes—what funny holes!" she giggled, poking her face through one, laughing at the old warrior in the most charming way possible. She then cleared his dinner tray, danced lightly to his cupboard, retrieved his whisky bottle, mixed a tumbler, and insisted on tasting a tiny drop. In exchange, the Captain sang one of his dear songs, teaching it to her. As he performed an Irish melody in his rich, quavering voice, pretending to fascinate the little siren, she finally asked about Arthur Pendennis and his novel. Once satisfied with the answer, she cared for nothing else. Leaving the Captain at the piano, the dinner tray

Let's revisit the charming little town of Clavering, that same spot where Pen's ancestral home, Fairoaks, stands nearby. We've mentioned it before, mostly because the local society wasn't exactly known for its wit or charm, so our previous reports were brief. However, things get interesting when we introduce Mr. Samuel Huxter. Remember him? The lively fellow we met at Vauxhall. During his vacations, Sam was the lifeblood of Clavering. He'd drag his friends into his mother's parlors, regaling them with the sharp repartee of Bartholomew's Club and the latest gossip from London's high-society circles.

Then there was Mr. Hobnell, the young scoundrel Pen once thrashed over the Fotheringay incident. While a student at the Grammar School, Hobnell was Mrs. Huxter's favorite guest. He knew exactly how to sneak past the surgery door to raid the tamarind pots and could scent his handkerchiefs with rosewater faster than anyone else. It was during this golden era that he fell for Miss Sophy Huxter. After her father passed, he married her and moved them to "The Warren," a few miles out of town.

The Hobnells used to be simple yeomen, but Hobnell Sr. decided to upgrade. He tore down the old farmhouse and erected a glaring white mansion complete with massive stables, a grand piano in the drawing room, and a pack of hunting hounds. With those additions, they officially became County Gentry. When the elder Hobnell died, his son inherited the title of Squire. Meanwhile, Sam Huxter, back in London, loved nothing more than bragging to his mates at Bartholomew's about his brother-in-law's hounds, horses, and endless hospitality.

Every year, usually when Mrs. Hobnell was too busy with her nursery duties to travel, Squire Hobnell would jet up to London for a spree. He'd check into the Tavistock Hotel, and he and Sam would tear through the city—Ascot, the theaters, Vauxhall, and the raucous taverns around Covent Garden. True to his motto, "do as London does," the Squire would indulge in every vice the city offered. Once he returned to the countryside, he'd gift Mrs. Hobnell a new bonnet and shawl, ready to trade urban elegance for rural sports for the next eleven months.

Sam and Hobnell maintained a perfect balance: letters full of metropolitan gossip exchanged for baskets of fresh hares, partridges, and clotted cream sent from the Warren. To the locals, Sam was the brightest star. When he arrived home, he was the center of attention. His songs, jokes, and pranks turned the Warren upside down. Legend even claimed he once saved their eldest child by fishing a bone out of her throat. Simply put, he was the delight of the village.

Unfortunately for Arthur Pendennis, fate had other plans. Just three days after their awkward encounter at Vauxhall, Pen bumped into Mr. Huxter again. Sticking to his vow, Pen hadn't visited little Fanny. He was desperately trying to drown his thoughts in work and distraction. He slaved away in his rooms, writing savage critiques for the *Pall Mall Gazette* on poems and romances that clearly deserved to die. Afterward, he retreated to the lonely Polyanthus Club, where the empty halls only made him moodier. He attended plays hoping for relief, but instead found himself bored by an "ignoble farce" that made him miserable. Seeing Pen's gloomy face would have dampened the spirits of any actor on stage.

Feeling adrift, Pen decided to visit the Back Kitchen, his old haunt with Warrington. He wasn't tired, exactly, but he needed comfort. Thinking of Bows, the pianist, gave him hope. Sure enough, Bows was there, presiding over the keys while the room erupted in laughter at some ridiculous comic songs. To Pen, it felt surreal. Inside what he claimed was an "extinct volcano" of emotion, he suddenly felt a fierce flame ignite. Two days of indulgence had started it; two days of abstinence had fueled it to madness.

As he sat there, nursing his drinks, Arthur noticed Mr. Huxter enter with two or three friends. Huxter had also been to the theater. He whispered something to his companions that annoyed Pen, then sauntered across the room, nodded familiarly, and extended a dirty hand for a handshake. Pen, feeling unnecessarily harsh after their last meeting, shook it. Huxter, however, was in a jovial mood. The slight disagreement at Vauxhall was a mere trifling joke to him; he couldn't imagine being disagreeable to anyone.

Deciding to make conversation, Huxter's friends suggested a topic that guaranteed disaster: the Vauxhall incident.
"A jolly night at Vauxhall, wasn't it?" Huxter winked knowingly.
"I'm glad you liked it," Pen groaned internally.
"I was terribly upset," Huxter continued, eyeing Pen's arm. "Been dining with some chaps at Greenwich. That was a pretty bit of muslin hanging on your arm—who was she?"

That question broke Pen.
"Have I asked you any questions about yourself, Mr. Huxter?" Pen snapped.
"Oh, beg pardon!" Huxter looked genuinely surprised. "I didn't mean offense. Hang it, you're acting quite savage."
"Do you remember what happened the other night?" Pen demanded, his anger rising.
"You forget? Probably. You were tipsy and rude," Huxter replied smoothly.
"Hang it, sir, I apologized!" Huxter blushed slightly.
"Yes, and I accepted it gladly. But recall my request: omit me from your acquaintance list forever. If we meet in public, do not bother recognizing me. Please remember this henceforth."

Huxter grabbed his hat, bowed to the stunned Pen, and exited. His friends paused in wonder before erupting into a roar of laughter, loud enough to summon the room's president, who shouted, "Silence, gentlemen! Do have silence for the 'Body Snatcher'!"—the popular song currently playing as Pen fled the room.

Pen convinced himself he had mastered his temper. He actually wished Huxter had challenged him to a fight; violence would have been preferable to this humiliation. He went home, but nothing soothed him. Work, dinner, the play, the whiskey, and the quarrel left him unrested. He slept poorly.

A few days later, Sam Huxter wrote a letter to his brother-in-law, Squire Hobnell, detailing Arthur's "confounded insolence" in London. Sam painted a damning portrait: an "abandoned criminal," a "regular Don Juan," and a danger to honest households. He claimed to have seen Pen dancing with an innocent girl from the lower ranks and, thanks to an Irish ex-army officer in his club, knew exactly who she was. He threatened to warn her father. The letter ended with polite thanks for the recent basket of game and a hint that more favors were welcome.

Coincidentally, a christening at the Warren occurred the very next day. The baby girl was named Myra Lucretia, after her godmothers. Since Hobnell had shown Sam's letter to his wife, Mrs. Hobnell immediately shared the scandalous tale with her gossip network. By afternoon, the entire town of Clavering knew the story.

Myra was too shocked to speak, but Mrs. Pybus had no such reservations. She discussed the matter with everyone from Mrs. Portman to Madame Fribsby, ordering the children out of the room whenever the topic arose. Madame Fribsby, peering at her portrait of a dragoon, sighed that "men will be men" and quoted *Marmion*. Mrs. Pybus, however, reserved her strongest condemnation for Pen, blaming his "early indulgence," "aristocratic airs," and attendance at "corrupt modern Babylonian parties." Mrs. Portman worried that literary success had turned Pen's head, causing him to forget the morals taught by Dr. Portman. Even Captain Glanders, the Dragoon, whistled and joked about it until Mrs. Glanders silenced him by sending the girls away.

At the local chapel, Pen became the subject of a second sermon. The congregation, eager to throw stones, assumed his guilt without hearing his side. They preached against the dangers of London and the sins of novel-writing, ignoring the fact that they were condemning an innocent man based on hearsay.

The following day, Mrs. Pendennis, trembling with fear and exhaustion, rushed to Dr. Portman's house. She held an anonymous letter—a vicious attack signed with biblical threats, accusing Pen of sin and detailing his alleged crimes. Terrified, she had dropped the letter, allowing her daughter Laura to read it. Laura blushed and trembled with rage.
"The cowards!" Laura cried. "It isn't true!"
"It is true, and you've done it, Laura!" Helen screamed hysterically. "Why did you refuse him? Why did you break my heart? You led him into this! You threw him into the arms of this woman! Don't speak to me! Never forgive you!"

She stormed out, refusing help, leaving Laura and the servants behind. Dr. Portman glanced at the letter, recognized the handwriting, and knew the accusation. Despite his better nature, he tried to console Helen, suggesting the rumor might be false and that Pen should be heard before judgment. But Helen saw right

Doctor Portman's letter was dispatched to London, and the good clergyman busied himself soothing Mrs. Pendennis into a semblance of composure until a reply arrived—a response he optimistically believed (or at least stubbornly maintained) would vindicate Mr. Pen's morality. For the moment, however, Helen's plan to travel to London and confront her son personally was impossible. The apothecary strictly forbade her leaving her bed, let alone venturing to Fairoaks, for the first twenty-four hours. By the following morning, she was finally permitted to return to her sofa at home, attended by the faithful, albeit silent, Laura.

Unfortunately for everyone involved, Mr. Pen never read Doctor Portman's earnest homily until weeks after it had been written. Day after day, the widow awaited her son's reply to the accusations laid against him, her own health deteriorating with every passing hour. It was a harrowing trial for Laura: to witness her dearest friend's suffering, to endure Helen's estrangement, and to absorb the sting of that withheld affection. Yet, this young lady possessed a remarkable capacity for endurance. Through the "gracious assistance" of Heaven, poured out via her pure and constant prayers, she fulfilled her duty. Since this duty was performed in absolute silence—her supplications taking place in her private chamber, invisible to mortal eyes—we, too, must remain silent regarding these virtues. They are as unsuited for public display as a flower blooming in a ballroom. We need only say that a good woman is the loveliest flower under heaven; we gaze with love and wonder at her silent grace, her pure fragrance, and her delicate beauty. Alas, it is a tragedy to see such flowers bowed down or consumed by inexorable grief, wasting in disease, pining under long pain, or snatched away by sudden fate in their prime. We may deserve sorrow, but why should *these* be unhappy? Only because Heaven chastises those it loves most, refining pure spirits through repeated trials.

So, the letter never reached Pen. Despite being duly posted and faithfully delivered by the postman into his letterbox in Lamb Court, it was subsequently carried by the laundress to his writing table amidst the rest of his correspondence. Recall, if you will, the portrait of him entering from his small bedroom just as Mrs. Flanagan, his laundress, was in the act of drinking his gin.

For those readers who have tracked Mr. Arthur's career thus far, the prevailing flaw in his character and his greatest adversary should now be obvious. Many among us, my beloved public, struggle with the very same rascal. This scoundrel seizes every opportunity to drag us into mischief, plunge us into quarrels, and lead us toward idleness and bad company. In short, Pen's greatest enemy was himself. Having pampered, coaxed, and indulged this internal rogue all his life, the fellow had grown insolent, much like a spoiled servant. At the slightest attempt at coercion or the demand to perform an unpleasant task, he became frantically rude and unruly. While someone accustomed to sacrifice, like Laura, who regularly gives up her pleasures for others, handles such demands with ease, Pen—who was unused to any self-denial—suffered grievously when called upon to pay his share, grumbling savagely at the prospect of forgoing anything he desired.

Consequently, he resolved never to see Fanny again. He vowed he wouldn't. To banish the image of that fascinating little person from his mind, he threw himself into constant occupation, vigorous exercise, and dissipation. He worked excessively, walked and rode too much, and consumed food, drink, and tobacco in prodigious quantities. Yet, despite the endless stream of cigars and punch, little Fanny's image remained burned into his inflamed brain. After a week of this rigorous discipline and self-denial, our young gentleman ended up in bed with a fever. Let any reader who has never suffered a fever in a garret pity the wretch condemned to endure such a calamity.

A committee of marriageable ladies, or any Christian group dedicated to propagating domestic virtues, should commission a Cruikshank or a Leech to illustrate the horrors of bachelor life in chambers. Consider the bachelor's lonely breakfast: the black kettle simmering in a dreary fire in midsummer, or worse, the fire extinguished at Christmas, half an hour after the laundress has departed the sitting-room. The owner enters shivering, forced to hunt for coal and wood before beginning his studies, effectively performing the duties of a housemaid in the absence of Mrs. Flanagan. Or consider the bachelor's shirt—the garment needed just at dinner-time, found lacking buttons. Picture, furthermore, the bachelor's return after a merry Christmas spent in a cozy country house filled with pretty faces and kind welcomes. He leaves his portmanteau at the barber's, lights his dismal candle at the sputtering stair-lamp, and enters his blank, familiar room. The only tokens greeting him are Christmas bills, amiably spread across his reading table. Add to this an appalling depiction of bachelor illness, and the rents in the Temple would surely plummet. To be well in chambers is melancholy and selfish enough; but to be *ill* in chambers—to spend long nights in pain and watchfulness, longing for the morning and the laundress, serving oneself medicine while watching one's own fevered thoughts, with no kind hand to offer water or smooth the hot pillow—is a fate so dismal and tragic that we shall not enlarge upon its horrors. We can only heartily pity those bachelors in the Temple who brave it daily.

This lot befell Arthur Pendennis after the excesses described above. One night he went to bed ill; the next, he awoke worse. His only visitors that day, aside from the laundress, were the Printer's Devil from the *Pall Mall Gazette*, whom the narrator tried desperately to appease. These efforts to finish his work exacerbated his fever. He could produce only a fraction of his usual copy. With Shandon absent and Warrington not in London, the political and editorial columns of the *Gazette* looked dreadingly blank, leaving the sub-editor at a loss.

Mr. Finucane rushed to Pen's chambers and found the gentleman so exceedingly unwell that the good-natured Irishman immediately stepped in to fill his place. He produced a series of political and critical compositions that undoubtedly greatly edified the readers. Magnificent allusions to the greatness of Ireland and the genius of its inhabitants flowed freely from Finucane's pen. Shandon, the Chief Editor, who was relaxing placidly at Boulogne-sur-Mer, received the journal and instantly recognized the handwriting of the great Sub-editor. Laughing, he tossed the paper to his wife, "Mary, look here! Here is Jack at work again!" Indeed, Jack was a warm friend and a gallant partisan. When holding the pen, he rarely missed an opportunity to inform the world that Rafferty was the greatest painter in Europe, lamenting the petty jealousy of the Academy; to report that Mr. Rooney, M.P., was appointed Governor of Barataria; or to insert compliments to the Round Towers or the Giant's Causeway into any subject. Beyond doing Pen's work, his kind-hearted comrade offered to forego his Saturday and Sunday holidays to act as nurse-tender to Arthur. However, Arthur insisted that Finucane not sacrifice his pleasure, assuring him he could bear his malady alone.

After finishing the paper's work and eating supper at the Back Kitchen on Friday night, Finucane informed Captain Costigan of their young friend's illness in the Temple. Remembering the matter two days later, the Captain visited the invalid on Sunday afternoon.

He found Mrs. Flanagan, the laundress, weeping in the sitting-room. She had received a terrible report of the poor young gentleman inside. Pen's condition had so terrified her that she resorted to brandy to sustain herself against the grief his illness caused. Hanging about his bed and attempting to minister to him, her attentions became intolerable to the invalid, who begged her peevishly to stay away. This drove the laundress to fresh tears and renewed applications to the bottle, which she used as an anodyne. The Captain reprimanded the woman soundly for her intemperance, pointing out the fatal consequences of persisting in such imprudent behavior.

Pen, though in a highly fevered state, was delighted by Costigan's arrival. Hearing the familiar voice in the sitting room while lying in the bedroom, he called the Captain eagerly, thanking him profusely and begging him to sit and talk. The Captain checked the young man's pulse with gravity—his own trembling, clammy hand steadying momentarily as his fingers pressed Arthur's throbbing vein. The pulse beat fiercely; Pen's face was haggard and hot; his eyes were bloodshot and gloomy. His "bird," as the Captain termed it, hadn't been shaved in nearly a week. Pen gestured for his visitor to sit, tossing and turning in his comfortless bed, before attempting to chat lively about the Back Kitchen, Vauxhall, future outings, and Fanny. "How is little Fanny?" he asked.

Indeed, how was she? We know she returned home very sadly the previous Sunday evening after seeing Arthur light his lamp in his chambers during his interview with Bows. Bows returned to his own rooms shortly after, passing the lodge door and glancing into Mrs. Bolton's as per his custom, but with a deeply melancholy countenance. She endured another weary night. Her restlessness woke her little bedfellows repeatedly.

Fanny's heart sank the moment she spotted the two ladies entering the room. The elder woman's face was a mask of terrifying anxiety, her eyes wide with a look that suggested sheer panic. In that instant, the poor girl recognized Mrs. Pendennis. There was a haunting similarity between the widow's haggard gaze and the fever-dream eyes of Arthur, tossing restlessly in his bed.

Fanny cast a desperate glance from Mrs. Pendennis to Laura. The younger woman's face was as unyielding and expressionless as a block of granite. Both newcomers radiated a cold, hard-hearted gloom; neither offered even a flicker of mercy or sympathy toward Fanny. Desperate, she turned her gaze to the Major standing behind them. Old Pendennis lowered his eyelids, peeking up at Arthur's tiny nurse with a stealthy, worried scrutiny.

"I—I wrote to you yesterday, if you please, ma'am," Fanny stammered, her limbs trembling violently as she spoke. She was as pale as Laura, whose sad, menacing visage loomed over Mrs. Pendennis's shoulder.

"Did you, madam?" Mrs. Pendennis replied coolly. "I suppose I may now relieve you from nursing my son. After all, I am his mother, you understand."

"Yes, ma'am. I—this is the way to his—" Fanny began, but stopped abruptly. "Oh, wait a minute!" she cried out. "I must prepare you for his..."

The widow, whose face had previously been hopelessly cruel and ruthless, suddenly recoiled with a sharp gasp and a stifled cry.

"He's been so since yesterday," Fanny whispered, her teeth chattering uncontrollably.

At that moment, a horrific shriek of laughter erupted from the open doorway of Pen's room. Following several raucous shouts, the poor wretch began singing a college drinking song, then hurrahed and shouted as if he were in the thick of a wine party, thumping his fists against the wainscoting. He was completely delirious.

"He does not know me, ma'am," Fanny sobbed.

"Indeed. Perhaps he will recognize his mother," Mrs. Pendennis said briskly. "Let me pass, if you please, and go in to him." With that, the widow hurried past little Fanny and swept through the dark passage leading to Arthur's sitting-room. Laura sailed by without a word, and Major Pendennis followed close behind.

Left alone, Fanny collapsed onto a bench in the hallway. She wept and prayed as best she could. She would have died for him, yet they hated her. These "fine ladies" offered not a single word of thanks or kindness. She sat there in the passage, unsure of how long she remained. They never emerged to speak to her. Finally, the silence was broken by the sound of Doctor Goodenough arriving for his second visit of the day. He found the poor little thing crouching at the door.

"What, nurse? How's your patient?" the kindly doctor asked warmly. "Has he had any rest?"

"Go and ask them. They're inside," Fanny replied flatly.

"Who? His mother?"

Fanny nodded silently, refusing to speak further.

"You must go to bed yourself, my poor little maid," the Doctor advised gently. "You will fall ill too if you don't."

"Oh, mayn't I come and see him? Mayn't I come and see him! I—I love him so!" the little girl pleaded. As she spoke, she fell to her knees, clutching the Doctor's hand in such an agony of devotion that it melted the physician's heart, causing a mist to cloud his spectacles.

"Pooh, pooh! Nonsense! Nurse, has he taken his draught? Has he had any rest? Of course you must come and see him. So must I."

"They'll let me sit here, won't they, sir? I'll never make no noise. I only ask to stop here," Fanny insisted.

The Doctor labeled her a "stupid little thing," tapped her pale cheek with his finger, placed her back on the bench where Pen's printer's devil used to sit for hours, and bustled into the far room.

Inside, Mrs. Pendennis was ensconced in a great chair by Arthur's bedside, looking pale and solemn. Her watch lay on the medicine-laden table beside her. Her bonnet and cloak were draped in the window. She held her Bible in her lap—a necessity she never traveled without. Upon entering, her first act had been to retrieve Fanny's shawl and bonnet from Arthur's drawers, bringing them out and dropping them onto the study table. She had shut the door firmly on the Major and Laura, effectively claiming possession of her son.

She had harbored a terrible fear that Arthur wouldn't recognize her, but that pang was largely spared. Pen knew his mother perfectly; he smiled and nodded familiarly. Upon her entrance, he instantly imagined they were back home at Fairoaks. He began talking, chatting, and laughing in a rambling, wild fashion. Laura could hear him clearly from the hall. His laughter sent shafts of pure poison into her heart. It was true, then. He had been guilty—an intrigue with a servant-maid! She had loved him, and now he was dying, likely raving and unrepentant. The Major occasionally hummed a word of consolation, which Laura barely registered.

It was a dismal sitting for everyone involved. Just as the atmosphere grew heavy, Doctor Goodenough appeared like an angel.

The doctor's role extends far beyond the sick man; it is equally vital for the sick man's friends. His presence often brings as much relief to the family as to the patient. We have all watched him approach with eager anticipation—the thrill of hearing his carriage wheels roll down the street, the suspense as he reaches the door, the hanging on his every word, seeking comfort in a simple smile. Have we not all seen mothers peering intently into a physician's face, searching for hope in the eyes of a silent, fever-stricken child? How she gazes! What thanks if there is light there; what grief if he casts his eyes down, unable to utter the word "hope."

Or consider the father struck down. The terrified wife watches while the physician checks the pulse, suppressing her agonies as children are ordered to cease their play. Standing over the feverish patient, surrounded by the expectant wife and unconscious children, the Doctor becomes Fate itself—the dispenser of life and death. He must decide whether to grant respite. One can imagine the crushing weight of responsibility on a conscientious man: the cruelty of administering the wrong remedy, the torment of wondering if better could have been done, the harassment of sympathizing with survivors in tragic cases, and the immense, overwhelming delight of victory.

After a brief introduction to the new arrivals, whose arrival the heartbroken nurse had reported, the Doctor examined the patient. The high fever was undeniable, requiring immediate and strong antiphlogistic remedies. He consoled the distraught mother with the most comforting assurances he dared offer—that there was no cause for despair yet, citing the boy's youth, constitutional strength, and so on. Having done his utmost to calm the alarmed matron, he ushered the elder Pendennis into the empty room (formerly Warrington's) for a private consultation.

"The case is very critical," the Doctor noted. "If the fever isn't stopped immediately, it will carry off the young man. He must be bled forthwith. The mother must be informed of this necessity."

"Why was that other young lady brought along?" he asked, gesturing vaguely. "She is entirely out of place in a sickroom."

"And there was another woman still, be hanged to it!" the Major interjected. "The—the little person who opened the door." His sister-in-law had retrieved the poor little devil's bonnet and shawl, throwing them onto the study table. Did Goodenough know anything about the—the little person? "I just caught a glimpse of her as we passed in," the Major added. "Begad, she was uncommonly nice-looking."

The Doctor looked queasy. He smiled. Even in the gravest moments, with life and death hanging in the balance, strange contrasts and opportunities for humor arise. Such smiles pass, serving to satirize the gloom, thereby making it even deeper.

"I have it," the Doctor finally declared, re-entering the study. He hastily wrote two notes at the table and sealed one. Taking up Fanny's shawl and bonnet along with the notes, he stepped out into the passage to find the poor little messenger.

"Quick, nurse," he instructed. "You must carry this to the surgeon and bid him come instantly. Then go to my house in Hanover Square, ask for my servant Harbottle, tell him to prepare this prescription, and wait until I return. It may take a little time to compound."

Poor Fanny trudged away with her two notes. She found the apothecary in the Strand, who arrived promptly with his lancet in his pocket to operate on the patient. Then Fanny headed for the Doctor's house.

By the time the prescription was fully compounded—a process that took Harbottle, the Doctor's servant, an unusually long time—the Doctor was already home again. For the remainder of Arthur's illness, Fanny never appeared in the capacity of nurse at his chambers once more. However, for that day and the next, a small, sad figure could be seen lurking about Pen's staircase. A sorrowful little face

It is time to record a rather embarrassing fact about Pendennis. While it might seem undignified to admit such a flaw in the biography of a novel's protagonist and godfather, the truth demands to be told to the public reading his genuine memoirs. After falling ill with fever and succumbing to the pangs of love, Pendennis underwent a rigorous medical regimen: he was bled, blistered, had his head shaved, and subjected to whatever treatments the doctor prescribed. Once his body recovered, his mind followed suit. Just as surely as the fever broke, the infatuation vanished. He was no longer smitten with Fanny Bolton; he was as indifferent to her as anyone else who is too wise—or too moral—to let their hearts wander after the daughters of porters.

Lying on his pillow, Pendennis chuckled at himself, reflecting on this "second cure." He couldn't fathom why he'd ever cared for Fanny. Dissecting his own dead passion for his poor little nurse, he concluded that neither her wit, breeding, nor beauty held the charm he once attributed to her. Hundreds of women were prettier. The fault wasn't in her; it was in his own feverish imagination. She remained unchanged, but the eyes that viewed her had shifted.
