He felt a friendly disposition toward the "little thing," but the violent adoration of weeks prior had fled, vanquished by the pill and lancet that cured his flesh. A profound sense of relief washed over him (though he admitted a touch of selfishness to this feeling); he realized he had resisted temptation precisely when the danger was greatest, leaving him free of guilt regarding his conduct toward the young girl. Looking back at the "Fanny Bolton snare" from which he had escaped, he felt a strange mix of comfort and shame at the ease with which he had stopped loving.

Amidst this internal shift, the kind smiles and tender vigilance of his mother at his bedside filled him with peace. Seeing health return was all the unwearied nurse required; executing his slightest whim was her supreme joy. Surrounded by her love, he felt a gratitude akin to that of a child recovering from weakness.

Though Pen vaguely recalled Fanny nursing him during his delirium, the memory was so foggy he couldn't distinguish reality from fever-dreams. Consequently, just as he hadn't previously confided in his mother about Fanny, he now withheld his current sentiments from her. It was an unfortunate lack of trust on both sides; a few timely words might have spared his mother and her circle considerable anguish.

Mrs. Pendennis, seeing Miss Bolton installed as nurse and caring for her son, naturally interpreted the intimacy between the two young people as proof of Arthur's alleged misconduct. Why stop to inquire? Stories damaging to a man's reputation tend to find fertile ground among those who adore him most. Wasn't a wife often the first to suspect her husband? Poor Pen absorbed a heavy dose of this suspicious affection from his nurse. The pure-hearted woman believed her boy had suffered a malady far worse than physical fever—one that stained his character as well as his strength. She bore this silent terror, masking her dread with a forced cheerfulness.

Meanwhile, at Boulogne, Captain Shandon read the latest *Pall Mall Gazette* and remarked to Mrs. Shandon that Jack Finucane's handwriting was absent from the leading articles, replaced by Mr. Warrington. "I know the crack of his whip," Shandon observed, using a brutal metaphor comparing Warrington's criticism to a butcher's cleaver. Mrs. Shandon protested, defending Warrington's kindness to the children, but Shandon retorted that while he was gentle with kids, he was savage with men. He urged his wife to avoid understanding newspaper politics, preferring the easy life at Boulogne where wine flowed freely. "Mix us another tumbler, Mary," he commanded, dismissing the grim reality with a Latin quote and a toast to future endeavors.

Indeed, Warrington stepped in with vigor to write Pen's share of the paper. He penned articles, reviews, and theatrical critiques with his characteristic ferocity. Though his hand was too powerful for such delicate subjects, he delighted in telling Arthur's mother, uncle, and sister that no pen was more graceful or elegant than Arthur's. "The people here don't understand style," he explained to Mrs. Pendennis, claiming Arthur as his own charge. Despite admitting a bit of wilfulness and dandyism in the youth, Warrington swore he knew no more honest or loyal creature, noting that while Arthur's pen could be wicked, his heart was as kind as a young lady.

Helen sighed deeply, and Laura felt wounded, yet both were grateful for Warrington's high praise of Arthur. Major Pendennis echoed these sentiments with unusual enthusiasm, declaring Warrington a true gentleman of the ancient Suffolk lineage, a man of immense talent destined for greatness. Laura blushed as the Major spoke, secretly speculating that Warrington's melancholy demeanor hid a tragic romantic attachment.

Warrington secured chambers near Grenier's in Flag Court. After working tirelessly in the mornings, he spent his afternoons visiting the sick man and the household. He even escorted Miss Bell for walks in the Temple Gardens. When Laura asked her mother's permission, the Major eagerly approved, assuring her that everyone walked there safely under the watch of beadles. With the moral arbiter's blessing, Helen allowed her daughter the fresh air of the river, delighted to see her return rosy-cheeked and spirited.

Prior to this, Laura and Helen had reached a fragile truce. When news of Pen's critical illness arrived, Laura insisted on traveling to London with the terrified mother. Even after Helen initially refused, and later when hope seemed lost due to Pen's scandalous behavior, Laura tearfully confessed her secret love. Sharing their grief softened the blow, allowing them to endure the journey together.

Now, consider the precarious position of Fanny Bolton. Facing judgment from such stern observers, she faced swift condemnation. Women are notoriously harsh critics in matters of morality; they form a vigilant network to protect their own, ready to tear apart any who stray. The narrator, adopting the persona of a Turkish judge, warns these female "Mahmouds" to exercise extreme caution before sentencing. Ensure the accused truly deserves the sack before casting them into the Bosphorus. If she is guilty, let justice prevail; if not, spare her.

Despite the tension surrounding Fanny, the Major encouraged Warrington's friendship with Laura. Their interactions were innocent and refreshing. Warrington, encountering a woman of purity and high mind for the first time, found her captivating. Similarly, Laura, isolated and longing for connection, discovered in Warrington a man of natural power, humor, and sincerity—a stark contrast to Pen's dandyish indifference and languid arrogance. Where Pen was a spoiled despot, cloyed by obedient slaves, Warrington offered energy, respect, and a hearty humanity.

How, then, did Laura develop such a rapturous devotion for Pen? During the journey from Fairoaks to London, she recounted endless tales of his heroic childhood deeds—braving tooth extractions, sparing birds' nests, sharing food with beggars. These stories, sung through tears, painted a picture of a saintly figure. Yet, the narrator points out that Pen is merely an ordinary man. Why does a sensible girl love him? Because she sees no one else at Fairoaks, hears him constantly praised, and possesses an innate human need to attach herself to someone. Love, the narrator suggests, is often less about the beloved and more about the lover's own nature and thirst for connection. Laura fell for Pen because he was available, praised, and gentlemanly, and because her heart needed an anchor. Once she formed this image, she cherished it fiercely.

Later, when fate scattered the group across Lamb Court, they might have reflected on how happy those days were. The Major developed a newfound appreciation for London in September, finding the "dead season" surprisingly pleasant. He frequently visited the Temple, climbed stairs with benevolent energy, and arranged special meals at Bays's restaurant—jellies, clear soups, and aspics suitable for invalids. Thanks to Dr. Goodenough's leniency regarding alcohol, the Major orchestrated a grand celebration involving rare Amontillado sent by the Marquis of Steyne. The widow and Laura drank respectfully despite disliking the taste, while Pen, invigorated, cheered feebly. Warrington toasted the Major and the aristocracy, teaching Laura to shout "Hear! hear!" as they clattered glasses and cards.

Inside the rooms, life hummed gently. Warrington, knowing Sibwright, accepted the offer of his chambers and furniture. A piano stood in the room, belonging to Sibwright, a talented amateur musician. Here, Laura, trembling slightly at first, played simple airs and sang old home songs. Her rich contralto voice captivated Warrington, who sat rapt, following the rhythm even if he couldn't grasp the harmony. He watched her with growing enthusiasm, admiring the pure, tender creature creating the music.

Outside, a pale girl in a black bonnet occasionally stood at the lamp-post, listening to the melodies drifting from the open windows. When Pen retired, the music ceased, lights flickered in the upper room, and the Major, Warrington, and Laura gathered for games of ecarte or backgammon. Sometimes Laura worked on a pair of worsted slippers, perhaps intended for Arthur, George, or the Major—all of whom would have given anything for them.

As the evening wound down, a shabby old gentleman led the pale girl away. The street musicians dispersed.

Since her son fell ill, Mrs. Pendennis had effectively seized command of the household, turning the young man's private quarters into a fortress of maternal oversight. She controlled access to his drawers, closets, and every scrap of clothing within them. Whether it was shirts missing buttons, stockings needing repair, or—must we admit it?—letters buried among the laundry that demanded answers while Arthur was too weak to handle them, everything flowed through her hands.

Mrs. Pendennis was driven by a mix of genuine concern and a desperate need to solve the "Fanny Bolton Mystery," a topic she had never explicitly broached with her son but which haunted her thoughts with terrifying clarity. To protect her patient from the shock of the outside world, she ordered the brass knocker removed from the inner door. Now, when the postman's jarring double knock echoed through the house, it served only to startle the invalid further. No letter reached Arthur's eyes—not from bootmakers demanding payment, nor from hatters threatening to withhold hats until his accounts were settled next Saturday.

These documents, however, did not vanish entirely. Pen, ever generous and careless with his finances, inevitably received his fair share of debt notices. Though not overwhelming, they were sufficient to terrify his scrupulous mother. Having saved diligently through her own frugality and Pen's surprising self-denial, Mrs. Pendennis had set aside a modest sum specifically to clear her son's debts. Many a respectable young man would happily surrender his mail to his parents under similar circumstances; indeed, the willingness to face the postman is often the truest test of a man's integrity. Blessed is the ear that delights in the *rat-tat* of delivery! The virtuous welcome it; the guilty tremble at its sound. Thus, Mrs. Pendennis performed a double kindness by sparing Pen both the noise and the necessity of replying during his sickness.

A thorough search of Arthur's wardrobe yielded no incriminating evidence regarding Fanny Bolton. Finding nothing, the widow turned to her brother-in-law, Major Pendennis, for guidance. One sunny afternoon at Richmond, while Pen and Warrington lounged on a terrace bench, the Major held court with Helen. She poured out her terrors, though not entirely honestly. Much like a spendthrift hiding ledgers or a fashionable wife concealing dressmaker's bills, she withheld the full extent of her anxieties, offering only a curated selection of her fears to her temporary director.

"Major," she pleaded, "what course should I take regarding this dreadful, horrid affair? Do you know anything?"

The Major puckered his face, his expression unreadable, casting a sidelong glance at her before dropping his eyes to the carpet. "My dear, good creature," he murmured, "I know nothing of it, and I prefer not to know. In my humble opinion, you had best remain ignorant as well. Young men will be young men. Begad, if you suspect our boy is a 'Jo—'"

"Pray, spare me!" Helen interjected, standing tall and dignified.

"My dear creature, I did not initiate this conversation," the Major replied with a bland bow.

"I cannot bear to hear such sins spoken of lightly!" the widow cried, tears pricking her eyes. "To think my boy committed such a crime! I wish he had died before doing it. How do I survive this? It breaks my heart, Major, to imagine his father's son—my child, whom I remember as so honorable—has fallen so dreadfully low, as to..."

"As to flirting with a little grisette?" the Major suggested gently. "Egad, if every mother in England broke her heart over such trifles—" He paused, seeing her distress. "Nay, do not agitate yourself. I cannot bear a woman's tears. But how do we know anything is serious? Has Arthur said anything?"

"His silence confirms it," she sobbed, dabbing her eyes with a pocket handkerchief.

"Not necessarily. There are subjects a young man simply cannot discuss with his mother," the Major insinuated smoothly.

"She has written to him!" the lady gasped, clutching her cambric veil.

"What, before he fell ill? Nothing more likely."

"No, since," the mourner, wearing a mask of grief, stammered. "Not before... that is, I don't think so... I—"

"Only since. And you have intercepted it, haven't you? Since he was too ill to read his own mail, you took charge, did you not?"

"I am the most unhappy mother in the world!" Helen wailed.

"The most unhappy mother in the world because your son is a man and not a hermit!" the Major retorted sharply. "Be careful, sister. If you suppress letters, you may cause a rift between you and Arthur that you will regret forever—a difference far more significant than the trivial cause that sparked it."

"There was only one letter," Helen insisted, her voice trembling. "Just a few words. Here it is—Oh, how can you speak so cruelly?"

At the mention of "only a little one," the Major found himself unable to speak, fighting back laughter despite his pity for the grieving woman. Each viewed the situation through their own moral lens; the Major's morality, as the reader knows, was less rigid than that of an ascetic.

"I advise you," he said gravely, "to seal it up—perhaps with a wafer—and return it to his pile of correspondence. Let him open it when he is well. Or, if we cannot seal it properly, mistake it for a bill."

"I cannot lie to my son," the widow declared firmly.

The letter had been slipped into the box two days prior to their departure from the Temple and delivered to Helen by Martha. Unfamiliar with Fanny's handwriting, Helen recognized the author instantly upon holding the paper. She had been waiting daily for this specific missive, opening other envelopes just to reach it. The offending note currently poisoned her bag. She extracted it and handed it to her brother-in-law.

"Arthur Pendennis, Esq.," the Major read aloud in a shaky, sprawling script, a sneer playing on his lips. "No, my dear, I refuse to read further. But you, having read it, tell me its contents. Prayers for his health in atrocious spelling, and a desire to see him? Well, there is no harm in that. And since you ask for information—" Here the Major adopted a demure expression, feigning ignorance. "As you inquire, my dear, I must inform you that Morgan, my servant, has investigated this affair. Furthermore, Doctor Goodenough looked into it. It appears this young lady was deeply infatuated with Arthur. Morgan learned from an old acquaintance—an Irish gentleman, formerly close to Pen—that she paid for her own entertainment and took her to Vauxhall Gardens. The girl's father, a violent drunkard, beats her mother, who insists on her daughter's innocence to her husband, yet simultaneously told Goodenough that Arthur treated her child like a brute. The story remains a mystery. Shall I clear it up? I need only ask Pen, and he will confirm immediately; he is as honorable a man as ever lived."

"Honorable!" the widow spat with bitter scorn. "Brother, what is this you call honor? If my boy is guilty, he must marry her. I would kneel and beg him to do so."

"Good God! Are you mad?" the Major shrieked. Suddenly, the truth struck him: if Helen prayed for this match, Arthur would undoubtedly comply. He was wild and obstinate enough to commit any folly for a woman he adored. "My dear sister, have you lost your senses?" he asked after a brief, agitated pause. Then, softening his tone, "What right do we have to assume anything passed between them? Let us see the letter. Her heart is breaking. Poor little Fanny—spelled, as you say, with a lack of decorum. But good heavens, what is there here? Only that the little devil continues to make love to him. She didn't enter his chambers until he was delirious and unaware of her presence. Flanagan, the laundress, told Morgan so. She arrived with an old gentleman, Mr. Bows, who kindly brought me away from Stillbrook. By the way, I left him in a cab and never paid the fare; he was very kind. No, there is nothing in this story."

"Do you really think so? Thank Heaven! Thank God!" Helen exclaimed. "I will take the letter to Arthur now. Look at him on the terrace with Mr. Warrington. Talking to children. My boy was always fond of children. He is innocent, thank God! Let me go to him."

Old Pendennis, however, harbored his own doubts. Just moments ago, he had championed the "not guilty" verdict, but privately, he may have judged Arthur by his own standards. If Helen confronted Arthur and he spoke the truth as a rogue usually does, the whole scheme would collapse. He made one final attempt.

"My dear, good soul," he said, seizing Helen's hand and kissing it. "Since your son has not informed you of this affair, consider if you have the right to investigate it. Believing him to be honorable, what right do you have to doubt his honor here? Who is the accuser? An anonymous scoundrel bringing no specific charges. If there were proof, wouldn't the girl's parents have stepped forward? He is not obligated to refute baseless rumors, nor are you

Could Helen have guessed that as Pen's strength returned, his unfortunate fondness for little Fanny would also wake up? Though she never said a single word about the young girl after talking to the Major, and though she pretended to ignore Fanny completely, Mrs. Pendennis kept a very sharp eye on Master Arthur. Under the excuse of bad health, she hardly let him leave her side, and was especially worried that he shouldn't have to deal with any letters for the time being. Probably Arthur looked at his own mail with a bit of a tremble; probably, as he took them at the dinner table while feeling his mother watching him (even though her good nature seemed fixed on her teacup or her book), he waited every day to see a tiny handwriting he'd never seen before, and his heart raced as he opened envelopes addressed to him. Was he happier or more annoyed that, day after day, nothing happened? Was his mind relieved because there was no letter from Fanny? Although, surely, in matters like this, when Lovelace gets tired of Clarissa (or vice versa), it's best for both sides to break ties immediately and go their separate ways, living solitary lives; yet our self-love, pity, or sense of decency doesn't like such a sudden bankruptcy. Before announcing to the world that "Lovelace & Co." can't pay its debts, we try to make deals: we hold sad partner meetings; we delay closing the shutters and making the gloomy announcement of failure. It must happen eventually: but we pawn our jewels to keep things going a little longer. On the whole, I suspect Pen was rather annoyed that Fanny didn't send any protests. What! Could she leave him and never even glance back? Could she sink without holding out a little hand or crying, "Help, Arthur?" Well, well: not everyone who sets sail on that voyage goes down. A few drown when the ship sinks; but most are just dipped and scramble onto the shore. And anyone who knows A. Pendennis, Esquire, of the Upper Temple, can tell whether that gentleman belongs to the group likely to sink or to swim.

Although Pen was still too weak to walk half a mile, and couldn't be trusted to take a carriage ride alone without a nurse, Helen couldn't keep watch on Mr. Warrington forever, and had no power to stop him from going to London if business required it. In fact, if he had gone and stayed, perhaps the widow, for her own reasons, would have been glad; but she checked these selfish thoughts as soon as she admitted them. Remembering Warrington's deep respect and service, and his constant friendship for her boy, she welcomed him almost as a family member, treating him with her usual melancholy kindness and submissive acceptance. Yet, one morning when his affairs called him to town, she guessed what Warrington's mission was: he had gone to London to find news about Fanny for Pen.

Arthur had indeed spoken with his friend and told him more fully about his adventures with Fanny (which the reader already knows) and his feelings toward her. He was very grateful he had escaped the great danger, to which Warrington heartily agreed. He felt he had no major faults to blame himself for regarding his behavior, but if they had to part—which they must—he would be glad to say, "God bless her," and hope she remembered him kindly. In his talks with Warrington, he spoke with such gravity and emotion that George, who had strongly supported the separation, began to fear his friend wasn't as healed as he claimed. That if the two met again, all the danger and temptation might have to be fought once more. With what result? "It is hard to struggle, Arthur, and easy to fall," Warrington said. "And the best courage for us poor wretches is to flee from danger. I wouldn't be what I am now if I practiced what I preach."

"And what did you practice, George?" Pen asked eagerly. "I knew there was something. Tell us about it, Warrington."

"There was something that can't be mended, and that wrecked my whole fortune early," Warrington answered. "I said I'd tell you about it someday, Pen: and I will, but not now. Take the moral without the fable now, Pen, my boy; and if you want to see a man whose entire life has been ruined by an unlucky rock he hit as a boy—here he is, Arthur: and so I warn you."

We have seen how Mr. Huxter, writing home to his Clavering friends, mentioned a fashionable London club where he met a distinguished Irish officer who gave the information about Pendennis that the young surgeon sent to Clavering. This club was none other than the Back Kitchen, where the disciple of Saint Bartholomew usually met the General. The peculiarities of the General's brogue, appearance, disposition, and conversation greatly amused many young gentlemen who used the Back Kitchen for nightly entertainment. Huxter, possessing a fine natural talent for mimicry—whether of a favorite tragic or comic actor, a rooster on a dungheap, a corkscrew plunging into a bottle, or an Irish officer of gentle connections who offered himself as an object of imitation with too much eagerness—talked his talk and twanged his poor old longbow whenever drink, an audience, and an opportunity arose. He studied our friend the General with particular gusto, drawing the honest fellow out many nights. A bait consisting of sixpence worth of brandy-and-water was always enough to make the worthy old man swallow; and under the influence of this liquor, who was happier than he to tell stories of his daughter's triumphs and his own exploits in love, war, drink, and polite society? Thus, Huxter presented his friends with many pictures of Costigan: of Costigan fighting a jewel in the Phoenix, of Costigan's interview with the Duke of York, of Costigan at his son-in-law's table surrounded by the nobility of his country, and of Costigan, when crying drunk, lamenting his daughter's ingratitude and stating that his gray hairs were hastening to premature grave. Through these tales, Huxter brought many young fellows to the Back Kitchen, who drank the landlord's liquors while enjoying the General's quirks, so the host forgave many of the latter's foibles in exchange for the profit they brought. Not the highest position in life was this—certainly, or one we would wish for an old man if we revered him—but of this aged buffoon, it may be noted that he had no particular idea his condition wasn't high, and in his whiskied blood there was not a black drop, nor in his muddled brains a bitter feeling, against any mortal being. Even his child, his cruel Emily, he would have taken to his heart and forgiven with tears; and what more can one say of Christian charity than that a man is ready to forgive those who have done him every kindness, and with whom he is wrong in a dispute!

Some among the young men frequenting the Back Kitchen believed the Captain hid his lodgings to avoid debt collectors or desired privacy, living in some wonderful place. Neither the landlord, when questioned, nor Costigan in his most intoxicated moments would answer inquiries. There was no real secret, as we have seen, having entered his apartments more than once; but due to the vicissitudes of a long life, he had often resided in places requiring privacy where visitors' presence would bring him nothing but discomfort. Hence, wild legends sprang up. It was said he slept in a watch-box in the City, in a cab at a mews, or in the Duke of York's Column—the wildest theory coming from the facetious Huxter. For Huxter, when not silenced by "swells" and in the company of his own friends, was a very different fellow from the youth cowed by Pen's impertinence earlier; he was the life and soul of the circles he joined, whether around a festive board or a dissecting table.

One bright September morning, as Huxter enjoyed coffee at a Covent Garden stall after a delightful night dancing at Vauxhall, he spotted the General staggering down Henrietta Street, pursued by a crowd of hooting blackguard boys who had left their beds under the river arches early, prowling for breakfast and the day's strange livelihood. The poor old General wasn't in a condition where the sneers of these beggars affected him much: cabmen and watermen recognized him and commented; policemen glared after him, warning the boys off with looks of scorn and pity. What did the scorn of men or the jokes of ribald children matter to the General? He reeled along with glazed eyes, having just enough sense to know where he was bound and to follow his accustomed path home. He went to bed unaware of how he arrived, waking to find himself there, asking no questions. He continued this daily perilous voyage until, from his coffee-stall vantage point, Huxter saw him. To note his friend and pay his twopence (he had only eightpence left, otherwise he would have hired a cab from Vauxhall) was instantaneous work for the eager Huxter. Costigan dove down the alleys near Drury Lane Theatre, where gin-shops, oyster-shops, and theatrical wardrobes lay dormant behind closed shutters as the pink morning light touched their chimneys. Huxter followed through these courts until reaching Oldcastle Street, where the gate of Shepherd's Inn stood.

Just as he neared home, a piece of orange peel slipped between the General's

Major Pendennis kept his word to Warrington just enough to clear his own conscience and spare Helen's heart. He assured her that the connection between her son, Arthur, and the detestable little gatekeeper was severed forever, freeing her from fear of an imprudent romance or a humiliating match for "Pen." Young Arthur, too, found relief once his vanity healed; he reasoned that Miss Fanny wouldn't die of unrequited love and that their brief, unfortunate liaison would bear no bad fruit.

With these anxieties lifted, the entire party set sail for their planned continental journey. Arthur Pendennis, *rentier* and *voyageant*, accompanied by Madame Pendennis and Mademoiselle Bell, joined George Warrington, described in the passport as a particular of thirty-two years, six feet tall (English), ordinary features, black hair, and a matching beard. They secured passports from the Belgian consul at Dover and crossed to Ostend. From there, they leisurely toured Bruges and Ghent on their way to Brussels and the Rhine. We shall skip the details of this well-worn tour, Laura's delight in ancient cities, Helen's awe of the Beguine convents, and her near-terror at seeing black-veiled nuns kneel before illuminated altars, defying the laws she held dear. The sight of barefoot friars, crowned saints worshipped in open defiance of statute, priests in gorgeous robes hiding in dark confessionals, and theaters open on Sundays overwhelmed the simple country lady.

When the young men returned from their evening drives, they found Helen's books of devotion spread on the table. Laura would usually stop reading the Psalms—which Helen loved above all else—as soon as they entered. The recent events had cruelly shaken Helen. Laura watched her friend with intense, hidden anxiety, while poor Pen remained constant and affectionate. Yet, a secret hung between them. Helen's wounded bosom yearned for her son, even as she harbored a secret anguish and rage at feeling dispossessed of his heart. She missed the days when Arthur's heart had no secrets, when she was his universe, when he poured his hopes, childhood griefs, and vanities into her tender embrace. Now, fate and selfishness had driven him forth on wayward wings to sing his own song and seek his own mate.

Watching Laura's devouring care and racking disappointment, the younger lady once confessed, "If Pen had loved me as you wished, I should have gained him, but I should have lost you, Mamma. I know I should. And I like you to love me best. Men do not know what it is to love as we do." Helen sighed, agreeing with this sentiment while protesting the rest. In truth, Miss Laura was right: love is an hour for men, but an eternity for women. Damon has his taxes, sermons, and tailors' bills; Delia has only Damon. He is the sturdy oak standing firm; she is the ivy clinging to his trunk, kissing his feet and hanging from his branches. Is it not so, Delia? Is it not your nature to cling to him while he stands with his hands in his pockets?

Old Pendennis saw his friends off at the water's edge, handing command of the expedition to Warrington. He intended a brief visit to a great friend's house before joining his sister-in-law at the German spa. Believing his long care for his sick family entitled him to relaxation, the Major headed to Stillbrook. Despite the thinning of the partridge population, the pheasants were still plentiful. Surrounded by a Royal Duke, foreign dignitaries, and illustrious statesmen, the old fellow felt his heart swell. Seeing his name listed in the *Morning Post* among the Marquis of Steyne's distinguished guests delighted him. He was invaluable at a country house: entertaining young men with bawdy anecdotes, flattering ladies in the morning rooms, guiding tourists through the park, pointing out the best views of the lake, explaining where timber was to be felled, and recounting local legends like the discovery of Sir Phelim O'Neal. He knew every domestic's name and counted the diners in the servants' hall. Richly deserving of his vacation, he enjoyed every moment, perhaps secretly pleased to transfer the burden of managing the ladies to Warrington—a servitude George gladly accepted for his friend's sake and the allure of the society.

Warrington, a proficient German scholar, taught Miss Laura the language, while Pen, too weary or lazy to study, relied on Warrington as courier, interpreter, and manager of baggage and finances. Warrington ensured the ladies attended the English church with decorum, walked beside Helen's donkey during evening strolls, procured newspapers like *Galignani*, and arranged comfortable seats under lime trees. Many a dashing Prussian or Frenchman, drawn to the pretty, fresh-faced English girl accompanying the pale widow, longed to dance with her. But Laura rarely appeared in the ballroom unless Pen escorted her. As for Warrington, the rough diamond lacked the polish of a dancing master and couldn't waltz, though he envied the chance to try with such a partner. Such a partner! Pshaw, what had a stiff bachelor to do with waltzing? Why drink in sweet pleasures risking future sadness and loneliness? Yet, he stayed. To observers, he seemed the widow's devoted son, a selfless guardian, or perhaps an adventurer eyeing her fortune. Likely, he desired some great benefit from her. After all, this is a selfish story, and everyone, however generous, is ultimately occupied with Number One. Warrington selfishly devoted himself to Helen, who selfishly devoted herself to Pen, who was currently selfishly devoted to himself, save for his genuine worry about his mother's health. Though they sat together, silence and a heavy cloud separated them.

Laura grew increasingly eager to see Warrington, welcoming him with frank enthusiasm. He found himself speaking to her with a fluency he hadn't known he possessed, performing acts of gallantry that later astonished him. Gazing in the mirror, he noticed crow's-feet around his eyes, streaks of white in his hair, and silver bristles in his beard. He watched the young bucks, the tight-waisted Germans, the capering Frenchmen with lacquered mustaches, and the English dandies like Pen, with their domineering airs. He envied their youth and beauty. Every night, he left the little circle with growing reluctance, retreating to his lodging to feel lonelier and unhappier. Helen couldn't miss his attachment. She now understood why Major Pendennis, her usual opponent, had insisted on Warrington joining them. Laura openly declared her enthusiastic regard for him. Arthur made no move to intervene; he chose not to see what was happening, neither preventing nor encouraging it. He recalled saying he couldn't understand how a man proposed to a woman twice. Helen was in torment, secretly feuding with her dearest son, doubting Laura, and averse to Warrington despite his goodness. No wonder the healing waters of Rosenbad failed her, nor did Dr. von Glauber find her improving. Meanwhile, Pen recovered rapidly, sleeping twelve hours a day and eating huge meals, regaining his strength within two months.

Fifteen days into their rest, a letter arrived from Major Pendennis announcing his imminent arrival at Rosenbad. Soon after, the Major appeared, accompanied by his faithful valet, Morgan, without whom he could not move. Traveling light and jaunty, he looked remarkably young, though his ancient knees betrayed him only when he moved, impeding the action of his natty varnished boots. Magnates from Britain and abroad gathered that autumn. The elder Pendennis read the guest list with gratification, planning to introduce his nephew to a German Grand Duchess, a Russian Princess, and an English Marquis. That same evening, leaning on his nephew's arm, the Major entered the Kursaal halls, winning or losing a few napoleons at *Trente-et-quarante*. He claimed he played neither to win nor lose, simply following fashion. He pointed out Russians and Spaniards gambling greedily, denouncing their behavior as sordid, while praising the English gentleman's ability to remain unmoved by fortune's swings. He recounted how the Marquis of Steyne had lost eighteen thousand pounds in a sitting without a flicker of emotion. "That's what I call being an English gentleman," he boasted, warming to his tale. As Russian princesses, whose reputations were long past doubt, passed him, the Major eagerly shared scandalous details of their lives with his nephew. When Princess Obstropski recognized him, smiling broadly with her enormous chasseur trailing behind, the Major remembered her as an actress in Paris in '14, married to the Emperor's aide-de-camp. He politely introduced his nephew to her and pointed out other famous figures. Poor Helen, if she had heard these tales or seen the company her brother-in-law presented her son to, would have been horrified. Once, passing the green gaming tables where croupiers shouted "Rouge gagne!" she had shrunk in terror, imploring Pen to never gamble. The scene frightened the simple widow but thrilled the worldly veteran, breathing the cheerful air that stifled her. To his credit, Pen kept his promise to his mother, firmly telling his uncle he would abide by it.

However, the Major's presence cast a dampening shadow over the party. Laura held no respect for him; Warrington met him with involuntary haughtiness; and the timid widow dreaded his interference with her plans for her son. Unknowingly, the Major carried tidings destined to cause a catastrophe.

The poor widow, aided by her loyal maid Martha—who marveled at the German customs and managed the simple household's affairs—had prepared a modest feast to welcome Major Pendennis. Only the Major and his two younger companions ate; Helen had sent word that she was too unwell to dine, and Laura accompanied her. During the meal, the Major chatted cheerfully, oblivious—or perhaps choosing to ignore—the heavy silence and gloom shared by the other two guests.

Only when evening fell did Helen and Laura enter the sitting-room. Helen leaned heavily on Laura, her back turned to the dying light, hiding the pallor and despair etched onto her face. As she approached Pen, whom she hadn't seen all day, she wrapped her arms around his neck and kissed him tenderly. Laura stepped aside, leaving the scene. Pen noticed immediately that his mother's voice and entire frame trembled; her hand was ice-cold as she pressed it to his forehead, clutching him with pathetic intensity. Yet, instead of soothing her, the sight of her misery only fueled a surge of wrath and irritability in the young man. He barely returned her kiss, meeting her desperate gaze with a hardness that felt cruel. *She persecutes me,* he thought bitterly. *She comes to me acting like a martyr!*

"You look dreadfully ill, my child," she whispered. "I cannot bear to see you looking like that." She stumbled toward a sofa, still gripping his limp hand with her thin, freezing fingers.

"I've had so much to annoy me lately, Mother," Pen said, his chest heaving. As he spoke, Helen's heart hammered so violently against her ribs that she sat nearly dead with terror.

Warrington, Laura, and the Major stood frozen, sensing the storm was about to break.

"I received letters from London," Arthur continued, his voice rising. "And one has caused me more pain than anything in my life. It says that my previous letters were intercepted and stolen—that a young woman who showed me nothing but love and care has been cruelly mistreated... by you, Mother!"

"For God's sake, stop!" cried Warrington. "Look at her! She's ill!"

"Let him finish," the widow murmured faintly.

"Let him go on and kill her!" Laura shouted, rushing to her mother's side. "Speak, sir! See her die!"

"It is you who are cruel!" Pen roared, his normally soft nature revolting against the injustice laid at his feet. "It is you who blame me for this pain! You heap wicked reproaches, cast unjust doubts, and persecute those who love me—those who risk everything for me, only to be despised and trampled because they are of lower birth! Now that I know your conduct, tell me what I'll do! I'm resolved to go back to that poor girl you threw out of my house and ask her to return and share my home. I'll defy the pride that drives her away and the suspicion that insults us both!"

"Do you mean, Pen, that you—" Helen began, her eyes wide and hands outstretched, but Laura cut her off. "Silence, hush, dear mother," she pleaded, and the widow obeyed. Despite Pen's savagery, she hung on his every word, almost swooning.

"By Gad, he shan't go on, or I won't listen!" the Major thundered, trembling with rage. "After all we've done for you, after everything I've sacrificed for you personally, to insult your mother and disgrace the Pendennis name by marrying a low-born kitchen-maid? Go ahead and do it, by Gad—but consider yourselves washed clean of me! I'm an old man, not long for this world. My family is ancient and honorable, by Jove, and I hoped that the boy I raised and nursed would prove our name wasn't left dishonored behind. But if he won't, then damn it, amen. Both my father and my brother Jack were the proudest men in England, and I never thought such disgrace would touch us. Never! And I'm ashamed it's Arthur Pendennis."

The old man's voice cracked into a sob—the second time Arthur had drawn tears from those wrinkled eyelids. The sound instantly halted Pen's fury. He stopped pacing. Laura stood by Helen's sofa, and Warrington, previously a silent observer, watched the family tempest. Darkness had fallen as they argued. Following the Major's passionate outburst, George Warrington's deep voice broke into the twilight room, filled with emotion.

"May I tell you something about myself, my kind friends?" he asked gently. "You've been so good to me, ma'am, so kind to me, Laura—I hope I may call you so sometime. Pen and I have been such close friends that I've long wanted to share my true story. It's sad and holds another's secret, but it might help Arthur understand. It's right everyone knows. Perhaps it will distract you from a topic born of fatal misunderstanding that has caused so much pain. May I speak, Mrs. Pendennis?"

"Please, go on," Helen said softly. Though she tried to listen, her mind raced with the possibility that Pen's words hinted at the truth she secretly feared.

Warrington filled a glass of wine and drained it before speaking. "You all see me now—a man with no ambition, careless of reputation, living in a garret from hand to mouth. I have friends and a name, and surely the capability to succeed if I chose. But I have no mind for it. I'll probably die alone in that garret. I nailed myself to this fate early on. Do you know what sparked my interest in Arthur years ago? College gossip mentioned his early affair with the Chatteris actress—about whom Pen has spoken since. Without the Major's intervention, she might have been your daughter-in-law, ma'am. I can't see Pen in the dark, but I'm sure he blushing; Miss Bell likely does too; and Major Pendennis, I expect, laughs appropriately—he won, after all. Imagine Arthur's lot had he been tied at nineteen to an illiterate woman older than himself, lacking any common ground, equality, confidence, or love. What could he have been but miserable? When he threatened such a union just now, believe me, it was anger speaking—a very natural reaction after generous, manly, self-denying conduct (rare in him!) met with unkind suspicion from friends and unfair treatment of an innocent person to whom you are all indebted."

Helen rose to interrupt, but Warrington saw her move. "Do I tire you, ma'am?"

"Oh no—go on, go on," Helen urged, delighted.

"I liked him because of that early history, and because I admire a man who shows unreasonable attachment to a woman. That's why we're friends—all of us. Pen has been a great comfort to a lonely, unfortunate man."

"I'm not complaining of my lot; no man gets what he wants. Up in my garret, with the flowers you left there, my old books, and my pipe for a wife, I'm fairly contented. Occasionally, I envy men with brighter careers or those who can soothe their misfortune with the affection of a woman or child—things Fate and my own faults denied me." A sigh drifted from near Warrington in the dark, followed by a hand reaching out toward him. Instantly, it withdrew. Female prudery dictates that before any expression of feeling, a woman is taught to prioritize propriety and blush at the slightest attention. Modesty checked the spontaneous gesture, shrinking back ashamed, and Warrington resumed his tale.

"My fate is what I made, unlucky for me and others. Like Arthur, I had an adventure before college. No one saved me as the Major saved Pen. Pardon me, Miss Laura, if I confess this. At eighteen, working as a private tutor, I became attached to a woman of lower station and advanced age. You recoil from me..."

"No, I don't," Laura declared firmly. This time, her hand went out resolutely, taking his. She had guessed his story from earlier hints.

"She was a yeoman's daughter nearby," Warrington said, his voice faltering. "I fancied her—as all young men do. Her parents knew my father and encouraged me with crude artifices and scoundrel-like flattery. To her credit, she never cared for me; she was forced into it by her family's threats. Oh, that I hadn't been deceived! But in these matters, we deceive ourselves because we wish to. I thought I loved that poor woman."

"What could such a marriage bring? Soon I realized I'd wed a boor. She couldn't grasp a single subject I cared about. Her dullness bored me until I loathed it. After a wretched, secretive union, I found letters—terrible letters!—revealing her heart had never been mine, but belonged to someone of her own class. Upon my father's death, I paid my college debts and settled every remaining shilling in an annuity for those bearing my name, on the condition they hide and never use the title. They honored it, mostly, unless they needed more money. Had I gained fame, she would have claimed it; had I made a name, impostors would wear it. I entered life at twenty, hopeless and ruined beyond redemption. I was a boyish victim of vulgar cheats, and perhaps only recently have I understood how hard it is to forgive them. I told you the moral, Pen; now the fable. Beware marrying outside your degree. I was

It happened, amidst the grand festivities of the English season when all London descends upon Epsom Downs, that a motley assembly of the characters we have previously encountered gathered to witness the Derby. Among them, in a commodious open carriage drawn by a spirited pair of horses, sat Mrs. Bungay of Paternoster Row, resplendent in attire befitting King Solomon in all his glory. Beside her walked the modest Mrs. Shandon, a friend to whom the worthy publisher's wife had maintained a steadfast affection since their first meeting. Having refreshed himself with a copious luncheon, Mr. Bungay was currently engaged in furiously shying at the nearby sticks, his efforts causing sweat to pour down his bald pate. Meanwhile, Mrs. Shandon ambled among the drinking tenants and gypsies, while Finucane remained constantly at the service of the two ladies, intercepting gentlemen of acquaintance connected with the publishing house who sought to pay their respects.

Mr. Archer soon approached to offer his compliments and inform Mrs. Bungay of the events unfolding on the course. "Over there," he announced, pointing toward the Prime Minister, "His Lordship has just instructed me to back *Borax*. However, I personally prefer *Munmeer*." He then proceeded to identify countless dukes and grandees to the delighted Mrs. Bungay. "Gaze yonder in the Grand Stand," he directed. "Seated there is the Chinese Ambassador, accompanied by his Mandarin suite. Fou-choo-foo procured letters of introduction from the Governor-General of India for me; we were close friends, and I even ensured chopsticks were laid for him whenever he visited to dine. Yet, he insisted on bringing his own cook. Would you believe it, Mrs. Bungay? One day, while I was absent and the Ambassador was dining in our garden with Mrs. Archer, feasting on gooseberries—a delicacy the Chinese adore—that beast of a cook, spotting my wife's beloved Blenheim spaniel (an inheritance from the Duke of Marlborough, whose ancestor's life was saved at Malplaquet by Mrs. Archer's great-great-grandfather), seized the poor little creature, slit its throat, skinned it, and served it up stuffed with forcemeat as the second course!"

"Law!" exclaimed Mrs. Bungay.

"You can imagine my wife's agony upon learning the truth!" Archer continued. "The cook screamed upstairs, claiming he had found Fido's skin in the yard just as we were tasting the dish! She never spoke to the Ambassador again—not a word—and, truly, he hasn't dined with us since. The Lord Mayor, however, enjoyed the meal immensely; paired with green peas, it bears a striking resemblance to duck."

"You don't say so!" cried the astonished publisher's lady.

"It is fact, upon my word. Observe that lady in blue seated beside the Ambassador; that is Lady Flamingo. Rumor has it she intends to marry him and return to Pekin with His Excellency. She is deliberately squeezing her feet to prepare for the journey. Alas, she will only cripple herself and never walk properly again. My wife possesses the smallest feet in England, wearing shoes suitable for a six-year-old, but compared to a Chinese lady's foot, what difference does it make?"

Mrs. Bungay shifted her gaze. "Who is that carriage Mr. Pendennis is conversing with? He and Mr. Warrington arrived just moments ago. He seems haughty, though he has cause to be, keeping such top-tier company. Does he possess a substantial fortune, Mr. Archer? I notice he remains in mourning."

"Eighteen hundred pounds annually in land, plus twenty-two thousand five hundred in Three-and-a-Half Per Cent consols," Archer replied.

"Law! You know everything, Mr. A!" the lady of Paternoster Row exclaimed.

"I happen to know, for I was consulted regarding poor Mrs. Pendennis's will," Archer explained. "Her uncle, the Major, rarely acts without my counsel; fearing his extravagance, we have secured the property to prevent him from squandering it. —Good day, my lord. Do you recognize that gentleman, ladies? You've surely read his speeches in the House; it is Lord Rochester."

"Lord Fiddlestick!" shouted Finucane from the box. "Surely it's Tom Staples of the *Morning Advertiser*, Archer."

"Is it?" Archer responded simply. "Well, I am quite short-sighted, and truly, I mistook him for Rochester. That gentleman with the double opera-glass (nodding politely) is Lord John; and the tall man accompanying him, don't you recognize him? It is Sir James."

"You know them merely because you spot them in the House," grumbled Finucane.

"I know them because they are kind enough to consider me their most intimate friend," Archer persisted. "Behold the Duke of Hampshire; what a model of a fine old English gentleman! He never misses 'the Derby.' 'Archer,' he remarked to me only yesterday, 'I have attended sixty-five Derbies! I first appeared on the field at age seven on a piebald pony alongside my father, the Prince of Wales, and Colonel Hanger. I missed only two races: one due to measles at Eton, and another during the Waterloo campaign, when I was serving with my friend Wellington in Flanders.'"

"And who is that yellow carriage adorned with pink and yellow parasols, to which Mr. Pendennis is speaking, surrounded by so many gentlemen?" Mrs. Bungay inquired.

"That is Lady Clavering of Clavering Park, adjacent to my friend Pendennis's estate. On the box sits the young son and heir; he is dreadfully tipsy, that little scoundrel! Beside him is Miss Amory, Lady Clavering's daughter from a former marriage, who is remarkably sweet toward my friend. Though I suspect his affections lie elsewhere. You recall young Mr. Foker, the great brewer? He attempted suicide out of a fatal passion for Miss Amory, who rejected him, but was rescued just in time by his valet. He is now abroad under guard."

"How fortunate that young man is!" sighed Mrs. Bungay. "Who would have guessed that the quiet, demure lad who dined with us three or four years ago would evolve into such a distinguished figure? I saw his name listed at Court recently, presented by the Marquis of Steyne and all, and in every noble gathering, his name appears as certain as a gunshot."

"I introduced him significantly when he first arrived in town," Mr. Archer noted. "His uncle, Major Pendennis, completed the rest. Hallo! There is Cobden, of all people in the world! I must go speak with him. Farewell, Mrs. Bungay. Good morning, Mrs. Shandon."

An hour prior, at a distant section of the course, a battered old stagecoach known as Wheeler's (the Harlequin's Head) drag had arrived, carrying a crew of ragged but spirited fellows from Bow Street, complete with a lavish lunch stored in the boot. As the magnificent Derby race thundered across the greensward, watched by millions, the occupants of the drag bellowed the names of their chosen horses—"The Cornet!" "It's Muffineer!" "Blue Sleeves!" "Yellow Cap!"—until the fluttering signal revealed *Podasokus* as the victor. Instantly, one of the gentlemen leaped onto the roof of the precarious coach, shouting, "Hurray, hurray! *Podasokus* wins! Supper for ten, Wheeler, my boy! Ask around, and damn the expense!" The others cheered, whispering that the Colonel stood to win fifteen hundred pounds on favorable odds. Suspicion rippled through the group; each man eyed his neighbor warily, fearing a plot to isolate the winner and borrow money, ensuring no one could be alone that afternoon.

Elsewhere, a far more modest, if equally shabby, vehicle—cab number 2002—carried a gentleman and two ladies from the Strand. Inside, one lady, seated on the box with her mother and companion, enjoyed a feast of lobster salad and bitter ale. Her beauty was so striking that many splendid young dandies, previously distracted by the fashionable crowds on the hill, cast envious glances at the rosy-cheeked girl. Youthful blushing and good humor flushed her face like clouds across a serene sky. Her mother's cheeks were permanently mottled with the color of the lobster she devoured, deepening further with every sip of pale ale and brandy-and-water.

The gentleman escorting them was indefatigably attentive, cracking jokes throughout the journey. He fearlessly bantered with solemn guardsmen in heavy drags and humble dustmen driving donkey-chaises. He fired volleys of "chaff" into endless windows, grinning girls' schools, shouting urchins behind academy railings, and casements where maidservants, nurses, and stern dissenting matrons peered out. Both the girl in the straw bonnet with pink ribbons and her lobster-devouring mother agreed that when "in spirits," there was no one like Mr. Sam. He had filled the cab with trophies stolen from bankrupt stick players, pincushions, wooden apples, jack-in-the-boxes, and toy soldiers. He even brought a gypsy with a tawny child to tell fortunes. The only cloud in their sunny day occurred when the fortune-teller warned the young lady to beware of a false fair man, predicted a serious illness, and foretold that a

It has been nearly a year since the events of the previous chapter unfolded. Time, that relentless editor, has made significant revisions to Arthur Pendennis's appearance. The somber black coat has been retired in favor of a cheerful blue; his wig, once a mandatory accessory, has been discarded, revealing hair that, while slightly thinned, proudly claims public attention once more. Most notably, Arthur has achieved the distinction of appearing at Court in the uniform of a Cornet in the Clavering troop of the ———-shire Yeomanry Cavalry, presented to the Sovereign by the Marquis of Steyne. This elevation was vigorously, almost tearfully, advocated by Arthur's uncle, Major Pendennis. The Major simply refused to let a single year pass without this ceremonial stamp of "gentlemanhood."

To the Major, his nephew belonged in far more exclusive circles than the Megatherium Club. Consequently, he has broadcast across the globe his profound disappointment regarding the yield of Arthur's inheritance. Let it be recorded officially: the world now values Pendennis's estate at just under £1,500 per annum. At this valuation, publishers treat him with newfound respect, and mothers cease to be entirely uncivil. Why? Because if the dazzling daughters of high expectation are destined to marry Earls, then the plain ones—like Beatrice, who possesses a slight asymmetry in her shoulders—are perfectly suited to secure a provider. Thus, Mr. Pendennis becomes the logical anchor for Beatrice's future.

Indeed, the Major's foresight proved prescient. In the first winter following his mother's acquisition of wealth, Mrs. Hawxby hosted a country gathering where she insisted Beatrice learn billiards from Mr. Pendennis and be driven exclusively by him in her pony carriage. Her rationale? They were both "literary" souls. She loudly proclaimed that the young man, goaded by his "horrid old uncle," had behaved infamously by trifling with Beatrice's affections. The truth, however, was that the Major, possessing a keen understanding of Mrs. Hawxby's predatory nature toward unwary youths, had spirited Arthur away from her immediate grasp at that very country house. He saved him from her claws, if not from her sharp tongue. While the elder Pendennis had wished for Arthur to spend Christmas at Clavering, the young man lacked the heart for it; the proximity to the haunted grounds of Fairoaks was too laden with sorrow.

Meanwhile, the Clavering family re-emerged on the Epsom racecourse, having vanished from the narrative for a year. During this interim, fate had dealt them a series of cruel hands. Lady Clavering, a woman of exceptional natural goodness and a palate for fine dining, found her appetite and temper severely tested by endless domestic squabbles. "I'd sooner eat a turnip for dessert than that pineapple and those Muscatel grapes from Clavering," she lamented to her loyal companion, staring mournfully at the table. "If only I could enjoy my meal in peace. How vastly happier I was when I was a widow, before this mountain of money descended upon us!"

The Claverings had launched their social career with a disastrous stumble, offering hospitality without gaining gratitude, conducting themselves without earning respect. Their initial London season was shaky, followed by a notorious collapse. Gossip ran rampant: "Human patience cannot endure Sir Francis Clavering," the townspeople whispered. "He is hopelessly low, dull, and disreputable. There is a rotting taint about the house. Who is that Begum with her money and missing 'h's'? What a pretentious little creature is the daughter, with her affected French manners, unfit for proper English company! And what strange creatures surround them!"

Sir Francis was labeled a gambler, notorious for keeping the company of blacklegs and profligates. Even Hely Clinker, serving in his regiment, claimed the Baronet cheated at cards and displayed the "white feather." Lady Rockminster, initially supportive, soon cast Lady Clavering aside. High-society ladies stopped bringing their daughters to her gatherings; the young men attending behaved with shocking familiarity and disdain. Poor Lady Clavering admitted she was forced to admit "the canal"—the lower-class visitors—into her drawing room, as the elite refused to come. Yet, she harbored no malice toward them, nor did she feel superior to her neighbors; she had merely followed the social dictates handed down by her godmother. It is easier to abandon society than to be abandoned by it.

Among the faithless, one figure remained steadfast: Harry Foker, Esq. However, his management of his affections lacked prudence. His secret passion for Pen became public knowledge, reaching the ears of his weak mother and eventually landing on the desk of the stern, bald-headed Foker Senior. The resulting confrontation was violent and painful, ending with Harry's banishment from England for a year. He was ordered to return and either marry his cousin or retire to private life on a mere £300 a year, forever barred from seeing his parents or brewery. Harry departed with the characteristic gloom of an exile, finding even the Parisian Boulevards and the Italian skies bleak beneath his crape-covered eyes.

For Sir Francis Clavering, that same year was catastrophic. As sportsmen recall, Lord Harrowhill's horse, *Podasokus* (named after a hero of the *Iliad*), won the Derby, stunning the experts who had backed the non-factor, *Borax*. Sir Francis, intimately connected with the scoundrels of the turf and boasting of "valuable inside information," had heavily wagered against the winner and supported the favorite. The result, as his son later confessed to Lady Clavering, was a staggering loss of seven thousand pounds.

This was a devastating blow to the lady, who had repeatedly bailed out her husband's debts, paid his lenders and horse dealers, and furnished both town and country homes. Now, she faced the immediate demand for this colossal sum, the price tag for her husband's cowardice.

Amidst this chaos, Major Pendennis stepped in as the family's advisor. Having toured every room of Clavering Hall—including the dreaded closet where families lock their skeletons—he discovered that the Baronet hid his financial entanglements so deeply within a web of lies that no lawyer or accountant could untangle them. However, the Major knew Lady Clavering's situation intimately. Upon hearing of the Derby disaster, he meticulously audited her remaining resources, uncovering the vast sacrifices the widow Amory had made for her second husband.

The Major did not hide his opinion: Lady Clavering's daughter, Blanche, had been unfairly treated financially by her second marriage. He gently suggested she deserved a better provision. Revealing his knowledge of the Begum's tragic past in India—including the details of her first husband's trial, which he had deliberately ignored to remain a friend—he leveraged this goodwill.

"My dear Lady Clavering," the Major declared, "I may have interested motives. We all do. Mine is clear: to arrange a marriage between my nephew and your daughter."

Lady Clavering, surprised yet open, agreed. But the Major pressed further. "Frankly, my lady, my boy has only five hundred a year. A wife with ten thousand pounds would barely improve his lot. We could do better for him. He is a shrewd, ambitious young man who has sown his wild oats. His goal is advancement. If you and Sir Francis cooperate—and trust me, the Baronet will refuse nothing—you can propel Arthur significantly forward. What use is that empty seat in Parliament to Clavering, who rarely appears or speaks? Gentlemen tell me my boy was famous as an orator at Oxford! Give him a start, good friends, and an opportunity, and he will make a name his sons will boast of.

"There is no path for a man like him except through a prudent marriage—not with a penniless heiress to stagnate on fifteen hundred a year, but with someone he can assist, who can in turn elevate him. You deserve a distinguished son-in-law rather than keeping your husband in Parliament, where he achieves nothing. That is why I am involved; I believe this is a bargain beneficial to both."

The Begum, touched by the Major's candor, reflected on Arthur's integration into the family. "I look upon him almost as kin," she said. "He comes and goes as he pleases. I wept when I heard of his mother's death; I would have worn mourning, only black does not suit me. I know who his mother wanted him to marry: Laura, favored by old Lady Rockminster. She is a finer girl than my Blanche. And my Blanche... she hasn't been much comfort, Major. It is Laura Pen who should wed."

"Marry on five hundred a year?" the Major exclaimed, incredulous. "My dear soul, you are mad! Consider what I've said. Never act in your affairs without consulting me. Remember, old Pendennis is always your friend."

Previously, the Major had shared these sentiments with Miss Amory. He argued passionately that mutual utility was the bedrock of a happy marriage. "Observe love matches," he lectured. "They are the quickest to quarrel. A girl who elopes to Gretna Green often flees to Switzerland next. The essence of marriage is agreement on usefulness. The lady provides the capital; the gentleman utilizes it. My boy's wife brings the horse; I guarantee he will win the plate. That is a sensible union. Such couples have substance to discuss. Even if Blanche and Pen were Cupid and Psy

Our benevolent Begum, Lady Clavering, was initially so incensed by yet another instance of her husband's duplicity and foolishness that she flatly refused to assist Sir Francis Clavering in meeting his "debts of honor." She declared her intention to separate from him immediately, leaving him to face the dire consequences of his incorrigible weakness and wastefulness. Following the disastrous financial transactions at the Derby, the unfortunate gambler was in such a state of mental collapse that he sought to avoid everyone. This included his turf associates, whom he owed money to and feared he couldn't repay, and his wife, his long-suffering banker, whom he now doubted would allow him further credit.

When Lady Clavering inquired the next morning if Sir Francis was home, she was informed that he had not returned. Instead, he had dispatched a messenger to his valet, instructing him to send clothes and letters via the courier. Captain Strong anticipated a summons from the Baronet within the day or two. True to his nature, Strong received a frantic note begging him to visit his distraught friend at the Short Hotel in Blackfriars and inquire for "Mr. Francis." Sir Francis possessed a peculiar psychological trait: he would rather fabricate a falsehood than admit the truth, often beginning a contest with Fortune by fleeing and hiding.

The bootboy of the Short Hotel, who delivered Clavering's message to Grosvenor Place and retrieved his carpet-bag, instantly recognized its owner. He whispered this revelation to the footman setting the breakfast table, who relayed it to the servants' hall, then to Mrs. Bonner, the lady's housekeeper and confidante, who finally passed it to Lady Clavering. Consequently, every member of the Grosvenor Place staff knew that Sir Francis was holed up under the alias "Francis" at an inn in the Blackfriars Road. Furthermore, Sir Francis's coachman spread the news among other gentlemen's drivers, who shared it with their masters and the neighboring Tattersalls, where grim predictions arose that Sir Francis Clavering was preparing for a tour of the Levant.

That day, the volume of mail addressed to Sir Francis Clavering, Bart., arriving at his hall table, was extraordinary. The French cook submitted his bill to Lady Clavering. Suppliers for her household, along with Messrs. Finer and Gimcrack (mercers and ornament dealers) and Madame Crinoline (the renowned milliner), forwarded their invoices to her, alongside Miss Amory's substantial private accounts from each establishment.

By the afternoon following the Derby, after a conversation with his principal at the Short Hotel—who was found sobbing and drinking Curaçao—Strong visited Grosvenor Place to conduct business as usual. He discovered all these suspicious documents arrayed on the Baronet's study desk. With a rueful expression, he began opening and examining them.

Just then, Mrs. Bonner, Lady Clavering's maid and housekeeper, descended upon him. As integral to the family as the Chevalier was to Sir Francis, Mrs. Bonner naturally sided with her mistress in the marital dispute, harboring anger equal to, if not exceeding, Lady Clavering's own.

"She won't pay if I advise her," Mrs. Bonner declared. "Please go back to Sir Francis, Captain, and tell him we refuse to pay his debts anymore. Look at him, lurking in a low public house, lacking the courage to face his wife like a man! We made a gentleman of him; we pulled him out of gaol (perhaps saving others too); we've paid his debts repeatedly. We set him up in Parliament, provided him with a town and country house. Yet here he is, a shabby sneak, daring not show his face! We furnished the horse he rides, the dinner he eats, and the clothes on his back. We will provide nothing more. Our remaining fortune belongs to ourselves; we shall not squander it on this ungrateful man. We'll give him enough to survive and leave him. Tell him that from Susan Bonner."

At this moment, Lady Clavering summoned Strong. Hoping to find his mistress more pliable than her factotum, the Chevalier approached her. On previous occasions, he had successfully appealed to Lady Clavering's generosity. He painted a dismal picture of Sir Francis's plight, warning of potential catastrophic consequences if his engagements remained unpaid.

"Let him kill himself!" Mrs. Bonner laughed harshly. "Dying would be the best thing for him." Strong insisted he had found the Baronet surrounded by razor blades. At this, Lady Clavering laughed bitterly. "He won't harm himself as long as there is a shilling left for him to steal from a poor woman. His life is safe, Captain; mark my words. Ah! What a terrible day it was when I first laid eyes on him."

"He's worse than the first man," cried Lady Clavering's aide-de-camp. "Before, he was a wild devil, yes, but he had the courage of a man. This fellow? Why bother paying his bills, selling your diamonds, and forgiving him? Next year he'll be just as bad. The first chance he gets, he'll cheat and rob her again. Her money will sustain a pack of rogues and swindlers—I don't mean you, Captain; you've been a good friend, barring our wish to never have met you."

Seeing Mrs. Bonner's mention of the diamonds, the Chevalier realized the kind-hearted Begum might soften slightly, offering hope for his client.

"Upon my word, ma'am," he said, feigning deep sympathy and admiration for her tireless goodness, "any criticism of Clavering or Mrs. Bonner is deserved by us both. It was indeed an unlucky day for you to encounter either. He has treated you cruelly, and unless you are the most generous and forgiving woman in existence, there is no hope for him. But you cannot allow the father of your son to remain a disgraced man, sending little Frank into the world with such a stain. Bind him; tie him down with any promises you like. I vouch for him; he will sign them."

"And break 'em," retorted Mrs. Bonner.

"And keep 'em this time!" cried Strong passionately. "He must keep them. Have you seen him weep, ma'am? 'Oh, Strong,' he said, 'it isn't for myself I suffer now; it's for my boy—it's for the finest woman in England whom I have treated basely. I know I have.' He didn't intend to bet on this race, ma'am—he truly didn't. He was cheated; the entire ring was rigged. He believed he could place the bet safely, without risk. Let this serve as a lifelong lesson. To see a man weep... oh, it is dreadful."

"He doesn't seem to care much about making my dear missus cry," Mrs. Bonner remarked, pointing toward the room. "Poor soul! Look if he does, Captain."

"If you possess the soul of a man, Clavering," Strong said later, recounting the scene to his principal, "you will keep your promise this time. So help me Heaven, if you cross her word, I will turn against you and expose everything."

"What exactly?" shrieked Mr. Francis, to whom Strong delivered the message back at the Short Hotel, where the Baronet was currently crying and drowning his sorrows in Curaçao.

"Pshaw! Do you imagine I am a fool?" Strong exploded. "Do you think I could navigate this world for so long without keeping my eyes open? Know this: I need only speak, and tomorrow you are a beggar. Moreover, I am not the only one who knows your secret."

"Who else knows?" gasped Clavering.

"Old Pendennis does, or I am vastly mistaken. He recognized the man the very first night he arrived drunk in your house."

"He knows it, does he?" Clavering shrieked. "Damn him—kill him!"

"You'd like to kill us all, wouldn't you, old boy?" Strong sneered, puffing his cigar.

The Baronet slapped his weak hand against his forehead; perhaps Strong had correctly interpreted his desire. "Oh, Strong!" he cried, "If only I dared, I would end it all. I am the damnedest miserable dog in all England. It is this misery that drives me wild and reckless. It compels me to drink"—(he trembled as he raised a bumper of Curaçao)—"and to associate with these thieves. I know they are thieves, every single one of them, damned thieves. And how can I help it? I didn't know! By Gad, I am innocent! Until I saw that damned scoundrel, I knew less than the dead! I will flee, I will go abroad, far from these confounded hells, and bury myself in a forest! I will hang myself from a tree! Oh, I am the most miserable beggar in all England!" Thus, amidst groans, despair, and blasphemy, the impotent wretch vented his grief, lamenting his fate while vowing his miserable repentance.

The honored proverb stating that "an ill wind blows no good to anyone" proved false in the case of Sir Francis Clavering and another occupant of Mr. Strong's chambers in Shepherd's Inn. The latter was "good" by sheer luck, having wagered with Colonel Altamont. On the Derby's settling day—as Captain Clinker, appointed by Major Pendennis to handle Sir Francis's books since

Shortly after Colonel Altamont's stroke of luck at Epsom, he executed his plan for a continental tour. The chroniclers of high society, those who descend to London Bridge to bid farewell to the fashionable exiles, reported that aboard the *Soho* bound for Antwerp last Saturday sat "Sir Robert, Lady, and the Misses Hodge; Mr. Serjeant Kewsy, and Mrs. and Miss Kewsy; Colonel Altamont, Major Coddy, and others." The Colonel traveled in full state, befitting a gentleman of his station. He donned a rich traveling suit, consumed brandy-and-water with gusto throughout the voyage, remained perfectly sober while others suffered seasickness, and was accompanied by his personal attendant: the loyal Irish legionary who had served him and Captain Strong in their Shepherd's Inn chambers.

Before departing, the Chevalier shared a hearty dinner at Blackwall with his leaving friend, the Colonel, along with a couple of others. They toasted Altamont's health repeatedly at the Colonel's expense. "Strong, old boy," the Chevalier's reliable companion declared, "if you find yourself needing a bit of cash, now is your lucky hour. I'm your man. You've been a good chap to me, and a twenty-pound note makes no difference to me." But Strong demurred. He didn't need money; he was flush, quite flush—"though perhaps not flush enough to repay your last loan, Altamont, but certainly able to carry on for a while." With a greeting that wasn't entirely cordial, they parted ways.

Had the possession of money truly made Altamont more honest and amiable, or merely seemed so to Strong? Perhaps the Colonel was genuinely better, wealth having polished his character. Or perhaps it was simply the radiance of riches that Strong admired. Inside himself, Strong reasoned, "This poor devil, this unfortunate outcast, a returned convict, is ten times the man my friend Sir Francis Clavering, Bart. possesses. He has pluck and a code of honor. He sticks to friends and faces enemies. The other lacks the courage to do either. What casts a shadow over this poor soul? He was merely reckless and signed his father-in-law's name. Many men have done worse and walk tall today. Clavering does not. In fact, he never held his head up in his prime."

Strong perhaps regretted the lie he'd told the open-handed Colonel—that he wasn't in want of funds. It was a lie born of honesty; the Chevalier simply couldn't bring himself to borrow again from his outlawed friend. Besides, he could manage. Clavering had promised money—though promises from Clavering were rarely kept—but the Chevalier was optimistic, trusting in various chances to intercept stray remittances meant for his patron, a task in which Mr. Strong primarily engaged.

Though he had complained about sharing rooms with Altamont at Shepherd's Inn, the Chevalier found the solitude drearier without his partner. His social nature craved company, and he had developed extravagant, luxurious habits thanks to a servant who ran errands, arranged his toiletries, and cooked his meals. Now, seeing the portly, handsome gentleman paint his own boots and fry his own mutton chops was a rather grand and touching sight.

It was previously noted that the Chevalier had a wife, a Spanish lady from Vittoria, who had returned to her family after a few months of marriage, having broken the Captain's skull with a dish. He began to wonder if he shouldn't return to see his Juanita. Since the Colonel's departure, the Chevalier grew melancholy—or, in his own vivid phrasing, "down on his luck." Such moments of depression and bad fortune happen constantly in the lives of heroes: Marius at Minturnae, Charles Edward in the Highlands, Napoleon before Elba. What great man hasn't faced adversity?

No supplies came from Clavering anytime soon. The twenty-five pounds, or "pony," the exemplary Baronet had received from Mr. Altamont, had vanished from Clavering's possession as quickly as many previous ponies. He had recently joined a select group of sportsmen who evaded the police to land in Essex, where they staged a boxing match featuring Billy Bluck versus Dick the Cabman, backed by the Baronet. For thirteen rounds, Dick dominated until an unlucky blow to the windpipe claimed his life.

"It's always my luck, Strong," Sir Francis lamented. "The odds were three to one on the cabman, and I felt as secure of thirty pounds as if it were in my pocket. Dammit, I owe my man Lightfoot fourteen pounds now—he lent and paid for me—and he duns me, that confounded impudent blackguard! I wish to heaven I knew how to draft a bill or squeeze a little something out of my lady! I'll give you half, Ned, upon my soul and honor. I'll give you half if you can get anyone to advance us a fifty."

"Ned" responded sternly that, as a gentleman, he had given his word of honor not to participate in any further financial dealings involving her husband (who had also given his word). The Chevalier pledged to keep his vow, promising to polish his own boots for the rest of his life rather than break his promise. Furthermore, he vowed to warn Lady Clavering that Sir Francis intended to betray his faith toward her the moment he detected such intent.

Armed with this intelligence, Sir Francis Clavering, per usual, erupted in tears and curses. He spoke of death as his only recourse, imploring his dear Strong, his best friend, his dearest Ned, not to abandon him. Yet, as he descended the stairs of Shepherd's Inn, he swore and blasphemed against Ned as the most infernal villain, traitor, blackguard, and coward under the sun, wishing Ned were buried in a worse place, provided the rascal lived long enough for Frank Clavering to exact his revenge.

Inside Strong's chambers, the Baronet encountered a gentleman whose visits to Shepherd's Inn had become increasingly frequent: Mr. Samuel Huxter, of Clavering. This young man, who had once poached walnuts in Clavering Park in his youth and watched the Baronet parade through town with four horses and powdered footmen, held immense respect for his Member and delighted greatly in making his acquaintance. He introduced himself with flushed cheeks and trembling hands as a "Clavering man"—son of Mr. Huxter of the marketplace, whose father had tended Sir Francis's gamekeeper, Coxwood, when his gun burst and severed three fingers. Proud to meet Sir Francis, he delivered this introduction with much affectation. Sir Francis received it affably. Honest Huxter then praised Sir Francis to the lads at Bartholomew's and told Fanny in the lodge that, ultimately, nothing beat a thoroughbred, a proper old English gentleman of the old school.

Fanny retorted that she thought Sir Francis was an "ojous" creature—she didn't know why—but she couldn't abide him. She was sure he was wicked, low, and mean; she knew it. When Sam countered that Sir Francis was very affable and had kindly borrowed half a sovereign from her, Fanny burst into laughter, tugged at Sam's long hair (which lacked impeccable cleanliness), patted his chin, and called him a "stoopid, stoopid, old foolish stoopid." She added that Sir Francis was always borrowing money from everyone, noting that Mary had actually refused him twice and made him wait three months to retrieve seven shillings he'd borrowed from her.

"Don't say 'er' but 'her,' 'borrer' but 'borrow,' 'actially' but 'actually,' Fanny," Mr. Huxter replied—not correcting her logic, but her grammar.

"Well then, 'her,' and 'borrow,' and 'hactually'—there then, you stoopid!" the other exclaimed. The scholar made such a charming face that the grammar master was instantly appeased and would gladly have taught her a hundred more lessons on the spot for the price of one.
