Mrs. Bolton happened to be present, and presumably, by this time, Fanny and Dr. Sam were on exceedingly familiar and confidential terms. Time had brought Fanny certain consolations and soothed certain regrets that are deucedly bitter when they arise, yet no more painful than tooth-pulling or any other temporary pang; they are eternal, nonetheless.

Consider, sitting surrounded by respect and affection, honored and flattered in your old age; your foibles gently indulged, your slightest words kindly cherished, your garrulous tales received with dutiful forbearance and hypocritical smiles; the women of your house constant in their flatteries; the young men hushed and attentive when you speak; the servants awestruck; the tenants capping hats, ready to serve as mounts when your honor takes a drive—it has often struck you, O thoughtful Dives! that this respect and glory are largely transferred, with your fee simple, to your successor. Servants will bow, tenants will shout, for your son as for you. The butler will fetch him the wine (improved by aging) currently in your cellar. When your night comes, and the light of your life fades, as surely as the morning rises after you and without you, the sun of prosperity and flattery shines on your heir. Men bask in the halo of consols and acres that surrounds him. Reverence transfers with the estate; of which, with all its advantages, pleasures, respect, and goodwill, he becomes the life-

Part of Mr. Strong's business in life was to procure money, and the rest was to ensure it vanished into the pockets of those least deserving of it. This particular chapter serves as a belated explanation for the events of Chapter LXI, setting the stage for a tale of financial entanglement, hidden secrets, and the peculiar dynamics of a household in crisis.

The information Major Pendennis had gathered regarding the Clavering family—acquired partly through Mr. Strong's diligence and partly through his own benevolent interference as the family's "friend"—was enough to make the old gentleman hesitate. His original plan to bestow a wife upon his nephew, Arthur, seemed suddenly fraught with peril. To marry Arthur to a woman with two such formidable father-in-laws as the current guardians of the Clavering estate was, in the Major's estimation, a recipe for disaster.

One of these guardians, the late Sir Francis Clavering, was a man of volatile temperament who had recently engaged in a duel with a convict named Altamont. While the convict had successfully escaped justice for cutting down the officer in charge, the mere possibility of Altamont resurfacing under British authority meant a swift return to the gallows. No sensible guardian wished to secure a wife whose father was currently awaiting execution, even if the threat of exposure served as a powerful lever to control the fugitive. However, the Major reasoned that Altamont, facing the noose, would naturally avoid recognition, while Clavering, terrified of losing everything if his secret lover appeared, would become a willing puppet in the hands of anyone holding that fatal knowledge.

Yet, there was a darker economic reality looming. The Begum, Clavering's wealthy second wife, had already spent fortunes clearing her husband's debts. Her vast resources were destined to be drained entirely by this "irreclaimable reprobate." Consequently, her heirs would inherit an empty treasury. Furthermore, Miss Amory—the Begum's daughter—would arrive at her husband's side not with a dowry or a parliamentary seat, but with her person and a pedigree tarnished by the infamous legal notation *sus. per coll.* (suspicion of collusion) attached to the name of her deceased father.

Despite these grim prospects, the Major saw a potential opening. He needed to speak privately with Sir Francis Clavering, the Member for Clavering, to discuss a scheme that required absolute confidentiality.

Finding a man in financial distress is akin to finding a lost bird; he vanishes from his usual haunts, only to reappear in the strangest places. One might expect a gentleman of fashion to be found in a club or on Rotten Row, yet such men often surface happily eating cheap food in Billingsgate or seeking refuge in obscure pubs like the "Fox under the Hill" while fleeing the law. Similarly, Sir Francis Clavering, embroiled in domestic strife and debt, had ceased to exist in his own home. As his servant, Mr. Lightfoot, noted, the Baronet pretended to sleep when water was brought and slipped out the moment the footman descended the stairs. Following his quarrel with Mr. Strong, Clavering had fled to unknown haunts, escaping not only the Chevalier's reproaches but also the nagging voice of his own conscience.

The Major, determined to catch this elusive target, faced significant difficulty. Since becoming the family's confidant, Clavering had treated him with the same avoidance he reserved for lawyers and agents. Invitations to dinner at Greenwich were politely declined; requests for brief conversations resulted in appointments made with military precision ("four o'clock precisely") that invariably ended with the Baronet's absence. Clavering was as timid as a borrower who fears the lender, proving that seeking a man is one thing, but finding him is quite another.

Meanwhile, the wheels of deception continued to turn. Despite pledging his honor to stop issuing new bills, Sir Francis had secretly signed a promissory note via his associate, Mr. Moss Abrams. The transaction occurred in the back parlor of Mr. Santiago's cigar shop near St. James's Street. Santiago, a man well-versed in the habits of London's rogues, recognized the handwriting immediately. "He is at his old work again," Santiago observed to a passing customer. "A bill for fifty pounds, dated two months ago. He'll claim he forgot it during his recent settlement with his wife. They'll give him more money now that he's 'clear.'"

Santiago's shop stood directly across from the Major's lodgings. As the Major walked daintily toward his door, key in hand, he encountered Mr. Strong, the Chevalier, striding along the pavement with a cigar glowing ominously in his mouth and a mustache that screamed of disrepute.

"Confound these young men!" the Major muttered internally. "Mustachios and cigars are the trademarks of low fellows." He paused, offering a stiff, dignified bow. "Good evening, Mr. Strong. I trust you are well?"

Strong stopped, extending a hand that the Major barely reciprocated with two ungracious fingers. "What brings you to town, Mr. Strong?" the Major asked, attempting to sound patronizing despite his confusion.

"My news is bad, sir," Strong replied gravely. "It concerns our friends at Tunbridge Wells. Clavering is at his old tricks again, Major Pendennis."

"Indeed! Come inside," the Major urged, leading Strong into his drawing room. Once seated, Strong unloaded his indignation. "No promise binds him, sir. Remember when we met with his lawyer? He refused to give his word unless he could swear on a Bible, kneeling and invoking damnation. Yet today he signed a fresh bill. He will sign as many as you please for ready cash. There is a bill of his and mine coming due next week."

"I thought we had settled all of this," the Major protested.

"Not that one," Strong admitted, flushing slightly. "He asked me not to mention it. I had half the money for it, but... well, they will be down on me. But I don't care; I'm used to it. It is Lady Clavering who angers me. It is a shame that such a good-natured woman, who has paid him out of prison a dozen times, should be ruined by his heartlessness. A pack of bill-steealing boxers and rascals get his money, and he doesn't scruple to throw an honest fellow over. Did you know he took money from Altamont?"

"Altamont?" The Major feigned ignorance. "That singular man who visited your house tipsy once?"

"I don't know who he is if you don't," Strong replied suspiciously.

Seeing the Chevalier's defensiveness, the Major shifted tactics instantly. Adopting a tone of worldly wisdom, he suggested that Clavering likely harbored secrets that Altamont exploited for profit. "Many men of the best families pay through the nose to keep their private affairs quiet," the Major mused smoothly. "But those are not my business, Mr. Strong. I am interested only in the welfare of his wife and that charming daughter. If I am called upon to intervene, I shall do so to the best of my ability. By the way, is this Mr. Altamont in town?"

Strong rose abruptly, grabbing his hat in a huff. "I am not at liberty to disclose his whereabouts, Major Pendennis."

"Precisely!" the Major exclaimed, seizing on the evasion. "Caution is the mark of a true soldier. We cannot know what ears walls may have. You have your ideas, I have mine; let us simply agree that some things are best left unknown until necessity demands otherwise." He patted Strong's arm warmly. "An old brother soldier agrees with me! Good evening, Captain Strong. Thank you for your visit. Morgan, show the door."

As Strong departed, bewildered by the Major's sudden shift from hostility to camaraderie, the Major's valet, Morgan, remained in the shadows. Having overheard the entire conversation, Morgan later approached Mr. Lightfoot at the Club.

"Do you know who Altamont is?" Morgan whispered.

"Strong's man of business," Lightfoot replied casually. "He draws the Governor's bills and does odd jobs. Kite-flying requires a team to lift the paper. Altamont put the pot on at the Derby and won big. I wish the Governor could get some luck, and I'd love to see my book paid up."

Morgan then pressed Lightfoot on whether Lady Clavering would pay the debts again. Intrigued, Morgan revealed a startling secret: he himself had been profiting from the chaos. Through careful speculation and leveraging his position within the Major's household, Morgan had amassed a small fortune. He had purchased numerous bills of exchange bearing Clavering's name, including those endorsed by Edward Strong.

When the Major finally confronted Morgan about his newfound wealth, the valet confessed with humble pride. "Yes, sir, a little," Morgan admitted, looking down. "Having gained some information and acquaintance with the family through your kindness, I put my money on the pot. I bought Sir Francis's bills, many of which bore the name of the gentleman who just left, Mr. Strong. Knowing the scandal in Grosvenor Place, I figured I might as well make my money as anyone else. Please tell me if Lady Clavering intends to pay any more."

The Major was stunned. He felt a mixture of indignation at the audacity of a servant growing rich under his nose and a begrudging admiration

On a picturesque common near Tunbridge Wells, Lady Clavering had secured a charming villa, retreating there to escape the marital storms of the previous London season. Naturally, Miss Amory accompanied her mother, while Master Clavering returned for his holidays. For Blanche, the young schoolboy's presence was primarily an opportunity for fighting and quarreling—a delightful domestic pastime, though one the lad himself preferred to avoid. Instead, he discovered cricket, horses, and a legion of friends at Tunbridge.

The "good-natured Begum's" household had transformed into a chaotic sanctuary for a constant swarm of thirteen-year-old gentlemen. These youths consumed tarts and champagne with alarming frequency, raced across the lawns, terrified the fond mother, smoked until they felt ill, and generally rendered the dining room unbearable for Miss Blanche. She harbored no affection for this specific breed of young gentleman. Conversely, any change was inherently pleasant to the fair young creature; for a week or two, she would have traded her current comforts for poverty, a cottage, bread and cheese, and perhaps even a night in a dungeon with watered-down bread. Consequently, the move to Tunbridge was far from unwelcome to her.

Blanche wandered the woods sketching trees and farmhouses, devoured French novels, and frequently drove into Tunbridge Wells to attend plays, balls, conjurers, or musicians wherever they appeared. She slept excessively, quarreled with her mother and Frank during the mornings, attended the local village school with gusto—first coquettishly fondling the girls and thwarting the mistress, then scolding them and mocking the teacher—and remained dutifully present at church.

It was a lovely, ancient little church, an Anglo-Norman jewel seemingly constructed the day before yesterday, adorned with painted windows, carved saints' heads, gilded scripture texts, and open pews. Blanche immediately set about creating a perfectly correct high-church altar cover for it. To the clergyman, she soon passed for a saint. She charmed, coaxed, wheedled, and fondled him with such artful manipulation that poor Mrs. Smirke—initially captivated, then resigned, and finally barely speaking to her—was driven nearly mad with jealousy.

Mrs. Smirke was the wife of our old friend Smirke, Pen's former tutor and the suitor of poor Helen. Having consoled himself for Helen's rejection with a young lady from Clapham provided by his mother, his views hardened daily upon her death. He cut off his coat collar, allowed his hair to cascade over his shoulders, abandoned the curl atop his forehead and the necktie he once prized, opting instead for no tie whatsoever. He skipped Friday dinners, read the Roman Hours, and announced his readiness to hear confessions in the vestry.

The most harmless creature imaginable, he was denounced by Muffin of the Dissenting chapel and Mr. Simeon Knight of the old church as a black, dangerous Jesuit and Papist. Smirke had built his chapel-of-ease using the inheritance left by his mother in Clapham. Heaven help us if she heard him refer to a table as an altar, place candlesticks upon it, or sign letters on the Feast of Saint So-and-so! Yet, these were precisely the practices of the boy from Clapham, faithfully emulated by his equally zealous wife.

When Blanche held a conference lasting nearly two hours in the vestry with Mr. Smirke, Belinda paced anxiously on the grass, where only two small gravestones stood. She wished desperately for a third, fearing that her husband would soon offer himself to the creature who had infatuated him within a fortnight. No, she decided; she would retreat, enter a convent, make her profession, and leave him. Such dark thoughts plagued Smirke's wife and neighbors, who suspected him of direct correspondence with the Bishop of Rome and bewailing errors even more odious than hers. Yet, our friend meant no earthly harm. The post office never delivered a single letter from the Pope. Initially, he considered Blanche the most pious, gifted, and fascinating person he had ever met; her singing of the Chants delighted him. But gradually, he grew weary of Miss Amory. Her ways and graces seemed to lose their luster; doubt crept in. Then, she caused a disturbance in his school, lost her temper, and tapped the children's fingers.

Blanche possessed a peculiar power to inspire both admiration and satiety in many men. She attempted to please them, deploying all her charms simultaneously—jewels, smiles, cajolery, coaxing, and ogles. Once exhausted by the effort and realizing she never truly cared for them, she dropped them. In turn, the men grew tired of her and discarded her as well.

It was a blissful night for Belinda when Blanche departed. Her husband, blushing slightly and sighing, admitted he had been deceived. He claimed he thought her endowed with precious gifts, fearing they were merely tinsel; that she had been a right-thinking person, whereas she had simply made religion an amusement. He noted she had indeed lost her temper toward the schoolmistress and beaten Polly Rucker's knuckles cruelly. Belinda rushed into his arms; questions of graves and veils vanished instantly. He embraced her tenderly on the forehead. "There is none like thee, my Belinda," he declared, casting his fine eyes upward to the ceiling, "precious among women!"

As for Blanche, from the moment she lost sight of him and Belinda, she ceased to think or care about either of them. However, when Arthur descended to spend a few days at Tunbridge Wells with the Begum, this stage of indifference had not yet arrived for Miss Blanche or the simple clergyman. Smirke still regarded her as an angelic wonder. He had never witnessed such perfection; he sat listening to her music in summer evenings, mouth agape, rapt in wonder, forgetting tea and bread-and-butter entirely. Although he had once attended an opera exhibition (a memory he recalled with a blush and a sigh, recounting the day he accompanied Helen and her son to the play at Chatteris), he could conceive nothing more delicious or celestial than Miss Amory's music.

She was, in his eyes, a most gifted being with a precious soul, remarkable talents, and a heavenly disposition. Thus, while burning with his own fever and bewitchment for Blanche, Smirke discoursed to Arthur about her. Their reunion had been cordial. Arthur loved anyone who loved his mother, and Smirke could speak on that theme with genuine emotion. They exchanged hundreds of anecdotes about their respective lives.

"Arthur will perceive," Smirke remarked, "that your views on Church matters have developed significantly since our acquaintance." Mrs. Smirke, a model of exemplarity, seconded him with all her efforts. He explained that he had built the little church following his mother's demise, who had left him sufficient worldly means. Though living in a sort of cloister, he had heard of Arthur's reputation. Speaking in the kindest, most sorrowful tones, with lowered eyelids and a bowed head, he expressed his sentiments.

Arthur was immensely amused by him—by his airs, follies, simplicity, blank stocks, and long hair; by his true goodness, kindness, and friendly feelings. His praises of Blanche pleased and surprised our friend greatly, causing him to regard her with eyes of particular favor. Indeed, Blanche was very glad to see Arthur. Like anyone else, she rejoiced to encounter an agreeable man in the countryside who brought fresh news and stories from the great city, capable of speaking that darling London jargon so essential to Londoners and so little understood by provincials.

The first day Pen arrived, he kept Blanche laughing for hours after dinner. She sang her songs with renewed spirit, stopped scolding her mother, and instead fondled and kissed her to the honest Begum's surprise. At bedtime, she exclaimed, "Deja!" with the prettiest air of regret, genuinely sorry to sleep, and squeezing Arthur's hand affectionately. He pressed her palm warmly in return.

Our young gentleman was of a turn where moderately bright eyes could dazzle him. "She has improved remarkably," Pen thought, gazing out into the night. "Very much. I suppose the Begum won't mind my smoking with the window open. She's a jolly good old woman, and Blanche is immensely improved. I liked her manner with her mother tonight. I enjoyed her laughing way with that stupid young cub of a boy, whom they shouldn't allow to get tipsy. She sang those little verses very prettily; they were devilish pretty verses too, though I say it who shouldn't." He hummed a tune Blanche had composed for some verses of his own.

"Ah! What a fine night! How jolly a cigar is at night! How pretty that little Saxon church looks in the moonlight! I wonder what old Warrington's doing? Yes, she's a devilish nice little thing, as my uncle says."

"Oh, heavenly!"

Suddenly, a voice broke from a clematis-covered casement nearby—a girl's voice, the author of *Mes Larmes*. Pen burst into laughter.

"Don't tell about my smoking," he called out, leaning from his own window.

"Oh! Go on! I adore it," cried the lady of *Mes Larmes*. "Heavenly night! Heavenly, heavenly moon! But I must shut my window and not talk to you on account of *les moeurs*. How droll they are,

It seemed strange that Arthur Pendennis, usually so open and frank with his friend Warrington, withheld the details of his recent escapades at the villa near Tunbridge Wells. He freely discussed the rediscovery of his old tutor, Smirke, his new wife, the peculiarities of his Anglo-Norman church, and his journey from Clapham to Rome. Yet, whenever the subject turned to Blanche, his responses became evasive. He dismissed her as merely "a good-natured clever little thing," suggesting that with proper guidance she might not turn out such a disastrous wife after all. He declared his romantic days over, content with his current state, and generally avoided committing himself.

Meanwhile, however, the truth was arriving at Lamb Court in the form of elegant satin envelopes, penned in neat script and sealed with a cryptic cipher. If Warrington had possessed the curiosity to inspect his friend's mail—or if the code had been decipherable—he would have discovered that Arthur was corresponding with a young lady whose initials were B.A. In return, Mr. Pen replied with his customary gallantry, mixing town gossip, witty remarks, and even original verse in response to the rhyming efforts of Blanche's muse, 'Mes Larmes.' After all, since Blanche rhymes with words like "branch" and "launch," a man of Pen's ingenuity would surely exploit such poetic advantages. Indeed, it is highly probable that the love poems which later achieved such fame in *The Roseleaves*—that charming annual edited by Lady Violet Lebas and illustrated by the renowned artist Pinkney—were originally composed during this period as private missives to Blanche before ever appearing in print.

"Don't let your head spin on poetry," advised the elder Pendennis, watching his nephew scratch out one of these innocent verses at the club while awaiting dinner. "Letter-writing is fine if Mother permits it, especially with old country friends, but beware, Pen. Don't tie yourself down. Who knows what mischief might ensue? My advice is to keep your letters safe. I've never written a letter in my life that committed me, and *demmy*, sir, I have considerable experience with women." The Major, growing increasingly garrulous as he aged, regaled his nephew with harrowing tales of societal disasters caused by a lack of caution. He recounted how young Spoony, after sending overly ardent verses to the Widow Naylor, was forced by her brother, Colonel Flint, into a marriage with a woman old enough to be his mother. Similarly, he detailed how Hopwood of the Blues, having secured Louisa Salter, was exposed by letters she had written to him, leading to his withdrawal and subsequent union with Miss Stickney of Lyme Regis. The Major, lacking books but rich in worldly observation, backed his wisdom with countless modern examples drawn from the great book of the world.

Pen laughed at the anecdotes, blushing slightly at his uncle's warnings, and promised to be cautious. He likely blushed because he already was; instinctively, or perhaps driven by honesty, he had refrained in his letters to Miss Blanche from any declarations that could compromise him. "Remember the lesson I learned in Lady Mirabel's regarding Miss Fotheringay's affair?" Arthur said with mock humility. "I shall not be caught twice, Uncle." Old Pendennis congratulated himself and his nephew on this newfound prudence, pleased to see Arthur maturing into a man of the world.

Had Warrington been consulted, his verdict would undoubtedly have differed. He would have argued that the boy's foolish letters were superior to the man's slick compliments and slippery gallantries. To truly win the woman one loves, only a knave or a coward hides behind subterfuge and secures a retreat. Yet, Pen knew his friend was guilty of such tactics and wisely chose not to discuss the matter with him.

Just as fate began to weigh heavily upon the remaining partner of the firm at Shepherd's Inn, Colonel Altamont returned from his foreign tour, and Sir Francis Clavering retired to the countryside. Captain Strong, however, faced sudden misfortune. Having refused a generous loan from Altamont earlier—a refusal born of excessive delicacy and conscience—Strong now regretted his scruples bitterly. Why deny a thirsty man a pitcher of water simply because it was slightly soiled? His conscience gnawed at him as he realized that Altamont's cash would have been safer in his pocket than in the hands of gambling den owners in Baden or Ems. Rumors spread quickly among tradesmen and bill-discounters that Strong and the Baronet had fallen out, rendering Strong's credit worthless. Once trusted for his jolly demeanor, Strong was now besieged. Tailors, bootmakers, and pastry cooks descended upon his chambers, followed closely by the silent, dangerous threat of young clerks carrying writs demanding his appearance before the Queen.

Cornered within his fortress, Strong shut the doors against the creditors. While the foes cursed outside, the Chevalier peered from behind a curtain covering his letterbox, enjoying the sight of furious debtors retreating in defeat. Though they could not guard his gates twenty-four hours a day, the siege was relentless. Fortunately, Strong had allies. Friends like Huxter and Miss Fanny Bolton maintained a supply line via private signals. When hostile visitors prowled, Fanny's younger sisters would emit a distinctive whoop from the courtyard. Upon hearing this signal, Fanny and Huxter would approach, singing the same note, prompting Strong's loyal garrison to emerge, provisions and ale in tow, allowing the beleaguered captain to pass a comfortable night. Strong, a brave soldier who had never lost heart in peril, thrived on this excitement.

Beyond his allies, Strong had secured a vital asset: a retreat. Years ago, he had discovered a leaden water-pipe connecting his kitchen window to the room of his neighbors, Messrs. Costigan and Bows. Laughingly demonstrating this shortcut to Altamont, they had agreed to keep the secret from Costigan, whose own debts were numerous. Now, in times of trouble, Strong utilized this passage. One afternoon, bursting into Bows and Costigan's apartment with his characteristic jolliness, he explained that the enemy was blocking his stairs and that he had escaped via the pipe. Thus, while the sheriff's officers waited in the hallway of No. 3, Strong descended the steps of No. 4, dined at the Albion, attended the theater, and returned home at midnight, baffling Mrs. Bolton and Fanny, who could not imagine how he had bypassed the sentries.

For weeks, Strong endured this siege with admirable spirit, though the pain and privation tested even a veteran's resolve. What truly riled him was the cold indifference of Clavering, to whom he wrote repeatedly yet received not a single acknowledgment or remittance. A five-pound note, Strong noted, would have been a fortune at that time.

However, better days were soon in store. Amidst his despair, a welcome aid arrived. "If it hadn't been for this good fellow here," Strong exclaimed, referring to Altamont, "Pendennis, I think it would have been all up with Ned Strong. I was in my fifth week of captivity, unable to risk crossing the pipe daily, and my spirit was broken. I considered ending it all when—who should descend from heaven but Altamont!"

"Heaven isn't exactly the location, Ned," Altamont corrected cheerfully. "I came from Baden-Baden, where I'd had a remarkably lucky month." True to his word, Altamont settled Marks's bill and paid off the other creditors. "I'll buy a magnum of claret for this company," the Colonel announced, blushing slightly. "And may every good fellow like Strong find another good fellow to stand by him at a pinch. That's my sentiment, Mr. Pendennis, though I don't care for your name."

"No! And why?" asked Arthur.

Strong pressed the Colonel's foot under the table. Excited, Altamont refilled his glass, nodded to Pen, drained his wine, and declared, "He was a gentleman, and that was sufficient; they were all gentlemen."

This gathering of "gentlemen" occurred at Richmond, where Pendennis had gone to dine. Finding the Chevalier and his friend hilariously intoxicated in the coffee room, Strong launched into a vivid account of his siege, the signals of Fanny, Costigan's groans, and his dramatic rescue by Altamont.

"As for me, it's nothing," Altamont boasted. "When a ship is paid off, a chap spends his money. The fellows at the black and red at Baden-Baden did it. I won a good sum there and intend to win more. I'm taking someone with me—I've got a system. I'll make fortunes, yours included. But seriously, I'm going to pursue that little Fanny. Did you know she lent two pounds to Ned Strong? Let's drink her health."

"With all my heart," Arthur pledged cordially.

Altamont then unveiled his "infallible" gambling system, claiming it originated from a man at Baden who had lost only due to insufficient capital. He planned to establish a bank, investing everything he owned, and intended to take Strong along as his aide-de-camp, trusting his temperament more than his own or that of his partners, Bloundell-Bloundell and an Italian named "Stand-in." As he emptied his bottle, the Colonel outlined his grandiose plans to Pen, who listened with interest to the confessions of his daring and lawless humor.

Later, Pen recounted the encounter to his uncle. "That's

Sir Francis Clavering's primary objective in life was to secure capital, and currently, he was residing in a state of genteel destitution at Clavering Park. Once a bustling estate, the great house now resembled a skeleton stripped of its flesh; the majority of the mansion stood shuttered and silent. The Baronet confined himself to a handful of ground-floor rooms, where a beleaguered housekeeper and her lodge-gate assistant served him with the somber efficiency of refugees. They prepared portions of the venison he hunted during his dreary mornings, while his former manservant, Lightfoot, had defected to Lady Clavering's household.

As Pen learned via a letter from Mr. Smirke—the officiant who had quietly married Mrs. Bonner to Lightfoot—the situation was evolving. Mrs. Bonner, despite her advanced years, possessed a magnetic charm for the youthful Lightfoot and had generously transferred her savings to him. Their shared dream was to transform Lady Clavering's domain into the "Clavering Arms" inn. Until Quarter Day arrived to effectuate this transfer, however, they remained in service, waiting for the moment when Pen graciously pledged to hold his election dinner at their future establishment. This promise was contingent upon the Baronet vacating his parliamentary seat—a favor Uncle Arthur had seemingly coerced from the reluctant Sir Francis.

Consequently, Arthur descended upon Clavering Park in September. The owner, eager for companionship and perhaps a loan, welcomed the young man. True to form, Pen supplied his host with a modest sum of cash within two days. Instantly, Sir Francis Clavering vanished, citing urgent business in Chatteris and various neighboring watering-places. As one might expect, these affairs were conducted amidst the dust of county racecourses and the smoke-filled billiard rooms.

Arthur, conversely, proved surprisingly self-sufficient. A man of considerable mental resources, he entertained himself with walks alongside the gamekeeper and an extensive library collection that kept him occupied until late. Yet, within mere days, he found the company of Sir Francis utterly insufferable. With a mischievous relish, he then offered the Baronet further financial assistance, effectively funding his escape from the house.

Beyond his domestic maneuvers, Pen embarked on a campaign of political ingratiation. Remembering his past unpopularity among the simple folk of Clavering, he resolved to erase any lingering odium. His natural reserve dissolved into a performance of boisterous joviality; he laughed with everyone, shook hands with a dexterous cordiality, and attended every market day and farmers' ordinary. He played the role of the consummate hypocrite with gusto, mimicking the behavior of high-born gentlemen seeking votes. How easily we are duped by a glib tongue and a ready smile! We recognize the falseness of such coin, yet we accept it, preferring the cheap flattery of universal distribution to the silence of indifference.

Pen moved through Clavering as a laboriously simple, adroitly pleasing figure, a stark contrast to the scornful dandy the villagers recalled from a decade prior. The Rectory stood empty; Dr. Portman had fled to Harrogate with his gout and family—an exile Pen lamented deeply in a letter, expressing regret at missing his old mentor's counsel. Instead, Pen cultivated alliances with Mr. Simcoe, the opposition preacher, the partners of the local cloth factory, and the Independent minister, all convened at the newly established Clavering Athenaeum. Founded by the Liberal party to reflect the age's progressive spirit—and likely to exclude the aristocratic old reading room where *The Edinburgh Review* had once struggled for entry—this venue became Pen's stage.

He wooed the younger cloth-factory partner with a friendly dinner invitation at the Park, gifted Honorable Mrs. Simcoe game from the estate along with a complimentary copy of her husband's latest sermon, and exploited a minor illness to solicit medical attention from Mr. Huxter. Old Pendennis would have been proud of his pupil's antics. Pen found the sport delightful, his success breeding a wicked sort of good humor.

Yet, as he departed Clavering at nightfall, having "presided" over an Athenaeum meeting or spent an evening with Mrs. Simcoe—who viewed him with awe due to his London reputation—different tides rose within him. Walking across the familiar bridge over the rushing Brawl, hearing the water's eternal roar, and glimpsing his childhood cottage of Fairoaks silhouetted against the stars, memories struck with painful clarity. He recalled the warm light in the window where his saintly mother had spent hours in prayer and care. That faint light seemed to follow him now, a wan, reproachful gaze, as if her spirit watched and warned.

How clear the night air was! How sharp the stars! The Brawl flowed ceaselessly, and the ancient trees whispered overhead, waving their dark branches over the cottage roof. There lay the terrace where, as a boy, he had wandered in summer evenings, ardent and trustful, untouched by doubt or passion, sheltered in his mother's loving bosom. Suddenly, the town clock tolled midnight, shattering his reverie and driving him onward toward his lodging in Clavering Avenue, beneath the rustling lime arches.

When he returned the following afternoon, the scene had transformed. The cottage smiled in the sunset; the bedroom windows were open, filled with light. His tenant, Captain Stokes of the Bombay Artillery (accompanied by his mother, Mrs. Stokes), greeted him with great warmth. Stokes showed him the grounds and a new pond dug in the garden from the stables, eagerly inviting Pen to inspect the roof and chimneys.

Pen, having stayed for a fortnight, confessed with a straight face that he lacked the heart to call sooner. "I understand you, sir," Captain Stokes replied. Mrs. Stokes, having retreated at the bell's ring (a gesture that seemed oddly formal to Pen), emerged in her finest gown, surrounded by her children. The youngsters clamored around Stokes; the boy leaped into an armchair—Pen's father's very own armchair. Arthur remembered days when sitting in that chair seemed as impossible as ascending the King's throne. He asked if Miss Stokes, who resembled her mother, could play the piano. She obliged. The notes drifted from the aged instrument, weak but recognizable. Yet, Pen did not truly listen to the music; he heard Laura singing as in their youth, and saw his mother bending beside the girl, beating time.

Later, at a dinner hosted by Captain Stokes in Pen's honor, attended by Squire Hobnel, the vicar of Tinckleton, and others, the atmosphere was initially stiff and melancholy. The mood shifted dramatically when Hodson, a waiter formerly known to Pen as a street urchin and now a barber, accidentally dropped a plate over Pen's shoulder. Squire Hobnel chuckled, remarking, "I suppose, Hodson, your hands are slippery with bear's-grease. He's always dropping the crockery about, that Hodson is—haw, haw!" Hodson blushed crimson, looking thoroughly disconcerted, causing Pen to burst into laughter. Good humor and hilarity took command of the evening. For the second course, a hare and partridges were served top and bottom. After the servants withdrew, Pen jokingly told the Vicar, "Mr. Stooks, you really should have asked Hodson to carve the hare." The clergyman seized the jest immediately, followed swiftly by Captains Stokes and Glanders, and finally Mr. Hobnell, who arrived late but with an immense guffaw.

While Pen orchestrated these rural schemes, events unfolded in London involving the object of his affection, Blanche. Having traveled from Tunbridge Wells for shopping and business accompanied by Mrs. Bonner, the old nurse, the pair sought to part ways with gifts. Mrs. Bonner, intending to become Lightfoot's wife and landlady of the Clavering Arms, wished to leave a token of respect. Blanche, guided by her mother's taste, selected items for herself and her mother, including a massive Bible for the elder woman and a large Johnson's Dictionary for the younger. Mrs. Bonner also invested in linens for the future inn and purchased a red-and-yellow neckerchief clearly destined for Lightfoot.

Younger than herself by twenty-five years, Mrs. Bonner viewed the youth with a blend of maternal and conjugal affection, lavishing jewelry upon him at her own expense. In the Strand, aided by Blanche, she completed her purchases. As the old lady departed, Blanche, radiant in a shot-silk dress and a magnificent mantle, politely asked a shop assistant for directions to Shepherd's Inn.

"Shepherd's Inn?" the shopman queried. "Do you want to see Mr. Strong?"

"I'd like to see the Captain very well," Blanche admitted, though her true intent was elsewhere. "But I'm not looking for him. I want to see a dear little girl who was so kind to Mr. Arthur when he was ill last year; she almost saved his life. I want to thank her." She glanced at Mrs. Bonner with an expression of virtuous pride.

Mrs. Bonner muttered, "Pretty girl... I know I want no pretty young women about Lightfoot," mentally populating the Clavering Arms with a harem of hideous chambermaids. Meanwhile, Blanche, adorned with pink ribbons, blue feathers, and a charming parasol, presented a vision of elegance that bewildered Mrs. Bolton, a scrubwoman cleaning the floors of Shepherd's Inn.

"My good woman," Blanche said sweetly, "I wish to see Fanny—Fanny Bolton.

While Pen was busy hatching his own selfish schemes and parliamentary plots in his native county, news arrived that Lady Rockminster had reached Baymouth, bringing with her our friend Laura. Upon hearing that his sister was nearby, Pen felt a sudden pang of guilt. His deepest desire was to stand higher in her esteem than in anyone else's; she was his mother's legacy, the one person to whom he owed protection and patronage. How would she react to the news he carried? How could he possibly explain the ambitious plans swirling in his mind? He feared that neither he nor Blanche could withstand Laura's dazzling, calm gaze of scrutiny. He felt he dared not reveal his worldly hopes and ambitions to such a spotless judge.

Upon her arrival, Pen wrote a letter filled with flowery phrases, professions of affection, and a dash of easy satire. Yet, amidst the fine writing, he couldn't help feeling panicked, acting like a rogue and a hypocrite. How could a simple country girl inspire such terror in an accomplished gentleman like Mr. Pen? His diplomatic tactics and cynical knowledge of the world simply couldn't survive the test of her purity. He admitted to himself that his affairs were in such a precarious position that he couldn't tell the truth to that honest soul. Riding from Clavering to Baymouth, he felt as guilty as a schoolboy facing his master without knowing his lesson. After all, Truth is always the master, and she holds the book.

Under the care of her kind, albeit somewhat wayward and absolute, patroness Lady Rockminster, Laura had seen something of the world in the past year, gathering accomplishments and learning from society. Many girls, accustomed to the excessive tenderness of Laura's early life, would have crumbled under this change. Her mother, Helen, had worshipped her two children, believing the whole world existed for them. She watched over Laura with an affection that never wavered. If Laura had a headache, the widow was as alarmed as if it were the first ache in history. Now, however, that watchful eye was withdrawn, along with the tender heart that would soon beat no more. There were undoubtedly painful moments of grief and depression when Laura stood alone in the vast, careless world. No one seemed to heed her sorrows or her solitude. She wasn't socially equal to the lady she served, nor to the friends of the imperious dowager. Some bore her no goodwill; some perhaps slighted her. Servants were occasionally rude, and her mistress often was. Laura frequently found herself intruding on family gatherings, her sensitivity wounded by the realization that she caused annoyance.

"How many governesses are there?" mused cheerful Laura. "How many ladies whose necessities make them professional slaves and companions? What bad tempers and coarse unkindness haven't they encountered? How infinitely better my lot is with these truly kind and affectionate people!" It was with this cordial spirit that she adapted to her new position, moving ahead of her fortune with a trusting smile.

Does anyone who meets Fortune with such openness fail to win the Goddess's favor? Even bad people are won over by constant cheerfulness and a pure, affectionate heart. Recall the babes in the woods in the ballads, looking fondly at the notorious rogues sent to kill them. We know how one rascal relented, sparing the child due to their innocence and beauty. Oh, happy are those who possess that virgin-loving trust and sweet-smiling confidence, fearing no evil because they believe none exists! Miss Laura Bell was one of these fortunate souls. Beyond the gentle widow's occasional crosses, she wore a sparkling, brilliant Koh-i-Noor in her bosom—a gem far more precious than the famous diamond. It fetched a high price, held its value in the next world where jewels count for nothing, and here too, it was invaluable: a talisman against evil, lighting up life's darkness like Cogia Hassan's stone.

Before a year had passed in Lady Rockminster's household, Laura had won everyone's love. From the old lady to the lowest dependent, she secured universal goodwill. Consider the mistress's woman, who had endured forty years of claws, scolding, and jibes. Naturally, she was initially angry at Miss Laura, just as she had been with the previous fifteen companions. But when Laura fell ill in Paris, this old woman nursed her in defiance of her mistress, who feared catching the fever, fighting desperately for the medicine alongside Martha from Fairoaks. As Laura recovered, Grandjean the chef nearly killed her with an avalanche of delicacies, weeping when she finally ate a slice of chicken. The Swiss major-domo praised Miss Bell in almost every European language he knew (albeit with indifferent accuracy); the coachman delighted in driving her; the page cried when she was sick; and Calverley and Coldstream—the two footmen usually so large, calm, and immovable—burst into hilarity at the news of her recovery, intoxicating the page at a wine shop to celebrate. Even Lady Diana Pynsent, who had disliked Laura for some time, declared her an agreeable person and a great *trouvaille* for her grandmother. All this goodwill was earned not by art or flattery, but by the sheer force of good nature and the blessed gift of pleasing and being pleased.

On the rare occasions Pen had seen Lady Rockminster, the old lady—who didn't particularly admire him—had been pitiless and abrupt. Perhaps Pen expected to find Laura installed as a humble companion, treated no better than himself. Instead, upon hearing of his arrival, she rushed downstairs. Whether she embraced him in front of Calverley and Coldstream remains uncertain, though those gentlemen never reported it. If the fractured orb had shattered, or if Laura had snipped off his head with her scissors, Calverley and Coldstream would have watched impassively, undisturbed by the calamity.

Laura had improved so greatly in health and appearance that Pen couldn't help admiring her. Her frank, kind eyes beamed with vitality; the cheek he kissed flushed with beauty. Looking at her, artless and graceful, pure and candid, he realized he had never seen her so beautiful. Why did he notice her beauty now, and realize he hadn't noticed it sooner? Taking her fair, trustful hand, he kissed it fondly, gazing into her bright, clear eyes and reading the kindling welcome always found there. He was deeply affected by her tender tone and pure sparkle; their innocence struck him and moved him profoundly.

"How good you are to me, Laura—sister!" Pen said. "I don't deserve that you should be so kind to me."

"Mamma left you to me," she replied, stooping to brush his forehead with her lips. "You know you were to come to me when in trouble, or to tell me when very happy; that was our compact, Arthur, last year before we parted. Are you very happy now, or in trouble? Do you like going into Parliament? Do you intend to distinguish yourself there? How I shall tremble for your first speech!"

"Do you know about the Parliament plan, then?" Pen asked.

"Know? Everyone knows! I've heard it discussed many times. Lady Rockminster's doctor talked about it today. It'll be in the Chatteris paper tomorrow. It's all over the county that Sir Francis Clavering is retiring in favor of Mr. Arthur Pendennis of Fairoaks, and that the young, beautiful Miss Blanche Amory is—"

"What! That too?" Pen exclaimed.

"That too, dear Arthur. *Tout se sait*, as someone would say, whom I intend to be very fond of, and who I'm sure is clever and pretty. I've had a letter from Blanche. The kindest of letters. She speaks so warmly of you, Arthur! I hope—I know she feels what she writes. When is it to be, Arthur? Why didn't you tell me? May I come and live with you then, mayn't I?"

"My home is yours, dear Laura, and everything I have," Pen said. "If I didn't tell you, it was because... because... I don't know. Nothing is decided yet. No words have passed between us. But you think Blanche could be happy with me, don't you? Not a romantic fondness, you know. I have no heart, I think; I've told her so. Only a sober-sided attachment: wanting my wife on one side of the fire and my sister on the other—Parliament in session and Fairoaks in the holidays, and my Laura never leaving me until someone with a right comes to take her away."

"Somebody with a right! Some- body with a right!" Why, as he looked at the girl and slowly uttered the words, did Pen begin to feel angry and jealous of this invisible somebody with the right to take her away? Just a moment ago, anxious about how she'd take the news of his arrangement with Blanche, he was hurt that she received the intelligence so easily, taking his happiness for granted.

"Until somebody comes," Laura said with a laugh, "I will stay at home and be Aunt Laura, taking care of the children when Blanche arrives in the world. I've arranged it all. I'm an excellent housekeeper. Do you know I've been to market in Paris with Mrs. Beck and taken lessons from M. Grandjean? I also had singing lessons in Paris with the money you sent me, you kind boy. I can sing much better now, and I've learned to dance, though not as well as Blanche. And when you become a Minister of State, Blanche shall present me!" With this provoking good humor, she performed the last Parisian curt

If you've ever stepped inside the *Wheel of Fortune* public house—where Mr. James Morgan's club usually congregated, and where Sir Francis Clavering once had a rather heated chat with Major Pendennis—you'll recall the layout. On the ground floor, aside from the bar where the landlady presides, there are three guest rooms. One is a general parlour for the public; another caters to gentlemen in livery; and the third, marked "Private" on the door, belongs to the exclusive "Confidentials," a club of which Morgan and Lightfoot were proud members.

Mr. Morgan, a man of considerable stealth, had eavesdropped on the earlier conversation between Strong and Major Pendennis at the Major's lodgings, gathering enough material for private speculation. Curiosity drove him to shadow the Major to the *Wheel of Fortune*, slipping quietly into the Confidential Room while Pendennis and Clavering argued loudly in the adjacent parlour. There existed a specific vantage point within the Confidential Room offering a perfect acoustic view of the next room. Since the argument between the two gentlemen was fierce and carried on in a high register, Morgan caught nearly everything. What he heard only confirmed the suspicions swirling in his mind.

"He recognized Altamont instantly upon seeing him in Sydney, didn't he?" Morgan muttered to himself, his eyes widening. "Clavering isn't married to my Lady any more than I am! Altamont is the man. Altamont is a convict. Young Harthur gets into Parliament, and the Governor promises not to cause a scandal. By Jove, what a cunning old rogue that Governor is! No wonder he's pushing the match between Blanche and Harthur so hard. Why, she'll bring a hundred thousand pounds to the marriage—and secure a parliamentary seat for her husband in the bargain."

No one else witnessed the transformation on Mr. Morgan's face as this bombshell landed, but a skilled physiognomist would have noted the shift immediately. "For my sake, and the confounded prejudices of society," he whispered, eyeing his reflection in the mirror, "damn it, James Morgan, you might marry her yourself!" Yet, realizing he couldn't wed Miss Blanche and her fortune, Morgan calculated that possessing this secret could enrich him in ways far more lucrative. Most everyone affected by this revelation would prefer ignorance: Sir Francis Clavering, whose fortune was at stake, would want silence; Colonel Altamont, whose neck was implicated, would want to bury it; and that uppity young upstart, Mr. Harthur—who was eager to enter Parliament on the strength of this arrangement and acted as proud as a duke with half a million a year (though Morgan privately believed his employer's nephew was less than that)—would pay anything to hide that he married a convict's daughter and bought his seat through trafficking secrets.

As for Lady C., Morgan reasoned, whether she was tired of Clavering or terrified for her son's safety, she would pay. And Miss Blanche would certainly reward whoever restored her rightful inheritance. "Damn it," concluded the valet, marveling at the incredible hand of luck dealt to him, "with cards like these, James Morgan, you're a made man. It might be a regular bonanza. Everyone must subscribe. With what I've already amassed, I could retire, warn my old Governor, turn gentleman, hire my own servant, and begad, live like a lord." While entertaining these feverish calculations that threatened to shake his spirit, Mr. Morgan displayed remarkable self-control, maintaining a calm demeanor and ignoring his skyrocketing future prospects to fulfill his current duties.

Colonel Altamont, central to this drama, was currently absent from London. Knowing of Sir Francis Clavering's favorite haunt, Shepherd's Inn, Morgan walked there shortly after the Baronet and Pendennis parted ways. Alas, the bird had flown; Altamont had cashed his Derby winnings and departed for the Continent. This absence was deeply vexing to Morgan. "He'll squander that money in the gambling dens along the Rhine," Morgan fretted. "I might have pocketed a good share. It's confounded annoying he's gone and couldn't wait a few days." Whether hope was triumphant or deferred, ambition realized or disappointed, Morgan bore it all with an equally serene countenance. Until the right moment arrived, the Major's boots remained varnished, his hair perfectly curled, his morning tea delivered to his bedside, and his oaths and senile sarcasm endured with silent, obsequious fidelity. Who would guess, watching him pack trunks and assist at table in the country houses where the Major stayed, that Morgan was richer than his employer, knowing his secrets and those of others? In his profession, Morgan was highly respected, renowned for his supposed wealth and wisdom at supper tables. The younger gentlemen dismissed him as stupid, devoid of ideas, and a fogey; yet, none would refuse to join in the earnest prayers of the serious-minded: "When I die, may I cut up as well as Morgan Pendennis!"

True to the customs of a man of fashion, Major Pendennis spent the autumn moving from house to house among country friends willing to host him. If the Duke was abroad, he visited the Marquis in Scotland; if not, he stayed with Sir John or some plain squire. Truthfully, the old gentleman's reputation was fading. Many of his contemporaries had passed on, and the current holders of their titles knew him not, caring little for his tales of "the Wild Prince and Poins" or the heroes of a bygone fashion era. Walking past many London doors, it must have struck the good man with melancholy how rarely they opened for him now, compared to the banquets and welcomes he once enjoyed decades prior. He began to admit he was no longer of the present age, dimly sensing that the young men mocked him. Such melancholic reflections plague many a Pall Mall philosopher. "The men aren't what they used to be," he thought. "The old grand manner and courtly grace are gone. What is Castlewood House today compared to the magnificence of the old mansion and owner? The late Lord arrived with four post-chaises and sixteen horses; the entire North Road rushed out to gaze upon his cavalcade; even Londoners stopped to watch his procession. Today's Lord travels in a railway carriage with five porters, sneaking away from the station in a brougham while smoking a cigar. The late Lord filled Castlewood with guests drinking claret until midnight; today's man hides in a hut on a Scottish mountain, spending November in a couple of closets in an entresol in Paris, where his entertainment consists of a café dinner and a ticket to a small theater. What a contrast between Lady Lorraine of the Regency—beautiful, gorgeous, magnificent in diamonds and velvet, daring in her rouge, with the wits of the world (the old, polished gentlemen, not the rabble of today with their cab-stand slang and smoke-stained coats) bowing at her feet—and today's Lady Lorraine! A little woman in a black silk gown, looking like a governess, talking of astronomy, laboring classes, and emigration, lurking in church at eight in the morning."

Abbots-Lorraine, once the noblest house in the county, had become a monastery—a real La Trappe. They didn't drink two glasses of wine after dinner; instead, every other diner was a country curate in a white neckcloth discussing Polly Higson's school progress or Widow Watkins's lumbago. "And the other young men, those lounging guardsmen and great lazy dandies—sprawling over sofas and billiard tables, stealing off to smoke pipes in each other's bedrooms, caring for nothing, reverencing nothing, not even an old gentleman who has known their fathers and betters, not even a pretty woman—" The Major pondered the difference between these men, who poisoned turnip fields with their tobacco, and the gentlemen of his time. "The breed is gone," he thought. "There's no use for them; they're replaced by a bunch of damned cotton-spinners and utilitarians, and young parson sprigs with their hair combed down their backs." He felt old; they were surpassing him; they laughed at old boys like him. And he wasn't entirely wrong. The times and manners he cherished were nearly extinct. Gay young men teased him irreverently, while serious youth regarded him with grave pity and wonder—a pain even worse if the Major had realized its full extent. But he was rather simple; his moral examinations hadn't been profound. Perhaps it never occurred to him until recently that he was anything other than a most respectable and fortunate man. Was there no old age without reverence? Did youthful folly never mock other bald heads? For the past few years, he'd sensed his day was ending and the new generation was reigning.

After a somewhat unsuccessful autumn, during which he was faithfully trailed by Mr. Morgan (while his nephew Arthur was busy at Clavering's), Major Pendennis returned to London for a brief stay at the dismal end of October, when the fogs roll in and the lawyers descend upon the city. Who hasn't watched with interest those cab-loaded cabs, piled boxes, and crowded children rattling through the streets on dull October evenings? Stopping at dark houses to unload nurses, infants, girls, matrons, and fathers whose holidays are over? Yesterday was France and sunshine, or Broadstairs and liberty; today brings work and a yellow fog. Oh, gods! What a heap of bills lies in the Master's study! The clerk brings the lawyer's papers from chambers; in half an hour, the literary man knows the printer

Early the next morning, the shutters of Bury Street were raised by none other than Mr. Morgan. He presented himself with his customary air of grave respect, carrying the old gentleman's clothes, cans of water, and an elaborate array of toilet requisites.

"It's you, is it?" grunted the Major from his bed. "I shan't take you back again, you understand."

"I have not the least wish to be taken back again, Major Pendennis," Mr. Morgan replied with profound dignity. "Nor do I wish to serve you or any other man. However, seeing that I desire you to remain comfortable while you stay in my house, I have come to perform what is necessary."

Once more, and for the last time, Mr. James Morgan laid out the silver dressing-case and strapped the shining razor. With an indescribable solemnity, he turned to the Major and announced:

"Thinking that you would most likely require a respectable person to attend to you until you settle your affairs myself, I spoke to a young man last night who is here."

"Indeed," said the warrior in his tent-bed. "Has he lived in the first families? Can you vouch for his respectability?"

"You are monstrously polite," grinned the old Major.

The truth was, following the events of the previous evening, Morgan had retired to his own club, *The Wheel of Fortune*. There, he had encountered Frosch, a courier and valet recently returned from a foreign tour with young Lord Cubley and currently available. Representing to Frosch that he had "had a devil of a blow up with his own Governor" and was retiring from the business altogether, Morgan had suggested that if Frosch sought temporary employment, he might apply at Bury Street.

"You are very polite," said the Major, "and your recommendation, I am sure, will carry every weight."

Morgan blushed slightly; he sensed his former master was chaffing him.

"The man has waited on you before, sir," he stated with great dignity. "Lord De la Pole gave him to his nephew, young Lord Cubley. He accompanied him on his foreign tour. Not wishing to return to Fitzurse Castle—which Frosch finds difficult due to his delicate chest and inability to bear the Scottish cold—he is free to serve you or not, as you choose."

"I repeat, sir, that you are exceedingly polite," said the Major. "Come in, Frosch—you will do very well. Mr. Morgan, will you have the great kindness to—"

"I shall show him what is necessary, sir, and what is customary for you to wish done. Will you please take breakfast here or at the Club, Major Pendennis?"

"With your kind permission, I will breakfast here, and afterwards we will make our little arrangements."

"If you please, sir."

"Will you now oblige me by leaving the room?"

Morgan withdrew. The excessive politeness of his ex-employer made him nearly as angry as the Major's bitterest words. As the old gentleman commenced his mysterious toilet, we shall also modestly retire.

After breakfast, Major Pendennis and his new aide-de-camp prepared for departure. The establishment of the old bachelor was remarkably uncomplicated. He possessed no useless wardrobe. His collection consisted of a Bible (his mother's), a road book, *Pen's* novel bound in calf-skin, the Duke of Wellington's Despatches, and a few prints, maps, and portraits of the illustrious General, various sovereigns, and the Commander-in-Chief under whom the Major had served in India. Always ready to march at a few hours' notice, the cases in which he had brought his property into his lodgings fifteen years prior remained in the loft, ample enough to hold all his goods.

These items were brought down by the young woman who performed the household duties, known as "Betty" to her mistress and "Slavey" to Mr. Morgan. Under the watchful, terrifying gaze of Morgan, she diligently dusted and cleaned everything. His demeanor was guarded and solemn; he had spoken no word to Mrs. Brixham regarding his threats of the previous night, yet he looked as though he intended to execute them, causing the poor widow to tremble in anticipation of her fate.

Old Pendennis, armed with his cane, supervised the packing of his goods under Frosch's hands, while Slavey burned the papers the Major chose not to keep. Doors and closets were flung open until entirely empty. Now, all boxes and chests were sealed, save for his desk, which stood ready to receive the final accounts of Mr. Morgan.

That individual soon made his appearance, bringing his books.

"As I wish to speak to you in private, perhaps you will have the kindness to request Frosch to step downstairs," he said upon entering.

"Bring a couple of cabs, Frosch, if you please—and wait downstairs until I ring for you," commanded the Major.

Morgan sent Frosch away, watched him depart down the street, and then produced his books and accounts, which were simple and easily settled.

"And now, sir," he said, pocketing the cheque handed to him by his ex-employer and signing his ledger with a flourish, "now that accounts are closed between us, sir, I propose to speak to you as one man to another." (Morgan loved the sound of his own voice; whenever possible, he indulged in public speaking at the Club or in the housekeeper's room.) "I must inform you that I am in possession of certain information."

"And may I inquire of what nature, pray?" asked the Major.

"It is valuable information, Major Pendennis, as you know very well. I know of a marriage that is no marriage—of an honorable Baronet who is no more married than I am, whose wife is married to someone else, as you also know, sir."

Pendennis immediately grasped the situation.

"Hah! This explains your behavior. You have been listening at the door, I suppose," said the Major, looking haughty. "I forgot to check the keyhole when I went to that public house, or I might have suspected what sort of person was hiding behind it."

"I may have my schemes as you may have yours, I suppose," answered Morgan. "I may gather my information, act upon it, and find it valuable as anyone else. A poor servant may have a bit of luck as well as a gentleman, mayn't he? Do not put on your arrogant looks and pretend to be an aristocrat over me. That is all gammon. I am an Englishman, and as good as you."

"To what the devil does this tend, sir? And how does the secret you have discovered concern me? I should like to know," demanded Major Pendennis with majestic indignation.

"How does it concern me, indeed! How grand we are! How does it concern my nephew? How does it concern my nephew's seat in Parliament? And what of subornation of bigamy? How does that concern me? What, are you to be the only man to possess a secret and trade upon it? Why shouldn't I go halves, Major Pendennis? I've found it out too. Look here! I'm not going to be unreasonable. Make it worth my while, and I'll keep the matter close. Let Mr. Arthur take his seat and his wealthy wife, if you prefer; I have no intention of marrying her. But I will have my share, as sure as my name is James Morgan. And if I don't—"

"And if you don't, sir—what?" Pendennis asked.

"If I don't, I split and tell all. I'll smash Clavering and have him and his wife prosecuted for bigamy—so help me, I will! I'll expose young Hopeful's marriage and reveal that you and he are using this secret merely to squeeze a parliamentary seat out of Sir Francis and a fortune out of his wife."

"Mr. Pendennis knows no more of this business than the babe unborn, sir!" cried the Major, aghast. "Not more than Lady Clavering, nor Miss Amory."

"Tell that to the marines, Major," retorted the valet. "That cock won't fight with me."

"Do you doubt my word, you villain?"

"No bad language. I don't care one penny whether your word is true or not. I tell you, I intend this to be a nice little annuity for me, Major. For I have everyone of you. I'm not such a fool as to let you escape. I'd say you might make it five hundred a year among you, easy. Pay me down the first quarter now, and I'll be as mute as a mouse. Just give a note for one thousand two hundred and fifty. Here's your cheque-book on your desk."

"And there's this too, you villain!" shouted the old gentleman.

Pointing to the desk, Morgan indicated a small double-barreled pistol that had belonged to Pendennis's old patron, the Indian Commander-in-Chief, and which had accompanied him through countless campaigns.

"One more word, you scoundrel, and I'll shoot you like a mad dog. Stop—by Jove, I'll do it now. You'll assault me, will you? You'll strike at an old man, will you, you lying coward? Kneel down and say your prayers, sir, for by the Lord you shall die."

The Major's face glowed with rage at his adversary, who stared back in terror for a moment before letting out a shriek of "Mur

Arthur Pendennis had just touched down in London, though his stay was intended to be fleeting. After dropping off his fellow travelers at a hotel near the West End, he hurried toward the Chambers in Lamb Court. Despite the building's reputation for being dreary, the specific rooms he sought were currently bathed in whatever patch of sunlight dared to penetrate the gloom. In legal chambers, freedom acts as a substitute for sunshine; the barristers may grumble about the damp, but they eventually settle in to enjoy their ease.

Pen's domestic announced that Warrington was also present. Naturally, Arthur bolted upstairs to his friend's room. As expected, the air was thick with the scent of tobacco, and George Warrington was already deep in his newspapers and reviews. The two friends exchanged greetings with the characteristic rough cordiality of young Englishmen—a style that hides immense warmth beneath a crust of rudeness.

"Well, young one!" Warrington grinned, pulling his pipe from his mouth.
"How are you, old boy?" Pen replied, extending a hand.

It was a standard exchange between two Britons who hadn't seen each other in months. Contrast this with the French, who would rush into each other's arms screaming *Ce bon cœur!* or the Germans, who would shower one another with Havana-scented kisses on their mustaches. The British method is distinct: "Well, young one" and "How are you, old boy" serve as substitutes for declarations of undying loyalty. Tomorrow, they will merely nod at breakfast; yet, each holds the other's fate in their hands, ready to share their purse or defend their honor with the loudest enthusiasm. Then comes the abrupt "Good-bye," followed by a polite "How-d'you-do" upon meeting again, with no letters sent in the interim. It is a curious, stoic form of friendship, as noted by Hardman, who claims to feel no affection whatsoever.

"Yes, we aren't demonstrative like those confounded foreigners," Hardman might say.

"Been in Switzerland?" Pen asked.
"Yes," Warrington confirmed. "Couldn't find tobacco fit to smoke until we reached Strasbourg, where I finally procured some caporal."

Warrington's mind was undoubtedly filled with grand sights and profound emotions stirred by nature's vast works, yet his enthusiasm remained coy, veiled behind a cloud of smoke. He would save his true feelings for confidential evenings and express them later in writing, where his travels enriched his prose with brilliant allusions and witty illustrations. Speaking rapidly, he outlined his tour: Switzerland, Northern Italy, the Tyrol, returning via Vienna, Dresden, and along the Rhine. Yet, he delivered this list in a sulky, reluctant tone, as if mentioning the places made him miserable.

Then, the young man began to speak of his own journey. He had been in the country, finding it unbearably boring, canvassing slowly, and was now heading to Tunbridge Wells to visit equally slow-moving acquaintances. "How hard it is to make an Englishman admit he is happy!"

"And the seat in Parliament, Pen? Have you sorted it out?" Warrington inquired.
"All set," Pen assured him. "As soon as Parliament reconvenes and a new writ is issued, Clavering retires, and I slide into his shoes."

"Do we stand as Liberal Conservatives, Government men, or independents?"
"Hem! Politics are quiet now; everyone's views are roughly aligned anyway. I haven't got enough acres to be a Protectionist, nor could I be one even if I owned the county. I'll mostly follow the Government, though I plan to push ahead on some social issues I've been researching during the vacation—don't smirk, old cynic. I've been compiling the Blue Books and intend to be forceful on Sanitation and Colonization."

"We reserve the right to vote against the Government, though we're usually friendly. We are friends of the people first and foremost. We lecture at the Clavering Institute and shake hands with intelligent mechanics. We believe the franchise needs significant expansion. Still, we're open to accepting office once the House hears our crack speeches and the Administration recognizes our merit."

"I am not Moses," Pen said, his voice tinged with melancholy. "I have no divine laws to descend from the mountain. I don't belong to the mountaintop, nor do I aspire to lead or reform humanity. My faith isn't strong enough for that, nor my vanity or hypocrisy. I won't lie to you, George; I promise. I'll only coincide with necessary falsehoods that keep the currency circulating. Give a man the benefit of his skepticism. If I find a good point in the House, I'll make it; if a good measure arises, I'll support it; if a decent position opens up, I'll take it and be grateful. But I wouldn't flatter a great man more than a mob. Now you know my politics as well as I do. Why bother being a Whig? Whiggism isn't a holy institution. Why not join the Liberal Conservatives? They've achieved far more for the nation than the Whigs ever could without them. Who converted both? The Radicals and the public. I think the *Morning Post* is often right and *Punch* often wrong. I don't claim a calling; I just seize opportunities. Let's talk about something else."

"The next thing after ambition is love, I suppose?" Warrington asked gently. "How are our young loves progressing? Are we trading chambers for a hearth? Are you divorcing me, Arthur, to marry a wife?"

"I suppose so," Pen admitted. "She's very good-natured and lively. She sings, and she doesn't mind smoking. She'll have a fair fortune—though my uncle predicts everything based on the Begum's generosity, expecting a handsome dowry. And I think Blanche is terribly fond of me," he sighed.

"That implies we accept her caresses and her money," Warrington quipped.

"Haven't we decided life is a transaction?" Pen countered. "I don't pretend to break my heart over her. I've told her plainly what I feel, and we're engaged. Since I last saw her, especially over the past two months in the country, I'm convinced she's grown deeper in love. Her letters, particularly to Laura, prove it. Mine have been straightforward—no rapturous vows, you understand—treating it as a settled affair, neither rushing nor delaying the conclusion."

"And Laura? How is she?" Warrington asked directly.

"Laura, George," Pen said, staring intently at his friend—"by heavens, Laura is the finest, noblest, and dearest girl the sun has ever illuminated."

His voice faltered; the words seemed difficult to extract. He reached out a hand to his comrade, who grasped it and nodded solemnly.

"Did you only discover that now, young un?" Warrington asked after a pause.

"Who hasn't learned things too late, George?" Arthur cried impulsively, gathering emotion as he spoke. "Whose life isn't a disappointment? Who carries an intact heart to the grave without some mutilation? I've never known anyone truly happy, or someone who didn't pay a terrible price to ransom themselves from Fate's grasp. Lucky if we're left alone afterward, having paid our fine, if the tyrant stops visiting. Suppose I've realized I've lost the greatest prize in the world, now that it's impossible to have? For years, I had an angel under my tent, and I let her go? Am I the only one—oh, dear old boy, am I the only one? Do you think my lot is easier to bear because I admit I deserved it? She's gone from us. God bless her! She might have stayed, and I lost her. Isn't it like Undine, George?"

"She was in this room once," George whispered. He recalled seeing her there, hearing her soft voice, witnessing her kind smile and shining eyes—the face remembered through sleepless nights, blessed and beloved forever, now departed. Only a glass that had held a nosegay and a Bible with Helen's handwriting remained as tokens of that brief flower of his life. "Say it's a dream," George murmured. "Better the memory of a dream than waking from a blank stupor."

The two sat in silence, lost in their respective thoughts, acutely aware of the other's presence. Pen broke the quiet, noting he needed to find his uncle and report on his progress. The Major had written in a foul mood; the old man was aging.

"I'd like to see you in Parliament, comfortably settled with a house and an heir, before I make my final bow," the Major had written. "Show me these documents, and then let old Arthur Pendennis make room for the younger generation; he's walked the Pall Mall pavement long enough."

"There's a kindness in that old heathen," Warrington observed. "He cares for someone besides himself—for you and your line. He wants the Pendennis progeny to multiply and inherit the land. The old patriarch blesses you from the club window at Bays's, only to be carried off and buried under the flags of St. James's Church, visible from Piccadilly and the carriage stands heading to the levee. An edifying end."

"The new blood I'm bringing into the family," Pen mused, "is rather tainted. Had I chosen differently, I doubt my father-in-law Amory would have been the ancestor I desired, nor my grandfather-in-law Snell, nor our Oriental foreb

The dinner was finally served just as Arthur Pendennis stumbled back into the room. Immediately, Lady Rockminster launched into a scolding regarding his tardiness. However, Laura, observing her cousin's ghastly pallor and terrified expression, cut through her aunt's imperious tirade. With a voice trembling with concern, she asked what had transpired. Was Arthur ill?

To mask his agitation, Arthur downed a massive glass of sherry. "I've heard the most extraordinary news," he stammered, glancing nervously at the servants. "I'll explain later." Throughout the meal, he remained visibly shaken, his foot tapping rhythmically against the floorboards.

"Don't trample and beat the ground beneath the table!" Lady Rockminster snapped. "You've crushed Fido's saucer. Look at Mr. Warrington; he keeps his boots perfectly silent."

By dessert, the atmosphere was suffocating. "This dinner has been dreadfully dull," Lady Rockminster declared, sensing the storm brewing behind Arthur's eyes. "Clearly, something has occurred, and you wish to speak with Laura privately. I'm off for my nap. I doubt I'll have tea. Good night, Mr. Warrington. Do come again, but preferably when there isn't such heavy business to discuss." With a toss of her head and immense dignity, the old lady departed, followed by George and the others.

Warrington rose to follow, bidding Laura a farewell while she stared at Arthur with mounting alarm. Then, Arthur stopped him. "Please, stay, George. You must hear this news and offer your counsel. I am at a loss as to how to proceed."

"It concerns Blanche, Arthur," Laura whispered, her heart hammering against her ribs, her cheeks flushing a color she hadn't known since childhood.

"Yes," Pen confirmed, his voice tight. "And it involves the most extraordinary story imaginable. When I left you to visit my uncle's lodgings, I found his ancient servant, Morgan, standing guard at the door. He informed me that he and his master had parted ways that very morning; my uncle had fled to this very hotel. When I arrived, he wasn't dining, so Morgan invited me inside, claiming he possessed information of vital importance. Apparently, the scoundrel has amassed a fortune while serving my uncle—he's now a capitalist, a millionaire, for all I know.

"I entered the house, and what do you think he revealed? This must remain a secret among us, especially now that this villain holds the key. Blanche's father is not dead. He has returned from the grave. The marriage between Clavering and the Begum was never valid."

"And Blanche?" Warrington asked, his tone sharp. "Is she therefore the heir?"

"Perhaps," Pen replied grimly. "But consider the lineage: the child of a convict! Amory is an escaped prisoner. Clavering knows it; my uncle knows it. That fact was used as a weapon *in terrorem* to force the wretched old man to surrender his parliamentary seat to me."

"Blanche doesn't know," Laura interjected softly. "Nor does poor Lady Clavering?"

"No," Pen said. "Blanche believes her father died in New South Wales as a ship's captain, thanks to the lies spun by her nurse, Bonner. In reality, he was transported as a convict. Lady Clavering has confessed to me that their marriage was unhappy and that her husband was a man of bad character. She wept, telling me it was a terrible burden for a woman to admit she was glad to hear of her husband's death, noting that twice in her life she had made a fatal error in judgment.

"Now, what is to be done? If this man resurfaces, he cannot simply demand his wife; death likely awaits him if he reveals himself. Returning to transportation is certain. Yet, this rascal has held the threat of exposure over Clavering for years, extorting money repeatedly."

"It's our friend Colonel Altamont, surely," Warrington deduced, the pieces clicking together. "I see everything now."

"If this rascal returns," Arthur continued, "Morgan, who knows the secret, will leverage it against us all. Possessing this leverage, he intends to blackmail everyone. The damned fool assumed I was already aware of this plot," Pen added, his face draining of color with rage. "He asked me for an annuity to keep quiet, threatening me as if I were profiting from the Begum's misery, and even hinted at extracting a parliamentary seat from that miserable Clavering. Good heavens! Was my uncle truly mad to entangle himself in such a conspiracy? To imagine our mother's son trading on such treachery!"

"I cannot fathom it, dear Arthur," Laura said, seizing his hand and pressing a kiss to it.
