"No!" Warrington roared, his deep voice trembling. He looked at the two young lovers with a mixture of profound love and agony. "No. Our boy cannot meddle in such filth. Arthur Pendennis cannot marry the daughter of a convict and serve as a Member of Parliament for the hulks. You must wash your hands of this entire affair, Pen. Break it off immediately. Give no explanation; simply state that family reasons make the union impossible. It is kinder for those poor women to suspect you false to your word than to learn the brutal truth. Furthermore, I can easily secure from that dog Clavering an acknowledgment that your stated reasons are sufficient grounds for ending the engagement. Do you agree with me, Laura?"

He barely dared meet her gaze. He knew he was discarding the last fragile plank of his shattered fortune, allowing the waves of his calamity to sweep him under.

Pen sprang up, staring intently at Warrington. Warrington turned his head away. He saw Laura rise, walk to Pen, and once again take his hand, kissing it gently.

"She agrees too—God bless her!" George exclaimed.

"Her father's shame is not Blanche's fault, dear Arthur," Laura pleaded, her face pale, her words rapid. "Suppose you were married; would you abandon her because she bears no guilt? Are you not bound to her? Would you leave her in her distress? And if she suffers, would you not comfort her? Our mother would have done exactly that, had she been here."

As she spoke, the kind girl wrapped her arms around him, burying her face in his chest.

"Our mother is an angel to God," Pen sobbed. "And you are the dearest, the kindest woman in the world. Teach me my duty. Pray for me that I may fulfill it with a pure heart. God bless you—God bless you, my sister!"

"Amen," Warrington groaned, burying his face in his hands. "She is right," he murmured to himself. "That girl cannot do anything wrong." Indeed, she looked and moved like an angel. Days later, he would recall that radiant smile, the way she tucked back her curls, blushing and smiling, her fond gaze forever fixed on Pen.

"And now," Laura said, placing her small, fair hand on the table, "what next?"

"And now?" George asked.

"And now," Miss Laura smiled, "we shall have some tea."

Before this mundane conclusion could settle, a servant announced that Major Pendennis had returned to the hotel and was awaiting his nephew. Laura, feeling a fresh surge of alarm, cast an imploring look at Pen: *Behave yourself; hold fast to the right; be gentle yet firm.* Taking these unwritten warnings to heart, she excused herself and retired to her chambers.

Warrington, usually indifferent to tea, begrudged the impending cup immensely. Why couldn't the old Major have arrived an hour later? An hour sooner or later mattered little. The hour struck. The inevitable moment arrived to bid farewell. Hands shook, doors closed, and friends departed. As the brief joy faded, leaving him alone, Warrington wandered the streets, wondering, *"In which window of that hotel does her light shine?"* He strode to a neighboring club's smoking room, seeking solace in a cigar. Around him, men brawled loudly about politics, opera singers, horse racing, and the tyranny of committees. Carrying this sacred secret, he joined the fray. They shouted, cracked jokes, laughed at wild tales. It was strange to stand amidst the smoke and noise, realizing that every man present likely harbored a lonely, private secret, isolated in their own minds while participating in the collective chaos.

Meanwhile, Arthur traversed the hotel corridors, his anger boiling within him. He was furious that the elderly gentleman he was about to meet had reduced him to a mere tool, compromising his honor and reputation.

The Major's hand was ice-cold and trembling when Arthur grasped it. He coughed, grumbled over the fire, and complained that Frosch couldn't manage his dressing gown or organize his papers due to the impudence of Morgan. The old man lamented his situation, cursing Morgan's ingratitude with pathetic bitterness.

"The confounded impudent scoundrel! He was drunk last night and challenged me to a duel! I was so worked up I nearly drove a knife into him! That infernal rascal has made ten thousand pounds, I believe, and deserves hanging—but curse him, I wish he'd outlast me! He knew all my habits. Damn it, when I rang the bell, that thief brought me what I needed, unlike that stupid German brute. How have you managed in the country? Have you spent

When Arthur Pendennis, dressed in his dressing gown, ascended the stairs to Warrington's chambers the following morning, his customary mission was twofold: to report the outcome of his nocturnal audience with his uncle and to solicit George's counsel. However, upon entering the beloved rooms, he discovered only Mrs. Flanagan, the laundress. George was absent. A carpet bag stood ready, and his destination was his brother's estate in Suffolk. Scattered across the table were packages addressed to the newspaper and review for which he labored, waiting for collection.

"I caught him right at the table when I arrived!" Mrs. Flanagan exclaimed, her voice brimming with concern. "He was deep in his papers, one candle already burnt out. Even though his bed is notoriously uncomfortable, he hadn't touched it all night, sir."

Indeed, having endured the Club until the rowdiness became unbearable, George had returned home. He had spent the entire night wrestling with a piece of work he felt compelled to finish, bending his energies to the task with singular intensity. The labor was done; the night had been consumed; and the tardy dawn of November peered into the room to find the young man slumped over his desk.

In the next day's paper, or perhaps in a quarterly review, many readers would admire the brilliance of his genius, the variety of his illustrations, and the fierce vigor of his satire. Yet, hidden within those words was a different current: a tone more melancholy than usual, a bitterness and impatience sharper than anything he would later display. Only a handful who truly knew his style or his name would detect the shadow haunting this specific period of his life. One wonders, could we fully grasp the man's inner turmoil as easily as the author's thoughts? How vastly more interesting—and far less cheerful—books would be if they revealed the private struggles behind the public persona! Consider the harlequin: beneath the painted mask, the face is often grave, if not sorrowful. Every writer who reads this must recall their own solitary hours, the constant cares that sat beside the desk like silent guardians. Perhaps a fever raged in the next room; perhaps a sick child lay weeping while a wife prayed in terror; perhaps grief weighed him down so heavily that the cruel mist obscured the page, yet the inexorable necessity of the deadline drove the pen forward. Who among us has not known such nights? But to the manly heart, however severe the pang, the wound heals, the fever breaks, and the dawn arrives. One eventually learns to look back on that misery without bitterness.

On the table lay the relics of the struggle: two or three reference books, fragments of torn manuscripts, open drawers, pens, an inkstand, faint lines on blotting paper, and a piece of sealing wax twisted and bitten into shards. Arthur threw himself into George's empty chair, noting things as per his habit, or perhaps against his will. He noticed a gap in the bookcase, nestled beside the old *College Plato* and the volume bearing the Boniface Arms. This was where Helen's Bible usually resided.

"He has taken that with him," Arthur mused. He understood why his friend had departed.

"Dear, dear old George!" Arthur rubbed his hand over his eyes. "Oh, how much wiser, how much better, how much nobler he is than I!" he thought. "Where is such a friend? Where is such a brave heart? Where shall I ever hear such a frank voice, such kind laughter? Where shall I ever see such a true gentleman? No wonder she loved him. God bless him! Compared to him, what am I? What choice did she have but to love him? To the end of our days, we shall remain her brothers, for fate decrees we can be nothing more. We'll be her knights, serving her faithfully. And when we are old, we shall recount how we loved her. Dear, dear old George!"

Descending to his own chambers, Arthur glanced at the letterbox of his outer door, which he had previously missed. Inside was a small note addressed to *A. P., Esq.*, penned in George's distinctive hand. George had slipped it in as he left.

*"Dear Pen,—I shall be halfway home by the time you breakfast, and intend to stay over Christmas, either in Norfolk or elsewhere.*

*I have formed my own opinion regarding the matter we discussed in J——— St. yesterday; and believe my presence is quite superfluous (_de trop_).*

Thus, George was gone, leaving Mrs. Flanagan to rule over his vacant quarters. Naturally, Arthur had to visit his uncle the day after their conversation. Being denied entry, he proceeded to Lady Rockminster's apartments. Upon his arrival, the old lady immediately demanded to see "Bluebeard"—Arthur's nickname—and insisted he join them for dinner.

"Bluebeard is gone," Arthur replied, producing George's scrap of paper and handing it to Laura. She examined it briefly, refused to meet Arthur's gaze, and passed the paper back to him before walking away.

Arthur then launched into an eloquent eulogy of his dear old George for Lady Rockminster, who was astounded by his fervor. She had rarely heard him speak so warmly of anyone and frankly remarked that it was not in his nature to care so deeply for another soul.

Later, strolling through Waterloo Place on his daily walk toward the hotel where Laura resided, Arthur spotted an old schoolfriend emerging from Messrs. Gimcrack's celebrated shop. An obsequious shopman followed, laden with parcels. The gentleman was clad in the deepest mourning; the brougham, driver, and horses matched his somber attire. Grief, cushioned by wealth and comfort, was perfectly embodied in the equipage and its owner.

"What, Foker! Hail, Foker!" cried Arthur. The reader, surely, recognizes Arthur's old schoolmate, the heir to the late lamented John Henry Foker, Esq., master of Logwood and other estates, and principal partner in the great brewery of Foker and Co.—the greater portion of Foker's entire holdings. A small hand, gloved in deepest ebony and framed by a snowy wristband, extended to greet Arthur, clutching a small morocco case undoubtedly filled with treasures purchased moments prior.

Arthur's keen, satirical eyes instantly discerned the errand. He recalled the heir in Horace pouring forth the gathered wine of his father's vats, realizing that human nature remains remarkably consistent whether in Regent Street or the Via Sacra.

"*Le Roi est mort. Vive le Roi!*" Arthur declared.

"Oh!" came the response. "Yes. Thank you—very much obliged. How do you do, Pen? Very busy—good-bye!"

Foker jumped into the black brougham and sat like a little black Care behind the black coachman. He had flushed upon seeing Arthur, displaying signs of guilt and perturbation that Arthur attributed to the novelty of his inheritance, speculating sardonically on the scene.

"Yes: so wags the world," Arthur thought. "The stone closes over Harry the Fourth, and Harry the Fifth reigns in his stead. The old ministers at the brewery kneel before him with their accounts; the draymen, his subjects, throw up their red caps and shout for him. What profound deference and sympathy the bankers and lawyers display! There was too great a stake involved for them to ever love each other cordially. As long as one man excludes another from twenty thousand a year, the younger must forever hunger for the crown, and desire becomes the father of possession. Thank Heaven, there was no such thought of money between me and our dear mother, Laura."

"There never could have been," Laura interjected. "You would have spurned it! Why make yourself more selfish than you are, Pen? Allow your mind to entertain such... such dreadful meanness? You make me blush for you, Arthur; you make me—" Her eyes completed the sentence, and she dabbed them with a handkerchief.

"There are some truths women will never acknowledge," Arthur said gently, "from which your modesty always turns away. I do not claim to have felt the emotion myself, merely that I am glad I lacked the temptation. Is there harm in that confession?"

"We are all taught to pray for deliverance from evil, Arthur," Laura whispered. "I am glad you were spared that great crime; I am only sorry to think you might have been led astray. But you never could; nor do you believe you could. Your acts are generous and kind; you disdain base actions. You took Blanche without money, without a bribe. Yes, thank Heaven, dear brother. You could not have sold yourself away; I knew you couldn't when the day came, and you didn't. Praise be—where praise is due. Why does this horrid skepticism pursue you, my Arthur? Why doubt and sneer at your own heart, and everyone else's? Oh, if you knew the pain you cause me! How I lie awake thinking of those harsh sentences, wishing them unspoken, unthought!"

"Do I cause you many thoughts and many tears, Laura?" Arthur asked.

The fullness of innocent love shone in her reply. A smile of heavenly purity, a glance of unutterable tenderness, sympathy, and pity illuminated her face. Arthur beheld these signs of love and virtue, worshipping them as one watches a child or imagines an angel.

"I—I don't know what I have done," he

"Dearest Blanche," Arthur began his letter, "you are perpetually engaged in reading and dreaming up pretty dramas and exciting romances in real life. Are you now prepared to star in one? Not the delightful role where the heroine inherits her father's palace and wealth, welcoming her husband amidst loyal retainers to declare, 'All of this is ours,' but rather the tragic part of the unfortunate lady who suddenly realizes she is not the Princess's wife, but Claude Melnotte's—the beggar. Think of Alnaschar's wife arriving just as her husband has kicked over the tray of porcelain destined to make his fortune. Wait—Alnaschar wasn't actually married; he'd merely caught the eye of the Vizier's daughter, and his hopes crashed along with the shattered teacups.

Shall you be the Vizier's daughter, refusing and mocking Alnaschar? Or shall you be the Lady of Lyons, falling for penniless Claude Melnotte? I am willing to play whichever part you prefer. I will love you with my utmost devotion. I will strive to make your humble life happy—for humble it must be. The odds are heavily stacked against anything else; we shall simply live and die in a poor, prosaic, humdrum existence. No stars, no epaulettes for the hero of our tale. I shall write a couple more stories that will quickly fade into obscurity. I will call to the Bar and try to establish myself professionally; perhaps, if I am exceptionally lucky and work incredibly hard—which is absurd—I might secure a colonial appointment, and you could become the lady of an Indian judge. In the meantime, I intend to buy back *The Pall Mall Gazette*. The publishers are weary of it following the death of poor Shandon and will sell it cheaply. Warrington will serve as my right hand, editing it to a respectable standard. I will introduce you to Mr. Finucane, the sub-editor, and I know who will eventually become Mrs. Finucane—a very nice, gentle creature who has endured a sad life sweetly. We shall jog along, hoping for better times and earning our living decently. You shall oversee the opera boxes and manage the fashionable gossip, breaking your little heart in the poet's corner. Shall we live over the offices? There are four excellent rooms, a kitchen, and a garret for Laura in Catherine Street, Strand; or would you prefer a house in Waterloo Road? It would be charming, except for that halfpenny toll at the bridge. Can the boys attend King's College? Does all this sound like a joke to you?"

"Oh, dear Blanche, it is no joke, and I am sober while telling the truth. Our grand daydreams have vanished. Our carriage has whirled out of sight like Cinderella's; our Belgravia mansion has been whisked away by a malevolent genie, and I am no longer a Member of Parliament—not even remotely close to being a Bishop on the Lords' bench or a Duke with a garter. You know quite well what my assets are, and your modest fortune. With those two combined, we might achieve decent comfort: occasional cabs for outings, and certainly no shame in taking an omnibus when tired. But that is all. Is that sufficient for you, my little dainty lady? I often wonder if you can endure the life I offer. At least, it is fair that you know exactly what awaits. If you say, 'Yes, Arthur, I will follow your fate regardless, and be a loyal, loving wife to support and cheer you,' then come to me, dear Blanche, and may God grant me the strength to fulfill my duties. However, if you aspire to a higher station, I must not bar Blanche's fortune. I will stand in the crowd and watch your ladyship depart to Court upon your presentation, and you may offer me a smile from your chariot window. I recall seeing Lady Mirabel last season; her happy husband glittered with stars and cords, and all the garden's flowers bloomed in the coachman's bosom. Will you have the carriages and the glory, or shall we walk and mend my stockings?"

"I cannot reveal now—though I might later, when secrets are no longer necessary—what transpired in the last few hours to alter my life's prospects entirely. Yet, the fact remains: I have discovered something forcing me to abandon my plans and crush many vain ambitions. I have written to Sir Francis Clavering stating I cannot accept his parliamentary seat until after my marriage. Similarly, I cannot and will not accept any greater fortune from you than that which has remained yours since your grandfather's death and the birth of your half-brother. Your good mother is unaware, and I pray she never learns, the reasons behind this peculiar decision. They stem from a painful circumstance attributable to neither of our faults, yet once occurred, it is as fatal and irrepairable as the shock that overturned honest Alnaschar's porcelain, shattering all his hopes beyond repair. I write cheerfully, for there is no point in lamenting such a hopeless misfortune. We have not drawn the grand prize in the lottery, dear Blanche; but I shall be content without it, provided you are too. Once again, with all my heart, I pledge to do my best to make you happy."

"Now, what news? My uncle is gravely ill and takes my refusal of the seat in terrible dudgeon; the scheme was his, and he mourns its failure bitterly. But Warrington, Laura, and I held a council of war. They know this dreadful secret and fully back my decision. You must love George as you love what is generous, upright, and noble. As for Laura—she must be our sister, Blanche, our saint, our guardian angel. With two such friends at home, who cares for the world outside? Who matters if one is elected for Clavering or invited to the season's great balls?"

To this frank confession arrived Blanche's reply to Laura and one to Pen himself, a response perhaps justified by his own letter. "You are spoiled by the world," Blanche wrote. "You do not love your poor Blanche as she deserves, nor would you offer so lightly to take her or leave her, no, Arthur, you do not love me—a man of the world, you have pledged your troth and are ready to redeem it; but where is that entire affection, that love whole and abiding, the vision of my youth? I am but a pastime of your life, whereas I would be its all—a fleeting thought versus your whole soul. I would merge our two hearts into one; but alas, my Arthur, how lonely yours is! How little you give me of it! You speak of our parting with a smile on your lips; of our reunion, and you seem indifferent to hastening it! Is life merely a disillusionment, then? Have the flowers of our garden already faded? I have wept, prayed, spent sleepless hours, shedding bitter tears over your letter! To you I bring the gushing poetry of my being—the yearnings of a soul longing to be loved—that pines endlessly for love, love, love! That throws itself at your feet crying, 'Love me, Arthur!' Your heart beats no faster at my kneeling appeal; your proud eye dims with no tear of sympathy! You accept my soul's treasure as though it were dross—not pearls from the unfathomable depths of affection, not diamonds from the heart's caverns. You treat me like a slave, bidding me bow to my master! Is this the reward of a free maiden? Is this the price of a lifetime's passion? Ah me! When was it otherwise? When did love meet with anything but disappointment? Could I hope (fond fool!) to be the exception to my race's lot, laying my fevered brow on a heart that comprehends my own? Foolish girl that I was! One by one, all the flowers of my young life have faded; and this, the last, the sweetest, the dearest, the fondly and madly loved, the wildly cherished—where is it? But no more of this. Heed not my bleeding heart. Bless you, bless you always, Arthur!"

"The words of this letter were perfectly clear, penned in Blanche's neatest hand on her scented paper; yet the meaning puzzled Pen considerably. Did Blanche intend to accept or refuse his polite offer? Her phrasing suggested either that Pen did not love her and she declined, or that she accepted him despite his coldness, sacrificing herself to him. He laughed sardonically over the letter and the transaction that prompted it. He chuckled at how Fortune had jilted him and how he deserved his precarious position. He turned the musky, gilt-edged riddle over and over. It amused his humor; he enjoyed it as if it were a funny story.

Seated thus, twirling the strange manuscript and joking grimly to himself, his servant entered with a card from a gentleman wishing to speak particularly. Had Pen stepped into the passage, he would have seen his old acquaintance, Mr. Samuel Huxter, sucking his stick, rolling his eyes, and displaying great signs of anxiety.

"'Mr. Huxter on particular business! Pray, ask Mr. Huxter to come in,'" said Pen, amused. His amusement grew when poor Sam appeared.

"'Pray take a chair, Mr. Huxter,'" said Pen in his most superior tone. "'In what way can I assist you?'"

"'I'd rather not speak before the flunky—before the man, Mr. Pendennis,'" Huxter replied, prompting Arthur's attendant to withdraw.

"'

The train hurtled Arthur toward Tunbridge with a speed that felt almost cruel, leaving him little time to catch his breath but ample opportunity to dissect the wreckage of his own life. As the landscape blurred past, he reviewed every circumstance with a grim clarity, acknowledging the tragic destination to which his selfishness and capriciousness had steered him.

"Here lies the end," he murmured to himself, "of all my hopes, my aspirations, my romances, and my ambitions!" He weighed his choices with heavy regret. Whether yielding or stubbornly resisting, he found himself equally unfortunate. When he yielded to his mother's pleas, he refused an angel; when he obstinately rejected her, Laura—who had been thrust upon him anyway—would never have become an angel in his eyes. He realized he could never have given his heart at someone else's prompting, nor could he have truly known Laura's essence if he hadn't been allowed to discover her virtues directly. Conversely, imagine if he had accepted his uncle's advice, taken Blanche on that guarantee, secured a seat in Parliament, wealth, and a grand career. In that scenario, fortune would have arrived, only to leave him with a wife devoid of a dowry—a dowry he had foolishly traded for a heart.

"Why wasn't I more honest?" he asked, stung by the irony. "Or am I simply less so?" His uncle, a poor old man, would have suffered no pang accepting Blanche's fortune from wherever it came. Yet, the uncle was bitter, indignant, and nearly heartbroken by Arthur's scruples. Arthur was the one causing the dissatisfaction. He felt like a maimed, weak, imperfect wretch, utterly incapable of sustaining any fortune. He brought misery to everyone around him. What future awaited that poor, frivolous girl who would inherit his obscurity and share his diminished prospects? He lacked the ambition to stir himself or the self-esteem to console her—or himself—for his failures. Even if he wrote a bestseller running through twenty editions, he'd be the first to sneer at his own reputation. If he succeeded at the bar, achieving riches by bullying witnesses and twisting evidence, would that satisfy his longings? Was that a life worth living?

He cast his gaze at the passengers. He envied the priest opposite, who rarely lifted his eyes from his breviary until the train plunged into the Reigate Tunnel, forcing him to look up. Or perhaps the old gentleman next to him, who glowered with hatred over his newspaper. The priest shut his eyes to the world to focus on the book guiding him to eternity; his neighbor hated him as a monster, a tyrant, a persecutor, fantasizing about burning martyrs and pale, flame-lit faces. Both moved forward with absolute certainty, burdened by their own rigid logics.

"Would you care to see the paper, sir?" interrupted a stout gentleman holding a journal with a fiery article attacking the black-coated priest.

Pen thanked him, took the paper, and resumed his reverie without reading a single line.

"And yet," he pondered, staring blankly ahead, "would you adopt either of those men's creeds, with all their consequences?" He sighed deeply. "Ah, me! Every mortal must bear their own burden, forge their own faith, think their own thoughts, and pray their own prayers. To whom could I possibly explain it all, if I had the mind? Who could comprehend it? Who can judge another's shortcomings, missed opportunities, or the passions that overwhelm reason? Who can measure the extent of truth and justice a neighbor's mind is capable of perceiving? What invisible accidents, terrors of youth, or strokes of bad luck might have diverted the entire course of a life? A single grain of sand can alter a river's path; a thrown pebble can stop it entirely. Who weighs the circumstances, passions, and temptations that determine our good and evil accounts save One, before whose awesome wisdom we kneel, and at whose mercy we seek absolution?"

"This is where it ends," Pen decided. "Today or tomorrow will settle the account of my youth. A weary retrospect, alas! A sad history filled with pages I would dearly love not to revisit. But who hasn't grown tired? Who hasn't fallen? And who escapes that struggle without scars?"

His head dropped onto his chest. The young man's heart bowed humbly and sorrowfully before that Throne where Wisdom, Love, and Pity reside, offering a silent confession.

"What matters fame or poverty!" he thought. "If I marry the woman I've chosen, may I possess the strength and will to remain true to her and make her happy? If I have children, pray God teach me to speak and act truthfully among them, leaving them an honest name. There are no splendors awaiting my marriage. Does my life deserve any? I begin a new phase; may it be better than the last. I pray Heaven!"

As the train pulled into Tunbridge, Pen finished his reflections. He handed the newspaper to his neighbor, exchanged a polite farewell, and noted that the foreign clergyman in the opposite corner remained steadfastly glued to his book. Pen jumped from the carriage, clutching his carpet-bag, resolutely prepared to face his fate.

A fly whisked him rapidly from the station to Lady Clavering's house. During the ride, Arthur composed a speech in his head, intending to address Blanche. It was, in his own estimation, a virtuous, honest, and well-meaning oration—the best a man of his disposition could muster under the circumstances. Its gist was simple: *"Blanche, I fail to grasp the meaning of your last letter, nor whether my fair and frank proposal remains acceptable. You likely know why I must forego the worldly advantages a union with you offers—advantages I could not accept without feeling dishonored. If you doubt my affection, I am ready to prove it. Summon Smirke, and let us marry immediately. With all my heart, I promise to keep my vow, cherish you through life, and be a true, loving husband."*

Upon arriving at the hall door, Arthur was greeted by a domestic he didn't recognize. The man appeared surprised by the arrival of a gentleman carrying a carpet-bag but made no move to take it.

"Her Ladyship is not at home, sir," the man announced.

"I am Mr. Pendennis," Arthur replied. "Where is Lightfoot?"

"Lightfoot has gone," the servant answered. "My Lady is out, and my orders were..."

"I hear Miss Amory's voice in the drawing-room," Arthur interrupted, spotting the source of the melody. "Take the bag to a dressing room, please."

Ignoring the porter, he marched straight toward the apartment. As the door swung open, a warble of melodious notes floated out. Their Little Siren was at the piano, pouring her soul into the instrument with all her might and fascination. Master Clavering slept soundly on the sofa, indifferent to the music. Seated beside Blanche was a gentleman completely entranced by her performance—a piece of passionate, melancholy beauty.

At the sound of the opening door, the stranger startled upright with a "Hullo!" The music cut off abruptly with a small shriek from the singer. Frank Clavering woke from the sofa, and Arthur stepped forward, greeting the intruder warmly.

"Well, Foker! How do you do, Foker?"

Arthur glanced at the piano. Beside Miss Amory lay exactly the same purple-leather box he had seen in Harry's hand three days prior, outside a jeweler's shop in Waterloo Place. It was open, revealing a magnificent serpentine bracelet coiled around a white satin cushion, topped with a blazing ruby head and a diamond tail.

"How de-do, Pendennis?" Foker asked.

Blanche made several shrugging motions, signaling unrest and agitation. She covered the bracelet with her handkerchief, then rose, her hand trembling violently, to greet Pen.

"How is dearest Laura?" she asked.

The sight of Foker's face—looking up from his profound mourning with an expression both pitiful and bewildered—was something the reader must imagine for themselves. So too was the face of Master Frank Clavering, who surveyed the three figures with an air of supreme knowingness, managed to exclaim, "Here's a jolly go!" before vanishing with a snicker.

Pen had held his composure until that moment. But seeing Foker, whose ears and cheeks burned with shame, Arthur suddenly erupted into a fit of wild, loud laughter that terrified Blanche far more than any solemn display could have.

"And this was the secret, was it? Don't blush and turn away, Foker, my boy. Why, man, you are a pattern of fidelity. Could I stand between Blanche and such constancy? Could I stand between Miss Amory and fifteen thousand a year?"

"It is not that, Mr. Pendennis," Blanche declared with great dignity. "It is not money, it is not rank, it is not gold that moves me. It is constancy, it is fidelity, it is a whole trusting, loving heart offered to me—that I treasure. Yes, I treasure it!"

She reached for her handkerchief, then paused, realizing what lay beneath it. "I do not disown, I do not disguise. My life is above disguise. To him on whom it is bestowed, my heart must forever be bare. That I once thought I loved you... yes, I thought I was beloved by you, I own! How I clung to that faith! How I strove, prayed, and longed to believe it! But your

Part of Mr. Strong's business in life was to procure this money, and the rest was to ensure it reached the right hands without scandal. But tonight, the business belonged to Pen, whose sole objective was to secure a certain fortune for a specific purpose: the procurement of a bride.

Pen fumed and fretted until the arrival of the evening train to London, a journey that felt like a full half-hour—or perhaps six hours, depending on one's mood. Even this immense interval was finally conquered. The train arrived, sped on, and the London lights came into view. Just as the carriage was settling, a gentleman who had forgotten his carpet-bag in the train rushed out, hailed a cab, and commanded, "Drive as hard as you can go to Jermyn Street." The cabman, though a respectable hansom driver, graciously accepted the gratuity placed in his hand. Meanwhile, Pen sprinted up the stairs to Lady Rockminster's apartments.

Laura was alone in the drawing-room, reading by the lamp with a pale face. That pale complexion lifted slightly when Pen burst through the door.

"May we follow him?" someone whispered.

Great moments in life are often indistinguishable from ordinary ones; your fate is frequently sealed in a word or two. A single glance from the eyes, a mere pressure of the hand, or a quiver of the lips can decide destiny, even when silence reigns supreme.

When Lady Rockminster, having completed her post-dinner nap, rose to enter her sitting-room, we may proceed with her ladyship.

"Upon my word, young people!" were the first words she uttered, while her attendant cast wondering glances over her shoulder. And well might she exclaim so, and well might the attendant stare; for the young people were in an attitude. Pen occupied a position familiar to every young lady who has heard tell of it, seen it, hoped for it, or at the very least deserves to witness it.

In short, the moment Pen entered, he approached Laura, whose pale face showed no time to utter, "What, back so soon?" Seizing her outstretched, trembling hand just as she rose from her chair, he fell to his knees before her and declared quickly, "I have seen her. She has engaged herself to Harry Foker—and—and... Now, Laura?"

The hand pressed tight; the eyes beamed a reply; the quivering lips answered, though speechless. Pen's head sank into the girl's lap as he sobbed, "Come and bless us, dear mother," while arms as tender as Helen's once more enveloped him.

It was precisely at this juncture that Lady Rockminster entered, declaring, "Upon my word, young people! Beck! Leave the room. What do you want poking your nose in here?"

Pen sprang up with looks of triumph, still clutching Laura's hand. "She is consoling me for my misfortune, ma'am," he explained.

"What do you mean by kissing her hand? I don't know what you'll be doing next." Pen promptly kissed her Ladyship's hand.

"I have been to Tunbridge," he continued, adopting a tragic air, "and seen Miss Amory. Upon my return, I discovered that a villain has transplanted me in her affections."

"Is that all? Is that what you were whimpering on your knees about?" the old lady demanded, growing angry. "You might have kept the news until tomorrow."

"Yes—another has superseded me," Pen lamented. "But why call him a villain? He is brave, constant, young, wealthy, and beautiful."

"What stuff are you talking, sir?" cried the old lady. "What has happened?"

"Miss Amory has jilted me and accepted Henry Foker, Esq.," Pen reported. "I found her warbling ditties to him as he lay at her feet. Presents had been accepted, vows exchanged—all within the last ten days. Harry was merely old Mrs. Planter's rheumatism, which kept dearest Laura out of the house. He is the most constant and generous of men. He promised the living of Logwood to Lady Ann's husband and gave her a splendid present upon her marriage; then he rushed to fling himself at Blanche's feet the instant he found himself free."

"And so, as you can't get Blanche, you put up with Laura; is that it, sir?" asked the old lady.

"He acted nobly," Laura said softly.

"I acted as she bade me," Pen replied. "Never mind *how*, Lady Rockminster; but to the best of my knowledge and power. And if you mean that I am not worthy of Laura, I know it, and pray Heaven to better me. If the love and company of the best and purest creature in the world can aid me, at least I shall have these to help me."

"Hm, hm," replied the old lady, looking with a somewhat appeased air at the young couple. "It is all very well; but I should have preferred Bluebeard."

Now, Pen, wishing to divert the conversation from a theme growing painful to those present, recalled his interview with Huxter in the morning and Fanny Bolton's affairs, which he had momentarily forgotten amidst the excitement of his own crisis. He recounted how Huxter had elevated Fanny to the rank of wife and the terrors regarding the arrival of his father. He described the scene with considerable humor, dwelling especially on Fanny's coquetry and her irrepressible desire to captivate mankind. His underlying message was clear: "See, Laura, I was not so guilty in that little affair; it was the girl who made love to me, and I who resisted. Since I am no longer present, the little siren practices her arts upon others. Let that transaction be forgotten, if you please; or visit me with a very gentle punishment for my error."

Laura understood his meaning beneath the eagerness of his explanations. "If you did any wrong, you repented, dear Pen," she said. "And you know," she added, with meaningful eyes and blushing cheeks, "that I have no right to reproach you."

"Hm!" grumbled the old lady. "I should have preferred Bluebeard."

"The past is broken away. The morrow is before us. I will do my best to make your morrow happy, dear Laura," Pen vowed. His heart was humbled by the prospect of his happiness; it stood awestruck in the contemplation of her sweet goodness and purity. He liked his wife better because she had admitted to that passing feeling for Warrington and laid bare her generous heart to him.

And she? Very likely she was thinking, *"How strange it is that I ever should have cared for another! I am vexed almost to think I care for him so little, am so little sorry that he is gone. Oh, in these past two months, how I have learned to love Arthur! I care about nothing but Arthur; my waking and sleeping thoughts are of him; he is never absent. And to think that he is to be mine, mine! That I am to marry him, and not serve as his servant as I expected only this morning—I would have gone down on my knees to Blanche to beg permission to live with him. And now—Oh, it is too much. Oh, mother! Mother, that you were here!"*

Indeed, she felt as if Helen were there—physically present, though invisible. A halo of happiness beamed from her. She moved with a different step, blooming with new beauty. Arthur noticed the change, and the sharp-eyed old Lady Rockminster remarked upon it.

"What a sly demure little wretch you have been," she whispered to Laura, while Pen laughed loudly, recounting his story about Huxter. "And how you have kept your secret!"

"How are we to help the young couple?" asked Laura. Of course, Miss Laura felt an interest in all young couples, for generous lovers always cherish other lovers.

"We must go and see them," said Pen.

"Of course we must go and see them," agreed Laura. "I intend to be very fond of Fanny. Let us go this instant. Lady Rockminster, may I have the carriage?"

"Go now! Why, you stupid creature, it is eleven o'clock at night. Mr. and Mrs. Huxter have their nightcaps on, I dare say. And it is time for you to go. Good night, Mr. Pendennis."

Arthur and Laura begged for ten more minutes. "We will go to-morrow morning, then. I will come and fetch you with Martha," Lady Rockminster decided.

"An earl's coronet," said Pen, clearly pleased with the prospect, "will have a great effect in Lamb Court and Smithfield. Stay—Lady Rockminster, will you join us in a little conspiracy?"

"How do you mean, conspiracy, young man?"

"Will you please feign illness to-morrow? And when old Mr. Huxter arrives, will you let me call him in? If he is put into a good humor by the notion of attending a baronet in the country, what influence won't a countess have on him? When he is softened, when he is quite ripe, we will break the secret upon him, bring in the young people, extort the paternal benediction, and finish the comedy."

"A parcel of stuff," said the old lady. "Take your hat, sir. Come away, miss. There—my head is turned another way. Good night, young people."

Who knows but the old

Time, that relentless editor, had moved our cast of characters forward by a full month. In this interim, a significant portion of our familiar troupe had reconvened at the very provincial town where our saga first unfolded. Among them was Frederic Lightfoot, formerly the *maitre d'hôtel* to Sir Francis Clavering, who had recently secured a promotion of sorts. Having formally petitioned the local nobility and gentry of the county, he announced his acquisition of the "Clavering Arms," a establishment of noted comfort and antiquity. His public manifesto declared that the historic inn had undergone a renovation of the highest caliber, boasting spacious stabling for hunters riding the Dumplingbeare hounds, a newly fitted billiard room, and cellars stocked with the finest vintages, curated without fiscal restraint by C.L. Furthermore, he assured commercial travelers that the pricing structure had been meticulously adjusted to align with the prevailing spirit of economy.

Indeed, the atmosphere within the Clavering Arms was electric. The gateway had been freshly painted, the coffee-room windows gleamed with holiday cheer adorned by Christmas holly, and the Petty Sessions were convened in the card-room of the former Assembly Hall. The traditional Farmers' Ordinary drew larger crowds than ever, delighted by Mrs. Lightfoot's culinary innovations. Her Indian curries and Mulligatawny soup became instant hits, earning high praise from Major Stokes of Fairoaks Cottage, Captain Glanders, and other local dignitaries. These dishes were savored repeatedly at private gatherings and the grand dinner celebrating the inauguration of the Clavering Institute's reading-room. The event was chaired by Sir Francis Clavering, seconded by the Reverend Dr. Portman, with the Vice-Chairmanship ably handled by Barker, Esq.—a man of enterprise who headed the local ribbon factory and directed the Clavering and Chatteris branch of the Great Western Railway, whose tracks were currently being laid with industrious fervor.

Local press, specifically the *Chatteris Champion, Clavering Agriculturist, and Baymouth Fisherman*, a publication renowned for its unwavering loyalty to the British oak and its suitability for advertising, weighed in on the social shifts. They reported that rumors swirled regarding Arthur Pendennis's withdrawal from parliamentary candidacy. Whispers suggested that should Sir Francis Clavering's declining health necessitate a resignation, the seat would pass to a young man of colossal wealth and high aristocratic lineage, soon to wed a lady closely tied to the Clavering Park family. Indeed, Lady Clavering and Miss Amory had arrived for the Christmas festivities, promising a New Year's season of unprecedented celebration.

Through these announcements, the reader may deduce the events of our brief hiatus. While Lady Rockminster grumbled slightly at Laura's preference for Pendennis over her suitor Bluebeard, those privy to the latter's secrets knew the young woman had no other option. The kindly dowager, seeing her ward fulfill the ultimate destiny of young ladies, was secretly pleased. That same night, she confided in her maid, Mrs. Beck—a woman thoroughly briefed by Martha of Fairoaks—who reacted with delight. "Mr. Pendennis's income is substantial," Beck calculated aloud, adding projections for the railway expansion. "Miss Bell has her share, and perhaps more later. We shall manage comfortably. I shall speak to my nephew, George Pynsent, now Chief Secretary of the Tape and Sealing Wax Office, and arrange for Mr. Pendennis to secure a position. Tomorrow, Beck, deliver my compliments to Major Pendennis; I shall visit him at one o'clock." With a resigned mutter, the old lady added, "The Major must be reconciled, and he must ensure his fortune passes to Laura's children."

True to her word, at precisely one o'clock, Dowager Lady Rockminster presented herself at Major Pendennis's residence. The Major, expecting news of a broken engagement between Pen and Miss Amory, received his visitor with courtly bows and genuine warmth. He had just dispatched a letter to his uncle via his new servant, Mr. Frosch, explaining that Miss Amory had happily united with Harry Foker, a match yielding fifteen thousand pounds annually. "Tatham informs me," Pen's note read, "that he has sold three of my Fairoaks fields to the Railway Company at a premium. More details await our reunion."

Lady Rockminster, however, possessed different tidings. "I trust you appreciate my kindness in delivering this news," she stated gracefully. "How well you look! How benevolent you have always been toward that young man!" When the Major expressed relief at the resolution, noting Pen's increased fortune, she gently steered the conversation toward consolation. "We must secure him a wife," she urged. The Major's mind immediately grasped the true purpose of her embassy.

No lengthy negotiation was required. Given the mutual wishes of Pen and his mother, and bolstered by Lady Rockminster's subtle hints—which carried immense weight given her age and status—the Major was swiftly persuaded. Overcome by her determined graciousness and evident affection for Laura, he agreed. During a fleeting moment when the Major lamented that Pen was "throwing himself away," Lady Rockminster delivered a passionate defense of Miss Bell, declaring her a paragon fit for a king. Instantly, the Major's admiration shifted entirely to the young lady.

Thus, when Herr Frosch summoned Miss Bell and Arthur Pendennis to the Major's presence, Laura appeared radiant, clinging to Pen's arm. The Major exchanged cordial greetings with both, showering Laura with additional attention that elicited a deeper blush. "Happy blushes!" the narrator observed, hoping future audiences might share such luminous joy.

With Pen retreating amicably and Blanche bestowing her affections upon her fiancé, Harry Foker, a wave of happiness swept through Lady Clavering's household. The good Begum, unseen for years, rejoiced alongside Blanche. Foker, eager to shorten his period of mourning, pressed for the wedding date. Lady Clavering returned from Tunbridge Wells, and milliners and jewelers sprang into action. Even Sir Francis, buoyed by his wife's good humor, reconciled fully with her, returning to London to preside over his own table and resume his pastimes at the billiard rooms and gaming houses.

One evening, dining at Grosvenor Place with Major Pendennis and Arthur, they encountered Mr. James Morgan, now serving as major-domo. Dressed in black, he offered them champagne with grave politeness. The Chevalier Strong was also present, recounting his continental adventures in high spirits. Under his breath, Strong whispered to Arthur, "My Lady invited me. That fellow Morgan looked as black as thunder when I entered. He is no good here. I shall depart first and meet you at Hyde Park Gate." As the Major departed, Morgan assisted him with his coat, muttering about accepting a temporary role with the Clavering family while avoiding eye contact.

"I have a paper of yours, Mr. Morgan," the Major noted.
"Feel free to show it to Sir Francis, sir," Morgan replied, eyes downcast. "Thank you for your kindness, Major."

Arthur overheard the exchange and the look of hatred accompanying it. Pretending to forget his handkerchief, he fled upstairs. Finding Blanche still lingering near her "siren" (Foker), Pen exchanged a meaningful glance with her. Upon retrieving his handkerchief and exiting, Blanche called out, "Oh, Arthur—Mr. Pendennis! I need you to tell dear Laura something!"

"What?" she asked, closing the door against prying ears.
"Have you told Harry? Does Morgan know everything?"
"I know it," she admitted.
"Have you told Harry?"
"No, no. Won't you betray me?"
"Morgan will," Pen insisted.
"No, he won't. I've promised him—*n'importe*. Wait until after our marriage... Oh, how wretched I am!" she exclaimed, her earlier gaiety vanishing.
Arthur pleaded, "Tell him tonight! It is not your fault. He will forgive anything."
"And give her this—" Blanche handed him a note—*"Il est là"*—with my love. Please ask if she can meet me at Madame Crinoline's at half-past three, provided Lady Rockminster can spare her. I'd love to drive with her in the park." Singing softly, she slipped inside as Morgan ascended the stairs.

As Pen descended to rejoin his uncle, Blanche's piano erupted into brilliant music. Arthur recounted the encounter. "What is to be done?" the Major asked.
"Leave it alone," the old gentleman shrugged. "Let us be thankful we are out of the business and let those concerned handle it."
"I hope to heaven she tells him," Pen worried.
"She'll take her own course," the Major dismissed. "Miss Amory is a wide-awake girl; she plays her own cards. I'm glad you're out of it."

Just then, Strong attempted to interject, but the Major deftly changed the subject to the moonlight on Apsley House and the weather, steering clear of the scandal. He clung to his nephew's arm, walking down St. James's Street, reiterating his advice to avoid further meddling. "It nearly cost you dearly, sir. Take my counsel."

Outside, Strong's cloak and cigar were visible nearby. Laughing jovially as they met, the Chevalier congratulated Pen on his marriage and his escape. "I know a certain party is an arrant little..." he trailed off mysteriously. "You
